<meta>This page follows the preliminary title leaf and begins the formal Preface by Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, establishing the historical and intellectual context for publishing Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts.</meta>

# PREFACE<note>1</note>

For more than eighty years, the Institute of France <note>A learned society comprising five academies, including the Académie Française</note> has possessed a collection of manuscripts by Leonardo da Vinci which, even today, although important extracts have been published on several occasions, remain almost entirely unpublished.

Just as work has been done to restore ancient texts to their integrity and purity, to show the thoughts of Pascal <note>Blaise Pascal (1623–1662), French mathematician and philosopher</note> in their true light, and as was recently undertaken at the Louvre Museum to better understand and make known Ancient Art<note>2</note>, I believed it was desirable to also undertake this for these precious documents, which bear on every page the imprint of a genius as vast as he was profound.

The man whom my father recently called the great initiator of modern thought<note>3</note> did not only carry Art to a height that Michelangelo, Raphael, and Correggio <note>Antonio da Correggio (1489–1534), a master of the High Renaissance</note> did not surpass; he was, by the admission of the most competent judges—among whom must first be cited Venturi<note>4</note> and Mr. Govi<note>5</note>—the precursor of the greatest scientists of the modern era, and he considerably enlarged the circle of all human knowledge.

The thought of such a man, informed of everything that had been done in every order of things before him as well as everything being done in his own time, a "disciple of experience" and a philosopher in every circumstance, is interesting to follow at each of its stages.

Leonardo's manuscripts contain, at times, more or less completed parts of treatises, which the painter, sculptor, anatomist, botanist, architect, civil engineer, and

---

1. To highlight the full importance that the complete publication of the unpublished manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci must offer to scholars, artists, and men of letters, I believed it would be in no way useless to first offer the reader supplementary information, specifically that which directly concerns the knowledge of the manuscripts themselves; I would have liked to accompany these with an exact account of the body of biographical documents that have been collected about their author—several of which have only recently come to light—a history as complete as I could have written of his manuscripts now scattered across various countries, an overview of the main publications resulting from the few attempts at reading these manuscripts that have been made, and finally, new considerations on the nature of Leonardo's genius and character. I have had to postpone the publication of this account, because the abundance of materials that I could not have dispensed with using to fulfill such a program was great, and I have only had a limited space available here.
2. See: *The Criticism of Ancient Sculptures* <note>original: "La Critique des sculptures antiques"</note>, by Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, extract from the *Archaeological Review* <note>original: "Revue archéologique"</note> (Didier Academic Library).
3. *Philosophy in France in the 19th Century* <note>original: "La Philosophie en France au XIXᵉ siècle"</note>, by Félix Ravaisson.
4. *Essay on the Physico-Mathematical Works of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Essai sur les ouvrages physico-mathématiques de Léonard de Vinci"</note>, by J.-B. Venturi, Year V (1797).
5. *Essay on the Works of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Saggio delle Opere di Leonardo da Vinci"</note>, 1872, *Leonardo as Man of Letters and Scientist* <note>original: "Leonardo letterato e scienziato"</note>, by G. Govi.

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, detailing Leonardo da Vinci's habit of mirror-writing and the history of his manuscripts after his death.</meta>

# PREFACE

military man, scholar<note>1</note>, etc., he proposed to compose—for the instruction he was to direct at the Academy of Milan, and perhaps also for later publication—notes of all kinds taken day by day. These covered the readings, observations, experiments, and reflections that occupied his mind, as well as public events or private matters of which he wished to keep a precise record.

This habit of noting everything, of writing everything down, was considered by Leonardo to be as important for the artist as for the scientist, who can establish nothing certain except by comparing the results given by experiments repeated several times; thus, he expressly advises this practice to painters on several occasions.

For him, it can be said that he studied art and science simultaneously, as well as the teachings of his predecessors and nature in its phenomena and its laws. Finally, he utilized in his notes and treatises the marvelous talent he had acquired early on for tracing, with a few strokes of his "ineffable hand" <note>original: "ineffable main"</note>—as his friend the friar Lucas Paciolo <note>Luca Pacioli (c. 1447–1517), a mathematician and collaborator of Leonardo</note> called it—the spirit and principle of each thing; thus, everywhere, with very few exceptions, the text of his manuscripts is accompanied by explanatory drawings.

These writings and drawings by Leonardo da Vinci were, as is well known, traced in reverse of the ordinary direction. Leonardo was left-handed, as Paciolo stated regarding the figures Leonardo drew for his book, but that was probably not the only reason he made it a habit to write almost always in a way that could not be read otherwise—to use the expressions of the same author—"than with a mirror or by looking at the paper from the back against the light." He was not the only one among his contemporaries to use this method; for instance, Sabba da Castiglione also wrote from right to left. Leonardo, like any man of superior merit, had envious detractors; he said so himself: as an inventor, people tried to portray him as ignorant of the works of others. To express it much as he did: narrow and routine-minded people on one hand, and on the other, adventurers riding out against everything that had previously allowed for the establishment of those rules which—by determining the limit of the possible and the impossible—prevent the seeker from falling into despair and melancholy, took pleasure in exaggerating what he had said to react against abuses and paradoxes. They used his own expressions to represent him as a charlatan or a madman. When he spoke, at least, his persuasive eloquence gave his ideas their full value; but to allow his papers to be seen with incompletely expressed thoughts, unfinished drafts, and projects for inventions of all kinds, would have been to expose himself to calumny and theft.

However, when Leonardo recognized that death would not leave him the time to give his writings their definitive form, he desired that at least one of his best and dearest disciples, Francesco Melzi <note>original: "François de Melzo"</note>, should allow posterity to benefit from the seeds he had gathered. Indeed, by his will dated April 22, 1518, before Easter, he bequeathed to this Melzi "each and every book" <note>original: "tous et chacun des livres"</note> that he possessed at the time of his will, "as well as the other instruments and portraits relating to his art and the industry of painters." Now, everything leads us to believe that Leonardo had the complete collection of his manuscripts with him when he died, that in 1520 Francesco Melzi brought them to his villa in Vaprio, and that, while doing what he could during his life to ensure their contents were appreciated at their true value—by means of copies which were the origin of the publication known as the *Treatise on Painting*—he kept all the original texts

***

1. On Leonardo's erudition, see among recent publications: *Leonardo da Vinci and his Library. Notes of a bibliophile* <note>original: "Leonardo da Vinci e la sua Libreria. Note di un bibliofilo"</note>, by the Marquis G. d’Adda, Milan, 1873. (*Edition of only 75 copies, not for commercial sale* <note>original: "Édit. di soli 75 esemplari, fuori di commercio"</note>), and a *Study on the botanical knowledge of Leonardo da Vinci* by Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, regarding the *Conjectures relative to a Bust of Beatrice d’Este* by Mr. Courajod, published by Rapilly, 5, quai Malaquais.

<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from the strategic military movements in Italy to the administrative and cultural policies of the French Directory and Napoleon Bonaparte regarding the occupation of Lombardy and the acquisition of art.</meta>

# PREFACE

"The <term>Executive Directory<note>The five-man committee that held executive power in France from 1795 to 1799, following the Reign of Terror.</note></term> to citizen Saliceti, Government commissioner to the army of Italy:

"Notes that were delivered to the government suggested that the sum of fifty million could be extraordinarily imposed upon Lombardy. You judged that you should only set it at twenty. The Directory urges you to review these matters with care. Contributions must certainly not be raised to an exorbitant rate, but they must compensate us for part of the costs of the war. If you do not believe you can add to this contribution in cash, you must at least gather hemp for our navy and silk for our factories; certainly, the Duke of Parma is proportionally much more heavily taxed than Lombardy, and yet our relations with Spain—very friendly relations—militated in favor of the latter."

->Signed at the minute by Carnot, Le Tourneur, and Reubell¹.<-

The victory of the French was regarded then by the major part of the Milanese population, not as a defeat, but as a deliverance, in exchange for which they owed a just return of gratitude. Indeed, at first, it was with demonstrations of public joy that our arrival was welcomed: "In all of Lombardy and especially in Milan, public spirit was making rapid progress²." "In Milan, the entry of the French was a festival for the people, who seem imbued with their rights³."

It is true that almost immediately after, troubles arose. Bonaparte wrote regarding this on June 7 (<term>19 Prairial<note>A month in the French Revolutionary calendar, corresponding here to June 7, 1796.</note></term>) from the headquarters at Peschiera:

> I left Milan on the 5th to go to Lodi. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
> I left this city as I had entered it, amidst the applause of an entire gathered people; I was far from thinking that this joy was feigned, that plots were already being woven and a cowardly treason was on the point of breaking out.

Such reversals of opinion are frequent in some regions, and there is reason to believe that on this occasion, rather than the felony and duplicity of an entire people, there was the new influence exerted by a party of disgruntled individuals and dismissed officials, once the leader of the French army had left Milan and there remained in that city only representatives of our country who had less prestige than he and of whom some, it must be admitted, gave rise to just grievances.

The commander-in-chief of the army of Italy took a very great interest in the arts and sciences; the Directory wanted to make Paris a center of enlightenment, a common meeting place for scholars and artists. And to achieve this goal, they had obtained, through treaties concluded with previously defeated powers, the surrender and shipment to the republican capital of a great number of monuments and objects of science and art; they sought even more to attract men eminent for their knowledge or their talent. Masterpieces long neglected were being restored, and artists and scholars were surrounded with honors and marks of attention. "While the stripped sovereign was granted the ability to take copies of the originals reserved for France, delicate means were employed to restore masterpieces that were wasting away, neglected under the sky that had seen them bloom; they were snatched from the insults of men and time, and the *Madonna of Foligno* by Raphael and his immortal *Transfiguration* were restored to their original freshness⁴."

4. *Historical Essay on the services and scientific works of Monge*, by Dupin; Paris 1819, in-4° and in-8°.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's exploration of the French administration in Italy. It transitions from the financial levies discussed on the previous page to the strategic "cultural conquest"—the systematic collection of art and scientific talent to bolster the prestige of the French Republic.</meta>

# PREFACE 5

On May 16 (<term>27 Floréal<note>The eighth month of the French Revolutionary calendar, named after the Latin word for flower.</note></term>) Year IV, the Government Commissioner, Saliceti, received the following observations from Paris:

> The Directory is far from blaming the suspension of arms granted to the Duke of Parma. . . . . . .
> We have seen with satisfaction that in the midst of the great and powerful interests that occupy you, the Arts receive your particular attention. To adorn the Republic with spoils of this kind is to truly immortalize and enrich it. An even more beautiful conquest would be that of celebrated scholars and artists; if you cannot determine them to adopt France as their homeland, let them at least learn that we love them and that our protection is guaranteed to them¹.

At the same time, Bonaparte received a letter from the Directory, written in the same spirit, in which they particularly recommended to him, after the capture of Milan, the astronomer Oriani—a recommendation renewed by another letter on the following June 11 (<term>23 Prairial<note>The ninth month of the French Revolutionary calendar, associated with the harvest.</note></term>), signed by Carnot, Le Tourneur, and L.-M. Revellière-Lépeaux².

On May 24 (<term>5 Prairial</term>), Bonaparte consequently wrote a letter from Milan to Oriani which appeared in the *Moniteur*, the majority of which follows:

> The sciences which honor the human spirit, the arts which embellish life and transmit great actions to posterity, must be specially honored in free governments. All men of genius, all those who have obtained a distinguished rank in the <term>Republic of Letters<note>The long-distance intellectual community in the late 17th and 18th centuries that fostered the exchange of ideas among scholars.</note></term>, are French, whatever the country that saw them born. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
> I invite the scholars to gather and propose to me their views on the means to be taken, or the needs they might have, to give the sciences and the fine arts a new life and a new existence. All those who wish to go to France will be welcomed with distinction by the Government. The French people place a higher price on the acquisition of a learned mathematician, a painter of reputation, or a distinguished man, whatever profession he practices, than on the richest and most abundant city.
> Be then, citizen, the organ of these sentiments among the distinguished scholars found in the Milanese territory.
-> Signed: BUONAPARTE. <-

After the liberating conquest of Milan, the removal of objects of science and art intended for the Museum and the libraries of Paris—which they wished to make into a second Rome, a second Athens—was instituted in a regular manner, as much as the laws of war could allow.

In effect, at the same time that Bonaparte and Saliceti addressed a proclamation "to the people of Lombardy," they published two decrees: the first aimed to suppress, replace, or maintain "the authorities," and the second was intended to regulate the removal and transport of the monuments of science and arts found "in the conquered cities." Here is the text of the latter:

### DECREE.

-> Milan, 30 Floréal Year IV (May 19, 1796). <-

-> IN THE NAME OF THE REPUBLIC, <-

The General-in-Chief of the Army of Italy and the Commissioner of the Executive Directory to the Army of Italy and the Alps,
Considering that it is important to ensure, by secure means, the monuments of science and arts found in the cities conquered by the armies, order the following:

---

1. *War Archives*, correspondence of the Directory Reg. G. 2, folio 47.
2. *War Archives*, Reg. G. 2, p. 65.

<summary>This page documents the French Directory's policy of "cultural conquest," where Napoleon Bonaparte and government commissioners were instructed to treat art, science, and intellectuals as essential spoils of war for the enrichment of France.</summary>
<keywords>Napoleon Bonaparte, Saliceti, Barnaba Oriani, cultural looting, French Directory, Milan, Republic of Letters, 1796, scientific monuments</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the administrative regulations regarding the "cultural conquest" of Italy. While the previous page established the Directory's philosophical desire to enrich the Republic with art and genius, this page outlines the specific legal articles governing the extraction, transport, and cataloging of these treasures.</meta>

# PREFACE

### ARTICLE FIRST,

There shall be an agent attached to the French Army in Italy to extract and transfer to the territory of the Republic any objects of art, science, etc., found in conquered cities, the selection and number of which shall have been submitted in advance to the General-in-Chief and the Government Commissioner, or to one of them.

->ART. 2.<-

The agent shall also be responsible for the extraction and transport of objects of art and science granted by the Italian powers by virtue of treaties and armistices concluded with the armies of the French Republic.

->ART. 3.<-

He may not perform any extraction without having drawn up an <term>official report<note>original: *procès-verbal*; a formal legal record of an action or observation.</note></term> and without being accompanied by a member of an authority recognized by the French army.

->ART. 4.<-

The agent shall be required to coordinate with the officer in charge of military convoys and transport in order to obtain the means to convey the seized objects to the territory of the French Republic, provided, however, that the service of the army never suffers as a result.

->ART. 5.<-

In the event that the transport agent is unable to provide the means for removal, the <term>war commissioners<note>Administrative officers responsible for logistics, supply, and overseeing the needs of the troops.</note></term> or local commanders shall have them provided; and, should it be impossible to obtain them through this channel, the agent himself shall be authorized to requisition horses or carriages in the city where the extractions are taking place.

->ART. 6.<-

The costs of removal, crating, and transport shall be paid to him; for this purpose, based on the statements of expenses he presents, the <term>Chief Administrative Officer<note>original: *commissaire ordonnateur en chef*; the official responsible for authorizing military expenditures.</note></term> of the army shall issue payment orders for the necessary sums.

->ART. 7.<-

The agent shall be personally responsible for the objects entrusted to his administration. In the event that objects are found damaged or broken, he shall be required, as justification, to record them in an official report.

### ADMINISTRATION.

->ART. 8.<-

The agent shall be required to maintain three registers:

1st. A <term>master register<note>original: *registre matrice*; a primary ledger used for comprehensive tracking.</note></term> in which all objects of art and science, etc., shall be inscribed without distinction;
2nd. A register in which shall be inscribed the paintings and statues, noting the height and width of the paintings and the proportions of the statues;
3rd. A register for scientific objects, such as manuscripts, machines, mathematical instruments, maps, etc.

### NOMINATION OF THE AGENT.

->ART. 9.<-

Based on the favorable testimonies given regarding the talents and patriotism of Citizen Jacques Pierre Tinet, an artist attached to the Tuscan legation, we appoint him agent to the Army of Italy, charged—

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical preface regarding the systematic collection of Italian artworks and scientific treasures during the Napoleonic campaigns. It specifically details the appointment of Jacques Tinet and the formation of the Commission of Sciences and Arts.</meta>

# PREFACE

to collect, in the conquered countries, the paintings, masterpieces, and other ancient monuments that shall be judged worthy of being sent to Paris.

->Done at the headquarters of Milan, on the 30th of Floréal, Year IV of the French Republic. <note>May 19, 1796. Floréal was the eighth month of the French Republican Calendar, named after the blossoming of flowers.</note><-

->BONAPARTE, SALICETI.<-

When the decree relating to the monuments of sciences and arts appeared, "Mr. Tinet" had already left Tuscany to join the Army of Italy. As early as May 10 (21 Floréal), Miot, minister of the French Republic to the Grand Duke of Tuscany in Florence, had sent him to Divisional General Berthier, Chief of Staff of the Army of Italy².

On May 20, Berthier wrote to Tinet regarding his appointment³.

Later, Jacques Tinet was added to the Commission appointed by the Government to proceed with the selection and extraction of monuments worthy of being carried to Paris. Here is a letter on this subject from the Directory, signed by Carnot, Le Tourneur, and Reubell, written on May 31, 1796 (12 Prairial, Year IV), to the government commissioner Saliceti:

> The Directory, having received, Citizen Commissioner, your dispatches of the 3rd and 4th of this month, will respond to them in detail....
> The Directory likewise approves the appointment made by the General-in-Chief and by you of a provisional agent for the extraction of objects of Sciences and Arts. This citizen may be joined to the artists and scholars come from Paris. You must recommend to both that they extract only what is truly beautiful and good; to remove without taste, without selection, is ignorance and almost <term>vandalism</term> <note>The term "vandalism" was relatively new at this time, coined during the French Revolution to describe the destruction of art.</note>, etc.

The addition of Tinet to the Commission is also mentioned in a letter dated from Milan on June 20 (2 Messidor) and addressed by the Government commissioners for the search of objects of sciences and arts to the Minister of Foreign Relations, Charles Delacroix:

> We are leaving for Bologna; Citizen Tinet has preceded us. The General-in-Chief and the Government Commissioner Saliceti added him to our group upon our arrival in Milan. They had provisionally appointed him agent of the arts. The Executive Directory confirmed his appointment in a letter which was read to us by the Government Commissioner⁴.

This document, for which I owe my knowledge to the obliging research of the Director of Archives at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Mr. Faugère, is signed: La Billardière, Berthollet, Thouin, Moitte, Berthelemy, Monge.

In the same letter, the commissioners classified the manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci among the most important objects for the sciences. The said Commission had been formed at the request of Bonaparte. Indeed, on May 9 (20 Floréal), the General-in-Chief wrote from the headquarters of Piacenza to the Directory:

> You will find attached the articles of the armistice that I have granted to the Duke of Parma.....
> ...... I reiterate to you the request for some well-known artists, who will take charge of the selection and the details of transport of rare things that we judge should be sent to Paris.

---

1. *Correspondence of Napoleon*, vol. I, no. 455.
2. *War Archives, Inventory of Correspondence.*
3. *Ibid.*
4. *Archives of Foreign Affairs.*
5. *Correspondence of Napoleon*, no. 367.

On October 5, 1796 (14 Vendémiaire, Year V), Bonaparte received the following letter from the Executive Directory:

> You were the first, Citizen General, to call the Government's attention to the monuments with which the conquest of Italy must enrich the French Museum, and you requested that scholars and artists be charged with collecting these precious spoils; thus, it will be pleasing for you to concur in the execution of a measure useful to the Commission we have appointed for this purpose.....

->Signed in the original minutes: CARNOT, REUBELL AND P. BARRAS <margin>1. *War Archives*, Reg. G. 2, p. 111.</margin><-

I have found in the archives of the Ministry of War the formal report <note>original: "procès-verbal"</note> which was drawn up at the moment of the extraction of the manuscripts from the Ambrosian Library, in accordance with Article 3 of the decree of May 19 (30 Floréal); here is the form in which a copy was sent to one of the signatories of said decree:

When the decree of May 19 <note>original: "30 floréal"</note> appeared in Milan, the commissioners had already been working elsewhere, but they did not arrive in this city until Tinet had already chosen and packed Leonardo’s manuscripts. Indeed, on one hand, the official list of the scientific and artistic objects taken from Milan to be transported to Paris—signed by Tinet and Saliceti, which appeared in the *Moniteur* of June 6 <note>original: "18 prairial"</note>—was followed by the following note, which leaves no room for two interpretations:

> The paintings and manuscripts of Milan were taken and sent to Paris by the art agent, appointed for this purpose by the General-in-Chief and the Government Commissioner. The objects that were in Parma and Piacenza were only noted by him. The General-in-Chief entrusted another person with their transport and shipment to Paris.

On the other hand, a letter from La Billardière, written in Lyon on October 21, 1796 (30 Vendémiaire, Year V), and of which Mr. Faugère was kind enough to send me a copy, contains the following passage, which is no less assertive:

> I greatly feared that, among the two crates I could not find, the one containing Leonardo da Vinci's portfolios <note>original: "cartons"</note> was among them. The information I gathered in Milan from the carpenter who had crated the objects chosen by Citizen Tinet before the Commission's arrival led me to believe that we did not have to regret the loss of the precious manuscripts it contained. Indeed, comparing the dimensions with those he had provided me, I confirmed my conjectures.

Attached to the bulletin of the letter from the administrative officer <term>Lambert<gloss>Lambert was the chief administrative officer (ordonnateur) of the Army of Italy.</gloss></term>, the following note is found:

He sent a copy of this same formal report to the Minister of War on May 31 <note>original: "12 prair."</note> informing him of the departure of the paintings, which left on May 28 <note>original: "9 prair."</note>, under the escort of a supernumerary lieutenant in the 15th brigade.

The Minister of War was Petiet; he only acknowledged receipt of the "descriptive report of the precious monuments sent from Milan to Paris" on June 20¹.

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the transfer of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts from Italy to France. # PREFACE

The official report <note>original: "procès-verbal"</note> seems to implicitly testify that during the taking of possession of the various objects listed therein, there was neither violence, nor surprise, nor protest; "all of which effects," it reads, "have been placed at the disposal of Citizen Tinet <note>Pierre-Jacques Tinet (1753–1803), a painter appointed by the French Directory to select artworks from conquered territories.</note> to have them crated and shipped."

The removal of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts was not due solely to the initiative and personal choice of the artist Tinet. If we are to believe one of the most authoritative literary collections of the end of the last century, an eminent French scholar—then president of the Class of Sciences and Arts of the Institute—had requested these manuscripts because he wished to examine them for a scientific purpose. It was as a result of this request that they finally began to publish extracts from these precious writings:

> Citizen Venturi, a skilled professor of physics at Modena, having stayed in France during the war that ravaged his country and having won the esteem and friendship of all the scholars, requested and obtained access to these manuscripts; having collected all that seemed worthy to him, he proposes to publish, etc.; meanwhile, Citizen Lalande <note>Joseph Jérôme Lefrançois de Lalande (1732–1807), a prominent French astronomer.</note> had asked him regarding the ashen light of the moon <note>Known today as earthshine, the faint glow of the dark side of the moon caused by sunlight reflecting off the Earth.</note>, for which he had been told that Vinci had found the cause long before Maestlin <note>original: "Mœstlinus"; referring to Michael Maestlin, the teacher of Kepler.</note>, to whom the credit had been given; he had even specifically requested Leonardo's manuscripts in Italy because of this subject; it was the occasion for this essay, wherein Citizen Venturi gives us various pieces, etc¹.

And Venturi himself said, when presenting the fragments he had detached from Leonardo's writings to the Institute: "Citizen Lalande, that most zealous astronomer, encouraged me to offer you these few essays now²."

The arrival of the manuscripts from Milan to Paris suffered some delays. Mention was made of their shipment in the *Moniteur* of June 6 (18 Prairial), as has already been seen regarding the role Tinet had to play. They were probably first sent to Tortona along with many other objects of art or science, a list of which was published in Millin's *Encyclopedic Magazine* a little later, on July 3 (15 Messidor)³.

The greater part of these objects, one read in that collection, is already at Tortona.
The commissioners write that they are sending the remainder to the same city, where everything will remain in storage until it is decided which will be the easiest and safest route to bring it to France.

From the moment the thirteen Leonardo manuscripts left Milan, their final destination was perfectly determined; that is to say, the *Codex Atlanticus* <note>original: "manuscrit Atlantique"</note>—reclaimed in 1815 for the Ambrosian Library—was assigned to the National Library, while it was decided that the other volumes would go to the library of the Institute.

The Government commissioners for the search of objects of science and arts gave notice of the transport of the thirteen manuscripts to the Minister of Foreign Relations through two different *inventories* <note>original: "états"</note>, preserved today in the archives of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, of which here are extracts:

1st Inventory of crates destined for the National Library, August 14, 1796 (27 Thermidor, Year IV)
"..... No. 19.... The portfolio of the works of Leonardo da Vinci."
2nd Inventory of crates destined for the National Institute..... "Crate 1.... Twelve small manuscripts by Leonardo da Vinci on the sciences."

---

1. *Magasin encyclopédique* <note>Encyclopedic Magazine</note> by Millin, Keeper of the Museum of Antiquities at the National Library, 2nd year, Vol. II, p. 145.
2. *Essai sur les ouvrages physico-mathématiques de Léonard de Vinci* <note>Essay on the Physico-Mathematical Works of Leonardo da Vinci</note>, by J.-B. Venturi, Year V (1797).
3. *Magas. encyclop.* 2nd year, Vol. II, p. 276.

# PREFACE

Before the month of August, toward the beginning of June, Millin’s *Encyclopedic Magazine* <note>original: "Magasin encyclopédique," a major scholarly journal of the era</note> already announced, on one hand, the dispatch of five manuscripts by Leonardo da Vinci, and on the other, as intended expressly for the National Library, that of the "manuscript of the works of Leonardo Vinci on *Mathematics* and *Mechanics*¹."

However, many weeks were still to pass before the objects of art and science, long since announced, arrived in Paris. On September 9 (23 Fructidor) <note>Fructidor was the twelfth month of the French Republican Calendar</note>, the Government Commissioners to the Army of Italy, Garrau and Saliceti, received a letter from the Directory signed by Carnot, Reubell, and P. Barras, the end of which follows:

> You have replied nothing until now to what we wrote to you regarding the prompt dispatch to France of the artistic productions which have been gathered in Italy for this purpose; however, the season is advancing and the transport of these precious effects would become very difficult if the time were delayed any longer; see to it, citizens, that the Directory does not have to return to the same subject in its correspondence with you ².

And on October 21 (30 Vendémiaire), in a letter from which I have already quoted a passage relating to Tinet and the search for manuscripts that for a moment were believed lost, La Billardière wrote to the Minister of Foreign Relations:

> I received, in recent days, a letter from my colleagues by which I learn that the Directory is very dissatisfied that the objects of art and science chosen by the Commission have not yet been delivered to Paris. As I have been charged with supervising this convoy, it is important that you be informed of the obstacles I have encountered...

Finally, on November 25, the crates containing our manuscripts and other objects arrived, and the *Official Journal* published a detailed notice of them on November 28, 1796 (Octidi, 8 Frimaire, Year V.)

In 1815, when the Allies took back the greater part of the objects of art and science which had been transported from foreign countries to Paris, the Austrian commissioner representing Italy wished to recover the Leonardo da Vinci manuscripts for Milan. Here is a note regarding the manner in which he proceeded toward the National Library, which I owe to the kindness of Mr. L. Delisle, current director of that establishment:

*Note of the manuscripts from the Ambrosian Library of Milan transported to the Royal Library in Paris.*

> A volume covered in leather, 13 by 29 1/2 [inches/units], of 398 leaves, containing various drawings of mechanics, hydraulics, hydrostatics, geometry, civil and military architecture, tactics, various machines and weapons of war, both bladed and firearms, among which are two drawings of a mortar and a trumpet, figure drawings, etc., by Leonardo da Vinci, with various annotations written in left-handed script <note>original: "mano mancina," referring to Leonardo's famous mirror-writing</note> by the same. Collected by Pompeo Leoni and donated by the Marquis Galeazzo Arconati of Milan to the Ambrosian Library, as per the commemorative plaque.
> Another 12 volumes, both large and small, by the same Leonardo da Vinci, of which one in folio covered in leather treats of light and shadow; the others contain various geometric figures etc. and various thoughts of the author.
> Joseph the Jew.... <note>Likely a reference to a manuscript of Flavius Josephus</note>
> The works of Virgil.
> A chronicle of the Popes...
> A Divine Comedy by Dante....
> Galileo Galilei, on the ebb and flow of the sea...
> Galileo Galilei, treatise on fortifications....

---

1. *Encyclopedic Mag.*, 2nd year, vol. I, p. 568.
2. *Archives of the Ministry of War*, Reg. G 2, p. 100.

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the restitution of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically detailing the 1835 receipt by the Austrian commissioner and the distinction between original manuscripts and copies.</meta>

# PREFACE

At the end of this note, one reads, in a different hand and a different ink:

> The undersigned acknowledges having received from the curators and administrators of the King’s Library the manuscripts listed in the statement above, with the exception of *nine* manuscript volumes by the hand of Leonardo da Vinci, which, according to the declaration of the curators, did not arrive at the King’s Library.

->**BARON DE OTTENFELS.**<-
->*Imperial and Royal Commissioner*<-

Paris, this 5th of October 1835.

In reading this note, one might believe at first glance that the Baron de Ottenfels had taken back from the National Library four "manuscript volumes by the hand of *Leonardo da Vinci*," namely:

1st The Atlantic Manuscript <note>The *Codex Atlanticus*, Leonardo's largest collection of drawings and writings</note>: "*One volume... of 398 sheets... collected by Pompeo Leoni, etc., as stated on the stone tablet*."
2nd 12 volumes, both large and small, "*another 12 volumes between large and small*," with "the exception of nine, which did not arrive at the King’s Library," in other words, three manuscripts besides the Atlantic, one of which was "an *in-folio bound in skin, which treats 'of light and shadows'*."

This would be an error. Indeed, in the brief description of this last item, it is easy to recognize the manuscript marked C by Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822), the first scholar to seriously study Leonardo's scientific work</note>, given by Guy (Guido) Mazzenta to Cardinal Federico Borromeo, who placed it in the Ambrosian Library when he founded it. Now, this manuscript has never ceased to be part of the library of the Institute since 1790; consequently, the volume taken back by the Austrian commissioner was a copy of our original manuscript, and it is highly likely that the two other volumes by Leonardo were likewise copies. According to Mr. Govi, these copies were required to take the place of the originals; if this were so, it was an abuse of force, similar to so many others that took place elsewhere, especially at the Louvre.

Besides the manuscripts of the library of the Institute, we still have in Paris a certain number of sheets containing both mirror writing <note>original: "texte à rebours," referring to Leonardo's famous habit of writing from right to left</note> and drawings by the hand of Leonardo da Vinci.

The Louvre Museum possesses the collection formerly belonging to Vallardi which, among a great number of pages, contains some by Leonardo's hand; one of the most beautiful drawings found there, a head of a man, was engraved by Mr. Leroy; the engraving was accompanied by a notice by Mr. F. Reiset.

Two detached sheets, with text and drawings, were exhibited in 1879 at the School of Fine Arts and described in the Exhibition Catalogue under numbers 33 and 35. The first, belonging to the Marquis de Chennevières, represents a hanged man dressed in a long robe; beside it, the description of the costume. The second, whose owner is Mr. A. Armand, contains studies of soldiers variously armed with captions.

A drawing of men operating a capstan <term>capstan: a vertical-axled rotating machine developed for use on sailing ships to multiply the pulling force of seamen</term>, with a few lines of mirror writing, was sold a few years ago by Mr. Auvray to Mr. Boilly the younger.

In Milan, the Ambrosian Library possesses once again, as we have just seen, the Atlantic manuscript, marked N by Venturi at the National Library.

The Atlantic is 65 centimeters high by 44 wide; it contains 399 sheets and 1750 drawings and has just been, as we have seen, the subject of an important publication in Milan. "It is composed," says Mr. Govi, of several volumes whose pages were separated and pasted onto large sheets of drawing paper; if one could

It also lists various Leonardo fragments and drawings held in French collections like the Louvre and private estates.</summary>

# PREFACE

"by detaching them and reuniting them according to their various formats, one might perhaps succeed in com-
« posing five or six small volumes¹."

Furthermore, one finds in the Trivulzio collection <note>A private aristocratic collection in Milan</note> a small manuscript that seems authorized to be identified: 1st with the 5th book described in the Arconati donation deed; 2nd with the manuscript called the *Treatise on Casting Artillery* <note>original: "Trattatello del fondere le artiglierie"</note>; 3rd with the one that Calvi designates under the name of *Archinto Codex* <note>original: "Codex archintianus"</note>; 4th with the "4. Small Manuscript with Vocabulary" <note>original: "Kleine Handschrift mit Vocabularium"</note> from the description by Dr. Jordan².

In Lugo, there is a volume sold to Mr. J. Manzoni by Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869), a mathematician and notorious book thief who stole many Leonardo pages</note>, and additionally, in Florence, Venice, and Turin, a few detached leaves.

In London, three volumes composed of drawings sometimes interspersed with texts, described by Doctor Rigollot in his *Catalogue of the Work of Leonardo da Vinci*, when in 1849 they were at the new palace of the British Crown or Buckingham House; one of these manuscripts, a folio of 236 leaves, bears in gold letters on the cover the words:

->Drawings by Leonardo da Vinci<-
->Restored by Pompeo Leoni<-
<note>original: "Disegni. di. Leonardo. da. Vinci. Restaurati. da. Pompeo. Leoni." Leoni was a 16th-century sculptor who reorganized Leonardo's loose papers into albums.</note>

This volume contains a large number of anatomical figures, one among others that John Chamberlaine engraved and published in 1812 and which was later reproduced by lithography at Brunswick in 1830 with the title *Anatomical Table of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Tabula Anatomica Leonardi Vincii"</note>, which was the cause, according to Mr. Govi³; of the erroneous opinion that the original was kept at the Wolfenbüttel library. The collection of Pompeo Leoni, which is, along with the Atlantic <note>The Codex Atlanticus</note>, one of the most important manuscripts of Leonardo, is today at Windsor, in the Queen's collection; the two other volumes mentioned above are also found there; the leaves have been pasted onto large sheets of a uniform format. The drawings contained in these collections have been largely photographed.

The British Museum possesses a manuscript by Leonardo, which, I believe, could only have been described by inadvertence as forming two distinct volumes by Abbot Ceriani, in the appendix of the posthumous brochure of Abbot Dozio, published by Abbot Joseph Prestinoni and reproduced by Mr. Jordan; this quarto-sized manuscript reportedly contains 283 leaves.

At the South Kensington Museum <note>now the Victoria and Albert Museum</note>, one finds four small volumes bequeathed in 1876 by J. Forster, regarded by those who have seen them as being by Leonardo's hand⁴.

In England still, the son of Lord Ashburnham possesses the portions of quarto manuscripts which were sold to his father by Libri.

And at Holkham, a small autograph folio by Leonardo is preserved, concerning the nature, weight, and movement of waters <note>The Codex Leicester, later owned by Bill Gates</note>.

Finally, in Germany, at Munich, Mr. L. Courajod told me that a few separate pages with drawings and text by Leonardo are to be found.

A few detached leaves have been sold, though I do not know where they are currently located; here is an overview:

In the catalogue of the public sale held in Paris by Libri on December 8, 1845, one reads under No. 295: *Original drawing by L. da Vinci, done in pen, and 2 quarto pages filled with autograph writings.*

And in the catalogue of the sale held in London on June 1, 1864, under No. 142-4.

3. *Essay, etc.*, G. Govi. *Leonardo as Man of Letters and Science* p. 7, col. 1.
4. See the journal *The Academy* Nov. 8, 1879, and *Leonardo*, quarto, London, 1880, by Jean-Paul Richter.

<meta>This page continues the detailed inventory of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically tracking those that have been lost, misplaced, or separated from major collections like the Ambrosian Library.</meta>

# PREFACE

A leaf with designs and writing from right to left, by Leonardo da Vinci, very rare.

N° 143-9. A sketch by Leonardo da Vinci, with writing in his autograph.

N° 144-2. Sketches and handwriting of Leonardo da Vinci.

N° 145-6. A leaf with sketches by Leonardo da Vinci.

Finally, it will be seen, regarding the description of Manuscript B of the Institute (p. 17), that a leaf appeared in the catalog of Mr. Garnier, a judge at Melle, in 1847.

Some volumes have been misplaced or lost:

1. The second book of the Arconati donation, a standard folio <note>A large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once</note> containing 8 unnumbered leaves, then a pagination of 120 leaves: 87 for the text, 3 blank, and the remainder consisting of various colored drawings¹.

This manuscript was likely no longer part of the Ambrosian Library <note>The historic library in Milan founded by Cardinal Federico Borromeo</note> when the French army entered Milan on May 15, 1796; at least nothing proves it was still there. Perhaps it had left in the same manner as another volume, the fifth from the donation, which will be discussed later.

2. The volume given by Guido Mazzenta <note>One of the brothers who initially recovered Leonardo's manuscripts from the Melzi estate</note> to the painter Ambrogio Figino, known as "the Vena"; its whereabouts are unknown since it appeared in Smith’s catalog printed in 1755.

3. The volume given by Ambrogio Mazzenta to Duke Charles-Emmanuel; Venturi thought this manuscript might be the same one an Englishman had copied by Francesco Ducci, a librarian in Florence. According to Mr. Govi, it may well have perished in the fires that consumed a large part of the Royal Library in Turin in 1667 and 1679.

4. A treatise on colors, which reportedly passed into the hands of the King of England at the same time as the treatise on anatomy currently kept in London—if a copyist's note is to be believed. This note is found on apocryphal <note>Works attributed to an author but of doubtful authenticity</note> writings and drawings by Leonardo kept at the Ambrosian Library, and may have been written around 1640. One can suppose, based on other examples, that the aforementioned treatise on colors was not a continuous work, but rather contained materials intended to form part of the *Treatise on Painting* <note>original: "Traité de la peinture"</note>; it is not impossible that it is part of the autograph papers of Leonardo currently kept in England.

5. The manuscript or the "few writings of Leonardo traced with the left hand, backwards <note>Referring to Leonardo’s famous mirror-writing</note>, concerning painting and the various manners of drawing and coloring," which a Milanese painter showed to Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian artists</note> around 1565 and later took to Rome.

6. The *Book of Drawings* concerning the handling of all kinds of weapons, both offensive and defensive, composed for Gentile dei Borri <note>Gentile dei Borri was a master of arms to the Duke of Milan, Ludovico Sforza</note>, of which Lomazzo speaks².

Many of the original leaves by Leonardo currently kept at Windsor <note>The Royal Collection at Windsor Castle</note> contain drawings of various weapons accompanied by writing; a certain number of these leaves perhaps once belonged to the book composed for Borro. However, Leonardo da Vinci also wrote for his own account on matters of war and probably intended to prepare materials for a treatise on the weapons of all eras; this is what Manuscript B of the Institute <note>The Institut de France in Paris, which holds twelve of Leonardo's codices</note> seems to prove to me. The Vallardi volume at the Louvre also contains drawings of weapons accompanied by captions, etc.

***

1. See the description of the Arconati donation deed (*The Amateur's Cabinet* <note>original: "Cabinet de l'Amateur"</note>, 1861-1862, p. 53).
2. Lomazzo, *Treatise on the Art of Painting, Sculpture, and Architecture* <note>original: "Trattato dell'arte della pittura, scultura ed architettura"</note>, vol. II, p. 274.

<meta>This page continues the preface's discussion regarding the provenance and cataloging history of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically addressing lost volumes and the transition to the current lettering system (A, B, C, etc.).</meta>

# PREFACE

7th. A manuscript which, according to a letter written by the painter Louis-Antoine David to Louis-Antoine Muratori <note>Ludovico Antonio Muratori (1672–1750) was a famous Italian historian and scholar.</note> on March 15, 1704, was being kept in Rome at that time.

Perhaps the original manuscript that Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari, the 16th-century biographer of Italian artists.</note> saw in the hands of a painter who was going to Rome with the intention of publishing it was not returned to Francesco Melzi <note>original: "François Melzo"; Melzi was Leonardo’s pupil and heir.</note>, and perhaps it is the same one that we thus find in Rome at the beginning of the last century.

It is only by error, I believe, that the manuscripts of Count Rezzonico have been counted as missing originals.

It has been claimed that some original manuscripts by Leonardo da Vinci, which were formerly part of the Ambrosian Library, were lost after the French entered Milan in 1796 through the fault of French agents; this is not the case. Indeed, Father Dozio ¹ has shown that before the arrival of the French agents in Milan, there were no other autograph writings by Leonardo at the Ambrosiana than those which are today in Paris and Milan, and which have only lost what Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869), a mathematician known for stealing ancient manuscripts.</note> stole from them.

It was also believed, based on an assertion by Libri, that the National Library had, at the time he wrote, yet other original manuscripts that can no longer be found; it seems certain that such documents were never part of that establishment. It is known, moreover, that Libri's citations are often inaccurate.

Nine of the twelve manuscripts at the Institute were described with some care in the deed of the Arconati donation in 1637, but nevertheless in a manner far too summary to give a sufficient knowledge of them. Since that time, the best inventory that has been drawn up of the manuscripts we possess is the one that Mr. Eug. Piot published in 1861, in the previously cited article in the *Amateur's Cabinet* <note>original: "Cabinet de l'amateur".</note>.

I will attempt here—while borrowing from the descriptions of the 1637 deed and the 1861 *Amateur's Cabinet* those terms which I see no reason to change—to present a table of each of our autograph collections that allows one to form a completely accurate idea of them; I will apply myself especially to detailing with precision the peculiarities presented by certain parts of these volumes which the facsimiles of the following publication will not reproduce: I mean the covers and a few pages without text or drawings.

When the twelve manuscripts of the Institute were taken from Milan, they had already received various distinguishing marks.

Shortly thereafter, they were marked again by Venturi: "so that one can," he said in the Memoir he published in 1797, "find in Leonardo's manuscripts the fragments I have extracted from them, each of the twelve volumes at the National Institute has been marked with a Roman letter A, B, C, etc., on the back of the first cardboard cover. I call 'N' the thirteenth volume which passed to the National Library."

Venturi being, of all those who have undertaken to decipher our precious documents, the scholar who has published the most methodical analysis of them, I believed I would do well to adopt the classification he made, rather than using the old designations. I am therefore going to describe the manuscripts following the order in which he arranged them.

-> 1°. — *Manuscript A.* <-

— Fourth book of the Arconati donation, third of the description by Bishop Bonsignori, formerly marked S followed by b and (1?), then by Venturi as A. Quarto <term>In-4°: a book size where the sheet is folded twice to form four leaves.</term>,

---

1. *On the Writings and Drawings of Leonardo da Vinci, posthumous Memoir of the Reverend Doctor G. Dozio, Milan 1871.* <note>original Latin/Italian title: "Degli Scritti e Disegni di Leonardo da Vinci..."</note>

<summary>The author clarifies the provenance of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, debunking myths of lost volumes and explaining the adoption of Venturi's alphabetical cataloging system.</summary>

# PREFACE

...composed of gatherings <note>original: "cahiers"; groups of folded sheets sewn together.</note> assembled in 16 leaves, 21 centimeters high and 14 wide, bound in white vellum in a wallet-style format with a small clasp, also in white vellum. On the exterior cover, before the first numbered leaf, a large capital **A** in ink is visible—the mark of Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822), an Italian physicist and the first to highlight the importance of Leonardo’s scientific work.</note>—which is repeated on the inside on a page of white paper attached to the cover. At the end of the manuscript, there is a loose sheet of white paper, followed by another glued to the inside of the cover. On the front of the loose sheet, written in the opposite direction of the manuscript text, one reads a small capital **S** in an old hand; the same letter is repeated on the back, and it is found again on the sheet attached to the cover, but this time followed by a **6** or, more likely, a lowercase **b**, then another small letter (an **l**?) and a period. In the upper middle of this side of the cover is a large number **4** followed by a period, which appears to be written over another number; the same mark appears on the outside. This is the sequence number formerly assigned to Volume A as part of Leonardo's manuscripts entering the Ambrosiana <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan, which originally held many of Leonardo's notebooks.</note>.

The words *Leonardo da Vinci* on the spine and *Leonar* on the cover are now almost entirely faded.

According to the 1637 deed of donation, Arconati's <note>Galeazzo Arconati, the nobleman who donated twelve Leonardo notebooks to the Ambrosiana in 1637.</note> fourth manuscript formerly contained 114 leaves, but today 51 are missing. Indeed, leaf 54 has been torn out, and so clumsily that the tearing must have been done in great haste; the photograph will reproduce the remains of the inner margin of this leaf; at the bottom, one can still see some letters (the end of the word *re* and perhaps *are*).

Each leaf is numbered on the front from 1 to 53 and from 55 to 64; this pagination is in Leonardo's own hand.

Regarding this matter, here are some precise details published previously by Mr. Ludovic Lalanne (currently the assistant curator at the Library of the Institut) and Mr. Bordier:

> These manuscripts are kept in a cabinet from which they are very rarely removed, for only well-known persons are granted the favor of seeing these precious relics. In 1848, during the investigation of the Libri affair <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869) was a mathematician and notorious manuscript thief who stole many pages from the Leonardo notebooks in Paris.</note>, we examined these twelve notebooks in detail, and we noticed that recent thefts had been committed in two of them. Several circumstances aided us in making this verification as easy as it was certain: 1. Leonardo da Vinci’s manuscripts had been, in 1797, the subject of a memoir published by the scholar Venturi; 2. Mr. Libri, who had consulted them many times, cited them often in his *History of the Mathematical Sciences in Italy*; 3. In Mr. Libri’s papers, a note was found indicating the number of leaves each notebook should contain. Thanks to all these means of verification, we were able to establish the following gaps: The volume labeled A in-quarto should have had at least 114 leaves according to Mr. Libri’s note; in the third volume of his *History of the Mathematical Sciences* published in 1840, he indeed cites leaves 71, 81, and even leaf 114. Today the manuscript ends at leaf 64, after which traces of tearing are visible, proving that several gatherings were removed. Additionally, leaf 54 has disappeared.

On a few leaves, one encounters "witnesses" <note>original: "témoins"; fragments of original paper that protrude slightly from the edge, often used to show the original size of the page before trimming.</note> that extend a few millimeters past the margin—for example, on the back of leaf 6 and leaf 10. Elsewhere, there are points along the extreme edge of the margin, similar to the stippling of a tracing—for example, on the front of leaf 41—or parts of drawings by Leonardo's hand, such as at the top of the front of leaf 61. From these clues, it can be concluded that the margins were trimmed very long ago.

Manuscript A is almost entirely unpublished; the faithful reproduction of the 126 pages it still contains begins following these observations.

sig 6

-> 2. — *Manuscript B.* <-

— Arconati’s third book, Bishop Bonsignori’s <note>Matteo Bonsignori, Bishop of Cassano, who owned several Leonardo manuscripts before they were donated to the Ambrosiana.</note> second, formerly marked with an **S** followed by an **a** and (1?). — Quarto size <term>in-4°: a book format where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves, roughly square in shape</term> composed of gatherings <note>original: "cahiers"; groups of folded sheets sewn together.</note> of 10 leaves, 23 centimeters high, 17 wide, bound in white vellum. On the spine of the cover, although faded, one can read the words *leonardo da vinci*, and on the front cover, the large **B** assigned by Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi, whose labeling system remains the standard for identifying Leonardo's notebooks (A, B, C, etc.).</note>. Similarly, on the back of the cover, on the leaf attached to it in the center, and then on the following leaf, is a mark that appears to be a small **x**. At the end of the manuscript, after the text, is a blank leaf in the middle of which one sees, oriented in the opposite direction of the text, a capital **S** similar to the one mentioned as being followed by a **b** and an **l** on the inside cover of Manuscript A. Here, the said **S** is followed by a lowercase **a**; the same mark appears on the sheet attached to the cover, then further down a crossed-out letter which was similar to the small letter (l?) following the **S b** of Manuscript A. Finally, the number **3** appears on both sides of the cover, which was the shelf number of Manuscript B at the Ambrosiana <note>The library in Milan where the manuscripts were housed before being taken to France by Napoleon's troops.</note>, along with Venturi’s **B** mark.

The leaves are numbered on the front <term>recto: the front side of a leaf</term> in large handwriting up to leaf 90. Manuscript B, which was already missing its first leaf in 1637 but then contained 122 others, was treated like Manuscript A, thus losing 38 leaves and retaining only 84.

Following the observations regarding the mutilation of Manuscript A which were reproduced in the notes, Mr. Ludovic Lalanne <note>A historian and librarian who played a key role in identifying the thefts of Guglielmo Libri.</note> recorded the following observations:

> Volume B (quarto) should have had 100 leaves, according to the note by Mr. Libri, who cited leaf 96 (leaf 95 is cited by Venturi) in the first volume of both editions of his *History of Mathematical Sciences* (1st edition, vol. I, p. 195; 2nd edition, 1838, vol. I, p. 208). Furthermore, a note by Mr. Fallot (who was sub-librarian of the Institute from 1834 until his death on July 6, 1836) bears the following mention: "The twelve volumes of Leonardo da Vinci could count as thirteen, because Volume B contains an appendix of 18 leaves, which can be separated and considered a distinct volume." Now, this appendix, which likely was not paginated, has disappeared.
>
> The only authentic Leonardo da Vinci autograph to pass through a public sale in Paris appeared in the catalogue of the sale held by Mr. Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri, a mathematician and book collector who stole thousands of manuscripts from French public libraries.</note> on December 8, 1845 (no. 295) where it was announced as follows: *Vinci (Leonardo). Original pen drawing, 2 quarto pages representing on one side a standing nude soldier, seen from the back, a head, a hand, and a foot. On the reverse are various drawings of machines. These two pages are filled with autograph writing traced in reverse (from right to left), like everything written by this great artist.* — This piece, which had been sold for 200 francs, appeared again at the first sale of Baron de Trémont (1852), no. 1441. — Alerted by us, the librarian of the Institute, Mr. Landresse, examined this piece and, after comparing it to Manuscript B, was instantly convinced that it had been stolen from it. The manuscript bears a large water stain, which was found on the piece in question. It was not put up for sale... (Another leaf appeared in the catalogue of Mr. Garnier, a judge at Melle in 1847. Collectors had doubts about the authenticity of this piece, as it was sold for only 15 francs. We saw it at Mr. Lefèvre’s, a bookseller at the Colbert Arcade, and we ensured that it did not come from the Leonardo da Vinci manuscripts kept at the Institute Library.) — Finally, Mr. Libri, in whose papers no trace of the acquisition of Leonardo da Vinci’s autograph manuscripts was found (on this subject, see the indictment brought against him, specifically the paragraph devoted to the Institute Library), sold Lord Ashburnham two manuscripts which he described thus in his catalogue: *Two volumes filled with writing and drawings by Leonardo da Vinci, two quarto volumes, on 15th-century paper.* — Precious autograph manuscripts. — Lalanne and Bordier.

This is what Mr. Piot wrote on this subject in 1862:

# PREFACE

The pagination follows regularly up to the 83rd leaf; [leaves] 84, 85, 86, and 87 have been removed, and the volume ends at leaf 90. Therefore, in addition to the four leaves just mentioned, the following are missing: the 2nd leaf, which featured colored black cherries and leaves; the ten leaves at the end; the five unnumbered leaves placed after the 49th containing polearms <note>original: "armes de hast"; refers to weapons such as halberds or pikes</note>; and the eighteen-leaf quire <note>a gathering of parchment or paper sheets</note> sewn to the end of the volume, containing various figures and birds.

Venturi, who held the complete collection in his hands, had extracted from leaf 95 observations on the method of constructing fortress walls to be proof against cannonballs and, from the 1st leaf of the appendix sewn to the end of the volume, a theorem on the descent of heavy bodies <note>original: "descente des graves"; a reference to gravitational acceleration</note> which occurs more quickly along an arc than along a chord.

The barbaric mutilation of the manuscripts of a great man that we observe here is too closely linked to the heated discussion that took place in the early days of June for us to refrain from saying a word about it in passing. The author of these mutilations, beyond the reach of justice, has poured outrage and insult upon too many honorable people for us not to feel a certain satisfaction in bringing one more piece of testimony to the many proofs that condemn him; moreover, if the guilt of Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri (1803–1869), a mathematician and notorious book thief who stole thousands of manuscripts</note> is no longer a doubt for anyone, his audacity is far from exhausted. Once again, in the *Athenæum* of June 15, he attempts to mislead public opinion; we must therefore insist.

Manuscript B contains a large number of annotations written by one of those through whose hands it passed, in three different handwritings, two of which are Spanish and one Italian; these annotations are, for the most part, merely the transcription of the titles written in reverse <note>mirror writing</note> by Leonardo da Vinci.

The main subjects treated in Venturi's Manuscript B are machines of all kinds, primarily for war and industry, a history of weapons—especially those of antiquity—various architectural plans, and geometry; some parts of this manuscript appear to constitute finished portions of treatises, while others are merely simple notes; among the latter, one finds drawings of plants; furthermore, one finds citations and philosophical reflections.

The figures accompanying the text of this manuscript, written in reverse, numbering between three and five hundred, are particularly beautiful.

The full publication of this manuscript is to immediately follow that of Manuscript A.

The appendix of Venturi's Manuscript B was cited by Oltrocchi under the designation S in-quarto ^1^.

->3rd. — *Manuscript C.*<-

— The manuscript which passed from the Melzi family to the Mazzenta family and was later given by Cardinal Federico Borromeo, in 1603, to the Ambrosian Library <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan</note>, formerly marked G, then O, a folio composed of 28 leaves joined in the middle, preceded by one blank leaf and followed by two others, is written in reverse and accompanied by important figures; this volume is 31 centimeters high, 23 wide, bound in calfskin, with blind-tooled and gilded fillets. The cover bears the following inscription on the front:

->By the generosity<-
->of Guido Mazzenta,<-
->a Milanese Patrician.<-
->In the year 1603.<-
<note>original Latin: "Vidi. Mazentæ. Patritii. Mediolanensis. Liberalitate. AN. M. D. C. III."</note>

In this inscription *Vidi* is the genitive of the name Guy or Guido Latinized.

On the back of the cover, on a sheet attached to it, one reads in the middle of the page a capital O, which has been crossed out; above it, the C of Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822), an early scholar of Leonardo's scientific work</note> and opposite, on the blank leaf which appears to have been added to the manuscript: *Autograph of Leonardo da Vinci, of whom Raphael Trichet Dufresne makes mention in his account of the same; it deals, however, with light and shadow.* <note>original Latin: "Autographum Leonardi Vincij cujus in eiusdem rebus gestis meminit Raphael Trichet Fresneus ; agit autem de lumine et umbra."</note> These words and the O on the back of the cover are in a shaky hand. At the top of the blank leaf is a mark that appears to be a repetition of the O, a letter found again on the back of this leaf. On the front of the following leaf (leaf 1), one sees in the middle a capital G preceded and followed by a comma, and below it the letter O.

At the end of the book, on the back of the penultimate blank leaf, one finds again in the middle of the page a capital G preceded and followed by a comma, but crossed out, and on the front of the last blank leaf, in the middle: O.

The manuscript is paginated with numbers preceded and followed by commas, consequently by the owner or the reader of the manuscript who wrote the G on the 1st leaf and the penultimate leaf. Opposite most of the figures that accompany the text—numbering nearly 200—one finds a mark (some sign, a letter, or a star) originating from one of those who formerly examined this volume.

As seen by the inscription bearing the name of Raphael Trichet Dufresne <note>A 17th-century French scholar and editor of Leonardo's Treatise on Painting</note>, Manuscript C deals with light and shadow; one also encounters other subjects, for example, hydraulics, geometry, etc.; one finds some notes of a completely personal character, for example, on leaf 15. Some parts of this volume can be considered finished drafts; several have been published.

---

->4th. — *Manuscript D.*<-

A manuscript that was substituted for the fifth of the Arconati donation <note>Galeazzo Arconati donated twelve Leonardo manuscripts to the Ambrosian Library in 1637</note>, which probably passed into the Trivulzio collection after having belonged, since its departure from the Ambrosian Library, to Horace Archinti. A quarto of 10 leaves in a single quire, numbered by Leonardo on the front, preceded and followed by 4 blank leaves.

It is the small volume D which was found to replace, during the shipment of the Ambrosian manuscripts to Paris, the fifth manuscript of the Arconati donation.

Here is what Mr. G. Govi says regarding this substitution:

> A small manuscript by Leonardo is also found in the house of the Trivulzio family, sold around 1750 to Don Charles Trivulzio by Don Gaetano Caccia, a Knight of Novara. This small manuscript extremely resembles the fifth of the volumes described in the Arconati donation, and indeed, this fifth volume is not found among those from the Ambrosian Library that remained in France; in its place in the collection is another small volume.... The manuscript substituted for Arconati’s fifth is currently marked with the letter D; it deals with *the eye and vision*, counts only 10 leaves, and certainly did not figure among those which, in 1637, were given to the Ambrosian Library. How and when the substitution of this volume for the other was made is something that would be very difficult, if not impossible, to discover, unless one admits that the matter took place in the time of Galeazzo Arconati, who had reserved for himself the right to take home the volumes of Leonardo that he might please to consult. The donor himself might thus have made an exchange with the small volume D, which he perhaps judged to be of greater importance¹.

On the front of the first blank leaf of Manuscript D following the text, one sees a large capital S, written long ago in the opposite direction of Leonardo’s writing and

similar to the S marks that were noted on manuscripts A and B; the same S is found on the front of the fourth blank leaf, and at the bottom of the page one reads a large 5, a sequence number that was undoubtedly given to volume D when it replaced the fifth book of Arconati <note>Galeazzo Arconati, who donated several Leonardo manuscripts to the Ambrosian Library in 1637</note>, unless, however, one wishes to conclude from this clue that in reality this fifth book mentioned in the deed never entered the Ambrosian Library and that the substitution of manuscript D was due to Arconati himself.

Finally, in the middle of the cover, on the inside, one again finds the capital S of manuscripts A and B; it can be inferred from this that manuscripts A, B, and D once belonged to the same collection; and that, in this collection as with volume B, the S is followed by an *a* and probably an *l* <note>original: "scripta leonardi?" — Latin for "writings of Leonardo?"</note>, for volume A probably a *b* and an *l*; book D was listed first because of the greater degree of completion in its drafting, book B second, and book A third.

Since manuscripts from the same collection generally bear marks of the same nature, and since manuscripts C, E, F, G, H, I, J, K, L, and M of the Institute—formerly of the Ambrosian—do not bear the S mark, and as this letter appears to be of an old date, it is probable that it was placed on the three manuscripts before they were in the hands of Arconati and after they had ceased to belong to the Mazzenta family <note>The Mazzenta brothers acquired many of Leonardo's papers from the heir of Francesco Melzi in the late 16th century</note>.

As for the contents of manuscript D, it has been described as follows by Mr. Piot: "It deals exclusively with vision and the structure of the eye, a polished draft, which appears to contain the artist's definitive ideas on this subject. It is in this manuscript that one finds the description and theory of the <term>camera obscura</term> <note>original: "chambre noire" — a darkened box with a lens or hole used to project an image, the ancestor of the camera</note>, very nearly as we use it today for photographic purposes."

->### 5th. — Manuscript E.<-

Sixth book of Arconati, formerly marked X (by Oltrocchi? <note>Baldassare Oltrocchi, an 18th-century librarian at the Ambrosian Library</note>), B (by Oltrocchi?), octavo <note>a book size resulting from folding a sheet of paper three times to form eight leaves</note> (composed of quires of 16 leaves), numbered on the front of each leaf from 1 to 80 and bound in boards. This manuscript formerly had 96 leaves; 16 have therefore been taken (see on this subject what was said regarding manuscripts A and B; see also, further on, the sale made by Libri <note>Guglielmo Libri, a mathematician and infamous book thief who stole many Leonardo pages</note> of a Leonardo manuscript in which the flight of birds is discussed).

This small volume is 15 centimeters high and more than 10, but less than 11, centimeters wide. On the front of the cover before the text (which is written in reverse <note>Leonardo's famous "mirror writing"</note> and accompanied by figures) and on the exterior, one reads at the top a capital B, written by Oltrocchi, the remark: "the leaves are exactly 96 in number, that is, ninety-six" <note>original Italian: "le carte sono di nº giusto 96, cioè nonantasei"</note>, some erased arithmetic numbers, and at the bottom the E of Venturi; on the inside at the top, E, then at the bottom, in an old hand, a capital X followed by a lowercase *c*. At the end of the manuscript, at the bottom of the inside of the cover, the number 6, indicating that book E was the 6th of Leonardo's original manuscripts at the Ambrosian, written over a 4; below it, Oltrocchi's B, crossed out.

The main subjects of the manuscript are the elements and powers of nature, hydraulics, the flight of birds, geometry, and mechanics.

Seventh book of Arconati, formerly marked with a capital X followed by a lowercase *b* (by Oltrocchi?)

Octavo composed of 6 quires of 16 leaves (96 leaves in total), bound in boards, more than 14 and less than 15 centimeters high, 10 wide. Text in reverse accompanied by

On the front of the cover, on the outside, one finds the F of Venturi <note>Giambattista Venturi, a physicist who studied Leonardo's manuscripts in Paris in 1797</note> and a mark (?) in old ink (a rectangle with its diagonals); on the reverse, the F of Venturi and a capital X followed by a lowercase b (marked by Oltrocchi? <note>Baldassare Oltrocchi, a former prefect of the Ambrosian Library</note>) in the opposite direction of Leonardo's text. At the end of the volume, on the inside of the cover, the same letters X and b, and above them the number 7 (that is: the seventh book of the Ambrosian <note>The Ambrosian Library in Milan</note>) superimposed over the number 6. One can also notice a small capital D at the bottom, but this letter seems of no importance, placed there by chance. The word *Leonar*, indicated in the Arconati deed of donation as being written on the front of the cover, has been erased.

Manuscript F contains numerous materials intended for the treatise on water, observations on light and shadow, on the stars, on the earth, on the formation of stones, on fossils, philosophical and other reflections, historical or personal notes, dates, Latin, etc.

->7°. — *Manuscript G.*<-

The ninth book of Arconati, formerly marked Q and S, capital X followed by a lowercase a (by Oltrocchi?).

Octavo <term>In-8°: a book size where each sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves</term>, composed of notebooks of 16 leaves, numbered on the front of each leaf. This manuscript would have, like the previous one, 96 leaves if it had not lost, prior to the description in the Arconati deed of donation, the 7th, 18th, and 31st leaves; it is a little less than 14 centimeters high, a little less than 10 wide, and bound in cardboard. Text written in reverse <note>Leonardo's famous mirror-writing</note> accompanied by figures. Leonardo's handwriting is on both sides of the cover. On the front, on the outside, one reads *Leonar*, then, in the middle, a capital G marked by Venturi, and, at the bottom, in small capitals, Q and S; on the reverse, at the top, the G of Venturi and at the bottom a capital X followed by a lowercase a (marked by Oltrocchi?). At the end of the volume, at the top, the G of Venturi, at the bottom a capital X followed by a lowercase a, and, placed as in the other books, the Ambrosian number, 8 (8th of the collection) superimposed over another; this number is the same on the outside.

The main subjects of this manuscript are those discussed in the treatise on painting, as it has been published: various precepts, light and shadow, anatomy, landscape, and botany; one also finds notes relating to the powers of nature (weight, force, motion), geometry and optics, descriptions, reflections of various kinds, historical or personal notes, dates, etc.

->9°. — Manuscript I.<-

The tenth book of Arconati <note>Galeazzo Arconati, who donated twelve Leonardo manuscripts to the Ambrosian Library in 1637</note>, formerly marked with a capital Q and a capital Q followed by a small 3 (by Oltrocchi?).

Sextodecimo <term>In-16: a book size where each sheet is folded into 16 leaves, resulting in a small pocket-sized volume</term>, 11 centimeters high, 7 and a half wide, bound in white vellum, in the shape of a wallet. On the spine, one reads *by Leonardo da Vinci*. On the inside of the cover, at the top, one finds the capital letter I marked by Venturi, then further down, in the middle, in the opposite direction of Leonardo's text, a capital Q followed by a small 3 (marked by Oltrocchi?). At the end of the volume, on the front of the blank leaf, a capital Q followed by a small 3, then on the page pasted to the cover, on the inside, a capital Q (by Oltrocchi?) and on the outside, at the top, the number 9 (that is: the ninth original book of Leonardo at the Ambrosian), and finally, at the bottom, Venturi's I.

Manuscript I is primarily concerned with hydraulics, architecture, mechanics, and geometry.

A small number of extracts from this manuscript have been published.

In this volume, one encounters part of a Latin-Italian vocabulary, quotations, dates, and historical or personal notes.

The twelfth book of the Arconati collection, formerly marked with a capital Q (by Oltrocchi?).

The main subjects of Manuscript M are geometry and the powers of nature (weight, movement, and force). One encounters reflections and notes of all kinds, citations, jokes, historical or personal memories, dates, etc.
