<meta>This page continues the preface's discussion regarding the historical cataloging and provenance of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, specifically addressing the confusion caused by various marking systems used by previous scholars.</meta>

# PREFACE

Several people before Venturi, readers or owners of Leonardo da Vinci's original manuscripts, had marked these books with conventional signs. These volumes are difficult to read and sometimes easy to confuse due to the similarity of the subjects treated in some of them and the identical formats of several others. One of those who most frequently employed such signs to cite the manuscripts—from which he had made various extracts—was, at the end of the last century, Oltrocchi, librarian of the Ambrosiana <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan, which held a significant collection of Leonardo's papers.</note>. Generally, all the marks he used in the notes that Amoretti first published are attributed to him. Since Venturi published his *Essay on the Physico-Mathematical Works of Leonardo da Vinci* (1797) and, on the other hand, Amoretti published shortly thereafter (in 1804) his *Historical Memoirs of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Memorie storiche di Leonardo da Vinci"</note>, various systems have been used to refer to the text of Leonardo's autograph writings: sometimes the marks used by Oltrocchi, sometimes other signs found on the covers of the manuscripts, and finally the letters assigned by Venturi. From this, confusion and numerous errors have arisen. Thus, one of the most serious is the opinion that there exists a much larger number of manuscripts than the number actually read and cited by several authors, the principal ones being Oltrocchi, Venturi, and since then, Libri and Mr. Arsène Houssaye. The autograph writings of Leonardo that Oltrocchi examined were those of the Ambrosiana; however, according to the implicit testimony of Amoretti—librarian of the Ambrosiana and successor to Oltrocchi, who died in 1797—there were no other manuscripts of Leonardo at the Ambrosiana before the French conquest of Milan from the Austrians other than the thirteen volumes that came to Paris¹. This testimony from Amoretti proves that there were not, as has been claimed, original writings of Leonardo lost by the actions of French agents at the end of the last century; all those taken in Milan arrived in Paris.

Indeed, like Amoretti, Dozio, in his posthumous brochure on the writings and drawings of Leonardo da Vinci², after having enumerated the manuscripts that formerly entered the Ambrosiana and were later carried to Paris, stated that "thus fourteen were the Vinci codices possessed here until 1796" <note>original: "cosi quatordici furono i codici vinciani qui posseduti fino al 1796"</note>, the manuscript of Paciolo <note>Luca Pacioli, a mathematician and collaborator of Leonardo</note>, the 2nd of the Arconati donation, being counted in this number of 14, which must consequently be reduced to 13.

Dr. Max Jordan, at the same time as he published the important result of his laborious research on the origins of Leonardo da Vinci's treatise on painting, drew up a list of extracts that have been published from different original manuscripts—a new work performed with care. The citations of passages noted by him are classified under headings which are none other than the letters used by authors to designate each autograph volume of Leonardo; furthermore, Mr. Jordan began to establish a concordance between the different designations used for the same manuscripts, but several errors crept into this work.

I believe it useful, following the observations I have presented regarding the original manuscripts of the Ambrosiana and the citations made from them, to summarize—by grouping them in columns—those successive marks which appeared to me to have been affixed for a specific purpose on each of the volumes of the Institute <note>The Institut de France in Paris, where many of the manuscripts were relocated</note>, along with their origins and their different serial numbers in the inventories that have been drawn up. This table will make it possible to make Mr. Jordan's work entirely complete and exact; I regret that I lack the space here to show what conclusions are to be drawn from the comparison of the said concordance with the attached table.

---

1. Amoretti, p. 137, chap. xxxiii.
2. Dozio, p. 25.

From the very beginning, efforts were made to decipher Leonardo's writings. Francesco Melzi <note>original: "François Melzo"</note>—as has been glimpsed already—copied or had copied what he could in order to disseminate them; Vasari had one of the originals in his hands, and Benvenuto Cellini possessed a copy.

Ambrogio Mazzenta <note>original: "Ambroise Mazzenta"</note> studied Leonardo's manuscripts for his work on the canalization of the Adda river.

At the beginning of the last century, it was the painter Louis-Antoine David who was the first—according to Mr. G. Uzielli in his recent publication of several unpublished documents³—to seek exact information from Leonardo's manuscripts for biographical purposes. He was followed by Oltrocchi, librarian (*prefect*) of the Ambrosiana, whose papers passed into the hands of Cighera, and then to those of Amoretti, curator of the same library. Amoretti published the contents of these papers in 1804, at the same time that he reproduced a large part of the important memoir by Venturi published in France in 1797. This was a memoir that Venturi intended to follow with new series of extracts from the manuscripts held at the Institut <note>The Institut de France in Paris, where several of Leonardo's notebooks were held after being taken by Napoleon's forces.</note>, which were meant to comprise, in three complete Treatises, everything "that Vinci produced regarding mechanics, hydraulics, and optics."

Govi, *Essay* <note>original: "Saggio"</note>, etc., p. 6, col. 1). The fifth volume, for which manuscript D was substituted, appears to have passed into the Trivulzio collection in Milan (G. Govi, *Essay*, etc., p. 7, col. 1).
2. This description, which is very summary, includes eleven manuscripts; there are five that I have not yet succeeded in identifying. See: *On the Writings and Drawings of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "degli Scritti e Disegni di L. da V."</note>, Dozio, p. 23.

# PREFACE

In 1840, Libri presented in the third volume of his *History of the Mathematical Sciences in Italy* a series of excerpts he had taken either from the manuscripts still held by the Institute, or from pages of those manuscripts which he tore out to sell <note>Guglielmo Libri was a famous mathematician and book thief who stole many precious documents from French libraries.</note>.

In 1841, E.-J. Delécluze published a facsimile of page 33 of Manuscript B containing "the architonnerre <note>original: "l'architonnerre"; a steam-powered cannon</note>, an invention of Archimedes," with commentary, both in the journal *The Artist* and as a separate printing¹.

My father, Mr. Félix Ravaisson-Mollien, had dreamed, from the moment he entered his literary career, of bringing to light the unpublished works of Leonardo da Vinci, and he had begun reading those held by the library of the Institute of France. In 1853, at the same time that he proposed—in his *Report on the Teaching of Drawing*—to re-establish such instruction on the foundations laid by Leonardo in his treatise on painting, he discussed with the Minister of Public Instruction a project for publishing the Institute's manuscripts. Other occupations then forced my father to postpone this plan, and later to abandon it.

In 1869, Mr. Arsène Houssaye, in his *History of Leonardo da Vinci*, reproduced a good number of fragments scattered throughout various manuscripts after having consulted the originals.

Recently, Mr. Govi had resumed a reading of the Institute's manuscripts begun by him more than twenty years ago, with the aim of making known and scientifically explaining numerous passages; however, upon learning how the reproduction of the manuscripts starting hereafter is to be carried out, he was kind enough to tell me that he felt he should wait to comment on the text of each of our autograph volumes by Leonardo until they were published.

This is perhaps the place to mention an important work printed in Milan under the auspices of the Italian Government in 1872, the *Essay on the Works of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Saggio delle opere di Leonardo da Vinci"</note>, in atlas format and mainly composed of 24 photolithographic plates reproducing a selection of some of the 399 leaves of Manuscript N of the Ambrosiana <note>The Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan</note>, since one of the articles preceding the 24 facsimiles, *Leonardo as Scientist and Man of Letters* <note>original: "Leonardo scienziato e letterato"</note>, is primarily devoted by its author, Mr. Govi, to researching what conclusions it was already possible to draw from the reading of Leonardo's manuscripts that he had been able to do at that time in Milan and Paris.

Finally, I myself had the occasion, in October 1877, to publish in the *Gazette of Fine Arts* <note>original: "Gazette des Beaux-Arts"</note>, regarding the conjectures of Mr. Courajod relating to a bust of Beatrice d'Este, several unpublished passages from several of our manuscripts and to present some general views on the genius and character of Leonardo, as shown by our precious documents.

In summary, until the end of the last century, people hardly concerned themselves with deciphering Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts except from the point of view of art, or to seek biographical information. Furthermore, those who sought to compose the treatises on painting and the measurement of water made only a selection from the numerous materials amassed by Leonardo; they assembled the elements arbitrarily; and on the other hand, they neglected numerous passages of great interest, both in themselves and because knowledge of them is necessary to give their true meaning and real value to those that have seen the light of day.

Biographers also neglected passages from Leonardo's manuscripts that could serve to write his life with more accuracy, and nothing important appeared except—

<meta>This page continues the preface, contrasting the author's goal of a complete, literal publication of Leonardo's manuscripts against the selective approach favored by previous scholars like Mr. Govi.</meta>

# PREFACE

...very little of what a few individuals had been able to copy for the benefit of their personal research.

When people first began wanting to show what Leonardo was as a scholar, to make known both the entirety of his scientific knowledge and each of his principal discoveries, they still proceeded only by excerpts, each according to the particular point of view that was their own, so that it can be said with truth that even today the manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci remain unpublished.

A complete and literal publication alone can give a definitive result, such that it will no longer be necessary to resort to the original documents to conduct a new study. Only when such an undertaking has been brought to a successful conclusion will we be able to judge seriously, without risk of error and without arbitrariness, the thought, the spirit, and the knowledge of Leonardo.

One of our contemporary scholars most qualified to speak with authority on such a matter has, however, recently acted as the interpreter for the contrary opinion. Mr. Govi, who, as I have said, had proposed to publish excerpts from the manuscripts of the Institute, cited in his article on *Leonardo as Scientist and Man of Letters* <note>original: "Leonardo scienzato e letterato"</note>, as supporting evidence, this passage from an original manuscript preserved at the British Museum:

> Begun in Florence, in the home of Piero di Braccio Martelli, on the 22nd of March 1508. This is—"è questo"—a collection without order, drawn from many papers, which I have copied here, hoping to put them afterwards in their places, according to the subjects of which they treat; I believe that before reaching the end, I shall have to repeat the same thing several times. Do not blame me for this, reader, because the things are numerous and memory cannot have them all present together (*to retain* <note>original: "riservare"</note>), and say: This one, I do not want to write it, because I have already written it. If I did not want to fall into this error, it would be necessary that in every case where I had to copy, I would have, in order not to make a repetition, to reread all the rest, often written at long intervals of time.

Mr. Govi says, following this citation:

> "That is why it would be not only impossible, but dangerous to publish a part of Leonardo's notes without having the rest before one's eyes, since in none of his booklets does one find a subject entirely treated and very frequently the error committed in one volume is found corrected in another. Thus (to cite only one of the most serious examples), if we were to believe the compilation which was published under the title: *On the Motion and Measurement of Water* <note>original: "Du mouvement et de la mesure de l'eau"</note>, Leonardo would have taught the composition of a *Perpetual Motion* <note>term: "mouvement perpétuel"; the hypothetical machine that works indefinitely without an energy source, which Leonardo eventually proved impossible</note>, whereas we shall see further on that he himself recognized, exposed, and demonstrated all the reasons why one must regard perpetual motion as impossible.
> One must therefore limit oneself, as long as the times and men do not agree to do better <note>original: "finchè i tempi e gli uomini non consentan far meglio"</note>, to gathering from the manuscripts that we know and that we can see and study the little that Leonardo left there that is certain and that can be considered as such, as being in agreement with the teachings of modern science¹."

Quite to the contrary, I remain persuaded, after a careful reading of both the manuscripts of the Institute and the works written regarding the content of Leonardo's various writings, that the utility of a literal publication has been demonstrated.

Experience has proved that the selection applied to the autograph writings of Leonardo can only give rise, even when employed by men eminent in knowledge and judgment, to a provisional work, which must soon be redone. Thus a scholar will have hardly finished a work of selection, in which he believed he had left nothing important behind, when already another will be obliged, in order to work from a new point of view,

---

1. *Essay, etc.* <note>original: "Saggio, etc."</note>, p. 6, col. 1.

<meta>This page continues the argument for a complete, literal publication of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts, addressing the difficulties of his handwriting and the potential for contradictions or errors in his notes.</meta>

# PREFACE

another choice: to decipher everything, to sort through everything in turn within these manuscripts where it is so difficult to find one's way, as long as the classification of the subjects they contain has not been done by means of a transcription that allows for easy reading. With the continual discoveries of modern science, there would be no end to this "choice upon choice," always provoked by the hope of restoring to Leonardo some of those strokes of genius that are the admiration of his biographers.

The writings of Leonardo are of such a nature, as I have said, that one can always find, alongside an insignificant remark—at least in appearance—one of great value, often where one would least expect it.

In one place, you will find completed sections of one of those numerous Treatises <term>Treatises: Leonardo planned many formal books on subjects like painting, water, and anatomy, but most remained as scattered notes</term> he was preparing; in another, simple ideas thrown down as they came to the paper; or perhaps minute and profound observations, elevated thoughts, moral and philosophical reflections, composition projects for painting, inventions, recipes, experiments of every kind, citations, comparisons of all sorts, etc., etc.—in a word, a little bit of everything everywhere. Even in the parts of Leonardo's manuscripts that are best known, for example those concerning art and the study of water, there are passages still unpublished.

The difficulty of reading Leonardo's writings <note>Leonardo famously used "mirror writing," writing from right to left, which requires a mirror to read easily</note> is also a reason to regard as exceptionally desirable the execution of a total and textual publication. This would, in the future, dispense with the need to engage in a long and laborious deciphering just to search for what an editor of excerpts might have left out.

One might well object that the complete publication of Leonardo da Vinci's writings will present a disadvantage: namely, that in more than one case, what is said in one manuscript might be found to be absolutely contradicted by what is found in another.

It is true, on the other hand, that in many passages, one will find obscurity, or worse. "One must not hide it from oneself," said Venturi <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822), an Italian physicist and one of the first to highlight Leonardo's scientific genius</note>, "one encounters in these manuscripts some false conclusions, some useless speculations; perhaps he would have removed them himself when editing his works. However, there is gold in this sand." Indeed, some phrases seem thrown carelessly onto the paper, without corresponding to a very serious thought or observation; this is because for Leonardo, it was a habit, and he had made it a rule to note everything that passed before his eyes or through his mind, without always attaching equal importance to it. "It is always good for the understanding," he said, "to acquire knowledge, whatever it may be; one can then choose the good and discard the useless¹." And that is why he recommended that the painter always carry a notebook to mark down everything that offered itself to his sight².

But even the passages of his manuscripts which may seem devoid of interest in themselves are not only testimonies of his way of working—curious and instructive for that reason alone—it must be added that most often a remark that at first glance one is tempted to find insignificant or merely obscure, will take on importance and become clear through comparison with analogous passages in the same volume or in some other, and will then take on a meaning and a value. And even should some phrases remain without explanation or plausible justification, between the disadvantage of offering the reader such debris—of no value in the eyes of some—and that of sacrificing through a more or less arbitrary elimination, thus condemning to oblivion some words or thoughts of importance for lack of having understood them, I believe that one must not hesitate.

---

1. Venturi, *Essay* <note>original: "Essai"</note>, p. 31, § XV.
2. Manzi, *Treatise on Painting* <note>original: "Tratt. del. pitt."</note>, p. 107, 109, 110, 128.

# PREFACE

### PREFACE

There is, therefore, a real utility and a general interest in ensuring that all that remains of Leonardo's unpublished papers (among which the greater part of each of the manuscripts at the Institute <note>The Institut de France in Paris, which holds twelve of Leonardo's notebooks, labeled A through L</note> must be counted as primary) be finally brought to light, not in arbitrarily chosen fragments, but in the most complete manner, without any omission.

The slightest relic, the smallest memory of a great man, the minor sketch of an illustrious artist, and the shortest word in the handwriting of a profound thinker, take on an inestimable interest in the eyes of the generations that succeed his own—sometimes even when, at first, that sketch or autograph seemed to offer only a purely material value in itself.

This interest must undoubtedly be much greater if one can rediscover, no longer just the pencil or the hand, but the very thought as well as the observations and discoveries of an interpreter of nature—artist and scholar, seeker and philosopher, such as Leonardo da Vinci was. It is a matter of following him, so to speak, every day and at every step, through his intimate notes, his reflections, his studies, and his experiments; of finally seeing the man himself in his efforts to achieve perfection in art and to discover truth in science down to its very principles—whether out of a love for beauty and truth combined with a search for the unknown, or for the benefit of his contemporaries and posterity.

Everything touching upon one of those spirits who occasionally emerge from humanity to do honor to an era whose genius they summarize, inspires something of that pious admiration and vivid curiosity attached to that which, being superior to the ordinary spiritual strength of men, must seem supernatural to them, and worthy of being admired, meditated upon, and imitated.

And, for us Frenchmen, beyond the general interest, there is a very particular interest in publishing these writings ourselves, the possession of which others envy us. It is important to the honor of our country that we are no longer accused of sequestering the riches that owe it to us to see the light of day.

Leonardo da Vinci was the favorite and the painter of two of our kings; he loved France and made it his second homeland. If Francis I <note>King of France (r. 1515–1547) who invited Leonardo to spend his final years at Clos Lucé</note> could have, as he wished, had *The Last Supper* transported to France, and if the order that Napoleon I signed on his knee had not been ignored, *The Last Supper* would be better preserved today¹; it is at the Louvre that we possess the greatest number of Leonardo's masterpieces. It was at the request of Poussin <note>Nicolas Poussin (1594–1665), the leading painter of the classical French Baroque style</note> that the Cavalier Cassiano del Pozzo had a copy made of the extracts from Leonardo's manuscripts that were in the Barberini Library in Rome; a copy for which Poussin redrew the human figures with his own hand (later retouched, to his great displeasure, by the painter Errard) and which, after passing into the hands of M. de Chanteloup—Poussin's patron and brother to the Secretary of State and Superintendent of Buildings—served for the first edition of the *Treatise on Painting* published in 1651 by a relative of M. de Chanteloup, Raphael Trichet-Dufresne.

Thus, the honor ultimately returns to the French for having brought to light—according to the intentions of Leonardo's disciple <note>Francesco Melzi, Leonardo's heir, who lived with him at the estate of Cloux (Clos Lucé)</note> who shared with him the enjoyment of the estate of Cloux—a work whose publication was a tribute paid to the memory of the great artist.

So it was that when, nearly one hundred and fifty years later, a more complete edition of the *Treatise on Painting* was produced by Abbot Manzi, it was to the King of France, Louis XVIII, that he felt he should dedicate his book. And Venturi, when the Institute of France [gave] him...

---

¹ *Leonardo da Vinci*, by M. Charles Blanc, Lecture of March 1880, at the Sorbonne.

<meta>This page continues the administrative and technical history of the publication project, detailing the author's efforts to secure funding and his methodology for presenting Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts to the public.</meta>

# PREFACE

### PREFACE

having communicated the manuscripts that had just arrived from Milan, publicly declared that they "should only be entrusted to an assembly of scholars of the first order, who can appreciate their merit better than anyone, and who, far from hiding their treasures, are eager to share them with those who wish to draw from them."

The project that my father had formed in 1853, I took up again on his advice when I was able to enter the literary career myself. After having deciphered a portion of the manuscripts held by the Institute, I requested (in 1874) from the administrative commission of the Institute the authorization to reproduce them in facsimile and to publish them with a translation, a request which was granted to me. After realizing the difficulties of the undertaking, I submitted my project to the Committee—composed of eminent men in science, art, and letters attached to the National Printing Office—in the hope of obtaining its support. This Committee declared that it was impossible, given the insufficiency of the resources at its disposal, to cover the costs of the publication; but at the same time, it issued a vote of encouragement, and Mr. Dumas, perpetual secretary of the Academy of Sciences, was given the mission to express to the Minister of Public Instruction and Fine Arts, in the name of the committee, the wish that the Ministry do what the Committee could not do. A commission of the Superior Council of Fine Arts, consulted by the minister, concluded likewise, and the Committee of Subscriptions for Sciences and Letters voted for a subscription of 100 copies of the first volume of the publication.

For the reasons given in the preceding pages, I believed that, to make the manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci well known, it was good to publish the text with the spelling and punctuation of the original; but, as this text thus published would necessarily present great difficulties to the common reader, I felt I should add a French translation <note>The author is Charles Ravaisson-Mollien, who published the first complete edition of the Paris manuscripts between 1881 and 1891</note>, a translation which I have made as faithful and even as literal as possible.

I believed that Leonardo's manuscripts should be placed before the public in facsimile, not only because the text is accompanied by drawings that it is important to know exactly as their author traced them, but also because the reading of this very text presents, in many places, problems for which it seemed useful to place the elements before the eyes of those who might wish to search after me and might perhaps succeed better than I in resolving them.

The facsimiles, obtained by the <term>Arosa photoglyptic process</term> <note>An early form of photogravure or permanent photographic printing</note>, invented by <term>Tessier du Motay</term> <note>Cyprien Tessier du Motay, a chemist and pioneer in photography and industrial chemistry</note>, will occupy the upper part of the back of each page, the Italian transcription the lower part, and the English translation <note>Original: "traduction française"</note> will be placed opposite.

The facsimiles will be reproduced in the dimensions of the original pages.

In front of each facsimile, at the top left, one will find the indication of the folio of the manuscript it represents, with a distinction between the <term>recto</term> <note>the front of a leaf</note> and the <term>verso</term> <note>the back of a leaf</note>; the indication of the recto alone will be repeated at the head of the Italian transcription and the translation, because, as will be seen, the original pages to be transcribed and translated were only paginated, very long ago—probably by Leonardo himself—on the recto.

The translation will be preceded, for each page, by a summary of the subjects treated therein, and, for some passages that have already been published, by references to the works where they appeared.

The original text has been—due to the capricious irregularity it presents, both in the assembly of syllables and in the spelling—transcribed syllable by syllable, letter by letter; thus one can see to what extent Leonardo wrote, as <term>Mr. Govi</term> <note>Gilberto Govi, an Italian physicist and Leonardo scholar</note> said, as one speaks, for the ear rather than for the eyes.

# PREFACE 31

### PREFACE

One will notice in the Italian transcription a punctuation very specific to Leonardo’s era, which those who have previously made extracts from his manuscripts have neglected; it seems to me to be of real importance. More than any other circumstance, it can serve to exactly determine the rules glimpsed by Oltrocchi and Amoretti <note>Baldassarre Oltrocchi (1714–1797) and Carlo Amoretti (1741–1816) were early custodians and scholars of Leonardo's manuscripts at the Ambrosian Library in Milan.</note> on one hand; on the other hand, by taking it into account, one may find the relative dates of different drafts of the same thought. Indeed, it will be seen later that some of Leonardo's manuscripts—where pencil drafts were copied over by him—carry a very regular and consistent punctuation in the second version, whereas where his ideas are still undecided or obscure, the periods follow and interrupt each other in the most irregular manner.

Several pages of Manuscript A <note>Manuscript A is one of the twelve Leonardo notebooks held by the Institut de France, primarily covering topics of painting and physics.</note> present words or passages that Leonardo crossed out with his own hand; these will be found reproduced in *italic characters* in the Italian transcription and in this English translation <note>original: "traduction française"</note>.

Whenever the transcription or the literal translation of a word seems to me to present some obscurity, another word placed between brackets [ ] will clarify its meaning.

In the transcription and in the translation, the propositions—words followed by a development or an explanation relating to them—will be seen printed as headings; conversely, a simple statement or a question posed without an answer will be in standard characters and preceded by a dash.

The reproduction of words written in the manuscript simply to accompany and explain a figure <note>In this context, "figure" refers to Leonardo's sketches or technical diagrams.</note> will be preceded and followed by a dash.

I have only added notes to the translation that seemed absolutely indispensable to justify or clarify it. Regarding matters specifically involving mathematics, Mr. Évariste Bernès <note>A professor of mathematics at the Lycée Louis-le-Grand in Paris, known for his expertise in geometry.</note> was kind enough to assist me with his advice before the manuscript was printed. It will belong to men versed in the different sciences touched upon by Leonardo’s writings to bring light to what his thoughts may still seem to present as obscure, as well as to discuss their value.

A table of contents seemed to me to be of the greatest utility for navigating manuscripts where the same subject is often discussed in very different places, and very different subjects are discussed in the same place—or, to put it better, where the most diverse subjects are intermingled almost everywhere. Consequently, at the end of each volume of this publication, there will be a table of this nature, both alphabetical and systematic, arranged in such a way as to refer, as much as possible for each entry, to everything relating to it.

### [Facsimile Section]

3.

<note>Sketch of a rectangular block or wooden panel in the upper left margin, illustrating the support being described.</note>

The wood should be cypress, or laurel, or walnut, which you shall seal with mastic and twice-distilled turpentine, and white lead or lime <note>original: "biacha ovoi chalcina"</note>. Place it in a frame so that it may expand and contract according to whether it is humid or dry. Then, treat it with aqua vitae <term>aqua vitae: a high-proof alcohol used as a solvent</term> in which arsenic or sublimate <note>Corrosive sublimate, or mercuric chloride, used as a preservative against wood-boring insects</note> has been dissolved two or three times. Afterward, apply a varnish of boiled linseed oil so that it penetrates everywhere; before it cools, rub it well with a piece of bread so that it appears dry. Then, apply liquid varnish and white lead over it with a Polish spatula <note>original: "astecha polacca," likely a specific type of spreading tool</note> using urine <note>A common ingredient in historical alchemy and painting to alter the properties of pigments or cleaners</note> when it is dry. Then, pounce <note>To transfer a drawing by pricking holes and dusting with powder</note> and outline your design finely, and apply the priming <note>original: "lanprimiera," the first layer of color</note> made of 30 parts of verdigris <term>verdigris: a green pigment obtained from the weathering of copper</term>, one part of verdigris, and 2 of yellow.

<note>Geometric diagram in the center showing light passing through an aperture (window) marked with letters 'a', 'b', 'c', and 'd'.</note>

Through the window **a. b.** the sun sends its rays into the house. The size of this sun will increase through the window, and the shadow of the man will decrease, so that when said man approaches, that shadow—distributed by itself—will meet the one that carries the true size of the window. He will see, upon the contact of the shadows, separated and confused by the power of the light, how they close and no longer let the solar rays pass, and the shadow made by the man at said contact will produce the effect exactly as figured above.

<note>Lower section contains a small sketch of a plumb line and notes on figure drawing and proportions.</note>

TO PORTRAY A NUDE FROM NATURE OR ANOTHER THING — use a string with a lead weight <note>a plumb line</note> in your hand to be able to see the alignments of things.

MEASURES AND PARTITIONING OF THE STATUE — divide the head into twelve parts and degrees, and each degree divide into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and the minutes into minima, and the minima into semi-minima.

### [Printed Commentary Section]

<margin>I</margin> PREPARING THE WOOD FOR PAINTING UPON

The wood should be cypress, or laurel, or walnut, which you shall seal with mastic and twice-distilled turpentine, and white lead or lime. Place it in a frame so that it may expand and contract according to whether it is humid or dry. Then, treat it with aqua vitae in which arsenic or sublimate has been dissolved two or three times. Afterward, apply *varnice* <gloss>varnish</gloss> of boiled linseed oil so that it penetrates everywhere; before it cools, rub it well with a piece of bread so that it appears dry. Then, apply liquid varnish and white lead over it with a Polish spatula using urine when it is dry. Then, pounce and outline your design finely, and apply the priming of 30 parts of verdigris, one part of verdigris, and 2 of yellow.

— Through the window **a. b.** the sun sends its rays into the house. The size of this sun will increase through the window, and the shadow of the man will decrease, so that when said man approaches, that shadow—distributed by itself—will meet the one that carries the true size of the window. He will see, upon the contact of the shadows, separated and confused by the power of the light, how they close and no longer *piv* <gloss>more</gloss> let the solar rays pass, and the shadow made by the man at said contact will produce the effect exactly as figured above.

TO PORTRAY A NUDE FROM NATURE OR ANOTHER THING — use a string with a lead weight in your hand to be able to see the alignments of things.

MEASURES AND PARTITIONING OF THE STATUE — divide the head into twelve *parts* and degrees, and each degree divide into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and the minutes into minima, and the minima into semi-minima.
— degree — *minvto* <gloss>minute</gloss> — point — minute — minimum

# PAINTING PANELS — IRRADIATION — DRAWING — SCULPTURE

### 3.

<margin>I (1)</margin> <note>This number in the margin refers to the original pagination of Leonardo’s Manuscript A, where each leaf is numbered in the upper right corner of the front side.</note>
**TO PREPARE PANELS FOR PAINTING:**

The wood should be cypress, or perhaps [laurel]? or walnut, which you shall seal with mastic and "second" turpentine <term>second turpentine: turpentine that has been distilled twice for higher purity</term>, which should be distilled and white, or [if] you prefer, lime; place it in a frame, so that it may expand and contract according to the humidity or [and] the dryness. Then, treat it two or three times with aqua vitae <term>aqua vitae: a high-strength alcohol used as a solvent</term> in which arsenic or sublimate <note>original: "sublimé"—referring to corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride, used as a preservative to prevent wood-worm infestations</note> has been dissolved. Afterward, apply a varnish of boiled linseed oil so that it is thoroughly penetrated before it cools; rub it well with bread <note>historical practice used breadcrumbs to scrub or dry a surface without leaving residue</note> until it appears dry, and apply over it a layer of liquid white varnish with a Polish wooden spatula [?] <note>original: "couteau de bois polonais"—this likely refers to the "astecha polacca" mentioned in the original Italian, a specific type of spreading tool</note> using urine <note>Urine was frequently used in early modern workshops as a mild acid or degreaser to help layers bond</note>; once it has been dried and wiped, pounce <term>pounce: to transfer a drawing by pricking holes in the paper and dusting it with charcoal powder</term> and trace your design finely. Finally, apply a priming <note>original: "l’impression"</note> made of 30 parts of verdigris <term>verdigris: a green pigment made from the corrosion of copper</term>, plus one of verdigris and two of yellow.

— Through the window **ab**, the sun enters a house; this sun will increase the apparent size of the window and decrease the shadow of a man, so that when the man brings this blurred [confused] shadow closer to himself—the one that carries the true size of the window—he will see, at the contact point [he will see the contact] of the blurred and confused shadows caused by the power of light, that they close up and do not allow the solar rays to pass *as would have*.... And the shadow made by the man on said contact will produce precisely the effect figured above <note>Leonardo is describing "irradiation," an optical illusion where a bright light source appears larger than it is, causing shadows of objects placed in front of it to appear narrower or distorted where they meet. He explores this frequently in his "Treatise on Painting."</note>.

— **TO REPRODUCE A** [man] **NUDE FROM LIFE OR OTHER THINGS.** — Hold a plumb line <term>plumb line: a cord with a weight used to establish a vertical line for accuracy in proportions</term> in your hand to see the alignment of objects.

— **MEASURES AND DIVISION OF THE STATUE.** — Divide the head into 12 *parts* and each into degrees, then divide each degree into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, the minutes into seconds, and the seconds into half-seconds.

<meta>This page continues the transcription and analysis of Leonardo da Vinci's scientific notes, focusing on mechanics (weights and motion) and the principles of artistic perspective.</meta>

# — FOLIO 1 (verso). —

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of a Leonardo da Vinci manuscript folio featuring diagrams and mirror-script annotations, accompanied by a printed transcription in English below.</note>

<note>Facsimile Section: Leonardo's handwriting is written in mirror script from right-to-left. The following translation presents this text in standard left-to-right reading order, following the scholarly edition printed on the page.</note>

**ON WEIGHTS** <note>original: "DE PONDERIBUS" — Latin title used by Leonardo for his studies on mechanics</note> — if you wish to pull the lever *a b* at point *c*, while the rope is tied at *b*, you will exert exactly as much force as if it were tied at *d*.

**ON WEIGHTS** — if the nail *a d* is fixed on the axis *c d*, and if weight *a b* weighs as much as *b c d*, the nail and the axis that supports it will remain in equilibrium, and its pole <note>the pivot or center of rotation</note> will be at point *c*.

**ON WEIGHTS** — a wheel that is thin in the middle and thick at the edges will have a fast motion; and one that is thick in the middle and thin at the edges will have a short <note>slow or limited</note> movement.

— Every motion is either natural, or violent, or natural-violent—that is, mixed—as appears in the downward motion of a balance scale.

— Those blows that fall from an equal height and are made by an equal weight must necessarily create an equal force <note>original: "violenza" — here meaning physical impact or kinetic energy</note> upon their object. And the smaller the part of that weight that strikes the object, the more it will drive itself into that object.

— **METHOD FOR DRAWING A RELIEF AT NIGHT** — place a sheet of paper that is not too shiny between the relief <note>a three-dimensional sculpture</note> and the light, and you will have a good drawing.

**GLASS WALL** — Perspective is nothing other than seeing a site behind a flat and well-transparent glass, upon the surface of which are marked all the things that are behind that glass. These things can be traced by <term>pyramids</term> <note>Leonardo's term for the cone of light rays traveling from an object to the eye</note> to the point of the eye, and these pyramids are intersected by the said glass.

<summary>Leonardo investigates the mechanical principles of levers, equilibrium, and momentum, alongside a foundational definition of perspective as a 'glass wall' that intersects the visual pyramid.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, levers, equilibrium, momentum, perspective, optics, drawing techniques, Renaissance science</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of Leonardo da Vinci's notebooks, moving from mechanics and the physics of motion into principles of artistic technique and the geometry of vision (perspective).</meta>

# WEIGHTS. — MOTION. — IMPACTS. — DRAWING. — PERSPECTIVE.

**ON WEIGHTS** <note>original: "DE PONDERIBUS" (Latin). Leonardo frequently used Latin titles for his scientific observations.</note>. — If you are to pull the lever *ab* at point *c*, the rope being tied at *b*, you will produce exactly as much force as if it were tied at *d*.

**ON WEIGHTS**. — If the nail *ad* is fixed on the board <term>ais: a wooden plank or board</term> *cd* and if *ab* weighs as much as *bcd*, the nail and the board that supports it will remain in equilibrium, and their pivot <note>original: "pôle"</note> will be at point *c* <note>See the same proposition, repeated and developed, on folio 33 of Manuscript A, verso (Regarding the center of weights).</note>.

**ON WEIGHTS**. — A wheel that is thin in the middle and thick at the edges will have a fast motion, and one that is thick in the middle and thin at the edges will have a short <note>meaning slow or limited in duration</note> movement.

— Every motion is either natural, or violent, or natural-violent—that is to say, mixed—as is seen in the downward motion of a balance scale <note>Leonardo is using Aristotelian physics here: "natural" motion is toward the Earth’s center, while "violent" motion is caused by an external force.</note>.

— Impacts <note>original: "coups"</note> that fall from an equal height and are produced by an equal weight will necessarily act with an equal force <note>original: "violence" — referring to the kinetic energy or intensity of the strike</note> upon their object; and the smaller the part of that weight that strikes the object, the deeper it will sink into that object.

— **METHOD FOR REPRESENTING A RELIEF AT NIGHT**. — See that you place a sheet of paper, which is not too transparent, between the object and the light, and you will have a good reproduction <note>See: *Treatise on Painting* <note>original: "Tratt. della pitt."</note>, p. 75, for the same precept, phrased slightly differently.</note>.

**GLASS PLANE**. — Perspective is nothing other than seeing an object situated behind a flat and very transparent pane of glass, upon the surface of which all the things behind the glass are marked; these things travel to the point of the eye through various pyramids <note>Leonardo conceived of vision as a "pyramid" or cone of light rays with the object as the base and the eye as the apex.</note>, and these pyramids intersect on the glass.

---

1. This title, at the head of this paragraph and the two following, is in Latin (*de ponderibus*).
2. See the same proposition, repeated and developed, folio 33 of manuscript A, verso. (Regarding the center of weights.)
3. See: *Treatise on Painting*, p. 75, the same precept, phrased slightly differently.

# — FOLIO 2 (recto). —

<note>Manuscript facsimile section containing optical diagrams and mirror writing transcribed into standard left-to-right reading order.</note>

<note>Three diagrams at the top show rays intersecting circles from various focal points, exploring how light strikes spherical surfaces.</note>

<margin>distinct — indistinct</margin>

The amplification <note>meaning the apparent enlargement or spreading of light</note> of the luminous body derives from the atmosphere through which it is transferred.

<note>A block of text on the left relating to the diagrams on the right, exploring the relationship between the eye, the light source, and the object.</note>

The shadow-casting body <term>corpo onbroso: an opaque object that blocks light to create a shadow</term> that is viewed along the line of the light's incidence will not show any prominent relief to the eye—*for example* <note>original: "verbi gratia" (Latin)</note>, let the shadow-casting body be **a**, and let the light be **c. m.** and thus **c. n.** be the lines of incident light, that is, those lines that transfer the light to the body **a**. Let the eye be at point **b**. I say that by seeing the light **c.**, the entire part **m. n.**—and whatever reliefs are there—will be completely illuminated. Therefore, the eye placed at **e. c.** [at b] will not be able to see any shadow there. And by not seeing light and shadow, every part appears to be of the same color; hence, the differences between the prominent and rounded parts will not appear <note>Leonardo explains that if you look at an object from the exact same direction as the light source, you see no shadows, which makes the object look flat rather than three-dimensional.</note>.

<note>Large diagram on the right showing rays from a circular light source passing through a small aperture (point e) to a surface (c-d).</note>

**a. b** as many times as **a. b** fits into **c. d**, that many times it is brighter than **c. d**; and similarly, as many times as the point **e.** fits into **c. d**, that many times it is brighter than **c. d**. This light is good for those who carve <note>original: "tagliano", meaning to cut or engrave</note> fine works.

# PERSPECTIVE AND MECHANICS

<margin>28 v 5 3</margin>

<note>This page contains a facsimile of a manuscript folio by Leonardo da Vinci (folio 2 verso) accompanied by a printed transcription of its content. The manuscript portion includes mirror-script Italian and several mechanical and anatomical drawings.</note>

<note>The following is a translation of Leonardo’s mirror writing, which he used to prevent smudging and perhaps to provide a slight layer of privacy to his notes.</note>

Every body fills the surrounding air with its **likeness** <note>original: "similitudine". In Leonardo's theory of optics, every object broadcasts "species" or images of itself in all directions.</note>, which likeness is all in the whole and all in every part.

The air is full of infinite straight and radiant lines, intersected and woven together without one occupying the space of another; they represent to the eye, at whatever object, the true form of their cause <note>original: "lorchagione". Leonardo is explaining how light rays carry the "likeness" of an object to the eye without getting tangled or blocked by other rays.</note>.

<note>Diagrams of levers, weights, and a human profile study are positioned here in the manuscript facsimile. The profile likely illustrates the point of reception for the visual "likenesses" described in the text.</note>

I ask: if the beam **e d** weighs 300 pounds, how much does **c** support and how much does **d** support?

If the beam **f g** weighs 200 pounds, how much weight will **h** have and how much **m**?

What difference is there between the strength <note>original: "valitudine"</note> of a wheel with a small pivot <term>axle<gloss>Leonardo uses "polo" to refer to the central axis or pole upon which a wheel rotates.</gloss></term> and a wheel with a thick pivot, as shown in **s t**?

# — FOLIO 3 (recto). —

<note>This page consists of a manuscript facsimile containing Leonardo da Vinci's mirror writing at the top, followed by a printed scholarly transcription of those same notes in the bottom half.</note>

### [Manuscript Facsimile Blocks]

<note>The following sections are translated from Leonardo's mirror script in the upper portion of the page.</note>

Perspective is a demonstrative reason <note>original: "ragione dimostrativa"; meaning a logical or mathematical proof</note> by which experience confirms that all things send their own likeness to the eye by pyramidal lines. And those bodies of equal size will create a larger or smaller angle at the point of their pyramid according to the variation of the distance that exists between one and the other.

By pyramidal lines, I mean those which start from the surface edges of bodies and, through distant convergence, are led to a single point.

A point is said to be that which cannot be divided into any part; and this point is that which, being located in the eye, receives within itself all the tips of the pyramids.

Painting is founded upon perspective. Perspective is nothing other than knowing well how to figure <note>represent or depict</note> the function of the eye — 4 — that is, in what manner the likenesses of things come to said eye.

— 4 — Which function extends solely to capturing, by means of a pyramid, the forms and colors of all objects placed before it. I say "by pyramid" because there is no thing so minute that it is not larger than the place where they converge in the eye. These are pyramids; therefore, if you draw lines to the edges of each body and lead them to the eye, and you lead their convergence to a single point, it is necessary that said lines be pyramidal.

— Perspective is nothing other than a demonstrative reason, which extends to considering how objects placed opposite the eye send to it, by pyramidal lines, their own likeness. A pyramid is named from two lines <note>Leonardo likely refers to the visual angle formed by the boundary lines</note>; those lines are said to be those which start from the surface edges of each body and, through distant convergence, are led to a single point.

— Perspective is a demonstrative reason by which experience is confirmed by experiment.

— Perspective is a demonstrative reason by which it is effectively and clearly understood how objects placed opposite the eye send to it — 4 — their own likeness.

<meta>This page continues the technical formalization of Leonardo’s optical theories. # PERSPECTIVE

— Painting is founded on perspective. Perspective is nothing other than knowing how to properly depict the function <note>original: "office"; the biological or mechanical duty</note> of the eye, — 4 — that is to say, the manner in which the likenesses of things come to the eye.

— 4 — which function consists solely in capturing, by means of pyramids, the forms and colors of all the objects placed before it. I say: by pyramids, because there is no thing so small or minute that it is not larger than the spot where these pyramids are received in the eye; therefore, if you take the lines at the edges of each body and draw them to their convergence—if you lead their convergence to a single point—the said lines will necessarily be pyramidal.

— Perspective is nothing other than a demonstrative reason <note>original: "raison démonstrative"; a logical or mathematical proof based on geometry</note>, which consists in considering how objects placed before the eye send their own likeness from themselves to it, by pyramidal lines. By pyramids, one means two lines—one means the lines that start from the surface edges of each body and arrive, by a distant convergence, at a single point.

— Perspective is a demonstrative reason, by which, with a true experience true experience . . . . <note>The repetition suggests the author was searching for the right phrasing in his notes.</note>

— Perspective is a demonstrative reason, by which one effectively clearly understands how objects placed before the eye send to it — 4 — their own likeness.

— By pyramidal lines to those. . . .

— Likeness by pyramidal lines to the eye. . . .

— Perspective is a demonstrative reason by which effectively c. . . .

1 — Perspective is a demonstrative reason, by which experience confirms that all things send their own likeness to the eye by pyramidal lines. — And bodies of equal size will create a larger or smaller angle at their pyramid, according to the variation of the distance from one to the other.

2 — By pyramidal lines I mean those which start from the surface edges of bodies and, through distant convergence, are led toward a single point.

3 — One calls a "point" that which cannot be divided into any part, and this point is that which, situated in the eye, receives within itself all the tips of the pyramids.

<summary>This page contains Leonardo da Vinci's repetitive attempts to define perspective as a mathematical "demonstrative reason." He establishes the "pyramidal" theory of vision, where images converge from the edges of objects into a single, indivisible point within the eye.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, optics, geometry of vision, pyramidal lines, likenesses, eye function, mathematical proof</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>On the previous page, Leonardo defined the geometric rules of perspective and how "likenesses" of objects travel to the eye. On this page, he shifts his focus to broader principles of physics—specifically how the combination of small elements (forces, supports, or lights) results in a total power that is greater than the sum of its individual parts.</meta>

### — FOLIO 3 (verso). —

<note>This page features a reproduction of Leonardo da Vinci’s characteristic "mirror writing"—written from right to left—paired with a transcription of his notes on the collective power of physical forces, structural supports, and light.</note>

— Separate powers will not all exist at the same time or for a single function.
— That power <note>original: "virtu"</note> and that authority <note>original: "alturjta", a variant of "autorità"</note> which are meant to be united.
— Many small noises joined together are heard from further away than if they were separate.

->**ON FORCES**<-

— Many small forces joined together produce a greater impetus than when they are separate; because if you consider the impetus produced by the united force, and compare it to the portions of impetus produced by the parts of the divided and disintegrated force, you will find the power of that united impetus to be greater than that of the divided impetus.

**ON SUPPORTS** — Many small supports joined together will sustain a greater total weight than if they were separate. For example: 1,000 reeds of the same thickness and length; if they are separate and each is placed upright, each will bend when loaded with an ordinary <note>original: "d'un ordinario"</note> weight. But if you tie them together with tight bindings so that they touch each other, they will be able to support so much weight upright that for each individual reed, it will be found to support twelve times more weight than it did before.

**ON LIGHTS** — Many small luminous bodies joined together will have a greater power than each would have on its own if it were separate. You will see the proof if you place many lights in a straight line and stand at a certain distance facing the middle of that line; note the quality of the light produced by these lights, and then unite them together: you will see the place where you were standing becomes much brighter than before. Furthermore, it is known that the stars have a light equal to that of the moon if joined together; and if it were possible to join them, they would compose a body much larger than that of the moon. Nevertheless, even though it may be clear and they all shine, if the moon is not in our hemisphere, our part of the world remains dark.

**SUPPORTS** — If two columns, while separate, are each capable of supporting 100 pounds, if you join them together, they will support 300 pounds.

**ON THE PRESSURE OF WEIGHT** — It is impossible for a support, of whatever thickness and strength, to ever twist or break when loaded vertically with a weight that is equidistant to its center; rather, it may well be driven into the ground. But when the excess weight is placed more on one side of the support than the other, the support will bend toward the side where it is most pressed by the greater load, and it will break in the middle of the opposite side—that is, in that part which is furthest from the ends.

<summary>Leonardo explores the principle of synergy, arguing that forces, structural supports (like reeds or columns), and lights become exponentially more powerful when united than when acting individually. He also provides a geometric observation on how vertical supports fail when weight is distributed unevenly.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, structural engineering, synergy, optics, weight distribution, impetus, columns, stars and moon</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous folio, Leonardo expands on the concept of "united force." While the previous page dealt with the travel of images (perspective), he now applies the principle of synergy to physical mechanics, structural engineering, and optics, arguing that the whole is significantly more powerful than the sum of its individual parts.</meta>

<header>FORCES — WEIGHT — SUPPORTS — LIGHTS</header>

### FORCES — WEIGHT — SUPPORTS — LIGHTS

— Separate powers will not all be active at the same time or for the same function <note>original: "office"</note>.
— That power <note>original: "virtu"</note> and that authority <note>original: "alturita", a variant of "autorità"</note> which comes from being united...
— Many small noises joined together are heard from further away than if they were separate.

**ON FORCES.** — Many small forces joined together have a greater action than those that are separate; indeed, if you compare the action produced by the united force to the individual parts of the action produced by the parts of the force when divided and disunited, you will find that the power of the united action is greater than that of the divided action.

— *If a support...* **ON SUPPORTS.** — *Many small supports joined together will sustain a greater total weight than if they were separate; for example: 1,000 reeds of the same thickness and length being separate, each of those placed upright will bend if loaded with an ordinary weight; but if they are bound together with tightly cinched ties so that they touch, they will be able to stand and support a weight such that each reed will be found to carry twelve times more weight than before.*

**ON LIGHTS.** — Many small luminous bodies being joined together, each of them will have more power than when it was separate; you will see proof of this if you place many lights on a straight line and stand at a certain distance facing the middle of that line; if you note the quality of light produced by these lights, and then join them together, you will see the spot where you were standing illuminated more than before. Likewise, it is known that the stars have a light similar to that of the moon, and that by uniting them—if it were possible to unite them—they would compose a body much larger than that of the moon; and yet, even though the sky is clear and they all shine, if the moon is not in our hemisphere, our part of the world remains dark.

**SUPPORTS.** — *If two columns are separate and each can support 100 pounds alone, if you join them together, they will support 300 pounds.*

**ON THE PRESSURE OF WEIGHT.** — *It is impossible for a support of uniform thickness and strength, being loaded upright with a weight equidistant from its center, to ever twist or break, though it may well sink into the ground; but if the excessive weight is placed more on one part of the support than the other, the support will bend toward the side where it is most pressed by the heavier load and will break in the middle of the opposite part, that is to say, in that part which is furthest from the ends.*

---

1. *Alturita* for *autorità* (authority). This word seems to be used here in its etymological sense: *auctoritas*, from the Latin *augere*, meaning to increase or augment. — The form given here to the "r" in the word *alturita*, which makes it look like a "j" or our "y", is rare; another example can be found on folio 19r, in the word *chiaramente* (clearly) on the 8th line.
2. In other words: *this power and this increase that forces acquire by becoming united...* — This version seemed preferable to what could be given for the same phrase by viewing, in *equella*, the *e* as the third person of the verb *to be*.

<summary>Leonardo explores the principle of synergy, demonstrating how combined forces, structural supports (like bundled reeds), and lights produce effects far greater than the simple sum of their parts. He also notes how off-center weight causes structural failure in columns.</summary>
<keywords>synergy, mechanics, structural integrity, optics, bundled forces, columns, reed experiment, luminous bodies</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigations into mechanics, specifically focusing on the physics of impact, weight, and the trajectory of projectiles. He explores the differences between cumulative forces and single unified forces.</meta>

— FOLIO 4 (recto). —

<note>The following is a translation of Leonardo da Vinci's mirror-script Italian, where he records his observations on physics and mechanics.</note>

### On weights
Many small heavy bodies, when joined and united together, will be of greater weight than when they are separate; that is, if you take lead filings <note>original: "rasspatura di pionbo"</note> or crushed glass, weigh them, and then melt them together, you will find that the weight has increased. <note>Leonardo likely observed an apparent increase in weight due to the removal of air pockets or the oxidation of the metal during melting.</note>

### On impacts
If you let a one-pound hammer fall a hundred times from the height of one cubit <term>braccio: a Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or the length of an arm</term> onto a lead rod, and then you take a hammer or other weight that is the same thickness as the first hammer but long enough to weigh one hundred pounds, and likewise let it fall from the height of one cubit onto a lead rod similar to the first; you will see how much deeper the rod is pierced by the single united impact than by the first series of blows.

### On flight <note>original: "fuga" – here referring to the discharge or momentum of a projectile</note>
Flight behaves in the opposite way; for if a one-pound crossbow <term>balestro: a mechanical bow used for shooting bolts or stones</term> drives an arrow one hundred cubits, a ten-pound crossbow will not then drive its arrow one thousand cubits. All instruments behave in this same way which, through violent motion, generate a second motion in the object that is pushed and driven by said instrument.

### On flight
That stone or other heavy object which is thrown with fury will change the line of its course halfway through its journey. If you know your crossbow shoots two hundred cubits, stand one hundred cubits away from a bell tower and aim at the top of that tower and shoot your arrow. You will see that at one hundred cubits beyond that tower, the arrow will be driven in a perpendicular line; and if you find it so, it is a sign that it had finished its <term>violent motion: the artificial force imparted by the machine</term> and was entering into <term>natural motion: the inherent tendency of heavy objects to fall toward the center of the Earth</term>; that is, being heavy, it fell freely toward the center.

### On the arrow driven by the crossbow
If a crossbow or other machine drives a weight of four pounds a distance of four hundred cubits, then four weights of one pound each will not reach those first four hundred cubits.

### On the body
If a hammer falling four cubits onto a lead rod drives itself in by one finger's width, that which falls eight cubits will drive itself in by one and a half fingers.

### On weight
If a four-pound hammer falling from a height of three cubits drives into the lead by one finger, a hammer of the same thickness but double the weight, falling from the same height as the first, will not drive in twice as much as the first, but only one and a half times as much.

---

<margin>4</margin>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of percussion and ballistics, noting that a single large force is more effective than many small ones and describing the transition of a projectile from forced momentum to natural gravitational fall.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, percussion, ballistics, gravity, violent motion, natural motion, crossbow, measurement, lead, impact</keywords>

<header>WEIGHTS — IMPACTS — FLIGHT OF A PROJECTILE</header>

## WEIGHTS — IMPACTS — FLIGHT OF A PROJECTILE

4 **ON WEIGHTS.** — Many small heavy bodies joined and united together are found to have a greater weight than if they were separate; thus, if you take lead filings <note>original: "râpure de plomb"</note> or crushed glass and weigh them, and then melt them together, you will find the weight has increased. <note>Leonardo likely observed an apparent increase in weight due to the oxidation of the metal during heating or the removal of air pockets between the filings.</note>

**ON IMPACTS.** — If you let a one-pound hammer fall a hundred times from the height of one cubit <term>brasse: the French translation of the Italian "braccio," a unit of measurement roughly equal to 23 inches or 0.6 meters</term> onto a lead rod, and then you take a hammer or another weight that is the same thickness as the first hammer but of such a length that it weighs 100 pounds, and you let it fall in the same way from a height of one cubit onto a lead rod similar to the first, you will see how much deeper the rod is pierced by the single united impact than by the first series of blows.

**ON FLIGHT.** — *It* Flight <note>original: "la fuite" – referring to the discharge or momentum of a projectile</note> behaves in the opposite way; indeed, if a one-pound crossbow drives an arrow one hundred cubits, a ten-pound crossbow will not drive its arrow one thousand cubits. And it is the same for every instrument which, with a violent motion, generates a second motion made by the thing that this instrument pushes and drives.

**ON FLIGHT.** — A stone or other heavy thing thrown with fury will change the line of its course halfway through. And if you know of a crossbow of yours that shoots two hundred cubits, place yourself at a distance of one hundred cubits from a bell tower, set your sight above this tower, and shoot your arrow; you will see that at one hundred cubits beyond this tower, the arrow will strike in a perpendicular [vertical] line. If you find it so, it is a sign that it has finished its violent motion and was entering into natural motion—that is to say, being heavy, it was falling, free, toward the center <note>The "center" refers to the center of the Earth, which in the science of Leonardo's time was considered the natural destination of all heavy objects.</note>.

**ON THE ARROW DRIVEN BY THE CROSSBOW.** — If a crossbow or another machine drives a weight of 4 pounds <insert>ounces</insert> only 400 cubits away from it, four weights of one pound each will not add to those first 400 cubits <note>Leonardo is observing that splitting a mass into four parts does not result in the same aerodynamic or mechanical performance as a single unified mass.</note>.

**ON IMPACT.** — If a hammer, falling from 4 cubits onto a lead rod, sinks into it by one finger's width, the one that falls from 8 cubits will sink in by a finger and a half.

**ON WEIGHT.** — If a four-pound hammer, fallen from a height of 3 cubits, sinks into the lead by one finger, a hammer of the same size and double the weight, falling from the same height as the first, will not sink in twice as deep, but only once and a half.

---

1. According to Venturi: "The Milanese cubit is to the French foot as 11 : 6; it is 0.593 of a meter." (*Essay on the physical and mathematical works of Leonardo da Vinci*, p. 46, no. I.) <note>Giovanni Battista Venturi (1746–1822) was an Italian physicist and one of the first scholars to seriously study Leonardo's scientific manuscripts.</note>
2. Compare this passage with several others indicated in the table of contents, article: *proportion of the thing moved with that which moves it (of the motor with the mobile).*

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of weight, impact, and ballistics, observing that forces and results (like penetration or distance) do not scale in a simple linear fashion. He also describes the trajectory of a projectile as a transition from "violent motion" (the force of the shot) to "natural motion" (gravity).</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, ballistics, gravity, violent motion, natural motion, measurement units, impact force, physics experiments</keywords>

### [Translation of Leonardo's Mirror Writing]

ON IMPACT AND WEIGHT — The impact made by one pound <note>original: "libbra"</note> of weight, striking with it <gloss>with it</gloss> upon a rod of lead.
— If you strike with two hammers of equal weight, but one is of double the width, the one that is twice as wide will drive itself twice as less into the lead than the other, falling from the same height.

IMPACT AND WEIGHT — 2. Weights of the same quality, falling from the same height, will give a lesser impact one than the other in proportion to how much more oblique the line of the fall is for one than the other. That is: as much as the line *m-n* enters into the line *m-s*, so much will weight *b* give a lesser impact than weight *a*.

IMPACT — If one gives a blow with the rod *m-s* at point *n*, while holding *m* in the hand: as many times as *m-n* enters into *n-s*, so many times the impact will be less than if it were given with the length of *m-n*. This is because *n-s* creates so much leverage <note>original: "lieua"</note> after point *n* that it causes the impact to lack sufficient strength <note>original: "ualitudine"</note>.

— A method of weighing how much weight placed upon the lever *m* will turn the screw with eighty thousand pounds of weight.
— There will be as much effort in turning the screw *m-n* if it has one thread <gloss>pane</gloss> <note>Leonardo uses "pane" (literally 'bread') to refer to the thread or tooth of a screw.</note> as if it had a hundred; however, if there is only one thread, it will support 10 pounds; if there are 10 threads, it will concern one pound of weight for each.

IMPACT AND WEIGHT — 2. Weights of the same quality, falling from the same height, will give a lesser impact one than the other in proportion to how much more oblique the line of the fall is for one than the other. That is: as much as the line *m-n* enters into the line *m-s*, so much will weight *b* give a lesser impact than weight *a*.

IMPACT — If one gives a blow with the rod *m-s* at point *n*, while holding *m* in the hand: as many times as *m-n* enters into *n-s*, so many times the impact will be less than if it were given with the length of *m-n*. This is because *n-s* creates so much leverage after point *n* that it causes the impact to lack sufficient strength.

— A method of weighing how much weight placed upon the lever *m* will turn the screw with eighty thousand pounds of weight.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the force of a blow is affected by the surface area of a hammer, the angle of descent, and the length of a lever, while also calculating the mechanical advantage of screw threads.</summary>

# STRIKES — WEIGHT — LEVER

**ON IMPACT AND WEIGHT.** — The impact delivered by one pound of weight by striking with it upon a rod of lead . . . .

— If you strike with two hammers of equal weight, but one is of double the width, the one that is twice as wide will sink half as deep <note>The original French "une fois plus moins" suggests a proportional decrease in penetration.</note> into the lead as the other, when falling from an equal height.

**IMPACT AND WEIGHT.** — 2 impact weights of equal quality, falling from an equal height, will deliver a lesser blow than the other in proportion to how much more oblique the line of the fall is for one than for the other; that is to say, as much as the line *mn* enters into the line *ms*, so much will weight *b* deliver a lesser impact than weight *a*.

**IMPACT.** — If you deliver a blow with the staff *ms* at the point *n*, while holding *m* in your hand: as many times as *m-n* goes into *n-s*, so many times the impact will be less than if you delivered it with the length of *m-n*, because *n-s* acts as a lever <term>lever (levier): a rigid bar used to transmit or modify force</term> beyond point *n*, which causes the blow to lack sufficient force.

— A method of weighing what weight placed upon the lever *m* will turn the screw with eighty thousand pounds of weight.

— There is as much fatigue <note>In this context, "fatigue" refers to the mechanical work or effort required.</note> in turning the screw *mn* having one thread <note>original: "pas" (step/pitch)</note> as if it had a hundred, for if there is only a single thread, it will support 10 pounds; if there are 10 threads, one pound will be distributed to each.

<vocab>strike, weight, lever, screw, hammers, lead</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the physics of percussion and mechanical advantage, noting how the surface area of a hammer affects its penetration into lead and how screw threads distribute weight. Leonardo demonstrates that increasing the number of threads on a screw divides the load proportionally, though the total effort remains constant.</summary>

# FOLIO 5 (recto)

- escape is caused by motion, and motion is born from force, and force from weight and motion. <note>original: "fuga echavsata dalmoto..." Leonardo is exploring the interconnectedness of physical dynamics here.</note>

I ask: if the 2 arms of the balance scale are divided into equal parts, and at a, b, c, d, and e there is placed one pound for each, how many pounds will create resistance at f? You shall do it thus: a provides resistance to one pound placed at f; b provides resistance to 2; c to 3; d to 4; e to 5; so that the entire sum provides resistance to 15 pounds placed at f.

<note>Diagram of a balance scale with a fulcrum. The left arm is divided into segments labeled a, b, c, d, e, with weights hanging from each. The right arm ends at point f.</note>

If a balance scale has a weight which, in its length, is similar to one of its arms—that is, m-n—and it weighs 6 pounds, how many pounds placed at f will provide resistance to it? I say that 3 pounds will be sufficient; because if the weight m-n is as long as one of the arms, you can consider it to be located in the middle of the arm of the scale at point a. Therefore, if at a there are 6 pounds, another 6 pounds placed at r <note>Point r likely represents the equidistant point on the opposite arm.</note> would provide resistance to it; and if you move it further forward to the extremity of the scale at point r, 3 pounds will provide resistance to it.

<note>Diagram showing a beam with a distributed weight labeled m-n on one side and a counterweight at point f. Points a and r are marked on the beam to indicate centers of gravity or leverage points.</note>

Although the weight placed upon the scales may be of unequal protrusion, nonetheless the center of the weight falls below the center of the attachment of the scale. <term>attachment (pichatura): the pivot point or fulcrum from which the scale beam hangs.</term>

<note>Small sketches illustrating a simple lever and two tables/benches, demonstrating principles of weight distribution and center of gravity.</note>

- If the weight hangs outside of the scales by unequal protrusion, the weight is not justified. <note>i.e., the measurement will not be accurate or "true."</note>
- If one beam supports a thousand pounds, how much will four beams support placed one on top of the other?
- If a cord of one arm's length <term>arm (braccio): a Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches.</term> supports a hundred pounds, how many pounds will a cord of the same thickness support that is a hundred arms long?
