# FLIGHT OF A PROJECTILE — WEIGHT — SUPPORTS.

— *Flight is caused by motion, and motion is born from force, and force from weight and motion.* <note>original: "La fuite est causée par le mouvement..." Leonardo is defining a chain of physical causality where weight and motion generate the force necessary for a projectile's flight.</note>

<term>ON WEIGHT.</term> — I ask, if the two arms of the balance are divided into equal parts and one pound is placed at each of the points *a, b, c, d, e*, how many pounds will create resistance for them at *f*. You shall calculate it thus: *a* creates resistance to one pound placed at *f*, *b* creates resistance to 2, *c* to 3, *d* to 4, *e* to 5, so that the entire sum creates resistance to the 15 pounds placed at *f*. <note>Leonardo is demonstrating the law of the lever; weights further from the fulcrum exert more "moment" or turning force.</note>

<term>ON WEIGHT.</term> — If a balance has a weight that is similar in length to one of its arms, let it be *mn*, which weighs 6 pounds, how many pounds placed at *f* will create resistance for it? I say that 3 pounds are sufficient, because if the weight *mn* has the same length as one of the arms, you can estimate that it is placed at the middle of that arm of the balance, at point *a*; therefore, if there are 6 pounds at *a*, 6 other pounds placed at *r* will create resistance for them, and if you pull just as far forward, to the end of the balance at point *r*, three pounds will create resistance for it. <note>He is calculating the center of gravity for a distributed load, treating a long weight as if its entire mass were concentrated at its midpoint.</note>

<term>ON WEIGHT.</term> — Although the weight placed on the scales may have an unequal projection, nevertheless the center of the weight falls under the center of the suspension of the balance.

— *If the weight hangs outside the balance with an unequal projection, the weight will not be justified.* <note>By "justified," Leonardo means the scale will not be balanced or accurate.</note>

— *If one beam carries a thousand pounds, how much will four beams placed one on top of the other support?* <note>A fundamental question of structural engineering regarding the load-bearing capacity of stacked versus solid materials.</note>

— *If a rope of one fathom carries one hundred pounds, how much will a rope of the same thickness, which is one hundred fathoms long, carry?* <term>Fathom</term> <note>original: "brasse." A traditional unit of measurement based on the reach of a man's arms, roughly 6 feet.</note>

<summary>Leonardo investigates the mathematical laws of leverage and centers of gravity, while posing practical engineering questions about the strength of beams and ropes. He concludes that a distributed weight acts upon its central point and explores how force is derived from the interaction of weight and motion.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, balance scale, leverage, center of gravity, structural engineering, physics of motion, weight distribution</keywords>

# — FOLIO 5 (verso). —

<note>Facsimile reproduction of Leonardo's manuscript page containing mirror-script Italian and geometric diagrams regarding the extraction of square roots and the squaring of figures.</note>

<note>Geometric diagram showing a semicircle and a chord, used for the construction of a square root.</note>

If you wish to find the <term>root</term><note>The "square root" (radice) in Renaissance mathematics was often visualized as the side of a square with a given area.</note> of any number by way of geometry, do it thus: if you wish to find the root of 9, make nine spaces as appears in **c b**; then add another similar space to it and you will make the line **c d** divided into 10 spaces; then place the point of the <term>compass</term><note>original: "sesta." A common term for dividers or compasses used in drafting.</note> in the middle of the line **c d** at point **f**, and encompass it with the circle **c r d**; then draw a line between the ninth and the tenth space, raised upwards until it intersects at the point in the circle above, which line will be the line **a b**; and it shall be the root of nine spaces, that is, it enters 3 times into **c b**.

— And if you wish to find the root of any number, always add one "one" more, as appears in **b d**, and find its center and make the circle as you did above.

<margin>
<note>Diagrams of a right-angled triangle and a rectangle.</note>
</margin>
— When you want to <term>square</term><note>To "square" (riquadrare) means to find the area of a shape or to construct a square with an equivalent area.</note> the <term>right-angled triangle</term><note>original: "ortogonio." From the Greek 'orthogonios,' referring here to a right-angled figure.</note> where the base is 4 and the other side is 8: in the place of 4 you shall say 2, and 2 times 8 is sixteen, and that is what it will be. <note>Leonardo is using a shortcut for the area of a triangle: (Base ÷ 2) × Height.</note>

<margin>
<note>Diagrams of a pentagon, a tetrahedron (3D pyramid), and a trapezoid.</note>
</margin>
— To square any <term>faceted body</term><note>original: "chorpo affacciato." This refers to a polyhedron or a solid with flat faces.</note>, square one of the triangles and it is done.
— On squaring every body.

<note>Printed transcription of Leonardo's text from the scholarly edition.</note>

— If you wish to find the root of any number by way of geometry, do it thus: if you wish to find the root of 9, make nine spaces as appears in **c b**; then add another similar space to it and you will make the line **c d** divided into 10 spaces; then place the point of the compass in the middle of the line **c d** at point **f**, and encompass it with the circle **c r d**; then draw a line between the ninth and the tenth space, raised upwards until it intersects at the point in the circle above, which line will be the line **a b**; and it shall be the root of nine spaces, that is, it enters 3 times into **c b**.

— And if you wish to find the root of any number, always add one "one" more, as appears in **b d**, and find its center and make the circle as you did above.

— When you want to square the right-angled triangle where the base is 4 and the other side is 8: in the place of 4 you shall say 2, and 2 times 8 is sixteen, and that is what it will be.

— To square any faceted body, square one of the triangles and it is done.

— On squaring every body.

# ROOTS AND SQUARES (GEOMETRY).

— If you wish to find the <term>root</term><note>The square root, visualized here as the side of a square.</note> of any number by means of geometry, proceed as follows: If you wish to find the root of 9, create 9 spaces, as shown in **cb**, then add another similar space to it and you will form the line **cd**, divided into 10 spaces; then place the point of the compass at the midpoint of the line **cd**, at point **f**, and encompass it with the circle **crd**; then, upon this line, between the ninth and the tenth space, raise a line <insert>which is perpendicular to it and</insert> which intersects the circle above, which will be the line **ab**: it will form the root of the nine spaces, which is to say that it fits 3 times into **cb**.

— And if you wish to find the root of any <insert>other</insert> number, always add one more, as appears in **bd**, find its midpoint, and draw a circle just as you did above.

— When you wish to <term>square</term><note>To "square" a shape means to calculate its area or to find a square of equal area.</note> a rectangle whose base is 4 and whose other side is 8, instead of 4 you shall say 2, and instead of 8 you shall say 16 <note>Leonardo is likely describing a method of proportional transformation where the area remains constant: 4 x 8 = 32, and 2 x 16 = 32.</note>, and it will amount to the same thing.

— To square the surface of a body with flat surfaces, you must square <insert>each</insert> of the triangles, and the task will be complete.

— To square any body whatsoever.

# — FOLIO 6 (recto). —

<note>Manuscript facsimile area containing sketches and mirror writing in Leonardo's hand.</note>

<note>Diagram showing the measurement of height using the shadow cast by a pole versus a tower.</note>

If you wish to measure a height by the shadow of the sun, take a staff that is one <term>braccio</term><note>An Italian unit of measurement, literally a "length of an arm," roughly 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> long and fix it upright; wait until the sun causes it to cast a shadow of 2 *braccia*. Then, immediately measure the shadow of the tower, and if it is 100 *braccia*, the tower will be 50. And this is a good rule.

<note>Geometric diagram of triangles relating height to shadow length.</note>

Just as much as **b. c.** fits into **a. c.**, so much the height of the tower fits into **a. n.** <note>Leonardo is applying the principle of similar triangles: the ratio of the pole to its shadow is the same as the ratio of the tower to its shadow.</note>

<note>A drawing of a vertical column and a separate drawing of a cross-section of a bundle of ropes or rods.</note>

If one rope supports one hundred pounds, how much will ten similar ropes support if they are wound tightly together?

If one piece of timber sustains one hundred pounds, how much will 10 similar timbers support if they are tied tightly together? <note>Leonardo is questioning whether the strength of a bundle is simply the sum of its parts or if the binding increases the collective capacity.</note>

<note>Sketch of a frame saw or bow saw cutting through a timber.</note>

Just as much as **a. b.** receives **c. d.** within itself, so much more quickly will **c. d.** be sawn than **a. b.**

---

<note>Printed transcription of the manuscript text above.</note>

6 — If you wish to measure a height by the shadow of the sun, take a staff that is one *braccio* and fix it, and wait until the sun makes it cast 2 *braccia* of shadow. And immediately measure the shadow of the tower, and if it is 100 *braccia*, the tower will be 50. And this is a good rule.

— Just as much as **b. c.** enters into **a. c.**, so much the height of the tower enters into **a. n.**

— If one rope supports one hundred pounds, how much will ten similar ropes support that are wound tightly together?

— If one piece of timber sustains 100 pounds, how much will 10 similar timbers support, tied tightly together?

— Just as much as **a. b.** receives within itself **c. d.**, so much sooner will **c. d.** be sawn than **a. b.**

<vocab>shadow, arm-length (braccio), tower, rope, pounds, timber, will saw, sooner</vocab>

# MEASUREMENT OF HEIGHTS — WEIGHT — FORCE AND RESISTANCE

6 — If you wish to measure a height by the shadow of the sun, take a staff that is one <term>braccio</term><note>original: "brasse". A traditional unit of length based on the reach of the arms; in this context, it refers to the Italian "braccio" used by Leonardo.</note> long, fix it upright, and wait until the sun causes it to cast a shadow of 2 *braccia*; then, immediately measure the shadow of the tower, and if it is 100 *braccia*, the tower will be 50; this is a good rule.

— Just as much as **bc** goes into **ac**, so much the height of the tower goes into **an**. <note>This refers to the geometric principle of similar triangles where the ratio of the object to its shadow remains constant.</note>

*— If one rope supports one hundred pounds, how much will ten similar ropes support when wound tightly together?*

*— If one piece of timber sustains one hundred pounds, how much will ten similar timbers support when tied tightly together?* <note>Leonardo is investigating the physics of tension and compression, questioning if the collective strength of a bundle is equal to or greater than the sum of its individual parts.</note>

— Just as much as **ab** contains **cd**, so much sooner will **cd** be sawn than **ab**. <note>A note on the proportional time required for sawing wood based on its thickness or volume.</note>

# — FOLIO 6 (verso). —

<note>Leonardo's manuscript facsimile section follows, containing mirror writing and geometric diagrams.</note>

There is a beam of 5 <term>braccia</term><note>A "braccio" (plural: braccia) is an Italian Renaissance unit of measurement, literally an "arm's length," approximately 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> that is leaning against another similar one. I want to lower the top by one *braccio*; I ask how much the foot of said beam will move away. Do it this way: 5 times 5 makes 25. And because you lower it one *braccio*, it becomes 4; wherefore you shall say 4 times 4 makes 16. Subtract 16 from 25; 9 remains. Take the <unclear>square</unclear> root of 9, which is 3. And that is how much the pole moves away from the other beam at the foot.

<note>Diagram showing two beams or poles, one vertical and one leaning, illustrating the calculation of a right triangle's base.</note>

There is a pole that is leaning against a wall that is 4 *braccia* high, and the pole is of a similar height. I move it at the foot and I do not know by how much, and likewise it drops from the top and I do not know by how much. I ask for the <term>reason</term><note>original: "rasone" (ragione). In this context, it refers to the mathematical logic or formula.</note> to know it without measuring it. . . . . Drop a plumb line or use a square at point **a. c.** and as many times as **c. b.** goes into **a. b.**, so many times **d. b.** goes into the pole, which you know is 4 *braccia*.

<note>Diagram showing a beam leaning against a wall, forming a right triangle, with points labeled a, b, c, d, e.</note>

Place the foot of the compass at **n.** and the other at **e.** and make the line **e. b.**; and as much as there is from **b.** to **d.**, so much will the pole have dropped from the top. Then make the line **b. c.**; and as many times as **c. d.** goes into **b. d.**, so much the space between the foot of the pole and the wall goes into the pole of 4 *braccia*.

<note>Diagram similar to the previous one, with more complex labels including 'n' and 'e', showing the use of a compass to determine displacement.</note>

---

<note>Printed scholarly transcription of the above manuscript text.</note>

— There is a beam of 5 *braccia* that is leaning against another similar one. I want to lower the top by one *braccio*; I ask how much the foot of said beam will move away. Do it this way: 5 times 5 makes 25. And because you lower it one *braccio*, it becomes 4; wherefore you shall say 4 times 4 makes 16. Subtract 16 from 25; 9 remains. Take the *square* root of 9, which is 3. And that is how much the pole moves away from the other beam at the foot.

— There is a pole that is leaning against a wall that is 4 *braccia* high, and the pole is of a similar height. I move it at the foot and I do not know by how much, and likewise it drops from the top and I do not know by how much. I ask for the reason to know it without measuring it. . . . . Drop a plumb line or use a square at point **a. c.** and as many times as **c. b.** goes into **a. b.**, so many times **d. b.** goes into the pole, which you know is 4 *braccia*.

— Place the foot of the compass at *in* **n.** and the other at **e.** and make the line **e. b.**; and as much as there is from **b.** to **d.**, so much will the pole have dropped from the top. Then make the line **b. c.**; and as many times as **c. d.** goes into **b. d.**, so much the space between the foot of the pole and the wall goes into the pole of 4 *braccia*.

<vocab>braccia (cubits), beam, root, compass, pole, wall, reason/logic, from the top, at the foot, will move away, dropped/lowered</vocab>

# MEASURES, TRIANGLES (GEOMETRY)

— There is a beam of 5 <term>braccia</term><note>A "braccio" (plural: braccia) is an Italian Renaissance unit of measurement, literally an "arm's length," approximately 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> that is leaning against another similar one; I want to lower the top by one *braccio*; I ask how much the foot will move away from the other. Do it this way: from 5 times 5, make 25; and if you lower it by one *braccio*, leaving 4, you shall say: 4 times 4 makes 16; subtract 16 from 25, and 9 remains; you extract the <unclear>square</unclear><note>original: "situ" (likely a transcription error for "carrée").</note> root of 9, which is 3, and that is the distance the foot of the pole moves away from the other beam.

— There is a pole that is leaning against a wall that is 4 *braccia* high, and the pole is of the same height; I move it at the foot, and I do not know by how much; it also descends at the top, and I do not know by how much; I ask for the <term>reason</term><note>original: "raison." In this context, it refers to the mathematical method or formula.</note> to know this without measuring it. . . . Drop a plumb line, or trace along the square at the location **a. c. b.**; as many times as **c. b.** is contained within **a. b.**, so many times **d. b.** is contained within the pole that you know is 4 *braccia*.

— Place the foot of the compass at **n.** and the other at **e.**, and draw the line **e. b.**; however far **d.** is distant from **b.**, that is how much the top of the pole has descended; draw the line **b. c.**, and as many times as **c. d.** is contained within **b. d.**, so many times the space between the foot of the pole and the wall is contained within the 4-*braccia* pole.

# RESONANT BODIES.

### RESONANT BODIES.

->— A *blow* struck against resonant bodies <note>resonant bodies: objects that vibrate or produce sound when struck, such as metal or glass</note> is immediately felt throughout the entire body in every [cloud?$^2$] that it takes$^3$.<-

---

1. On folio 8, recto, Leonardo appears to distinguish resonant bodies from both hard bodies and soft bodies.
2. *Nugolo* is ordinarily used for *nuvolo*, meaning a *cloud*; what other word should be read here? Could it be *angolo* <note>original: "angolo" (angle)</note>?
3. This proposition is obscure. Does it mean that when a body strikes an elastic object, each of the parts of that body strikes at a different angle and that the angle of reflection of the body as a whole is the result of these different angles? — On folio 44, verso, one will find nearly similar terms used to express an idea of the same kind. — See folio 19, recto.

# FOLIO 8 (recto)

---

<note>The following is a translation of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript folio 8r, featuring his characteristic mirror writing in Old Italian accompanied by geometric diagrams illustrating the physics of impact and percussion.</note>

When the object is stationary and the thing <note>the "thing" refers to the moving projectile or striking body</note> strikes it, it rebounds backward.
A moving thing is fixed and penetrates into a soft object.
A soft moving thing, struck against a hard object, is dented <note>original: "amacha"</note>.
A blow against an object that yields and gives way to that blow.
A blow against a resonant object —

Equality of motion, weight, and blow produces an equal rebounding of bodies.
The percussion of two unequal weights with equal course <term>course: the distance or path traveled by a moving body</term> and motion:
the course of the greater weight will be much less than the course of the lesser weight.
The greater course is in the greater weight — <note>Leonardo is likely exploring the relationship between momentum and resistance here</note>
Equal weights at unequal distances: the lesser course will leap back as much more than the greater, as the lesser enters into the greater.
A collision that flees —
Equal blows strike an object between them —
Equal weights with equal force and unequal course bring down their object.
A blow that breaks and shatters itself in the percussion of its object.

# PERCUSSION — MOTION — WEIGHT

— If the object [struck] and the thing that strikes are hard, the percussion tends to leap backward.

— The thing in motion sinks and penetrates into the soft object.

— The soft thing struck against a hard object flattens.

— A blow upon an object that yields and consents to that blow.

— A blow upon a resonant object.

— Parity of motion, weight, and blow produces an equal rebound of the bodies.

— Percussion of two unequal weights, but equal in course <term>course: the distance or path traveled by a moving body</term> and in motion.

— The course of the greater weight is much less than the course of the smaller weight.

— Greater course in the greater weight.

— Equal weights of unequal distance. [The one that has] the lesser course will leap further back than the other, by as many times as its course is contained within the other.

— Encounter followed by flight.

— Blows of equal force maintain an object between them.

— Equal weights of equal force and unequal course throw their object to the ground.

— A blow that breaks and divides itself in the percussion of its object¹.

---

1. Leaf cited by Libri, *History of Mathematical Sciences in Italy*, vol. III, p. 56. <note>Guglielmo Libri was a 19th-century historian and mathematician known for his extensive studies—and controversial thefts—of Galilean and Vincian manuscripts.</note>

<summary>This page outlines a series of physical principles regarding the mechanics of impact, specifically how the hardness, weight, and distance of travel affect the rebound or penetration of objects. It reflects early Renaissance efforts to codify the laws of motion and percussion.</summary>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

### [Translated Transcription]

— Mention of the things which I demand be granted to me in the proofs of this, my perspective.
— I demand that it be granted to me to affirm that the visual rays and luminous rays, each ray passing through the air—*of whatever kind it may be*—of whatever thinness, travel in a straight line from their cause to the object or point of impact.

**PROPORTION OF COLORS** — If one ounce of black mixed with one ounce of white produces one degree of darkness, how many degrees of darkness will 2 ounces of black produce over 2 <insert>and</insert> one ounce of white? <note>Leonardo is exploring the mathematical relationship between pigment ratios and perceived tonal value.</note>

**ON THE DIMINUTION OF THINGS AT VARIOUS DISTANCES** — The second object, being as far from the first as the first is from the eye, will appear half the size of the first, even though they are of equal size to one another.

**ON THE DEGREES OF DIMINUTION** — If you place the wall <term>pariete: the vertical plane or "window" of the painting</term> one cubit <term>braccio: a Florentine measure of length, roughly 23 inches</term> from the eye, the first object which is 4 cubits away from your eye will diminish by 3/4 of its height on said wall. And if it is 8 cubits from the eye, it will diminish by 7/8; and if it is 16 cubits away, it will diminish by 15/16 of its height. And so, from step to step, by doubling the previous distance, you will double the diminution.

->perspective<-
A small object close by and a large one far away, being seen within equal angles, will appear to be of equal size.

->perspective<-
Why things from afar appear large to the eye, while the proof made on the wall shows them to be small.

->perspective<-
I ask how far the eye can see a body that is not luminous, such as a mountain. It will be seen clearly if the sun is on the other side of it, and it will be seen more or less distant depending on where the sun is in the sky.

<summary>Leonardo establishes the geometric axioms of perspective, defining how light travels in straight lines and how the size of objects diminishes mathematically as their distance from the eye increases. He also poses questions regarding atmospheric perspective and the effect of backlighting on distant objects.</summary>

# PERSPECTIVE — PROPORTION OF COLORS

— Mention of the things that I demand be granted to me; it is with these that I have arranged my perspective. — I demand that it be granted to me to affirm that the *visual rays* and the *luminous rays*—all rays passing through air *of a single kind* that is of a uniform thinness—travel in a straight line from their cause to the object or point of impact.

**PROPORTION OF COLORS.** — If one ounce of black mixed with one ounce of white makes one degree of darkness, how many degrees of darkness will 2 ounces of black make over one ounce of white?

**ON THE DIMINUTION OF THINGS AT VARIOUS DISTANCES¹.** — The second object, being as far from the first as the first is from the eye, will appear half as small as the first, even though they are of the same size as each other.

**ON THE DEGREES OF DIMINUTION.** — If you place the wall <note>the "pariete" or transparent plane used to intercept the lines of sight</note> at one cubit <term>brasse: "braccio," a Florentine unit of measurement approximately 23 inches</term> from your eye, the first object which is 4 cubits away from your eye will diminish by 3/4 of its height on the said wall [on the said screen]. And if it is 8 cubits away from the eye, it will diminish by 7/8; if it is 16 cubits away, it will diminish by 15/16 of its height, and so on, so that each time the distance covered is doubled, the diminution will also double.

**PERSPECTIVE.** — A small thing from nearby and a large thing from afar, being seen with equal angles, will appear to be of equal size.

**PERSPECTIVE.** — Why distant things appear large to the eye, while the test made with the wall [with the screen] shows them as small.

**PERSPECTIVE.** — I ask how far the eye can see [what distance it appears to have to the eye] a body that is not luminous, such as a mountain; it will be seen clearly if the sun is beyond it, and will appear more or less distant according to the place the sun occupies in the sky.

---

<margin>1. See folios 10 verso and 11 recto of Manuscript A.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo establishes the geometric laws of perspective, calculating how objects diminish in size as distance doubles and questioning the relationship between pigment ratios and darkness. He also notes how atmospheric conditions and the sun's position affect the perception of distant landscapes.</summary>

# Translation of the Manuscript

<note>The following is a translation of Leonardo da Vinci's mirror writing, which he wrote from right to left.</note>

->perspective<-
->on motion<-

If two things of equal motion are at different distances from you, they will appear to have different motion; and as many times as the first distance is contained within the second, so much slower will the second object appear than the first.

->on weight<-

f. <note>Leonardo often uses letters like "f" to refer to specific points in a diagram or as markers for a proof.</note> The ground supporting the foundations <term>sostentacoli: structural supports or footings</term> of weights will sink so much more under one than under the other, as the one is narrower than the other, even when they are pressed by an equal weight. <note>Leonardo is observing that a smaller surface area exerts more pressure, causing it to sink deeper into the soil.</note>

->on motion<-

Where there is greater contact, the movement of a weight is more difficult. Experience will confirm the above-mentioned proposition to you: when you take a rope a hundred pounds <term>libbre: "pounds," a unit of weight; in Florence, the *libbra* was approximately 12 ounces or 339 grams</term> long, and you move it first stretched out and then coiled, you will see that beyond comparison, the motion of the coiled one is easier than the stretched one.

---

— Giving motion to a weight with much contact will be as easy as giving it to one with little. The reason is that the one with little contact concentrates <note>original: "multiplies"</note> the entire sum of its weight upon the thing that supports it, making its weight so heavy that the motion is difficult; the movement of the one with greater contact imparts so much less weight of itself to each part of its support, as there are a greater number of parts than in the one with little contact. —

---

# Printed Commentary

<margin>9</margin>
PERSPECTIVE OF MOTION — If two things of equal motion are at different distances from you, they will appear to have different motion; and as many times as the first distance is contained within the second, so much slower will the second object appear than the first.

ON WEIGHT — *f.* The ground supporting the foundations of weights will sink so much more under one than under the other, as the one is narrower than the other, even when they are pressed by an equal weight.

ON MOTION — Where there is greater contact, the movement of a weight is more difficult. Experience will confirm the above-mentioned proposition to you: when you take a rope a hundred pounds long, and you move it first stretched out and then coiled, you will see that beyond comparison, the motion of the coiled one is easier than the stretched one.

— Giving motion to a weight with much contact will be as easy as giving it to one with little. The reason is that the one with little contact concentrates upon the thing that supports it the entire sum of its weight, making its weight so heavy that the motion is difficult; the movement of the one with greater contact imparts so much less weight of itself to each part of its support, as there are a greater number of parts than in the one with little contact. —

<summary>This page contains Leonardo's notes on the "perspective of motion," where distance alters perceived speed, and his early mechanical theories on how surface area and contact points affect friction and the sinking of heavy structures.</summary>
<keywords>perspective of motion, relative velocity, mechanics, friction, weight distribution, pressure, foundations, physics of ropes</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the mathematical laws of optics and physics. While the previous page focused on the geometric rules of perspective regarding light and color, this page extends those principles to the perception of motion and the mechanical laws of friction and weight distribution.</meta>

### PERSPECTIVE OF MOTION — WEIGHT — MOTION

**PERSPECTIVE OF MOTION.** — If two things with the same motion are at different distances from you, they will appear to have different motion; and as many times as the first distance is contained within the second, so much slower will the second object appear than the first. <note>This is a fundamental observation of relative motion: an object far away (like an airplane) seems to move slower than an object close by (like a passing car), even if their actual speeds are identical.</note>

**ON WEIGHT.** — *The earth, the support of the supports of weights, will sink so much more under one* [support] *than under another, as the first is thinner than the other, if they are pressed by an equal weight.* <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of pressure: a concentrated weight on a small surface area (like a stiletto heel) sinks further into soft ground than the same weight spread over a wide surface area (like a snowshoe).</note>

**ON MOTION.** — *Where there is more contact, the movement of a weight is more difficult; experience will confirm the aforementioned proposition to you; when you take a rope weighing 100 pounds* <term>livres: "pounds," a historical unit of weight; in this context, the French translation refers to the Italian "libbra"</term>*, and you move it first stretched out, and then coiled upon itself, you will see that its movement is much easier, beyond comparison, when it is coiled than when it is stretched*¹.

— *It is just as easy to move a heavy body that has much contact as one that has little. The reason is that the body with little contact multiplies* <note>original: "multiplie"; meaning it concentrates the force</note> *its entire weight upon the thing that supports it, and weighs so heavily upon it that moving it becomes difficult; the movement of the body with the greatest contact* [which has the most points of contact] *distributes so much less of its weight to each part of its support, as those* [its points of contact] *exceed in number those of the body with little contact*².

---

<note>Editor's Note: 1 and 2. We see here how Leonardo, after asserting a fact, often arrived—once the experiment was performed—at asserting precisely the opposite. In the example before us, he renounces the second proposition just as he did the first, in order to arrive elsewhere at a better formulation.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines how distance affects the perceived speed of objects and explores early theories of friction and pressure, specifically how surface area impacts the movement and sinking of heavy weights.</summary>
<keywords>perspective of motion, relative speed, weight distribution, pressure, friction, experimental method, Leonardo da Vinci, mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the mechanics of weight and motion discussed on previous folios to the physics of light and vision. Leonardo explores how images travel through space and how distance affects the size of the "visual pyramid" formed between an object and the eye.</meta>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>Leonardo’s mirror-script transcription (mentally reversed for standard reading)</note>

Those bodies of equal size situated at various distances and locations will be seen through different pyramids; these will be so much narrower as one body is further than the other, and as the cause of the image is further away. <note>In Leonardo’s optics, the "pyramid" refers to the cone of light rays traveling from an object to the eye; the further the object, the "sharper" or narrower the angle of the pyramid.</note>

Those bodies of equal size situated at various distances—in order for them to appear to the eye—will send their *species* <term>species: from the Latin "species," meaning the visual image or "likeness" that an object projects into the surrounding air</term> to the eye through pyramids; these will be narrower for those bodies of equal size and various distances as their cause is more distant.

The visual line

The convergence of the pyramidal lines, starting from and caused by objects and terminating in the eye, must and of necessity be through straight lines.

Just as a stone thrown into water becomes the center and cause of various circles, and sound produced in the air spreads circularly, so every body placed within the luminous air spreads itself circularly and fills the surrounding parts with infinite likenesses of itself; it appears all in all, and all in every smallest part. <note>Leonardo is describing a fundamental principle of his optics: the idea that the entire image of an object exists at every point in space, which is why multiple people can see the same object from different angles at the same time.</note>

This is proven by experience; for if you close a window facing west and make a small hole <note>original: "bus", likely short for "buso" or "buco" (hole)</note>...

---

# PERSPECTIVE — THE RADIATION OF SOUND AND LIGHT

— *Parallels*¹. <note>original: "Parallèles"</note>

— Bodies of equal size, situated at *distances* in different locations, are seen through various pyramids <note>The "pyramid of sight" was a standard Renaissance concept where the eye is the apex and the object forms the base</note>, which will be increasingly narrow as the body—the cause of the image—is further away.

— *Bodies of equal size, situated at different distances, must appear to the eye in such a way that they send their images to the eye through pyramids which will be narrower* as these bodies of equal size and different distances are *more distant from their cause.*

— *the visual line. . . .*

— The convergence of the pyramidal lines, *originating and terminating* from objects and ending in the eye, must necessarily occur in straight lines.

— Just as a stone thrown into water becomes the center and cause of various circles, and as sound produced in the air spreads circularly², *its voice, in the same way bodies spread*, so every body placed within the luminous air spreads circularly, fills the surrounding parts with its infinite images [its image indefinitely reproduced], and appears as a whole in the whole, and as a whole in every *small part*³.

— This is proven by experience; indeed, if you close a window facing the east and make a hole. . . . <note>This likely refers to a camera obscura experiment</note>

# — FOLIO 10 (recto). —

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript (folio 10 recto) featuring mirror writing and optical diagrams, followed by a printed transcription and commentary from a scholarly edition.</note>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>Leonardo’s original mirror script, transcribed here in standard reading order.</note>

Perspective. And the demonstrative reason. By which. Experience confirms all. Things. Send. To the eye. Through pyramidal lines. Their. Likeness. By pyramidal lines. I mean. Those. Which. Depart. From the surface. Extremities of bodies. And by distant. Convergence. Are conducted. To a. Single. Point. Which point. In this case. I will show to be. Located. In the eye. The universal judge of all bodies. A point. I say. Is. That. Which cannot. Be divided. Into any. Part. Therefore, since. This point is indivisible. Which is located in the sight. No. Thing or body. Shall be seen. By the eye. That is not larger than said point. This being so, it is necessarily required. That the lines which come from the body to the point. Must be pyramid-shaped. And if anyone wished to prove. That the visual power. Does not consist in that point <insert>but rather is that black point seen in the middle of the pupil</insert> to these one could respond. That a tiny thing could never decrease through any distance. Such as would be a grain of millet or of panic-grass <note>A type of small-seeded forage grass</note> or other similar thing. And that thing. Which was larger than said point could never be seen entirely. As appears in the proof below.

<note>Diagram showing a horizontal axis with points m, n and a central segment a, b. This illustrates the geometry of how light rays from a wide object must converge to a point.</note>

Let **a** be the visual power, **b** be the convergence of the lines that come to the eye. Let **c** and **d** be the grains of millet within said convergence. You see by reason that these never decrease by distance. And the body **m** **n** cannot be entirely understood <note>original: "conplendere" (comprendere), meaning to be fully perceived or encompassed by the sight</note> by them. Therefore it is necessary. To confess that the eye. Has in itself a single indivisible point to which all the tips of the pyramids departing from bodies converge, as appears here. Below.

<note>Diagram illustrating the visual pyramid converging from a rectangular base (a, b) towards the eye (c, d) through an aperture (f). This is a classic representation of the "camera obscura" principle applied to human anatomy.</note>

Let **a** **b** be the eye; let its center hold the aforementioned point. If the line **e** **f** is to enter as a likeness through the small. Hole of the eye. It is necessary. To confess that the smaller thing cannot enter into the smaller unless it decreases, and in decreasing, it is necessary that the pyramid occurs.

---

### [Printed Commentary]

<margin>10</margin>
— Perspective. And the demonstrative reason, by which experience confirms that all things send their likeness to the eye through pyramidal lines. By pyramidal lines I mean those which depart from the surface extremities of bodies and, by distant convergence, are conducted to a single point. Which point, in this case, I will show to be located in the eye, the universal judge of all bodies. I say a point is that which cannot be divided into any part; therefore, this point being indivisible, which is located in the sight, no thing or body shall be seen by the eye that is not larger than said point. This being so, it is necessarily required that the lines which come from the body to the point be pyramid-shaped. And if anyone wished to prove that the visual power <term>uirtu visiua</term> <note>The "visual virtue" or "power," a medieval/Renaissance term for the faculty of sight</note> does not consist in that point, but rather is that black point seen in the middle of the pupil, to these one could respond that a tiny thing could never decrease through any distance, such as would be a grain of millet or of panic-grass or other similar thing; and that thing which was larger than said point could never be seen entirely, as appears in the proof below.

Let **a** be the visual power, **b** the convergence of the lines that come to the eye; let **c** and **d** be the grains of millet within said convergence. You see by reason that these never decrease by distance, and the body **m** **n** cannot be entirely understood <note>comprehended/perceived</note> by them. Therefore it is necessary to confess that the eye has in itself a single indivisible point to which all the tips of the pyramids departing from bodies converge, as appears here below.

Let **a** **b** be the eye; let its center hold the aforementioned point. If the line **e** **f** is to enter as a likeness through the small hole of the eye, it is necessary to confess that the smaller thing cannot enter into the smaller unless it decreases, and in decreasing, it is necessary that the pyramid occurs.

<vocab>perspective, pyramidal lines, indivisible point, visual power, the eye, pupil</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates through geometry that the eye must perceive images as "pyramids" of light converging into a single, indivisible point within the pupil. He argues that if the visual center were not a point, objects would not appear to shrink as they move further away.</summary>
<keywords>optics, perspective, visual pyramid, geometry of vision, pupil, Leonardo da Vinci, Renaissance science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric and physical exploration of vision, specifically defining the "visual pyramid" and the "indivisible point" within the eye where all light rays converge, as introduced on the previous page.</meta>

# PERSPECTIVE — OPTICS

<page-num>10</page-num>

<margin>10</margin> — Perspective is a <term>demonstrative reason</term> <note>A logical proof based on geometry</note>, by which experience confirms that all things send their likeness to the eye through pyramidal lines. By pyramidal lines, I mean those which start from the surface extremities of bodies and, by converging from a distance, lead to a single point; a point which I will show to be, in this case, located within the eye, the universal judge of all bodies. I call a "point" that which cannot be divided into any part; therefore, since this point located in the sight is indivisible, no evident thing or body will be seen by the eye that is not larger than this point; since this is the case, *it is necessary* that the lines coming from the body to the point be *larger in pyramids* <note>meaning they must form a tapering triangular or pyramidal shape to reach the smaller point</note>; and if someone wanted to prove that the <term>visual power</term> <note>original: "vertu visuelle" — the faculty or strength of sight</note> does not consist in this point, <insert>but that it is instead that black point seen in the middle of the pupil</insert>, one could respond to them that a tiny thing could never diminish at any distance—for example, a grain of millet, or of <term>panic-grass</term> <note>A small-seeded cereal grass similar to millet</note>, or other such things—and that any object larger than the aforementioned point could never be seen in its entirety, as is shown in the proof below.

Let **a** be the visual power; let **be** be the convergence of the lines coming to the eye; let the grains of millet **cd** be inside said convergence. You see by logic that distance never makes these diminish, and that the body **mn** cannot be entirely contained [within said convergence]; therefore, one must admit that the eye contains within it a single indivisible point, at which all the tips of the pyramids departing from bodies converge, as is shown below.

Let **ab** be the eye; [let us suppose] that its center occupies the aforementioned point; if the line **ef** must send its image through this so small hole of the eye, one must admit that a smaller [very tiny] thing cannot enter into that which is [even] smaller, if it does not diminish, and, as soon as it diminishes, it must necessarily result in a pyramid.

<summary>Leonardo explains the geometry of vision, arguing that light rays form a pyramid that converges at a single, indivisible point within the eye. He refutes the idea that the entire pupil acts as the sensor, noting that if it did, we could not perceive objects larger than the pupil or see things diminish with distance.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, optics, visual pyramid, geometry of sight, anatomy of the eye, convergence, visual power</keywords>

# FOLIO 10 (verso).

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript folio 10 verso, featuring his mirror script and several geometric diagrams related to perspective, followed by a printed transcription of the contents.</note>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

PERSPECTIVE
No body shall be of such great magnitude that, through a long distance, it does not appear smaller to the eye than a smaller object that is closer.

<note>Diagram 1: Study of the visual pyramid and the reduction of perceived size with distance.</note>

<margin>Perspective.</margin>
The <term>pariete</term> <note>original: "pariete" — the picture plane; literally a 'wall' or transparent partition</note> is a perpendicular line which is imagined in front of the common point where the convergence of the pyramids joins. And this *pariete* performs the same function for said point as a flat pane of glass would, through which, while looking at various things, you were to draw them upon it. These drawn things would be as much smaller than the original as the space between the glass and the eye was smaller than the space from the glass to the object.

<note>Diagram 2: Simple sketch of a horizontal cylinder.</note>

<margin>Perspective.</margin>
The convergence of the pyramids caused by bodies shows upon the *pariete* the variety of the sizes and distances of their cause <note>i.e., the original objects that caused the visual pyramid</note>.

<note>Diagram 3: Geometric diagram showing the "pariete" (picture plane) and converging lines of sight.</note>

<margin>Perspective.</margin>
All those planes situated at various heights and of equal [width]—
All those planes whose extremities join with perpendicular lines causing right angles: it is necessary that, being of equal width, the more they rise toward the eye, the less they are seen; and the more they pass beyond it, the more their true magnitude is seen.

<note>Diagram 4: Converging perspective lines within a rectangular frame.</note>

<margin>Perspective.</margin>
The further a spherical body moves from the eye, the more of it you see <note>Leonardo refers here to the fact that as a sphere recedes, the cone of vision encompasses a larger portion of its surface area</note>.

---

### [Printed Transcription]

PERSPECTIVE
— No body shall be of such great magnitude that, through a long distance, it does not appear smaller to the eye than a smaller object that is closer.

PERSPECTIVE.
— The *pariete* is a perpendicular line which is imagined in front of the common point where the convergence of the pyramids joins. And this *pariete* performs the same function for said point as a flat pane of glass would, through which, while looking at various things, you were to draw them upon it. These drawn things would be as much smaller than the original as the space between the glass and the eye was smaller than the space from the glass to the object.

PERSPECTIVE.
— The convergence of the pyramids caused by bodies shows upon the *pariete* the variety of the sizes and distances of their cause.

PERSPECTIVE.
— All those planes situated at various heights and of equal [width]—
— All those planes whose extremities join with perpendicular lines causing right angles: it is necessary that, being of equal width, the more they rise toward the eye, the less they are seen; and the more they pass beyond it, the more their true magnitude is seen.

PERSPECTIVE.
— The further a spherical body moves from the eye, the more of it you see.

<summary>Leonardo defines the 'pariete' or picture plane as an imaginary glass pane, establishing the geometric rules for how objects diminish in size based on their distance from the observer. He also notes the counter-intuitive observation that more of a sphere's surface becomes visible as it recedes from the eye.</summary>

<keywords>perspective, picture plane, visual pyramid, geometry, optics, spherical bodies, Leonardo da Vinci, Renaissance science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric exploration of vision, specifically defining the "pariete" (picture plane) and the mathematical reduction of objects as they recede from the eye, building on the definition of the visual pyramid from the previous folio.</meta>

<header>PERSPECTIVE <note>original: "PERSPECTIVE ¹" — see footnote 1 regarding historical misinterpretations of this page.</note></header>

# PERSPECTIVE.

— No body can be of such great magnitude that, at a long distance, it does not appear smaller to the eye than a smaller object that is closer.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— A <term>pariete</term> <note>original: "paroi" — a wall or partition; in perspective, the transparent "picture plane" through which the artist views the world.</note> is a perpendicular plane imagined in front of the common point where the convergence of the <term>pyramids</term> <note>The "visual pyramid" is the cone of light rays traveling from an object to the eye.</note> meets. And this *pariete* performs the same office for the said point as would a flat pane of glass, upon which you would draw the different things you saw through it. The things drawn would be smaller than their original, in proportion as the space between the glass and the eye was less than that between the glass and the object.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— The convergence of the pyramids caused by bodies will show upon the *pariete* the variety of the magnitudes and distances of their cause.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— *All planes situated at different heights and of the same.....*

— [For] all planes whose extremities join at perpendiculars, forming right angles, it is necessary—these planes being of the same width—that the one which rises closest to the height of the eye is seen the least, and the more it passes this height, the more one sees its true magnitude.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— The further a spherical body moves from the eye, the more of it is seen <note>original: "plus on en voit²" — referring to the fact that as a sphere recedes, the visual rays become more parallel, allowing the eye to perceive a larger portion of the sphere's surface (approaching a full hemisphere).</note>.

***

<margin>1. Folio 10 of Manuscript A was cited in error by Libri (III, 14, 46) and after him by M. A. Houssaye (425) as containing the invention and use of algebraic signs (Jordan, 72).</margin>
<margin>2. See folios 8 verso and 11 recto of the manuscript: *On the diminution of things, etc.*</margin>

<summary>This page defines the "pariete" or picture plane as a transparent window and explains how the visual pyramid dictates the perceived size and proportion of objects based on their distance from the eye. It also notes a specific optical property of spheres and corrects a historical error regarding the presence of algebraic notation in Leonardo's work.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, visual pyramids, picture plane, optics, spherical bodies, geometry of vision, Leonardo da Vinci, Manuscript A</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the exploration of optical perspective and the geometry of vision, specifically how the position of the eye relative to objects affects their perceived height and size on the visual plane.</meta>

<header>- FOLIO 11 (recto). -</header>

<margin>Proposition</margin>
No second object will be so much lower than the first that, with the eye positioned above, the second does not appear to be higher than the first.

<margin>Proposition</margin>
And that second object will never be so much higher than the first that, with the eye positioned below, the second does not appear to be below the first.

<margin>Proposition</margin>
If the eye looks at a second square through the center of a smaller, closer one <note>original: "minore più visivo" — the smaller object closer to the sight</note>, it will appear that the second, larger square is surrounded by the smaller one.

<margin>Proposition</margin>
**Proposition** — Secondary objects will never be of such great size that the first, smaller objects do not occupy <note>i.e., obstruct or overlap</note> or surround them.

**DEFINITION** — This proposition is proven by experience; for if you look through a small <term>aperture</term> <note>original: "spirachulo" — a small hole or sight-hole used in perspective studies</note>, there will be no object on the other side so large that it cannot pass through it; and where you see these things, if you block said aperture, that blockage will obstruct your view, and the object seen will appear surrounded and terminated by the outer edges of that aperture. And if you block it, that small blockage will be what occupies the entire view of the large object.

<note>Three geometric diagrams showing lines of sight and triangles. The left diagram shows a point of view projecting through a vertical line to a base. The middle and right diagrams show triangles with internal bisecting lines and alphabetical labels.</note>

A right-angled triangle <note>original: "triangolo ortogonio"</note> which is cut from the middle of any side to one of the opposite angles will always be divided in half. Let **a b c** be the right angle; let **d e g** be when that triangle is divided from the midpoint of the side to the angle with the line **e f**. And to prove how those two partitions are equal, we shall make four parts of said two partitions, each of which will converge with one of the angles at point **l**; and finding those four parts to be equal to one another, you can judge the two to be equal to the other two. **k l m** is half of the right-angled triangle; **o p q** will be one eighth. Therefore, among any right-angled triangles, one cannot be made inside another without it resulting in an even number, even if you were to proceed to infinity.

---

<margin>II</margin>
->PROPOSITION<-
— No second object will be so much lower than the first that, with the eye positioned above, the second does not appear to be **above the first** and higher.

->PROPOSITION<-
— And that second object will never be so much higher than the first that, with the eye positioned below, the second does not appear to be below the first.

->PROPOSITION<-
— If the eye looks at the second square through the **middle** center of the smaller, closer one, it will appear that the second, larger one is surrounded by the smaller.

->PROPOSITION<-
— **Proposition** — Secondary objects will never be of such great size that the first, smaller ones do not occupy or surround them.

->DEFINITION<- — This proposition is proven by experience; for if you look through a small aperture, there will be no object so large on the other side that it cannot pass through it; and where you see these things, if you block said aperture, that blockage will obstruct your view, and the object seen will appear surrounded and terminated by the outer edges of that aperture. And if you block it, that small blockage will be what occupies **all** the view of the large object.

— The right-angled triangle which is cut from the middle of any side to one of the opposite angles will always be divided in half. Let **a b c** be the right angle; let **d e g** be when that triangle is divided from the midpoint of the side to the angle with the line **e f**. And to prove how those two partitions are equal, we shall make four parts of said two partitions, each of which will converge with one of the angles at point **l**; and finding those four parts to be equal to one another, you can judge the two to be equal to the other two. **k l m** is half of the right-angled triangle; **o p q** will be one eighth.

— Therefore, among any right-angled triangles, one cannot be made inside another without it resulting in an even number, even if you were to proceed to infinity.

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates how the position of the eye creates optical illusions regarding the height of objects and explains how a small foreground object can obstruct a much larger distant one. He concludes with a geometric proof regarding the proportional subdivision of right-angled triangles.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, optics, right-angled triangle, visual field, aperture, geometry, proportions</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the discussion on optical perspective and the geometry of vision from folio 10, specifically focusing on how the eye perceives relative height and the geometric properties of triangles when divided.</meta>

# PERSPECTIVE — TRIANGLES.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— No second object is located so much lower than the first that, for the eye placed above, the second does not appear to be the higher of the two.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— And this second object is never located so much higher than the first that, for the eye placed above, the second does not appear to be below the first.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— If the eye looks at a second square through the center of a smaller one closer to the sight, the second, larger square will appear to be surrounded by the smaller one.

### PERSPECTIVE.

— **PROPOSITION.** — Secondary objects are never of such a magnitude that the first, smaller objects cannot contain and surround them.

— **DEFINITION.** [Demonstration]. This proposition is proven by experience; indeed, if you look through a small <term>aperture</term> <note>original: "soupirail" — literally a vent or air-hole, here used as a sight-hole for perspective experiments</note>, there will be no object so large that it cannot be seen through it, and, *if you block the said aperture, it will contain you...*, and the object seen will appear surrounded and limited by the edges of this aperture; so that if you block this small opening, this small blockage will be what occupies the sight of the large object <margin>See folios 8 verso and 10 verso of manuscript A.</margin>.

— The right-angled triangle, which is cut from the middle of any side [by a line going] to the vertex of the opposite angle, is always divided in half. Let **a b c** be the right angle, let **deg** be the triangle divided by the line **e f**, drawn from the middle of a side to the angle; to prove how the two sections are equal, we shall divide these two sections into 4 parts, each of these parts meeting with one of the angles at point **l** <margin>In other words: The right-angled triangle being divided into two sections by a line **l k**, led from the middle **l** of the side **h n** to the vertex of the angle **k**, we shall divide each of these two sections into 2 parts, such that the triangle **l k n** will be divided into 4 triangles, each having for the vertex of one of their angles the point **l**.</margin>. And finding these 4 parts equal to one another, you can judge that two of them are equal to the other two, and that if **k l m** is 1/4 of the right-angled triangle, **o p q** will be one-eighth of it.

— Therefore, one cannot create, within the interior of a right-angled triangle, another right-angled triangle without it occurring in an even number [having its equal], even if one were to go to infinity.

<summary>This page explores the paradoxes of visual perspective, demonstrating how small foreground apertures can encompass large distant objects, and provides a geometric proof for the equal division of right-angled triangles.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, right-angled triangle, geometry, visual perception, aperture, proportionality, manuscript A</keywords>

— FOLIO 11 (verso). —

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of a Leonardo da Vinci manuscript folio featuring geometric diagrams and mirror-written Italian text, followed by a modern printed transcription of that text.</note>

### [Facsimile Section]

<note>Upper diagram: A large circle with an inscribed triangle and several smaller intersecting circles and arcs, demonstrating geometric divisions of a circle. Points are labeled with letters (a, b, c, d, m, n, f, r).</note>

[Line] **a. b.** fits 6 times into the entire circle illustrated above. **a. c.** fits 8 times into the said circle. **a. d.** fits

# DIVISION OF THE CIRCLE

— **a. b.** fits 6 times into the entire circle illustrated above; **a. c.** fits 8 times into the said circle; **a. d.** fits 12 times; **a. m.** 24, **a. n.** 48, and the line **f. r.** fits 3 times, so that the said figure comes to divide the circle into 3, 6, 8, 12, 24, 48 [parts] exactly; and it can be divided, with the same rule, into an infinite number of equal parts¹; the logic for this is illustrated below.

— A method for dividing a circle into many equal parts, by drawing the line **a. e.**² — First draw this line with your ruler, and have its head touch the circle [at point **a** on the circumference;] let it extend [above this point by an amount equal to one-third of the <term>radius</term> <note>original: "rayon" — the distance from the center of the circle to its edge</note> **a. m.**, then divide the entire line **f. e.** with similar thirds; you may make this line as long as you wish³. Then place one point of the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "compas" — a V-shaped tool used for drawing circles or measuring distances</note> at **n**⁴ — go to 4⁵ —

— 4 — **n. f.** fits into the entire circle 3 [times], **n. a.** 4, **n. b.** 5, **n. c.** 6, **n. d.** 7, **n. e.** 8.

***

<note>Facsimile of Leonardo's manuscript page containing geometric studies and mirror writing.</note>

<note>Geometric diagram showing a large circle (a) above a smaller one (e), with internal petal-like arcs and intersecting points labeled a, b, c, d, e.</note>

The proof is that a. e. is the sixth <note>original: "sesto," referring to a sixth part of the circumference, often the radius length when used in compass constructions</note> of said circle, and said sixth is divided into 4 parts; so you will say 4 times 6 is 24. a. b. 12 — a. d. is the eighth of the circle. The proof is that a. e. is the sixth of said circle, and said sixth is divided into 4 parts; so you will say 4 times 6 is 24. a. b. [d] e. are 3 of those 4 parts; since 3 goes into 24 eight times, a. b. goes 8 times into the circle.

# DIVISION OF THE CIRCLE. — SQUARE AND OCTAGON.

— ad is the eighth of the circle; the proof is that ae is the sixth of this circle; and the said sixth is divided into 4 parts; so that you will say 4 times 6 is 24; ad is 3 of these 4 parts, so that 3 entering 8 times into 24, ad enters 8 times into the circle¹.

— To divide a square into eight faces <note>meaning to construct an octagon from a square</note>. Let the square be rfms; place the foot of the compass on the angle f, place the other foot on the center of the square and move this one from the center to the side, at point b; draw a line from b to f; at the intersection n will be found the side [of the octagon] between n and b.

---

1. Leonardo deduces, by a geometric construction, the knowledge of the eighth part of the circumference from the knowledge of the sixth part (the side of an inscribed hexagon). — Let the chord ae be equal to the radius of the circumference ace; from a and e as centers, trace an arc with a radius equal to the chord ae; the two arcs intersect at the center outside the circumference; the extended radius c, which joins their intersections, divides the chord and the arc ae into two equal parts, at point c; then describe two circumferences with a radius equal to ac, one from a and the other from c as centers; the straight line b, which joins their intersections, divides the chord and the arc ac into two equal parts, at point b; the line d will likewise divide the chord and the arc ec at point d. — If one now divides the circumference into 24 parts, ae will contain 4 and ad 3. Therefore, ad will be the eighth part of the circumference, just as ae is the sixth. — On the other hand, if ae is the sixth of the circumference, ac being half of ae, ac will be the twelfth, etc. (See folio 11 verso of Manuscript A, 1st figure.)

<note>Vocabulary: division of the circle, square, octagon, geometry, circumference, hexagon, chord, radius, compass, Manuscript A</note>

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof for dividing a circle into eight parts based on the radius (the "sixth") and a method for constructing an octagon from a square. It includes a detailed scholarly note explaining Leonardo's proportional logic using a 24-part division of the circumference.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, circle division, octagon construction, Leonardo da Vinci, Manuscript A, proportional mathematics, Euclidean geometry</keywords>

### [Facsimile Transcription]

In all triangles of 2 and 3 equal angles <note>referring to isosceles and equilateral triangles</note> to which a line is drawn from the midpoint of one side to the other, it is necessary that these lines compose a triangle which is in such proportion to the larger area that it contains within itself the fourth part of the whole.

If you cut any triangle of 2 equal angles <note>an isosceles triangle</note> from the middle of the base to the middle of the side, you will find the smaller cut to be the fourth [part] of the whole, that is **a b c**.

<note>Diagram of a long right-angled triangle divided by vertical lines.</note>

Every angle divided into any part, the remainder of the pyramid will always be in proportion to the whole.

**RIGHT-ANGLED TRIANGLE** <note>original: "ORTOGONIO", from the Greek 'orthogonios'</note>
The two acute angles of the right-angled triangle, when they are equidistant to their right angle, will be equal to the right angle <note>Leonardo is noting that the two 45-degree angles of an isosceles right triangle sum to 90 degrees</note>.

— FOLIO 13 (verso). —

a.b.c.d. fits once and a third into the circle below the proof, and to the lower right in the square a.b.h.r.

**PROPOSITION**

The circle that has its center placed upon the midpoint of the base of the equilateral triangle, which extends with its line <note>referring to the radius</note> to the summit of the upper angle, must necessarily have its line <note>the circumference or perimeter</note> contain within itself 5 of the bases of the aforementioned equilateral triangle.

**PROOF**

If every line conducted from the center to the circumference is equal to every other <note>a fundamental property of a circle's radii</note>, the promised proof clearly appears; because the line **b.e.** and the line **b.d.** are similar to the line **d.e.** which completes the equilateral triangle, and six of these triangles create the 6-sided figure <note>a regular hexagon</note> **d.e.f.g.h.i.** The line is similar to the line **b.d.**, which line, placed 5 times in the circle **a.b.k.n.m.**, composes an equilateral and equiangular pentagon; and the smaller circle becomes 1/6 smaller than the quantity of the larger one, because its line is composed of 5 lines equal to the 6 mentioned above.

->--- false ---<- <note>Leonardo frequently audited his own work; here he realizes the geometric logic for this specific proportion does not hold true.</note>

---

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigations into geometry and the calculation of areas and volumes, specifically focusing on the relationship between triangles and quadrilaterals, and pyramids and columns.</meta>

— Every square, whether equilateral <note>original: "equilatero"; meaning a square with four equal sides</note> or non-equilateral <note>original: "nonequilatero"; referring to a rectangle</note>, provided it has equal angles: if its entire base is shared by the base of a triangle (whether equilateral, non-equilateral, or of whatever shape it may be), as long as the tip touches the top edge, it always occupies half of the area <note>term: "possessione" (possession); Leonardo uses this term to mean the area or spatial extent of a geometric figure</note> of the square.

— In every column of equal thickness, if one extracts the largest possible pyramid that has equal angles at its base, that pyramid will be equal in volume to the remainder of the column. The proof is that the base *c.b.* contains the entire thickness of the column at the foot, and the bases at the top contain the entire thickness at the head. The triangle *c.* is similar to *a.d.* and *a.b.*, and since the 4 pyramids are similar to one another, the pyramid *c.d.* contains 2 parts, and the remainder of the column contains the other 2.

— Every triangle placed within parallel lines that extends from the lower to the upper line, and is founded upon the same base line—no matter what its length may be—it is necessary that within each one there is enclosed the same quantity of area.

### CIRCLES AND TRIANGLES

— abcd fits one and one-third times into the circle below; the proof of this is shown below, in the square abhr, on the right.

### PROPOSITION. —

— If a circle has its center placed on the midpoint of the base of an equilateral triangle, and its line [circumference] passes through the vertex of the upper angle, this line will necessarily contain within itself five times the base of the aforementioned equilateral triangle¹.

### PROOF:

— If every line drawn from the center to the circumference is equal to every other, the promised proof appears clearly. Indeed, the line be and the line bd are equal to the line de, which completes the equilateral triangle; if, with triangles equal to this last one, one forms the 6 faces defghi, the line [b a] is equal to the line bd; this line, placed 5 times in the circle abknm, composes an equilateral and equiangular pentagon <note>A five-sided shape where all sides and angles are equal.</note>, and the smaller circle is found to be smaller by $\frac{1}{6}$ than the large one, since its line is composed of 5 lines equal to the 6 mentioned above. — false² —

# TRIANGLE — CYLINDER — PYRAMID

— Any rectangle, whether equilateral <note>A square</note> or non-equilateral, provided that its angles are equal: if its entire base coincides with the base of a triangle (equilateral or otherwise, and of whatever shape), and provided the apex of said triangle touches the top side of the rectangle, the rectangle will always occupy double the area of the triangle <note>original: "la moitié le double" - Leonardo likely corrected himself mid-sentence</note> <insert>triangle</insert>¹.

— If, from any column <note>A cylinder</note> of uniform thickness, you extract the largest pyramid <note>In this context, a cone</note> you can find within it, having angles equal to its base, this pyramid will be equivalent to the remainder of the column; the proof of this is that the base *c b* contains the entire thickness of the column at the foot, and the top bases contain the entire thickness at the head; the triangle *c* is similar to *d a b* <insert>the triangle *a b c* is equal to triangle *c d b*</insert>; now, if the 4 pyramids are equal to one another, the pyramid *c d* contains 2 of them and the rest of the column contains the other 2.

— If any triangles are placed between parallels, such that they extend from the lower line to the upper <insert>between two parallel lines, one passing through one of their sides and the other through the vertex opposite that side</insert>, and they are founded upon the same base of any length, there will necessarily be enclosed within each of them the same area.

---

1. <note>Leonardo must have written *quadrato* (square/rectangle) instead of *triangolo* (triangle) out of distraction.</note>

# — FOLIO 15 (recto). —

<note>The top half of the page is a facsimile reproduction of a manuscript folio by Leonardo da Vinci, containing geometric diagrams and mirror writing in Italian. The bottom half is a printed literal transcription of the same text.</note>

<note>Translation of Leonardo's mirror writing in the facsimile:</note>

<note>Below the top-left diagram:</note>
The area <note>original: "posessione," referring to the surface area or space occupied</note> of every circle contains within itself 4 equal circles.

<note>Below the top-right diagram:</note>
The area of every square contains within itself 4 equal squares.

<note>Below the middle-left diagram:</note>
The square *b. f. d. c* shall be half the size of the square *a. b. c. d*, because the smaller square is divided by the line *b. d.* into 2 equal parts of the same likeness and size as the four equal parts into which the larger square is divided.

<note>Below the middle-right diagram:</note>
If the half-diameter <term>radius</term> of circle *a. b* is half of the half-diameter *a. c*, the smaller circle fits 4 times into the larger one.

<note>Text at the bottom of the facsimile:</note>
PROPOSITION
— If you draw the line *e [a] b* where the larger circle intersects with 2 diameters of the square at points *a* and *b*, and where said line *e [a] b* intersects the diameter *e. f.* at point *n*, there you shall begin and end a circle. This circle will contain within itself as much area as that which is enclosed between the one circle and the other <note>referring to the annulus or ring-shaped area</note>, and it shall be smaller than the larger one by that same amount. The proof is shown above and to the right.

PROOF
— That the circle *b. c.* holds within itself as much area as there is between it and the larger circle is clearly seen if square *a. e. b. f.* is similar to square *s. g. r. h.*, as appears by the contact that one makes at the center of the other; and the circle *b. c.* touches the four sides of that square just as circle *o. p.* does. Therefore, one circle is similar to the other, and if you look above, you will see the outer square is half the size of the other.

---

<meta>This page continues the transcription and commentary of Folio 15 (recto). # SQUARES — CIRCLES.

— The area <note>original: *étendue*, referring to the surface area</note> of any circle contains 4 circles equal to one another.

— The area of any square contains 4 equal squares.

— The square *b f d e* is half the size of the square *a b c d*; indeed, the smaller square is divided by the line *b d* into 2 equal parts, matching the shape and size of the four equal parts into which the larger square is divided.

— If the half-diameter <term>radius</term> of circle *a b* is half the half-diameter of another circle *a e*, the smaller circle fits 4 times into the larger one.

->PROPOSITION.<-

— If you draw the line *a b* at the points of intersection *a* and *b* of the large circle by the two diameters of the [circumscribed] square, and where this line *a b* is cut by the diameter *e f*, at point *n*, you begin and end a circle—this circle will contain as much area as that which is enclosed between the one circle and the other <note>referring to the area of the ring or annulus</note>, and will be half the size of the large one; the proof for this is given above and on the right.

->PROOF¹.<-

— That the circle *b c* contains as much area as is found between it and the large circle is clearly seen if the square *a e b f* is equal to the square *s g r h*, as appears by the contact that one makes with the other²; and the circle *b c* touches the four sides of this square just as the circle *o p* does [just as the circle *o p* touches the sides of its own square]; therefore, these circles are equal to each other; and if you look above, you will see that the outer square is half as large as the other.

***

<note>1. In the manuscript, the Italian word *propositione* <gloss>proposition</gloss> accompanies the figure on the left at the bottom of the page, and the word *pruova* <gloss>proof</gloss> accompanies the figure on the right.</note>

<note>2. In other words: This results from the fact that the diameter *s b* is common to both of them.</note>

<note>The list of vocabulary terms provided in the original text:</note>
circle, square, diameter, proposition, area, geometry

<summary>This page provides a formal transcription of Leonardo da Vinci's geometric proofs demonstrating that doubling the radius of a circle or the side of a square quadruples its area. It also outlines a specific "proposition" for constructing a circle with exactly half the area of a given circle.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, proportional area, circle, square, radius, mathematical proof, Leonardo da Vinci, diameters</keywords>

# FOLIO 15 (verso)

<note>The following section transcribes Leonardo da Vinci's mirror writing from the facsimile plate.</note>

<margin>
<note>Near screw thread diagrams</note>
— Where there is greater contact, there is more resistance. The tooth of the square screw <note>original: "vite quadra"</note> describes a longer path than that of the triangular teeth. It is true that the screw with triangular teeth is easier for its motor <note>the force or person turning it</note> because there is less contact and resistance.
</margin>

<margin>
<note>Next to the bellows diagram</note>
— By as much as the small nozzle <term>bocchuccio</term><note>literally "little mouth," the air outlet</note> "a" enters by quantity into the whole vacuum of the bellows <term>mantacho</term>, into that many parts will the weight placed above the bellows be distributed. That is to say, if we suppose the said nozzle enters 1000 times into the whole bellows, and that the weight pressing the bellows is 1000 pounds, I say that only one single pound of that weight will act upon that nozzle, and the other 999 will exert force on the other 999 parts of the bellows that remain after the nozzle.
</margin>

To find the center of the line m. n.
