<note>Printed transcription of the manuscript text provided by the editor.</note>

— Where there is greater contact, there is more resistance. The tooth of the square screw describes a longer path than that of the triangular teeth. It is true that the screw with triangular teeth is easier for its motor because there is less contact and resistance.

— By as much as the small nozzle "a" enters by quantity into the whole vacuum of the bellows, into that many parts will the weight placed above the bellows be distributed. That is to say, if we suppose the said nozzle enters 1000 times into the whole bellows, and that the weight pressing the bellows is 1000 pounds, I say that only one single pound of that weight will act upon that nozzle, and the other 999 will exert force on the other 999 parts of the bellows that remain after the nozzle.

— To find the center of the line m. n.

— To find the center of the equilateral triangle.

— To make a curved line divided into odd and equal parts, as appears in a. b. c., with a single opening of the compasses.

<note>Vocabulary: screw, tooth/thread, bellows, nozzle, equilateral triangle, curved line, compasses.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical efficiency of different screw thread shapes based on friction and explores the proportional distribution of air pressure in a bellows. He also provides geometric proofs for finding centers and dividing curved lines using a compass.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, friction, screw threads, pneumatics, air pressure, geometry, equilateral triangle, compass constructions</keywords>

# RESISTANCE — VOLUME — GEOMETRY

— Where there is the most contact, there is the most resistance, and the thread of the square-toothed screw <note>original: "la vis dent de la vis carrée"</note> describes <note>original: "circonscrit," meaning the path it traverses or traces</note> a longer path than that of triangular teeth; it is true that the screw with triangular teeth yields more easily to its motor <note>the force or person providing the motion</note>, because there is less contact and resistance.

— By as many times as the volume of the nozzle <note>original: "la bouche," literally the mouth</note> fits into the entire vacuum of the bellows, into that many parts will the weight be divided within the bellows illustrated above. Thus, if we say that the nozzle of the bellows fits 1,000 times into the total volume of the unit, and that the weight pressing upon it is also 1,000 pounds, the nozzle of the bellows will bear for its part only a single pound of that weight, and the other 999 parts of the weight will act on the 999 parts of the bellows that remain beyond the nozzle.

— To find the midpoint of the line *m n*.

— To find the center of the equilateral triangle.

— To create a curved line divided into different equal parts, as seen in *a b c*, using only a single opening of a compass <term>compas</term> <note>a V-shaped tool used for drawing circles or measuring distances</note>.

<note>Technical Vocabulary: resistance, screw, square teeth, triangular teeth, volume, bellows, equilateral triangle, curved line, compass</note>

<keywords>mechanics, friction, screw design, bellows, air pressure, geometry, equilateral triangle, compass constructions</keywords>

# FOLIO 17 (verso)

## Transcription of Manuscript Facsimile

<note>Leonardo's original text is written in right-to-left mirror script. It is translated here into standard English, preserving his original structure.</note>

<note>Geometric construction of an equilateral triangle</note>

To make an equilateral triangle upon a line.

When it happens that I must make an equilateral <term>equilateral</term> <note>A triangle where all three sides are of equal length.</note> triangle upon a line **a.b**, I will place the point of the compass in **a** and make the circle **c.b**. Then I will place it in **b** and make **a.c**, and where the circles intersect at **c**, there shall be the third angle.

<note>Geometric construction of a right-angled quadrilateral</note>

To make a rectangular quadrilateral upon a line.

If I wish to make a quadrilateral <term>quadrilateral</term> <note>A four-sided figure; here Leonardo is describing the construction of a rectangle or square.</note> of 4 right angles upon a line, I will make the equilateral triangle **a.c.e** and I will pass with the circle **a.c** beyond the intersection **c**, as much as I believe will contain within itself half of **a.c**, and I will place it in **c.d.e**; at **d** shall be the right angle.

<note>Geometric construction of a regular pentagon</note>

To make an equilateral and equiangular pentagon upon a line.

I want to place upon the line **m.n** a pentagon <term>pentagon</term> <note>A five-sided polygon.</note> of equal sides and equal angles, and I will do it in this manner: I will always make the equilateral triangle **m.c.n** upon the line **m.n**, and I will take, with the line starting from angle **m**, the middle of the face **c.n** at point **b**, and I will join its extremity **b** to the line **c.r**. And where it touches at point **a**, there you shall fix the point of the compass, and with the other [point] you shall make a circle that touches the ends of the line **m.n**; and said circle will contain within itself five of those lines.

***

## Printed Transcription

— To make an equilateral triangle upon a line.
— When it happens that I must make an equilateral triangle upon a line **a.b**, I will place the point of the compass in **a** and make the circle **c.b**. Then I will place it in **b** and make **a.c**, and where the circles intersect at **c**, there shall be the third angle.
— To make a rectangular quadrilateral upon a line.
— If I wish to make a quadrilateral of 4 right angles upon a line, I will make the equilateral triangle **a.c.e** and I will pass with the circle **a.c** beyond the intersection **c**, as much as I believe will contain within itself half of **a.c**, and I will place it in **c.d.e**; at **d** shall be the right angle.
— To make an equilateral and equiangular pentagon upon a line.
— I want to place upon the line **m.n** a pentagon of equal sides and equal angles, and I will do it in this manner: I will always make the equilateral triangle **m.c.n** upon the line **m.n**, and I will take, with the line starting from angle **m**, the middle of the face **c.n** at point **b**, and I will join its extremity **b** to the line **c.r**. And where it touches at point **a**, there you shall fix the point of the compass, and with the other you shall make a circle that touches the ends of the line **m.n**; and said circle will contain within itself five of those lines.

<note>Vocabulary: triangle, equilateral, quadrilateral, rectangle, pentagon, equiangular, circle, intersection, line, compass.</note>

<summary>This page provides step-by-step instructions for the geometric construction of an equilateral triangle, a rectangle, and a regular pentagon using a compass and straightedge. It demonstrates Leonardo's practical application of Euclidean geometry to create perfect polygonal shapes.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, equilateral triangle, rectangle, pentagon, compass construction, Euclidean geometry, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

## EQUILATERAL TRIANGLE, SQUARE, PENTAGON.

— To make an equilateral triangle upon a line:

— If it falls to me to make an equilateral triangle <note>original: "triangle équilatéral"</note> **a** upon a line **b**, I will place the point of the compass at **a** and I will make the circle **c b**, then I will place it at **b** and I will make the circle **a c**; and where the circles intersect, at **c**, the third angle will be found.

— To make a square upon a line:

If I wish to make a square upon a line, I will make the equilateral triangle **a c e**, I will extend with the circle **a c** beyond the intersection **c** by a length that I see to be equal to half of **a c**, and I will place it at **c d** <insert>and having thus [found] c d</insert>, I will find the right angle at **d**.

— To make an equilateral and equiangular pentagon upon a line:

— I want to place upon the line **m n** a pentagon of equal sides and equal angles <term>equiangle</term> <note>A polygon where all interior angles are equal.</note>; I will proceed in this fashion: I will *always* make the equilateral triangle **m c n** upon the line **m n**, and I will take, with a line starting from the angle **m**, the middle of the side **c n**, at point **b**; I will join its extremity **b'** to the line **c r**; where it touches it, at point **a**, you will stop the point of the compass, and with the other [point] you will make a circle that touches the extremities of the line **m n**; and the said circle will contain five of these lines [five times the length of line **m n**].

---

1. [By an arc of a circle].

<note>The following list represents the technical vocabulary used in the geometric proofs above.</note>

- FOLIO 18 (recto). -

### Manuscript Facsimile

<margin>18</margin> — Every right angle <note>original: "angolo retto"</note> joined to the center of a circle <note>original: "circhulo"</note> necessarily has its two lines directed toward the fourth part of that circle; and the angle of an equilateral triangle <note>original: "triangolo equilatero"</note> joined to its center directs its two lines to the sixth part of the circle.

<note>Geometric diagram showing a circle with an inscribed square, an equilateral triangle, and smaller intersecting circles used to demonstrate angular relationships.</note>

— **a b f** is the sixth part of the larger circle; **a n b** is the half of the square <note>original: "quadrato"</note>; **t s** is one eighth of the smaller circle.

<note>Diagram of a large circle functioning as a protractor or instrument for measuring angles, with radial lines and lettered/numbered points along the circumference.</note>

<margin>b p 10°</margin> — **e b d** is the first degree of an obtuse angle <note>original: "angolo ottuso"</note> — **b f** 1st, **b g** 2nd, **b h** 3rd, **b i** 4th, **b k** 5th, **b l** 6th, **b m** 7th, **b n** 8th, **b o** 9th, **b p** 10th.

<margin>b 2 10 b 1 11°.</margin> — **d b 11** is the first degree of the acute angle <note>original: "angolo achuto"</note> — **b 10** 2nd, **b 9** 3rd, **b 8** 4th, **b 7** 5th, **b 6** 6th, **b 5** 7th, **b 4** 8th, **b 3** 9th, **b 2** 10th, **b 1** 11th.

— Here a certain rule is made for assigning names to the variety of angles, because among the ancient authors one finds only two names for angles other than the right angle: namely, acute and obtuse. But they do not possess "degrees," as the musicians <note>original: "imusici"</note> have done with human voices <note>original: "vocivmane"</note>; therefore, seeing it necessary to assign different names according to the diversity of angles larger and smaller than a right angle, I have illustrated above the degrees of the acute and obtuse angles. And from **a b** will be all the lines that originate from the obtuse angles placed at point **b**, and similarly at **b** will arrive all the acute angles starting from circle **d c**; and I will make the obtuse ones in 12 degrees, and likewise the acute ones, and the right angle will stand in the middle.

<note>Leonardo is attempting to create a more precise nomenclature for geometry by dividing the space between a 0° and 180° angle into degrees, much like a musical scale is divided into notes.</note>

<term>right angle</term> <note>An angle of 90 degrees.</note>, <term>circle</term> <note>A round plane figure whose boundary consists of points equidistant from a fixed center.</note>, <term>equilateral triangle</term> <note>A triangle with all three sides and angles equal.</note>, <term>square</term> <note>A plane figure with four equal straight sides and four right angles.</note>, <term>obtuse angle</term> <note>An angle greater than 90 degrees but less than 180 degrees.</note>, <term>acute angle</term> <note>An angle less than 90 degrees.</note>, <term>musicians</term> <note>Those who study or perform music; used here as a metaphor for systematic classification.</note>, <term>human voices</term> <note>Used by Leonardo to compare the "pitch" or "tone" of an angle to musical intervals.</note>

<summary>Leonardo proposes a systematic method for naming and measuring acute and obtuse angles by dividing them into degrees, explicitly comparing this geometric classification to the way musicians categorize intervals in human voices.</summary>

# DIVISION OF THE CIRCLE

— If a right angle <note>An angle of 90 degrees</note> is joined to the center of a circle, its sides necessarily encompass a quarter of that circle, and the angle of an equilateral triangle joined to the same center encompasses a sixth of the circle.

— **a b f** is the sixth part of the largest circle; **a n b** is half of the square **t s**; the eighth of the smallest circle.

— **e b d** is the first degree of an obtuse angle; **b f** 1st, **b g** 2nd, **b h** 3rd, **b i** 4th, **b k** 5th, **b l** 6th, **b m** 7th, **b n** 8th, **b o** 9th, **b p** 10th <margin>1. There is a distraction <note>A mistake or lapse in concentration</note> here; it is clear that Leonardo intended to say: **e b e** first degree of the obtuse angle, **b f** 2nd, **b g** 3rd, **b h** 4th, **b i** 5th, **b k** 6th, **b l** 7th, **b m** 8th, **b n** 9th, **b o** 10th, **b p** 11th.</margin>.

— **b d 11** is the first degree of the acute angle; **b 10** 2nd, **b 9** 3rd, **b 8** 4th, **b 7** 5th, **b 6** 6th, **b 5** 7th, **b 4** 8th, **b 3** 9th, **b 2** 10th, **b 1** 11th.

— Here a method is established for giving a name to various angles. Indeed, among the ancient authors, one finds only two names for angles other than that of the right angle, namely: the acute and the obtuse, but they do not establish degrees, as musicians have done for human voices; seeing therefore that it was necessary to find diverse names for the diversity of angles larger or smaller than the right angle, I have figured above the degrees of acute and obtuse angles. At **b** shall be located the vertices of all the obtuse angles, and likewise at **b** shall arrive all the acute angles of the arc **d c**. I shall divide the obtuse into 12 degrees, I shall do the same for the acute, and the right angle shall be found in the middle.

<summary>Leonardo proposes a new system for classifying angles by dividing the space between a right angle and a straight line into twelve degrees, drawing a parallel to musical scales. This system aims to provide more precision than the simple "acute" or "obtuse" labels used by classical mathematicians.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, division of the circle, acute angle, obtuse angle, right angle, degrees, Leonardo da Vinci, mathematical nomenclature</keywords>

# — FOLIO 18 (verso). —

<note>This page contains a manuscript facsimile of a geometric study by Leonardo da Vinci, followed by a printed transcription of the handwritten text.</note>

<note>Translation of Leonardo's mirror script in the facsimile section:</note>

It is possible to know every length of a cord <note>original: "chorda"; here referring to a line of sight or a physical measuring line</note> which is fixed at one of its ends and pulled from the other, without making any movement of the feet.

Let **a.b.** be your cord. Fix the cord at point **b** and place the square <note>original: "squadra"; a surveyor's L-shaped tool for ensuring right angles</note> upon the part of the cord **b. m.** and **m.** is its turn <note>the corner of the square</note> at **b.c.** and make the right angle **b**. Then move the end **b.** of the cord as far as **n.** for the space of one cubit <note>original: "braccio"; a Renaissance unit of measure roughly the length of an arm, approx. 58cm</note> and place the square upon the part of the cord **c.n.** and mark it, and draw the mark so far down that it arrives at **d.**, so that the line **b.d.** and likewise **e.d.** touch each other. Then with the square you shall make the line **e.c.** one cubit long; and as many times as **c.d.** enters into **c.e.**, so many times the cubit **b.d.** enters into the cord **a.b.**

---

<note>Printed transcription from the bottom of the page:</note>

— It is possible to know every length of a cord which is fixed at one of its ends and pulled from the other, without making any movement of the feet.

— Let **a.b.** be your cord. Fix the cord at point **b** and place the square upon the part of the cord **b. m.** and **m.** is its turn at **b.c.** and make the right angle **b**. Then move the end **b.** of the cord as far as **n.** for the space of one cubit and place the square upon the part of the cord **c.n.** and mark it, and draw the mark so far down that it arrives at **d.**, so that the line **b.d.** and likewise **e.d.** touch each other. Then with the square you shall make the line **e.c.** one cubit long; and as many times as **c.d.** enters into **c.e.**, so many times the cubit **b.d.** enters into the cord **a.b.**

<term>chorda</term> (cord/line), <term>lungeza</term> (length), <term>squadra</term> (surveyor's square), <term>braccio</term> (cubit/arm-length), <term>movimento</term> (movement), <term>langolo retto</term> (the right angle)

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates a geometric method for measuring the length of a distant line (or "cord") using a surveyor's square and the principle of similar triangles. This allows a surveyor to calculate distance from a single standing position by creating a proportional model.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, surveying, triangulation, measurement, similar triangles, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical application of geometry for distance measurement, specifically focusing on the use of a surveyor's square to calculate the length of a line (cord) from a single vantage point without moving from one's position.</meta>

# GEOMETRY (MEASURING A SUSPENDED CORD).

— It is possible to know any length of a cord fixed by one of its ends and pulled by the other without movement of the feet [without moving].

— Let **a b** be your cord; fix the cord at point **b**, place the square <note>A surveyor's tool used to create right angles for triangulation.</note> on the part of the cord **b m** and its return at **b c**, make the right angle **b**, then move the end **b** of the cord as far as **n**, at the distance of one cubit <note>original: "brasse"; a traditional unit of measurement based on the reach of the arms, roughly 1.8 meters or 6 feet in this context, though it varies by region.</note>; place the square on the part of the cord **c n**; then draw a line, and bring it down until you arrive at **d**, on the line **b d** so that **e** and **d** touch; you will then make the line **e c** with the square, one cubit long. As many times as **c d** goes into **c e**, so many times the cubit **b d** goes into the cord **a b**¹.

---

1. In the second figure, the difference between the lines **b d** and **b n** (**g i** and **g l**) is taken into account.

<vocab>geometry, cord, cubit (brasse), square, measurement</vocab>

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for measuring the length of a distant cord or line of sight using a surveyor's square and the principles of similar triangles. By creating a small-scale model of the distance using a single cubit as a base, the observer can calculate a long distance without physically traveling the length of the cord.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, surveying, triangulation, distance measurement, surveyor's square, Leonardo da Vinci, similar triangles</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the geometric measurement of physical lines (cords) to the geometric measurement of sound and echoes, applying the same principles of angles and reflection to acoustics.</meta>

# — FOLIO 19 (recto). —

### Facsimile

<margin>19</margin> — It is possible to recognize with the ear the distance of a sound <note>original: "tono"; here referring to a specific sound or note</note> by first seeing its "flash" <note>likely referring to the visual cause of a sound, such as a hammer strike or a flash of light, to measure the delay</note> through the similarity of the voice of the echo.

<note>Geometric diagram of wave reflection or motion zig-zagging between two parallel horizontal lines. The top line is labeled f and g; the bottom has labels b, a, and h.</note>

— The line of the percussion <term>perchussione: the initial impact or strike that creates a sound or movement</term> and that of the bounce <note>the reflection or rebound</note> are placed between equal angles.

— Every blow struck against an object leaps back at an angle similar to that of the percussion.

— This proposition appears clearly; for if you strike a ball against a wall, it will leap back at an angle similar to that of the percussion. That is: if the ball **b** is thrown at **c**, it will return back along the line **c b**, because it is constrained to leave equal angles on the wall **f g**. And if you throw it along the line **b d**, it will return back along the line **d e**; and thus the line of the percussion and the line of the bounce will make an angle upon the wall **f g**, situated between two equal angles, as appears with **d** between **m** and **n**.

<note>Diagram consisting of several vertical bars on a horizontal baseline, labeled with the letters l, m, n, and n.</note>

— Therefore, if one stands at **b** and shouts, his voice is everywhere along the line **f g**, and entirely in every part of it. Therefore, he who stands (as I said) at **b** and shouts, it will seem to him that he hears his voice at **c**, and that it returns to his ear along the line **c b**. And if at that same time someone is at **c**, it will seem to him that he hears the voice **b** at the location **d**, coming along the line **d e**.

<note>Geometric ray diagram showing angles of incidence and reflection from a horizontal line, with points marked a, b, c, d, e.</note>

— The voice is everywhere and in every part of the wall where it strikes. And that part which is formed in such a way that it is fit to stop and send back the voice—the percussion—returns the voice in as many various particles of itself as there are various positions of the listeners.

— The voice made at **n** will strike in the angles **a b c d**, and for every voice made at **n**, **a b c d** will send back one-fourth of it.

— If someone stands at **m** and shouts, his voice will be returned to him from **r**; and he who stands at **n** will hear the response from **r** so quickly and close to the percussion that one will confound the other, and he will not be able to discern the proposal <note>the original sound</note> from the response <note>the echo</note>.

— The ear receives the species <term>species: a philosophical/scientific term for the "image" or "form" of a sensation (sound or light) traveling through a medium</term> of voices through straight, curved, and broken lines, and no twist can break its office <note>its function or duty</note>.

<summary>Leonardo applies the laws of physical reflection (like a ball bouncing off a wall) to the behavior of sound and echoes, demonstrating that sound reflects at an angle equal to its impact. He explores how the distance and position of a listener affect the clarity of an echo and the perception of where a sound originates.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, echo, reflection, geometry, physics of sound, percussion, angle of incidence, sensory perception</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of acoustics and the geometry of sound reflection, specifically applying the laws of optics (angles of incidence and reflection) to the behavior of echoes and the human voice.</meta>

# LIGHTNING — ACOUSTICS — PERCUSSION.

— It is possible to know with the ear the distance of a thunderclap, by first seeing its flash, through the similarity of the voice of the echo¹. <note>The author is describing the method of timing the interval between seeing lightning and hearing thunder to determine distance.</note>

— The line of the percussion [the line of incidence] and that of the leap [of reflection] are placed in the middle of equal angles.

— Every blow struck upon an object leaps back at an angle equal to that of the percussion.

— This proposition appears clearly; indeed, if you strike a wall with a ball, it will leap back at an angle equal to that of the percussion; thus, if the ball **b** is thrown to **c**, it will return back along the line **c b**, because it is constrained to leave equal angles on the wall **f g**; and if you throw it along the line **b d**, it will return back along the line **d e**, and thus the line of the percussion and the line of the leap will make an angle on the wall **f g** situated in the middle of two equal angles, as one sees the angle **d** between **m** and **m**.

— Therefore, if someone stands at **b** and shouts, his voice is entirely in the whole line **f g** and entirely in the part; consequently, whoever is, as I have said, at **b** and shouts *sa* <insert>the syllable "sa"</insert> will believe that he hears his voice at **c** and that it returns to his ear along the line **c b**, and *the one who* if, at the same time, someone is at **c**, it will seem to him that he hears the voice **b** at the location **d** and that it comes along the line **d e**.

— The voice is entirely in the whole and entirely in the part of the wall where it strikes. And the part which is formed in such a way that it is fit to *stop*, to send back the voice <insert>the percussion</insert>, returns the voice in as many various small portions as there are different positions of listeners.

— The voice coming from **n** will strike in the angles **a b c d**, and **a b c d** will send back four times the whole sound emitted at **n**.

— If someone who stands at **m** shouts, his voice will be returned to him by **r**, and he who stands at **n** will hear the response from **r** so quickly <insert>near to the percussion</insert> that he will confuse one with the other and will not be able to discern the first sound from the one that responds to it.

— The ear receives the species <term>species: a philosophical/scientific term for the "likeness" or "image" of a sensory input traveling through a medium</term> of voices through straight, curved, and broken lines, and no twisting can prevent its function.

---

<note>Footnote at the bottom of the page</note>

1. Passage cited by Libri, *History of Mathematical Sciences in Italy*, III, 43 as being found at folio 9 (instead of 19). For the form of the **r** in the word *chiaramente* <note>original: "clearly"</note>, see above folio 3 verso, second part of the note.

<summary>This page establishes that sound reflects off surfaces at the same angle it strikes them, just like a bouncing ball or a ray of light. It explains how echoes are formed and why multiple listeners can hear the same sound from different reflected points.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, law of reflection, echo, percussion, sound waves, geometry of sound, Libri</keywords>

# FOLIO 19 (verso)

### PROPOSITION
Every body without color is colored entirely or in part by the color placed opposite to it.
This is seen through experience; for every body that reflects is tinted by the color of the object before it.
And that body which is tinted in part is the white one; that part which is illuminated by red will appear red, and so with every other color, whether luminous or shadowy.

<note>Geometric diagram illustrating optical reflection or acoustic propagation, with a point source C at the apex, a horizontal surface (the mirror) with points D, E, F, and observer points M, T indicating the path of rays.</note>

### PROPOSITION
Every opaque body without color participates in that color which it has as its object; this happens to a white wall.

### PROPOSITION
The voice, having struck an object, will return to the ear by an oblique line of such obliquity as the line of incidence—that is, the line that carries the voice from its cause to the place where that voice is fit to be reformed. And this voice acts in the likeness of a thing seen in a mirror, which is entirely in the whole mirror and entirely in the part. That is to say: let us suppose the mirror is **a b** and the thing mirrored is at **c**. Just as **c** sees all parts of the mirror, so all parts of the mirror see **c**. Therefore, **c** is entirely in the whole mirror because it is in all its parts; and it is entirely in the part because it is seen in as many various parts as there are various positions of the viewers. That is, if the object **c** is at **n**, it appears as far inside as it is outside. Therefore, **c** will be seen at **d**. And he who is at **f**, seeing the object **d**, it appears to him that he sees it by a straight line; therefore, the object **d** is upon the part of the mirror **e**. And whoever is at **m** will see the object **d** at **t**.
Therefore the li— <note>The author breaks off his sentence to provide a more relatable analogy.</note> let us take the example of the sun: if you walk along the bank of a river and see the sun reflected in said river, as much as you walk along that river, so much will it seem to you that the sun walks with you. And this is because the sun is entirely in the whole and entirely in the part.

<summary>Leonardo explores the shared geometric principles of light and sound, demonstrating how color reflects onto white surfaces and how images and echoes follow the same laws of incidence. He uses the famous observation of the sun "following" a traveler along a river to explain how a reflected image exists simultaneously across an entire surface.</summary>

# REFLECTION (PAINTING — OPTICS — ACOUSTICS).

### PERSPECTIVE [Reflection].

— Every body without color is colored entirely or in part by the color placed before it.

— *Every...* This is seen by experience, since every body that acts as a mirror is tinted by the color of the object it faces. And the body that is tinted in part is the white one, which, in the part illuminated by red, appears red, and likewise for any other luminous or dark color.

PERSPECTIVE [Reflection]. Every opaque body without color participates in the color of the object it faces; this occurs with a white wall.

PERSPECTIVE [Reflection].

— The voice <note>The author uses "voice" here to refer to sound waves in general.</note> which strikes an object will return to the ear by a line of obliquity equal to the line of incidence—that is, the line which carries the voice from its cause to the place where this voice can be reformed <note>i.e., where the echo is produced</note>; this voice behaves like a thing seen in a mirror, which is entirely in the whole mirror and entirely in the part. Thus, let us say the mirror is **a b**, and the thing seen is **c**; just as **c** sees all parts of the mirror, so all parts of the mirror see **c**; therefore, **c** is entirely in the whole mirror, because it is in all its parts; it is entirely in the part because it is seen in as many different parts as there are different positions of spectators. That is to say, if the thing **c** is at **n**, it appears as far inside as it is outside; **c** will therefore be seen at **d**; and he who is at **f** seeing the object **d**, sees it in that part by a straight line; therefore the object **d** on the part of the mirror **e**, and he who is at **m** will see the object **d** at **t**.

— *Therefore the line....*¹ — Let us take the example of the sun; if you follow the course of a river and you see the sun reflected in this river, as much as you walk along the river, the sun will appear to travel the same distance with you, and this *because you because the sun is entirely in the whole and entirely in the part.* <note>This describes the optical illusion where a reflection appears to "follow" the observer because the light source is at an infinite distance relative to the observer's movement.</note>

---

1. <margin>Here is how the letters attached to the figure in the manuscript (some of which are not very visible) are placed: **a** to the right, **b** to the left of the horizontal; **c** at the bottom and **d** at the top of the vertical; **n** at the intersection of lines **a b** and **c d**; the letters <unclear>f</unclear> and **m** at the bottom right; **e** at the intersection of lines **a b** and **f d**, **t** at the intersection of lines **a b** and **m d**.</margin>

<summary>This page explores the universal laws of reflection, demonstrating how white objects take on the colors of their surroundings and how sound (the voice) follows the same geometric rules of incidence as light in a mirror. It concludes with an observation on how the sun's reflection appears to follow a traveler along a river.</summary>

<keywords>reflection, optics, acoustics, perspective, angle of incidence, color theory, echo, mirrors, solar reflection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the physics of light and heat, moving from the general laws of reflection discussed on the previous page to specific observations on how shadows, mirrors, and temperature behave.</meta>

# - FOLIO 20 (recto). -

<note>Manuscript facsimile section (mirror script transcribed as normal text)</note>

<margin>PROPOSITION</margin>
That part of a shaded body which is illuminated will send to the eye the likeness of its particles more clearly discernible and distinct than that part which is found in the shadow.

<margin>PROPOSITION</margin>
Solar rays reflected upon a square mirror will bounce back upon a distant object in a round shape. <note>Leonardo is observing a phenomenon of optics where, at a sufficient distance, the shape of the light source (the sun) overrides the shape of the aperture or mirror.</note>

<margin>PROPOSITION</margin>
Every white and opaque body is tinted in part by the likeness of the colors that are placed before it as an object.

<margin>PROPOSITION</margin>
On the quality of the heat created by the rays of the sun in a mirror.

<margin>PROPOSITION</margin>
The heat of the sun that is found on the surface of a concave mirror—which heat will depart along pyramidal <note>conical</note> rays converging at a single point—that point, as much as it enters the surface, will be so many times hotter than the heat found upon the mirror. And thus, as much as **a b** or, if you prefer, **c d** enters the mirror, so many times its heat is more powerful than that of the mirror.

<margin>ON COLD</margin>
I say that cold is born of two causes: the first is by air deprived of heat; the second is by the movement of air. Air in itself is cold and dry; it is always empty <note>original: "vain"</note> and is deprived of all matter and vapors, and it converts itself so readily into all the things that truly infuse their nature and likeness into it—those things which touch it and which are placed before it as an object. And therefore, in those things that touch it, it occurs that when a fragrant thing like musk, or sulfur, or another powerful odor touches it, it immediately infuses through it. Furthermore, if a luminous body is placed in it, all the surrounding air will be illuminated. Now, to return to the cold: I say that just as the many rays of the concave mirror joined at one point create a supreme heat, so many bellows blowing at one and the same point create a supreme cold.

---

-> PROPOSITION

-> PROPOSITION

-> PROPOSITION

-> PROPOSITION

-> PROPOSITION

And thus, as much as **a b** or, if you prefer, **c d** enters the mirror, so many times its heat is more powerful than that of the mirror.

-> ON COLD
— I say that cold is born of two causes: the first is by air deprived of heat; the second is by the movement of air. Air in itself is cold and dry; it is always *empty* and is deprived of all matter and vapors, and it converts itself so readily into all the things that truly infuse their nature and likeness into it—those things which touch it and which are placed before it as an object; *and therefore* in those things that touch it, it occurs that when a fragrant thing like musk, or sulfur, or another powerful odor touches it, it immediately infuses through it. <term>concave mirror</term> (specchio concavo): A curved mirror that reflects light inward to a focal point.
<term>pyramidal rays</term> (raggi piramidali): Leonardo's term for a cone of light or energy.
<term>heat/cold</term> (calore/freddo): The fundamental qualities Leonardo seeks to quantify through geometry.

<summary>Leonardo explores the concentration of solar energy using concave mirrors and draws a physical parallel between the focal point of heat and the concentration of cold through moving air (bellows). He also notes how white surfaces absorb the colors of surrounding objects and how light reflection changes shape over distance.</summary>
<keywords>optics, concave mirrors, solar heat, theory of cold, reflection, atmospheric physics, focal point</keywords>

# PERSPECTIVE — LIGHT AND HEAT — COLD.

### PERSPECTIVE [Light].

— That part of a shaded body which is illuminated will send to the eye the image of its details more *clearly* distinct and more sharp than that which is found in the shadow.

### PERSPECTIVE [Reflection].

— The solar rays that have struck a square mirror will bounce back upon a distant object in a round shape. <note>Leonardo is observing that at a great distance, the image of the sun—which is round—overcomes the square shape of the mirror itself.</note>

### PERSPECTIVE [Light].

— Every white and opaque body is tinted in part by the likeness of the colors that it has as an object before it. <note>This refers to "color reflection," where a white surface takes on the hue of nearby colored objects.</note>

### PERSPECTIVE [Light and heat].

— On the quality of the heat produced by the rays of the sun in the mirror.

### PERSPECTIVE [Light and heat].

— The heat of the sun that is found on the surface of a concave mirror will be distributed among the pyramidal <note>conical or tapering</note> rays converging at a single point; as many times as this point fits into the surface area, so many times it will be hotter than the heat found on the mirror; also, as much as **a b**, or if you wish, **c d** fits into the mirror [into the surface of the mirror], so many times its heat will be more powerful than that of the mirror. <note>original: "autant a b... entre dans le miroir." Leonardo is using a geometric ratio to explain the concentration of energy at a focal point.</note>¹

### ON COLD.

— I say that cold is born of two causes; the first is by air deprived of heat; the second is by the movement of air. Air is by itself cold and dry, *and if and always goes in and is* deprived of all matter and vapors, and it changes itself willingly *into all the things that to it* and it readily assimilates the nature and likeness of the things that touch it and that it has as an object; *and then into those things that touch it*; it is thus that when a fragrant thing, such as musk or sulfur or another strong odor, touches it, the air is immediately impregnated with it; and further, if one places a luminous body in the air, all the surrounding air will be illuminated. Now, to return to cold, I say that just as the numerous rays of a concave mirror gathered at a single point create an extreme heat, likewise many bellows, blowing at a single point, create an extreme cold.²

2. See folio 2 recto.

METHOD OF MEASURING FROM THE SURFACE OF THE EARTH TO ITS CENTER

<note>Diagram showing a circle with a radius and an arc, and a balance scale with plumb lines and weights. This illustrates the theory that all heavy things fall toward a single central point.</note>

<margin>
Just as much as **a b** enters into **a n**, so much **c b** enters into **c f**. If you find that at a height of 100 <term>braccia</term> <note>A Florentine "braccio" is approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters</note> and a width of 25, **m n** is narrower than **c b** by one thread, then as many threads as fit into **c b**, so many hundreds of *braccia* will it be from **c b** to the center **f**.
</margin>

<margin>
If you make a tower of 400 *braccia* with plumb lines, it will be narrower at the foot than at the top, and it will be the beginning of a pyramid. <note>Leonardo is observing that because all plumb lines point to the center of the Earth, they are not perfectly parallel but converge slightly.</note>
</margin>

If you wish to measure how much space there is from the surface of the earth to its center, you shall do it in this form: climb atop a bell tower that is 100 *braccia* high, which is **d e**; then extend from it 2 rods, which are **c d** and likewise **d b**, and to each rod let there be attached 2 thin threads that go down to the ground with 2 lead weights, which threads are **c r** and likewise **b s**. Then look and measure how much narrower the space **r s** is than **c b**; and if it is narrower by the amount of **a b**, then however many times **a b** enters into **a s**, so many times **c b** enters from itself to the center of the world. And as many times as **a b** enters into **b c**, so many times the height of the bell tower enters from its summit to the center of the world.

<note>Diagram of a tower or campanile with arms and plumb lines, and a horizontal sighting tube aimed at a circle representing the center of the earth.</note>

METHOD OF KNOWING HOW LARGE THE SUN IS
Let the distance from **a b** be 100 *braccia*, and make the hole through which the solar rays pass be 1/500 of a *braccio*, and note how much the ray has grown at the point of impact. <note>Leonardo uses the principle of a camera obscura to calculate the Sun's diameter through proportional triangles.</note>

# — FOLIO 20 (verso). —

<note>The top section of the page contains a facsimile of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript with mirror writing and geometric/scientific diagrams. The bottom section is a printed transcription of the handwritten notes.</note>

### METHOD OF MEASURING FROM THE SURFACE OF THE EARTH TO ITS CENTER.

— Just as much as **a b** enters into **a n**, so much **c b** enters into **c f**. And if you find that at a height of 100 <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "brasses"; a Florentine unit of measurement approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters</note> and a width of 25, **m n** is narrower than **c b** by one thread, then as many threads as fit into **c b**, so many hundreds of *braccia* will it be from **c b** to the center **f**.

— If you make a tower of 400 *braccia* and you plumb it with lines, it will be narrower at the foot than at the top <gloss>the interval between the threads will be narrower at the bottom than at the top</gloss> and it will form the beginning of a pyramid. <note>Leonardo correctly deduces that because gravity pulls toward a single center point, vertical lines are not parallel but convergent.</note>

— If you wished to measure what distance there is from the surface of the earth to its center, you shall proceed in this manner: Climb onto a bell tower 100 *braccia* high, which is **d e**, and then suspend outside two rods which are **c d** and likewise **d b**; to each rod let there be attached a thin thread that goes to the ground with a lead weight, and let these threads be **c r** and likewise **b s**; look and measure then by how much the space **r s** is narrower than **c b**; and if it is narrower by as much as there is from **a** to **b**, as many times as **a b** enters into **a s**, so many times **c b** is found [to go] to the center of the world. And as many times as **a b** enters into **b c**, so many times the height of the bell tower, starting from its summit, is found [to go] to the center of the earth.

->— MAGNET — WEIGHT <insert>1</insert>.<-

### METHOD OF KNOWING THE MAGNITUDE OF THE SUN.

— Make it so that from **a** to **b** there are 100 *braccia*; make the hole through which the rays pass 1/16 of a *braccio*, then note by how much the ray has increased in its percussion <note>The "percussion" refers to the spot of light where the ray strikes a surface.</note>.

<insert>1. Words that accompany, in the manuscript, the figure placed on the left at the bottom of the page.</insert>

# — FOLIO 21 (recto). —

### Manuscript Facsimile

<margin>
[Top Left]
If you wish to know
the height of any
of the sun...

<margin>21</margin>
<note>Page number written in normal orientation at the top right of the facsimile plate.</note>

[Main text block below diagrams]
— To measure a planet, it is necessary that when the star is encountered at point **m**, one makes a flash of powder <note>original: "baleno di poluere"; likely a signal flare using gunpowder</note> and immediately the person at point **n** does the same. I will do likewise.
— These are how the instruments below should be placed to measure the sun. But in instrument **n**, it must be large, and for instrument **m**, a small one is sufficient.
— The instrument that measures large things: the larger it is, the closer it comes to the truth <note>Leonardo notes that larger scale instruments reduce the margin of error in angular measurements.</note>.
— 50 miles —
— If you want to know the true height of any part of the sun, arrange to find 2 mountains that can see one another, and let them be as far from each other as possible, and let them be almost similar in height. And place yourself on one of the mountains, and on the other, you shall place another friend of yours who is diligent. And in the place where you stay, make a small hut of boards which has a small hole <note>original: "boso"; likely a variant of "buso" or "buco"</note> through which the ray of the sun can <insert>pass</insert> enter, grazing the board **a b**, which board should be fixed by a perpendicular line <note>i.e., aligned with a plumb line</note>. And likewise, let your companion have a similar small hut with an aperture <term>spiracolo</term> <note>a small vent or opening for light</note> where the sun enters into said small hut. And when the sun enters into your small hut and grazes the line **a b**, you shall immediately make a great smoke with gunpowder <note>original: "poluere dabonbarde"</note> and straw dampened with vinegar. And immediately your companion will be able to note where his aperture strikes the floor of his small hut, which we shall say strikes at **c**. Measure then how many times **c e** goes into **c d**, and as many times as **c e** goes into **e d**, so many times the space between the small huts **c b** goes from point **b** exactly to the sun.

# MEASUREMENT OF THE STARS.

<margin>21</margin>
— To measure a planet, it is necessary that when the star is encountered at point **m**, you make a flash of powder <note>original: "éclair de poussière"; likely a signal flare using gunpowder to synchronize timing</note> and immediately the person who is at point **n** shall do the same.

— These instruments are placed as they ought to be below, to measure the sun; instrument **n** must be large, but a small instrument **m** is sufficient.

— The larger the instrument that measures great things, the closer it approaches the truth <note>Leonardo emphasizes that increasing the scale of the apparatus reduces the margin of error in astronomical observations.</note>.

— 50 miles¹ —

— If you want to know the true height of any part of the sun, arrange to find two mountains that see one another, which are as far apart from each other as you can manage, and which are nearly equal in height; place yourself on one of the mountains and on the other have one of your friends placed, who is attentive; at the place where you stand, make a small wooden hut, which has a small hole through which the ray of the sun can pass <insert>enter</insert> and graze the wood **a b**, which wood must be placed in a perpendicular <term>vertical|In Leonardo's geometry, perpendicular often refers to a line perfectly upright relative to the horizon</term> line; let your companion have a similar hut, with an opening where the sun enters that hut. When the sun enters your hut and grazes the line **a b**, you shall immediately make a great smoke with <term>bombard powder|Early gunpowder used for cannons</term> and straw moistened with vinegar <note>The vinegar was likely added to create a thicker, more visible smoke for signaling over the 50-mile distance</note>; immediately your companion will be able to observe where his hole strikes <insert>where the ray passing through the hole strikes</insert> upon the floor of his hut. Let us suppose it strikes at **c**; then measure how many times **c e** goes into **c d**, and exactly as many times as **c e** goes into **c d**, precisely that many times will the space between the huts **c b** be found from the place **b** to the sun.

---

1. These numbers are written on the lower side of the triangle in the figure at the bottom of the page; they indicate the assumed distance between the two huts.

<summary>Leonardo describes a trigonometric method for measuring the distance to the Sun or planets using two observers on distant mountains, synchronized by gunpowder signals. The method relies on the principles of similar triangles and the use of large-scale observations to ensure accuracy.</summary>

<meta>This page shifts from the previous page's astronomical measurements to a theoretical study of mechanics, specifically the physics of gravity, motion, and the behavior of weighted wheels on an incline.</meta>

# MANUSCRIPT FACSIMILE

<note>Transcription of Leonardo's mirror-written Italian text, mentally reversed for reading. Original spelling and abbreviations are preserved.</note>

Every heavy body desires to fall toward the center <note>original: "alcientro"; referring to the center of the Earth, the Renaissance understanding of gravity</note>. And that opposition which is more oblique offers it less resistance. If the weight were at **a**, its true upright resistance would be **a b**. In whatever part the wheel touches the ground, that becomes its pole <note>The "pole" here refers to the fulcrum or point of rotation</note>; and that part which remains larger outside of said pole is the one that falls. Since **s x** is the pole, it clearly appears that **s t** weighs more than **s r**; wherefore it is necessary that the part **s t** falls downward and overcomes and lifts **s r**, and moves down the slope with fury. And if this pole were at **n**, as much as **n c** enters into **c b**, so much more strongly would the wheel run by itself down the slope if the pole were at **n** than at **e**.

<margin>
at angle **p** in **s**
</margin>

### On Weight
This wheel gives of its own weight to the ground that supports it twice as much as the part **r s**, and no more.

### On Motion
No soulless thing <note>original: "chosa sanza vita"; an inanimate object</note> can push or pull without accompanying the thing pushed; and these pushers can be nothing other than force or weight. And if the weight pushes or pulls, it performs only this motion in the thing because it desires stillness <note>original: "fermanza"; the state of rest or equilibrium</note>. And when no thing moved by its falling movement shall ever be sufficient to return it upward to its first height, then the motion has ceased.
And if the thing that moves the other thing be force, even that force accompanies the thing moved by it, and moves it so much that it consumes itself; being consumed, no thing moved by it will be able to cause it again. Therefore, no moved thing can have long operation, because where the causes are lacking, the effects are lacking.

### On Motion
Whatever thing finds itself on a solid and perfect plane, whose pole is not found between parts equal in weight, will never stop. The example is seen in those who slide upon ice, who never stop as long as the parts do not return equidistant to their center.
On motion **b e** · **s a** ·

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of a weighted wheel on an incline, arguing that inanimate motion is finite because force and weight "consume" themselves until reaching equilibrium.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, mechanics, equilibrium, perpetual motion, center of gravity, kinetics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the mechanical investigations begun on the previous leaf, shifting from general observations to specific geometric proofs regarding the center of gravity in wheels and the finite nature of motion produced by weight and force.</meta>

### ON MOTION AND WEIGHT.

— *Every heavy body desires to fall toward the center <note>The center of the Earth, reflecting the Aristotelian and medieval understanding of gravity prevalent in the Renaissance.</note> and the opposition which is the most oblique offers it the least resistance.*

— If the weight is at ***a***, its true and direct resistance would be ***a b***. But in whatever part the wheel touches the ground, there its pole <note>The point of support or fulcrum upon which the object rotates.</note> is found, and the part which remains the largest outside of this pole falls; ***s x*** being the pole, it is clear that ***s t*** will weigh more than ***s r***; wherefore it follows that the part ***s t*** falls, that it overcomes and lifts ***s r***, and then moves down the slope with fury <note>original: "furie"; often used by Leonardo to describe the momentum or violent force of an object in motion.</note>. And if this pole were at ***n***, just as much as ***n c*** enters into ***c b***¹, so much faster would the wheel run on the slope than if it were at ***x***.

### ON WEIGHT.

— This wheel gives of its weight to the ground that supports it twice as much as the part ***r s***, and no more.

### ON MOTION.

— No inanimate thing <note>original: "chose sans vie"; literally "thing without life."</note> can push or pull without accompanying the thing pushed <insert>moved</insert>; these motors can only be force or weight; if the weight pushes or pulls, it only creates this motion in the object because it desires rest, and since no thing moved by its falling movement is capable of returning upward to its original height, the movement comes to an end.

— And if the thing that moves another thing is force, this force also accompanies the thing moved by it, and it moves it in such a way that it consumes itself; being consumed, none of the things that have been moved by it are capable of reproducing it. Therefore, no moved thing can have a long-lasting operation <insert>action</insert>, because when the causes are lacking, the effects are lacking.

### ON MOTION.

— Every thing that is on a flat and perfect ground, such that its pole <note>point of support</note> is not located between equal parts of weight, will never stop; an example of this is seen in those who slide on ice and who never stop, if the parts do not become equidistant to their center.

---

1. In the figure of the manuscript, at the top of the page, the letter *c*, which is barely visible, is located above the letter *x* and below the letters *s n* is near the circumference, between *c* and *r*.

<summary>Leonardo examines the mechanics of a wheel on an incline, arguing that motion is driven by the imbalance of weight around a fulcrum and asserting that all mechanical motion eventually ceases as its "force" or "desire for rest" is consumed.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, gravity, fulcrum, center of gravity, kinetic energy, conservation of motion, friction, equilibrium</keywords>

— FOLIO 22 (recto). —

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of a Leonardo da Vinci manuscript folio (mirror script) in the upper half, with a corresponding printed transcription in the lower half.</note>

### [Facsimile Section]

<margin>
48
</margin>

<margin>
22
</margin>

<note>Translation of Leonardo's mirror script in the upper block:</note>

A PERFECT SPHERICAL BODY PLACED ON A PERFECT PLANE WILL NOT HAVE ANY MOVEMENT UNLESS YOU GIVE IT SOME, AND THE REASON IS THAT ALL ITS PARTS ARE AT AN EQUAL DISTANCE TO THE CENTER; WHEREFORE IT ALWAYS REMAINS IN BALANCE. AND THE BALANCE WHICH HAS IN ITS ARMS EQUAL WEIGHT AND LENGTH STANDS WITHOUT MOTION. SINCE THE SAID SPHERICAL BODY IS EQUAL IN ONE HALF TO THE OTHER, IT LIKEWISE SHALL BE WITHOUT MOTION.

<note>Leonardo's text continues around the diagrams, discussed in the printed section below.</note>

---

### [Printed Commentary Section]

#### IMPACT AND WEIGHT
— The perfect spherical body placed on the perfect plane will not have any movement unless you give it some. And the reason is that all its parts are at an equal distance to the center; wherefore it always remains in balance. And the balance which has in its arms equal weight and length stands without motion. Since the said spherical body is equal in one half to the other, it likewise shall be without motion.
— The line *a-b* will receive at *c* the first degree of weight of the ball placed above it, if the line *e-a* will receive at *d* the first degree of the impact of that ball.
— The ball received in the <term>supine angle</term><note>An upward-facing or obtuse angle in this context</note>, its center will direct itself so much more onto one face than the other, as much as one face is more oblique than the other.
— Water received in the supine angle occupies so much more of one face than the other, as much as one face is more oblique than the other.

#### METHOD TO MAKE ONE PERFECT CUBE FROM TWO CUBES
— You shall do thus: make from the one so many small squares that the other square fits exactly, as appears in *a-b*.

#### ON IMPACT
— The impact will be so much less powerful than its <term>impetus</term><note>original: "enpito" (impulse or momentum)</note> as the angle of its percussion is smaller than a right angle.

#### RULE OF IMPACT AND WEIGHT
— If the small ball *c* runs along the line *c-b*, it will strike the line *a-g*. It will make, with the line of its course that passes through its center and the line of the place struck, an <term>acute triangle</term><note>original: "triangolo achoto"</note> *c-a-g*. And as many times as this acute triangle enters into a right angle, so much will the impact be weaker than is normal for its flight. Because the first degree of impact is between equal angles, which it makes in striking the line *a-e*; and the last degree is in the line *a-c*; and the middle is in the line *a-f*.
— Lead or wool <note>Likely a note comparing the impact of heavy vs. soft materials</note>

#### ON IMPACT
— If the hand, staying in the air and receiving a weight of 6 pounds of lead, does not hurt itself; if it touches the ground, what damage will the falling weight do to it?

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the equilibrium of spheres on flat surfaces and establishes a geometric rule for the force of impact based on the angle of percussion. He concludes that a perpendicular strike (right angle) is the most powerful, while oblique angles diminish the force.</summary>

<keywords>spherical bodies, equilibrium, center of gravity, impact, percussion, geometry of motion, impetus, mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Folio 22, completing the discussion on the physics of impact and weight. It follows the previous page's introduction to spherical equilibrium and begins to apply these principles to fluid dynamics and geometry.</meta>

# MOTION — IMPACT — WEIGHT.

### IMPACT AND WEIGHT.

— The perfect spherical body placed on a perfect plane will have no motion if you do not give it any. And the reason is that all its parts are at an equal distance from the center; consequently, it always remains in balance [in equilibrium], and the balance which has arms of equal weight and length remains without motion; if the said spherical body has its two halves equal to one another, it also remains without motion.

— The line *a b* will receive at *c* the first degree of the weight of the superimposed ball, if the line *e a* receives at *d* the first degree of the impact of this ball.

— When the ball is received in the angle open toward the top, its center will direct itself toward one side more than the other, insofar as one of the sides is more oblique than the other.

— Water received in the angle open toward the top will occupy one side more than the other, insofar as one of these sides is more oblique than the other.

### WAY TO MAKE ONE PERFECT CUBIC SQUARE FROM TWO CUBIC SQUARES.
<note>Leonardo uses "carré cube" to refer to a three-dimensional cube.</note>

You shall do it thus: make from one of them as many small squares as are necessary to exactly coat the other square, as it appears in *a b*.

### ON IMPACT.

— *The impact will be less powerful than its impulse insofar as the angle of its percussion is less than a right angle.*

### RULE OF IMPACT AND WEIGHT.

— If the ball *c* runs along the line *c b* and strikes the line *a g*, it will form—with the line of its course, which passes through its center, and the line of the place struck—*the triangle c a g*, and as many times as this acute triangle fits into a right angle, so much the impact becomes weaker than is appropriate to its flight; in effect, the first degree of the impact is found between equal angles, which it makes at the percussion of the line *a e*, and the last degree is found in the line *a c*, and the middle is in the line *a f*.

— Horsehair or wool ¹.

### ON IMPACT.

— If the hand placed in the air receiving a weight of 6 pounds of lead receives no harm from it, what damage will the weight do to it in falling, if the hand is touching the ground?

---

1. These words, in the manuscript, accompany the figure placed between two other figures at the bottom of the page.

— FOLIO 22 (verso). —

### [MANUSCRIPT FACSIMILE]

<note>Leonardo's mirror writing transcribed into English. The text discusses perpetual motion, the mechanics of the balance, acoustics, and the motion of falling bodies.</note>

->AGAINST PERPETUAL MOTION<-
— No inanimate thing will move by itself; therefore, if it moves, it is moved by an unequal power—that is, of unequal time and motion, or of unequal weight. And once the desire <note>Leonardo often uses the word "desiderio" (desire) to describe the "impetus" or "force" driving a physical process.</note> of the primary motor has ceased, the secondary will immediately cease.

->ON MOTION<-
— For example <note>original: "verbigratia"</note>, if you want weight *b* to lift weight *a*, the arms of the balance being equal, it is necessary that *b* be heavier than *a*. If you wanted weight *d* to lift weight *c*, which is heavier than it, it is necessary that it [d] makes a longer course in its descent than *c* does in its ascent. And if it descends more, it is necessary that the arm of the balance that descends with it be longer than the other. And if you wanted the small weight *f* to lift the large weight *e*, it is necessary that *f* makes a longer and faster movement than weight *e*.

->ON IMPACT<-
— The strike upon a bell leaves behind it its likeness impressed <note>original: "similitudine inpresa"</note>, just as the sun [leaves an image] in the eye or a scent in the air. But it is to be seen whether the likeness of this strike remains in the bell or in the air; and you will know this by placing your ear against the surface of the bell after the strike.

->ON IMPACT<-
A strike given to a bell will cause another bell similar to it to respond and move slightly; and a string played on a lute will cause another similar string of similar voice <note>meaning: pitch or frequency</note> on another lute to respond and move. And you will see this by placing a straw upon the string similar to the one played.

->ON MOTION<-
— If two bodies of the same material, which are spherical, fall at the same time and from the same height, one will fall faster than the other in proportion to how many times the diameter of one fits into the other <note>Leonardo is observing that larger objects often fall faster than smaller ones of the same material due to the ratio of their weight to air resistance.</note>.

->ON MOTION<-
— If two unequal spherical and heavy bodies of the same material fall from high to low, one will fall faster than the other in proportion to how many times the diameter of one fits into the other.

->ON MOTION<-
— If one pound of weight falls upon one pound of resistance, it will not move, but rather remain in equilibrium. And if another pound is attached above it, it will descend to the ground in a certain amount of time; and if you add another pound above it, the whole weight will descend with doubled speed.

# FOLIO 23 (verso).

<note>Manuscript facsimile featuring Leonardo da Vinci's mirror writing and diagrams related to hydraulic studies and the flow of rivers like the Po.</note>

— The course of the smaller volume of water obeys the greater volume during great floods; it changes its course, joins with the larger flow, and ceases its digging beneath the embankments. —

<note>Diagram illustrating the confluence of two water channels, showing a smaller stream merging into a larger, main current.</note>

— This experience is seen in the Po <note>The Po is the longest river in Italy, which Leonardo studied extensively for engineering and mapping projects</note>, which, when it is low, its water often runs in transverse courses drawn by low-lying places; directing itself toward these, it takes its course and strikes the embankment at its foundations, hollowing them out and causing great ruins. But when it runs full, the smaller volume—which previously, by crossing over, struck and dug beneath the embankments—leaves its — course, because it is drawn by the company of the greater volume; and going along the direction of its bed, it does not damage the embankments.

<note>Small circular diagram, possibly representing an eddy or a cross-section of a whirlpool caused by an underwater obstruction.</note>

— Of rivers that have the same origin, flowing through an equal plain and with equal width of embankments, and meeting at the same end: that which has the more crooked path will have a slower movement. And by as much as the length of the straight river enters into the twists of the other, by so much will its course be faster than the other. —

— The water that runs on the surface, in the covering it makes of the waves <note>original: "alle" interpreted here as "onde" or waves/disturbances</note> placed beneath it—even though those waves are transverse to its course—nevertheless, that surface does not twist from its first straight path. —

— This follows because its first path has more power on that surface, bordering as it does with the air, than the resistance of those waves placed and enclosed beneath it; the proof is seen by light objects that run upon it. —

---

<note>The following section is a translation of the printed transcription provided in the source, which mirrors the manuscript text above.</note>

— The course of the smaller volume of water obeys the greater volume during great floods; it changes its course, joins with the larger flow, and ceases its digging beneath the embankments. —
— This experience is seen in the Po, which, when it is low, its water often runs in transverse courses drawn by low-lying places; directing itself toward these, it takes its course and strikes the embankment at its foundations, hollowing them out and causing great ruins. But when it runs full, the smaller volume—which previously, by crossing over, struck and dug beneath the embankments—leaves its — course, because it is drawn by the company of the greater volume; and going along the direction of its bed, it does not damage the embankments.
— Of rivers that have the same origin, flowing through an equal plain and with equal width of embankments, and meeting at the same end: that which has the more crooked path will have a slower movement. And by as much as the length of the straight river enters into the twists of the other, by so much will its course be faster than the other. —
— The water that runs on the surface, in the covering it makes of the waves placed beneath it—even though those waves are transverse to its course—nevertheless, that surface does not twist from its first straight path. —
— This follows because its first path has more power on that surface, bordering as it does with the air, than the resistance of those waves placed and enclosed beneath it; the proof is seen by light objects that run upon it.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the volume and speed of a river like the Po dictate its path and erosive power, noting that higher water levels actually protect banks by straightening the current. He also observes that surface tension and momentum allow surface water to maintain a straight path despite turbulence underneath.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, Po River, embankments, erosion, fluid dynamics, river velocity, surface tension, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# CONSERVATION OF FORCE — WISDOM OF THE CREATOR — WATER.

— Any spherical and heavy body, with a dense and resistant surface, if moved by a specific power <note>original: "puissance"; here meaning force or energy</note>, will travel the same distance when it is made to strike and bounce off a hard, enameled floor <note>a floor covered with a very resistant coating</note> as it would if it were thrown freely through the air.

— How admirable is your justice, O you, First Mover! <note>A philosophical term for God as the initial cause of all motion in the universe</note> You did not wish for any created thing to lack the power, the order, and the qualities of its necessary effects. For if a force must drive an object overcome by it for 100 fathoms <note>original: "brasses"; an old unit of length based on the reach of a man's arms</note>, and that object, in obeying the force, encounters a shock [an impact], you have ordained that the power of the blow would cause a new movement, which, through various bounces, would recover the entire sum of the journey it was meant to make. And if you measure the path traveled by said bounces, you will find it to be of a length equal to that which would be required to move the same object, freely through the air, with the same force. — **EXPERIMENT.** — You shall perform this experiment with a small glass ball struck against a floor of hard, flat stone. Have a long spear marked with various colors; when you have marked it from one end to the other, have someone hold the staff and stand at a short distance to observe at which colors the ball rises successively at each bounce along the height of the staff, and note them down. If there are as many observers as there are bounces, each will more easily remember their own by heart. But ensure the staff is fixed either by the top or in a hole at the base, because whoever holds it with their hand might block the view of the judge. Ensure the first bounce occurs between two right angles <note>meaning a vertical drop</note>, so that the ball always falls in the same place, as the heights of the bounces along the staff will be better noted. Then, have that same power launch the ball with a free thrust and note the place where it hits; measure, and you will find that the second journey is a companion to [matches] the first¹.

— Water that falls along a line closest to the perpendicular [the vertical] is that which descends fastest and delivers the greatest blow and weight to the spot it strikes².

— For any watercourse that is near its fall, the curve of the inclination will begin sooner at the surface than at the bottom³.

---

1. See further on, folio 60 verso, 3rd paragraph.
2. See: *Treatise on the Movement and Measurement of Water* <note>original: "Tratt. del. m. e. mis. dell' acqua"</note>, p. 366, book 5, chapter XXVIII, plate 19, fig. 110, the same proposition, expressed slightly differently.
3. See: *Treatise on the Movement and Measurement of Water*, p. 358, book 5, chapter V, plate 17, fig. 95, the same passage, with differences in wording.

<summary>The author argues that the total distance an object travels remains constant whether it moves through the air or bounces off a hard surface, attributing this "conservation of force" to divine justice. He also provides observations on the velocity and curvature of falling water.</summary>
<keywords>conservation of energy, laws of motion, elasticity, First Mover, fluid dynamics, experimental method, hydraulics</keywords>

# FOLIO 24 (verso).

<note>This page from a scholarly edition presents a facsimile plate of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript featuring studies of water flow, accompanied by a printed transcription below.</note>

### Manuscript Facsimile (Folio 24v)

<note>Drawing of wave patterns showing intersecting lines</note>
Regarding waves that will clash together through contrary motions: the one that is in a lower position will follow its course beneath the higher one.

<note>Drawing of water current flowing over a bumpy riverbed</note>
Water that descends with a declining movement over a bumpy <term>globulous fondo: a riverbed characterized by rounded stones or uneven mounds</term> bottom will have a faster course on the surface than at the bottom below.

<note>Drawing of water impact and splashing against a surface or body</note>
That body of a light quality which finds itself between the percussion <note>the point of impact</note> and the rebounding of the waters will never change its position, provided the river remains in the same state of motion.

<note>Drawing of water flow showing surface vs. bottom velocity</note>
Water with a bumpy bottom and a flat surface will move with greater velocity at the bottom than at the top.

# Folio 25 (recto)

<note>This page consists of a manuscript facsimile by Leonardo da Vinci, featuring hydraulic studies and notes in mirror script, accompanied by a printed transcription below.</note>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>Leonardo's original handwriting is in right-to-left mirror script. The transcription below is presented in normal left-to-right reading order.</note>

The water that, with a declining <gloss>descending</gloss> course, strikes in a transverse line upon the flat surface of other water, will follow its initiated course in a straight line down to the bottom, making itself a cover for the struck water; it will have a greater flow <gloss>velocity</gloss> below than above.

---

Where the river is narrower than its entrance, the water there will have a greater flow; the water found in front of the narrow places of rivers raises its surface and runs with fury through the narrowness, at the end of which course it strikes into that of slower motion, which, finding some resistance, changes into circular movements <note>vortices or eddies</note> and battles and erodes the transverse banks —

---

That water will jump higher above its natural level which is closer to the submersion of that which falls upon the other water — water, in many and various impacts made by it through the ruinous courses of the shores, sends forth from itself a great part in a fine mist —
