# FOLIO 25 (verso).

<note>Facsimile of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscript with mirror script and hydrostatic diagrams.</note>

<note>Drawing of two shallow cylindrical vessels or basins with horizontal hoops, likely illustrating outward pressure on container walls.</note>

Every liquid body which is capable of movement will strike and push more against that part of the wall <note>original: "pariete"</note> that encloses it which is most distant from the center of its surface than against any other part of its receptacle.

<note>Drawing of a vertical cylindrical tube containing a submerged bag or second vessel surrounded by liquid.</note>

— There is a well <note>original: "pozo"</note> which has at its bottom a skin bag <note>original: "notro" or "otre," a leather bladder used for liquids</note> of such a size and situated in such a way that beneath it and at its sides there is no more than a finger's breadth of water. The water is arranged so that the water resting on the bottom weighs 100 pounds, and that which rests on top of the bag <note>original: "baga"</note> weighs 10,000 pounds. If this is so, will the bag burst, having such a great weight upon it? And if that weight does not press upon what supports it—and even if it were supported—why would the bag have to pass over the water? And if the water indeed loads its weight upon its bottom, why does a man not suffer the sensation <note>original: "passione," meaning the physical effect or suffering of a force</note> of weight while standing upon its bottom? Therefore, if the bag supports the water, the bag takes the weight of said water away from the bottom of the well. —

---

->**Printed Transcription**<-

— Every liquid body which is capable of movement will strike and push more against that part of the wall that encloses it which is most distant from the center of its surface than against any other part of its receptacle.

— There is a well which has at its bottom a skin bag of such a size and situated in such a way that beneath it and at its sides there is no more than a finger's breadth of water. The water is arranged so that the water resting on the bottom weighs 100 pounds, and that which rests on top of the bag weighs 10,000 pounds. If this is so, will the bag burst, having such a great weight upon it? And if that weight does not press upon what supports it—and even if it were supported—why would the bag have to pass over the water? And if the water indeed loads its weight upon its bottom, why does a man <insert>not at all</insert> <note>The transcription suggests "vnomo" might be read as "menomo," meaning "not in the least."</note> suffer the sensation of weight while standing upon its bottom? Therefore, if the bag supports the water, the bag takes the weight of said water away from the bottom of the well. —

<summary>Leonardo explores the distribution of hydrostatic pressure, questioning how the weight of a large volume of water affects a submerged object and the floor of its container. He uses the example of a leather bag in a well to investigate why the full weight of a liquid is not always felt by an object at the bottom.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, fluid pressure, buoyancy, skin bag, weight distribution, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of liquids</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of hydrostatic pressure, specifically examining how the weight of a liquid is distributed against the walls of a container and the effects of extreme pressure on submerged objects like leather bladders.</meta>

# WEIGHT AND PRESSURE OF LIQUIDS.

— Every liquid body, capable of movement, will fight against and push most upon that part of the wall <note>original: "paroi"</note> enclosing it which is more distant from the center of its surface than any other part of its receptacle <margin>See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, p. 414, book 8, chapter I, and plate 29, figure 164, for the same passage with some differences in wording.</margin>.

— There is a well which has at its bottom a skin bag <note>original: "outre," a leather bladder or wineskin</note>, of such a size and situated in such a way, that beneath it and on its sides, there is no more than a finger’s thickness of water; the water is such that the water resting on the bottom weighs 100 pounds and that which rests above the bag weighs 10,000 pounds; if this is so, the bag will burst by having *this* such a weight upon it; and if this weight does not press upon it, which it supports, and if it were also supported, because the bag would have to pass above the water, and also if the water loads [weighs] upon its bottom, because it supports nothing, it supports only a lesser weight, being on its bottom. Therefore, if the bag supports the water, the bag takes the weight of this water away from the bottom of the well <margin>A passage from Manuscript C, which like this one is difficult to understand and titled: *Water and Air*, also relates to the hypothesis of a skin bag placed at the bottom of a well.</margin>.

<summary>Leonardo explores the "hydrostatic paradox," questioning how the weight of a large volume of water affects a submerged object and the container's floor. He concludes that if an object supports the water's weight, that weight is removed from the surface beneath it.</summary>

<keywords>hydrostatics, fluid pressure, skin bag, weight distribution, Manuscript C, physics of water</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific study of hydrostatic pressure on submerged objects to a broader definition of water as an element, followed by fundamental principles of mechanics and the physics of collision.</meta>

# FOLIO 26 (recto).

Water is, by its weight, the second element <note>In the traditional Aristotelian system, the four elements were layered: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> surrounding the Earth. That part of it which finds itself outside of its natural sphere will seek to return there with velocity; and the higher it rises above its elemental circular site, with so much more velocity will it descend back upon it. Its quality is moist and cold. And its nature is to always seek the low places when it is without obstacles. It willingly rises in vapors and mists, and having been converted into mist, it falls back in rain because the minute parts of the cloud stick together and create droplets. Through various elevations, it creates various effects in itself: namely water, or snow, or hail. It always fights with, and is fought by, the motion of the air; it stays attached to it and within its body, where the cold makes its final trials <note>original: "ultime prove," likely referring to the extreme transformation into ice or hail</note>, and it easily takes on odors and flavors.

— The blow... <note>This heading introduces the following section on the physics of impact.</note>

<note>Diagram of two crossed rods, each with a spherical weight at one end, meeting at an angle.</note>

— Bodies of equal movement, weight, and force, which through impetuous course clash together, will leap backward with equal distance from the place where they were turned by the percussion <term>percussion: the physical impact or collision between two bodies</term>.

<note>Diagram of two horizontal rods with spheres at the outer ends, colliding head-on in the center.</note>

— If two bodies of the same material strike each other, moved by equal cause and course, one will leap backward as much further than the other as it is smaller than the other.

<note>Diagram of two small side-by-side circles representing bodies after collision.</note>

— If two bodies of the same material are moved toward one another by equal cause, as soon as their percussion is made, one will leap backward as much further than the other as it is smaller than the other.

— A stationary body struck by its equal: the one struck flees, and the striker remains in the place of the one that fled <note>Leonardo describes a perfect transfer of momentum, similar to what is seen today with Newton's Cradle.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo defines the elemental nature of water and its cycle of evaporation and precipitation before outlining fundamental laws of motion and collision between bodies of varying sizes.</summary>
<keywords>elements, water cycle, meteorology, mechanics, percussion, momentum, collision physics, kinetics</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's observations on the physics of light and motion, transitioning from optical illusions caused by speed to the mechanical laws of falling bodies.</meta>

— FOLIO 26 (verso). —

# [MANUSCRIPT FACSIMILE]

**Perspective** <note>original: "prospettiua"</note>

Every body that moves with velocity appears to tint its path with the likeness of its color —

This proposition is seen through experience; for instance, when <insert>a</insert> lightning bolt moves among dark clouds, because of the velocity of its slithering flight, its entire path appears like a luminous snake. And similarly, if you move a glowing brand in a circular motion, its entire path will appear to you as a circle of fire. And this is because the <term>apprehension</term> <note>The sensory perception of the eye</note> is faster than the judgment <note>The cognitive processing of the mind</note>.

<note>Geometric diagram showing a pendulum arc or projectile trajectory with points labeled a, b, g, e, d, c, f</note>

**Of Motion** <note>original: "del moto"</note>

If the weight **a** is let fall toward **e**, it will rise as high toward **b**, and with as much speed, as would a falling ball of similar quality along the line **a b**; and all the angles **b, g, e, d, c** will be as much smaller than one another as the stroke of the swinging is smaller than the other.

---

# [PRINTED COMMENTARY]

->PERSPECTIVE AND MOTION<-

— Every body that moves with velocity appears to tint its path with the likeness of its color —
This proposition is seen through experience; for instance, when a lightning bolt moves among dark clouds, because of the velocity of its slithering flight, its entire path appears like a luminous snake. And similarly, if you move a glowing brand in a circular motion, its entire path will appear to you as a circle of fire. And this is because the apprehension is faster than the judgment.

->OF MOTION<-

— If the weight **a** is let fall toward **e**, it will rise as high toward **b**, and with as much speed, as would a falling ball of similar quality along the line **a b**; and all the angles **b, g, e, d, c** will be as much smaller than one another as the stroke of the swinging <note>The oscillation of the pendulum</note> is smaller than the other.

<summary>Leonardo explains how rapid motion creates optical persistence, such as lightning appearing as a continuous streak, and analyzes the mathematical proportions of a swinging pendulum's arc.</summary>
<keywords>persistence of vision, optics, lightning, motion, mechanics, pendulum, velocity, geometry</keywords>

— FOLIO 27 (recto) —

# IMPACT, FORCE, WEIGHT, MOVEMENT — LIGHT.

<margin>27</margin>
— Impact is the end of the rapid movement caused by force and carried out by bodies upon resistant objects in an indivisible moment of time <note>The original text includes the numbers 2 and 4 between the lines, likely referencing related passages in the author's other notebooks</note>.

— The object being struck, which is similar to the one striking it, receives from it impact, weight, and movement; it flees from its position, leaving in its place the object that struck it, which is now entirely deprived of all its power.

### PERSPECTIVE [light].

— As soon as the air is illuminated, it is filled with infinite <term>species<note>From the Latin 'species,' meaning 'appearances' or 'images.' In Renaissance science, it was believed that objects emitted thin 'films' or 'images' of themselves through the air to reach the eye.</note></term>, caused by the various bodies and colors located there—images for which the eye acts as both a target and a magnet.

---

<note>The numbers 2 and 4, marked by Leonardo between the first two lines of the front of the 27th folio, probably correspond to other passages in his writings.</note>

<vocab>impact, force, weight, movement, light, perspective, species (images), eye</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the physical nature of an 'impact' as the transfer of power between bodies and introduces the optical theory that light fills the air with infinite images drawn toward the eye.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, kinetics, optics, theory of vision, species, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of impact</keywords>

— FOLIO 27 (verso). —

# Transcription of Leonardo da Vinci's Manuscript
<note>The following text is transcribed from Leonardo's mirror script, mentally reversed to read left-to-right. It represents his notes on the physics of impact (Colpo) and motion (Moto).</note>

— Impact. I say it is the end of rapid motion, made by bodies against resistant objects. This same thing is the cause of all sounds, a breaker and transformer of various things, a restarter of secondary motion. Nothing shall be briefer nor of greater power, and it goes on diversifying itself through its causes. —

— Impact. The end of rapid motion caused by force and operated by weight in the resistant object; the cause of sounds; the transformer of its cause's effect. And nothing shall be of shorter duration in operation nor of greater power; its likeness <note>original: "similitudine," likely referring to the reflected force or image of the impact</note> and rebound are of the highest velocity and penetration of the air — in every opposed variety of object, it is entirely separated from its cause by birth, by circular movement, and is entirely in the whole and entirely in the part. —

— Impact. The end of rapid motion caused by force and operated by weight of bodies against resistant objects; from this derive sounds, from this come breakages. And nothing shall be of briefer operation nor of greater power; its rebound is of the highest velocity and penetration in every opposed variety of objects.

— If water that falls one inch per mile travels one mile per hour, how much will it travel falling 2 inches? —

— The parts of a stone moved by force and struck against the ground shall have a longer and faster path and a greater noise than the first <note>referring to the stone as a whole before it shattered</note> — because said parts, after their percussion, create circular movement.

### ON MOTION

— Motion is caused by force and applied to bodies removed from their sites; it becomes fast through a quick and powerful cause. A slow cause makes it likewise of brief course; it does not end without the violence of its opposition <note>Leonardo describes what we now call inertia and friction; motion continues until an opposing force stops it</note>.

---

# IMPACT — MOTION — FORCE — WEIGHT — WATER.

### IMPACT.

— I say that impact is the end of rapid motion, produced by bodies against resistant objects. It is the cause of all sounds; it breaks and transforms various things; it also causes a second motion, and nothing is more rapid, nor of greater power, and it continues to diversify [itself] according to [various] causes.

— Impact is the end of rapid motion, caused by force and operated by weight within the resistant object; it is the cause of sounds and the transformer of the sound of its cause and its effect. And nothing is of more prompt action, nor of greater power, and its likeness <note>original: "sa ressemblance," likely referring to the wave-like propagation of the force</note> and its results are of extreme rapidity and penetration of the air into all kinds of opposing [resistant] objects; it is complete and it escapes from its cause by birth, by circular movement, and is entirely in the whole and entirely in the part. <note>Leonardo uses this phrase—"all in the whole and all in the part"—to describe how certain forces, like light or sound, exist fully at every point in the medium they occupy.</note>

— Impact is the end of rapid motion, caused by force and operated by weight of bodies against resistant objects; from it derive sounds, from it derive breakages, and nothing is of more prompt action nor of greater power; its results are of extreme rapidity and penetration into all kinds of resistant objects.

— If water descends by one finger's-breadth <note>a unit of measurement approximately 3/4 of an inch</note> per mile and it travels one mile per hour, how much will it travel when descending by 2 fingers?

— The fragments of a stone moved by force and struck against the ground make a longer and faster path, as well as more noise, than the first <gloss>than the stone itself</gloss> — because of this, these fragments make, after their percussion, a circular movement.

### ON MOTION.

— Motion is caused by force and is united with bodies changed in place; it is made rapid by a powerful and lively cause; a slow cause makes it travel a short distance; it does not end without the violence of that which obstructs it.

<summary>Leonardo defines impact as the terminal point of rapid motion and explores its role in creating sound, fragmentation, and secondary circular movements. He also poses a mathematical problem regarding the velocity of flowing water relative to its incline.</summary>

<keywords>impact, motion, force, weight, water velocity, percussion, acoustics, fragmentation, physics of fluids</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the physics of motion and ballistics, specifically focusing on the mechanics of impact and the principle of recoil.</meta>

# IMPACTS — FORCE AND RESISTANCE.

### ON IMPACT.

— *The indentation made on a wall struck by a bombard <note>A "bombard" is an early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th through 16th centuries.</note> becomes the center of the cannonball that strikes it from the opposite side.*

### ON IMPACT.

— *If an impact strikes a round fortress, the air located on the opposite side resists immediately, causing that fortress to burst open at the flanks.*

— *The bombard in which the multiplication of fire is most rapid—which increases, assaults, and strikes the resisting air most suddenly—will be the one that recoils the most.*

— If you find yourself in a boat and exert the greatest possible force within it, the boat will never move, unless said force has a firmer point of support <term>point d'appui: a fixed point used to exert leverage or force</term> outside of this boat than inside it.

— Likewise, if you are all huddled inside a sack and exert efforts to move, it will be impossible for you to change your position; whereas, if you pull one foot out of the sack and use it to push against the ground, and then place your head at the bottom of the sack, you will then cause the sack to move backward. — Thus acts the flame, wishing to multiply and expand within the bombard; for as long as it is entirely within this bombard, it does not recoil, but when this flame strikes and pushes against the resisting air while remaining joined to the flame pushing at the breech <note>original: "le fond" - referring to the back of the cannon's internal chamber</note>, it causes the bombard to recoil; indeed, the part of the flame that strikes the air, being unable to find the sudden passage it requires, exerts its force on the opposite side.

<vocab>bombard, cannonball, fortress, force, resistance, point of support, flame</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the physics of percussion and recoil, using analogies of a man in a boat or a sack to explain why a cannon moves backward only when its internal pressure meets external atmospheric resistance.</summary>
<keywords>ballistics, recoil, physics of motion, bombard, atmospheric resistance, point of support, mechanics</keywords>

# THE BALANCE OF MAN, THE CENTER OF GRAVITY. — DRAWING.

— A person who is seated cannot rise from their seat without using their arms if the part of the body that is in front of the pole <note>original: "pôle"; here meaning the point of support or fulcrum</note> does not weigh more than the part that is behind that pole.

— Anyone who climbs up to any place must shift a greater portion of their weight in front of their highest foot than behind it—that is to say, in front of the pole rather than behind that pole; therefore, a man will always shift a greater portion of his weight toward the side to which he wishes to move than toward any other place.

— The faster one runs, the more one leans toward the place where one is running, placing more weight in front of the pole than behind it. Consequently, one who runs down a slope has their pole on the heels; one who runs up a slope does so on the tips of the feet; and one who runs on a level plain goes first on the heels and then on the tips of the feet <insert>1</insert>.

— This person will not be able to carry their weight if they do not balance the weight in front by leaning backward, so that the foot that is planted is always located in the middle of the weight.

— When you draw nudes, take care to always draw them in their entirety, then finish the limb that seems best to you and apply it in relation to the other limbs <insert>and while executing it, do not cease to attend to the other limbs</insert>; otherwise, you would acquire the habit of never properly joining the limbs together <insert>2</insert>.

— Take care never *to* make the head turned in the same direction as the chest, nor the arm move in the same way as the leg; and if the head is turned toward the right shoulder, make its parts lower on the left side than on the right; and if you make the chest protrude, when the head turns toward the left side, let the parts on the right side be higher than those on the left.

---

<margin>1. The words written afterward in the ordinary direction in the manuscript <note>original: "del ritrar li nudi", meaning "on drawing nudes"</note> are not in Leonardo's hand.</margin>
<margin>2. Compare with the <term>Treatise on Painting</term> by Manzi, page 72 <note>original: "Del rittrare gl’ignudi", meaning "On drawing the nudes"</note>.</margin>

<summary>This page outlines Leonardo da Vinci's observations on the physics of human movement, specifically how the center of gravity shifts during sitting, climbing, and running, alongside practical advice for artists on maintaining anatomical proportion and contrapposto.</summary>
<keywords>center of gravity, human kinetics, anatomy, drawing techniques, equilibrium, locomotion, artistic composition</keywords>

# — FOLIO 29 (recto). —

<margin>29</margin>
<note>Handwritten text in mirror script</note>
<unclear>Just as much as</unclear> just as much as the part of the nude <note>original: "nvdo"</note> from **d** to **a** decreases due to the resting pose, so much does the opposite part increase. That is to say, that by as much as the part **d** to **a** decreases from its measure, the opposite part above increases and adds to its measure. The navel <note>original: "elbelicho"</note> never leaves its height, nor does the <term>virile member</term> <note>the male genitalia, used here as a central anatomical landmark</note>. And this lowering occurs because the figure that rests upon one foot makes that foot the center of the weight placed above it. This being so, the middle of the shoulders is directed straight above it, moving out of its <term>perpendicular line</term> <note>the vertical axis of gravity</note>, which line passes through the superficial centers of the body. And this line comes to twist its upper extremity over the foot that supports the weight; and the parts and the transverse lineaments, constrained at equal angles, make their ends lower on that side which rests, as appears in **a**, **b**, and **c**.

It shows how the shoulders and hips tilt in opposite directions to maintain equilibrium when the weight is shifted to one side.</note>

# DRAWING — THE BALANCE OF THE VARIOUS PARTS OF THE HUMAN BODY.

<margin>29</margin> — Just as much as the part of the nude man from **d** to **a** decreases on the side of the pose [the side which bears the weight of the body], so much does the opposite part increase; that is to say, that just as much as the part **d** to **a** decreases in its measure [loses its size], so much does the opposite part *increase* in its measure [gain]; but the navel or the <term>virile member</term> <note>the male genitalia, used as a central anatomical reference point</note> never leaves its height. This lowering occurs because the figure rests upon one foot; this foot becomes the center of the weight placed above it, and, this being the case, the middle of the shoulders directs itself [corresponds] there from above, moving out of its <term>perpendicular line</term> <note>the vertical axis of gravity</note>, a line which passes through the superficial centers of the body; and this line comes to bend its upper extremity over the foot that supports the weight *and the parts....*; the lineaments crossed by it, forced into equal angles, become lower [sink] with their extremities on that *the* part which supports the weight, as appears in **a b c**.

---

1. See: *Manzi, Treatise on Painting*, p. 107 and plate II, fig. 10 (On first placing a figure in the narrative). <note>original: "Tratt. dell. pitt." / "Del porre prima una figura nell’ istoria"</note>

<header>— FOLIO 29 (verso). —</header>

<note>This page features a facsimile reproduction of Leonardo's manuscript folio 29 verso, accompanied by a printed transcription. The manuscript portion includes mirror-script text and a central diagram comparing the mechanics of a longbow and a crossbow. Leonardo explores the relationship between force, the duration of its application (the "motor"), and the distance traveled by a projectile.</note>

### ON EYEGLASSES
— One can hold eyeglasses at a certain distance from the eye and look through them at the sun; this will take away your sight forever. <note>Leonardo warns of the dangers of solar observation through magnifying lenses, which act like burning glasses.</note>

### ON THE NATURE OF MOVEMENT
— At **n**, there will be an iron piece that will release the smaller bow.
— Make 2 notes [or knots]. That is, 2 release mechanisms: one for the crossbow and the other for the bow, and let both be released at the same time. The bow is positioned in such a way that the arrow does not touch the string of the crossbow at all.

### FOR WHAT REASON THE BOW DRIVES ITS PROJECTILE MUCH FURTHER THAN THE CROSSBOW.
— Every time the weight moved by a force is not proportional to that force, the object propelled will not complete its proper path or course.

— The weight driven by the fury of the force, not being of a quality proportional to its power, will not complete its proper course.

— This is clearly confirmed by experience; for if you throw away from you with fury or with a swift motion of the arms an object which, due to its lightness, is not a match for your strength, that object will have little movement. Likewise, throwing an object that exceeds your strength due to its heaviness will result in a short path.

— The force that moves a weight proportional to its quality will distance it from itself once more than another, according to how much the time of the action of the <term>motor</term> <note>the "motor" refers to the source of the power, in this case, the bow string or the arm</note> enters from one into the other.

— This is demonstrated by experience; for if you drive a projectile from a bow with a force of 50 pounds, and it travels 300 <term>braccia</term> <note>a "braccio" or "arm's length" was a common Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches</note> away from you; and then with a force of 300 pounds you drive a projectile of similar weight from a crossbow, this one—due to the disproportionate force—will not exceed 250 braccia. (The reason the bow drives further is that the motor accompanies the moved object longer, because the bow is longer by several braccia than the crossbow). The other reason is that the person who draws the bow bends the arm holding the bow somewhat through the force, and upon releasing the string, two movements are born: that of the arm which straightens itself, and that of the bow which returns to its place.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes why a longbow can outshoot a heavy crossbow, concluding that the "motor" (the bow string) stays in contact with the arrow for a longer duration and distance, providing better proportional acceleration. He also notes the danger of looking at the sun through lenses.</summary>

<keywords>ballistics, mechanics, proportional force, longbow vs crossbow, optics, Leonardo's experiments, physics of motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the mechanics of water-powered machinery and the physics of projectiles, likely following previous discussions on the conservation of energy and momentum.</meta>

FOLIO 30 (recto)

# FORCE AND MOTION

That wheel which is moved by a quantity of water in a certain time—which water cannot be increased in its flow, nor in its quantity, nor by a greater fall—the <term>output</term><note>original: "lussiti", likely a corruption of the Latin 'exitus' or 'uscita', referring here to the work produced or the result of the motion</note> of the said wheel is fixed. That is to say, if a wheel moves one millstone, it is impossible for it to turn two without doubling the time. It follows, then, that one millstone will perform as much work in one hour as two will perform in another hour. And thus, the same wheel will be able to turn infinite millstones, which will perform as much work over a very long time as the first millstone did in one hour.

### MOTION AND FORCE

A slow cause produces a fast and weak movement; a fast and weak movement as a cause produces a slow and strong movement.

The longer the moved object is accompanied by its mover, the further it will travel from it. Or: the more the moving force accompanies the moved object, the further it will drive it away from itself, and the longer the movement will be.

<note>Kinetics diagram of a crossbow bolt</note> This is demonstrated by a crossbowman on horseback, who, when he wishes to discharge the crossbow, spurs the horse and with sudden speed pushes his chest forward, extends his arms with the crossbow, and discharges it all at the same time. (Here, four motions occur in the movement of an arrow: the first is that of the horse; the second of the chest; the third of the arms which throw themselves forward from the chest; the last is that of the string). If this arrow is proportional to the movement and the force, it will go very far from its starting point.

As much force as you apply to the preparation of your crossbow, just as much will flee from it as it returns to its state of liberty, and it will join with the object moved by it. Or: with as much force as you prepare your crossbow, with that much will the arrow moved by it travel.

With as much <term>natural weight</term><note>The inherent weight of the person or object at rest</note> as you simply use to prepare it, charging your crossbow to its full potential with its natural motion, just as much <term>accidental weight</term><note>The kinetic energy or force acquired through motion</note> will attach itself and be infused into the arrow that flees from that crossbow.

---

<margin>30</margin>
->FORCE AND MOTION<-

— That wheel which is moved by a quantity of water in a certain time—which water cannot be increased in its flow, nor in its quantity, nor by a greater fall—the output of the said wheel is fixed. That is to say, if a wheel moves one millstone, it is impossible for it to turn two without doubling the time. It follows, then, that one millstone will perform as much work in one hour as two will perform in another hour. And thus, the same wheel will be able to turn infinite millstones, which will perform as much work over a very long time as the first millstone did in one hour.

->MOTION AND FORCE<-

— A slow cause produces a fast and weak movement; a fast and weak movement as a cause produces a slow and strong movement.

— The longer the moved object is accompanied by its mover, the further it will travel from it. Or: the more the moving force accompanies the moved object, the further it will drive it away from itself, and the longer the movement will be.

— This is demonstrated by a crossbowman on horseback, who, when he wishes to discharge the crossbow, spurs the horse and with sudden speed pushes his chest forward, extends his arms with the crossbow, and discharges it all at the same time. (Here, four motions occur in the movement of an arrow: the first is that of the horse; the second of the chest; the third of the arms which throw themselves forward from the chest; the last is that of the string). If this arrow is proportional to the movement and the force, it will go very far from its starting point.

— As much force as you apply to the preparation of your crossbow, just as much will flee from it as it returns to its state of liberty, and it will join with the object moved by it. Or: with as much force as you prepare your crossbow, with that much will the arrow moved by it travel.

— With as much natural weight as you simply use to prepare it, charging your crossbow to its full potential with its natural motion, just as much accidental weight will attach itself and be infused into the arrow that flees from that crossbow.

- FOLIO 30 (verso). -

# EXPERIMENT
<note>original: "SPERIENTIA"</note>
If you wish to prove how much faster a weight of one ounce falls than one of 2 ounces, falling from the same height, you shall do it thus: take 2 pieces of cork of the same thickness and of doubled length—that is, so that the one which weighs two ounces is twice as long as the other—and have them dropped at the same time from the height of a bell tower. And fix your eye upon that smaller one which remains behind, noting with your eye the marks on the wall or the stones where it passes. And when you hear the strike <note>The sound of the object hitting the ground.</note> of the 2-ounce weight on the ground, note at which stone of the bell tower the one-ounce weight was positioned, and then measure how much of the path the ounce had traveled when the 2 ounces struck [hit] the ground.

# ON WEIGHT IN THE AIR —
Weight in the air demonstrates the truth of its weight. Weight in the water will appear to be less weight by as much as the water is heavier than the air. A weight thrown upon molten metal will stay on top if it is lighter than the molten metal; and if the weight thrown upon said molten metal be by nature more ponderous <term>ponderous: heavy or dense</term> than that metal, it will go to the bottom.

# ON MOVEMENT IN THE AIR —
Movement made in the air will be as much faster than that in water (although caused by equal force) as the air is thinner than the water (and this you shall experiment with a sword).

# ON THE MOTION OF WEIGHT ALONG THE GROUND —
Where there is much contact of the weight with the ground, there is much resistance. You will see the experiment with a thick rope stretched along the ground, which, being taken by one of its ends, will offer resistance to the force of a man, the mover; but if that rope be gathered into a ball, it will be pulled by the mover with little force. — 7 —

# ON THE STRENGTH OF MAN —
A man pulling a weight on a balance scale <note>original: "inbilancia"</note> against himself cannot pull more than he himself weighs. And if he has to lift it, he will lift as much more than his weight as he exceeds the common strength of other men. The greatest force a man can exert with equal speed and movement is when he fixes his feet upon one of the heads of the balance scales and braces his shoulders against something stable; this man will lift from the opposite head of the balance as much weight as he weighs, plus as much weight as he has the strength to carry on his shoulders. — 8 —

<margin>The manuscript contains several small sketches in the right margin illustrating physical experiments with weights and balances.</margin>

<keywords>gravity, falling bodies, fluid dynamics, friction, mechanics, human strength, balance scales, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# FOLIO 31 (recto).

<note>Manuscript facsimile transcription (mirror script read normally)</note>

A blow struck upon a stone placed in the water will kill all the fish or other animals that are found beneath or near it.

— A blow struck upon the cord of a glue-pot <note>original: "chorda dela chola" - likely referring to a tensioned cord or a specific mechanical device used in a workshop</note> doubles the suffering of the one bound to it. — A blow struck upon a granary floor <note>original: "lostato" - likely 'lo staio' or 'lo stato', referring to a place where grain is measured or stored</note> lowers the wheat that is found there.

— I remind you that you should make your propositions and that you should allege the above-written things as examples, and not as propositions, for that would be too simple; and you shall say thus:

->EXPERIENCE<-

A blow struck into any body, dense and heavy, passes by nature through said body and strikes the surrounding thing that is found within the surrounding bodies, whether they be dense or rare. For example <note>original: "verbigrattia" - Latin: verbi gratia</note>: if there are many fish in a body of water which enters beneath a stone, if you give a great blow upon that stone, all the fish that are found beneath or beside that stone will come to the surface of the water as if dead. And the reason is that a blow struck—since every thing pushed by a power greater than its own desires to flee before it—and the greater the force of the power that pushes, the greater will be the flight of the thing that flees being pushed. Which, if it be a dense and heavy thing, will strike that thing which opposes its course in such a way; and if it be a light thing, it follows its course in part with little damage to itself. And if it be a stable and heavy thing, the first contact found in the blow will be damaged and ruined; which blow will pass by nature into the whole containing body that is struck, resounding with a roar <note>original: "istrepido" - noise or vibration</note> not only through the body struck, but also through those bodies that surround it. And if there be any animal in said neighboring bodies, they will also be penetrated by said roar and movement in such a way that, being unable to resist, they will remain as if dead; and their pain will be in the likeness of the pain that the hand receives when it is struck with a hammer against a stone that lies within it.

***

<note>Printed commentary and transcription</note>

— A blow struck upon a stone placed in the water will kill all the fish or other animals that are found beneath or near it.
— A blow struck upon the cord of the glue-pot doubles the suffering of the one bound. — A blow struck upon the granary floor lowers the wheat that is found there.
— I remind you that you should make your propositions and that you should allege the above-written things as examples, and not as propositions, for that would be too simple; and you shall say thus:

->EXPERIENCE<-

— *A blow struck into any body, dense and heavy, passes by nature through said body and strikes the surrounding thing that is found within the surrounding bodies, whether they be dense or rare. For example: if there are many fish in a body of water which enters beneath a stone, if you give a great blow upon that stone, all the fish that are found beneath or beside that stone will come to the surface of the water as if dead. And the reason is that a blow struck—since every thing pushed by a power greater than its own desires to flee before it—and the greater the force of the power that pushes, the greater will be the flight of the thing that flees being pushed. Which, if it be a dense and heavy thing, will strike that thing which opposes its course in such a way; and if it be a light thing, it follows its course in part with little damage to itself. And if it be a stable and heavy thing, the first contact found in the blow will be damaged and ruined; which blow will pass by nature into the whole containing body that is struck, resounding with a roar not only through the body struck, but also through those bodies that surround it. And if there be any animal in said neighboring bodies, they will also be penetrated by said roar and movement in such a way that, being unable to resist, they will remain as if dead; and their pain will be in the likeness of the pain that the hand receives when it is struck with a hammer against a stone that lies within it.*

# FORCE — IMPACT — MOTION — WEIGHT.

— Why there is more power in pulling and pushing by jerks <note>The author uses "secousses" to describe impulsive or sudden forces versus steady ones.</note> than by a steady momentum or a continuous force.

— Why an impact leaves a lingering sound in bells.

— If you strike [with] a hammer or another weight upon the edge [at the end] of a table, an object resting upon it will jump; upon falling back, it will either land in the spot from which it rose, or it will move toward the blow, or move away from it, or move sideways; this change will occur according to the manner in which the board [the table] is struck.

— If you drive the point of a nail slightly into a board [into a plank], and you strike that board hard with a hammer on the opposite side, the nail will pass through the board and come toward the hammer.

— If you deliver a great number of blows to a nail to drive it into a board, it will be quite long and difficult. But if you attach the nail to the hammer with wax, in a single blow equal to the others, you will drive it entirely into the board.

— Why force is equal to impact in the minting of coins.

— Why someone jumping on the balls of their feet makes less noise and as much weight <note>Here "weight" refers to the force of impact or pressure exerted on the ground.</note> as someone jumping on their heels.

— An impact made with a short and strong motion is similar to one made with a prolonged and weak motion, like the movement of a cherry stone squeezed between two fingers; with such a short cause, it will travel as far as a stone thrown by hand.

— When air that is forcibly compressed in some place escapes, the percussion made upon the other [the external air] will create a great noise, as seen in bombards <term>bombards: early muzzle-loading cannons used in siege warfare</term> and in a bursting bladder, and in the pops children make with seeds placed in a small elderwood tube, or even in the deflation of a ball or a bellows¹.

— The impact, after the first collision with an object, does not pass to others, except to that which is located on the side opposite to where the first object was struck.

— If a blow is delivered to an object placed under another, the struck object will fly away with the blow, and the one placed on top will fall to the spot from which the struck object departed.

— The more the impact is caused by a rapid motion, the more accurately the object placed on top of the struck one will fall exactly where the struck object departed.

— When the motion generating the impact becomes slower, the struck object will carry the one placed upon it further along as it flees the blow; you can experiment with this using pieces on a checkerboard.

— Natural motion was first accidental (thus the falling stone was first carried or thrown upward); it was called "accidental" when it ascended and "natural" when it descended. <note>The author is referencing Aristotelian physics, where "natural motion" is an object returning to its place of origin (like a stone falling to earth).</note>

— A weight falling into water enters with such speed that the water surrounding the impact, being held back by the other water, cannot make room [for that which the weight displaces]; it must therefore flee by the path where it finds the least resistance; since air resists less than water, the quantity of water vacating the spot where the blow strikes will jump into the air.

— What is achieved by shaking and what is achieved by pulling. For example, if someone pulling a sling used a continuous motion, the stone would not leave the instrument; it is by a jerk <note>A sudden release or change in velocity.</note> that it is made to fly.

---

1. See folio 32 recto: *the thunder made...* <note>original: "il tono fatto..."</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the mechanics of impact and percussion, moving from general observations about nails and hammers to specific experiments with clay, metal, and the physics of projectiles like crossbow bolts and cannon fire.</meta>

# PERCUSSION AND THE NATURE OF IMPACT

— If you have a uniform mass of fresh earth <note>original: "terra fresscha" – likely potter's clay or soft soil used for modeling</note>, take a small part of it and form a small ball with your hands. Then, throw it back into its original mass; you will see that the said ball will embed itself within, partially maintaining its round shape at the point where it strikes.

— If you strike together two pieces of metal of equal size, the one that is *less* <insert>more</insert> sharp <note>original: "achuto" – meaning pointed or having a smaller surface area</note> will embed itself into the one that is less sharp.

— Impact is motion interrupted by a resisting object. Although there may be motion without impact, there will never be impact without motion.

— The thunderous tone produced by a bombard <note>A large-caliber medieval cannon</note> is caused by fire striking into the air, just like that of a lightning bolt.

— The sound made with a bellows or the mouth is caused by air striking back against the surrounding air.

— If a weight of one hundred pounds falls ten times from a height of ten cubits <note>original: "braccia" – a traditional unit of measurement roughly the length of an arm</note> onto the same spot and embeds itself by one cubit, how far would it embed itself if falling from a height of one hundred cubits?

— If a weight of ten pounds falls ten times from a height of ten cubits onto the same spot and embeds itself by half a cubit, how far will a weight of one hundred pounds embed itself falling from the same height and onto the same ground, provided it has the same base <note>surface area</note>?

— It is possible to judge the impact by the weight, but not the weight by the impact.

— What is the difference between an impact where the object moves toward the blow and one where it is awaited by the object?

— What is the difference between an impact made upon a fleeing object and one made upon a stable object?

— That impact will be of greater strength whose motion has been more closely accompanied by its cause <note>The "cause" refers to the initial force or propellant acting upon the object</note>.

— That thing which is pulled furthest out of its natural state will return with greater impetus when it is set at liberty; and this is the principle of the crossbow <note>original: "balestro"</note>.

— That impact will be greatest which has the center of the spot struck, the weight that strikes, and the place from which it was driven all along the same straight line.

— If two balls <note>original: "balotte" – likely lead bullets or stone shot</note> are shot in a line toward one another so that they strike each other, and they are of equal power, vehemence, and weight, as soon as they collide they will stop, destroyed one within the other.

— If two balls are shot upward in such a way that they collide, the course of the right one will be transferred into the course of the left, and thus the course of the left will change into that of the right.

— An impact given upon the string of the crossbow will return upward more quickly than it descended, and it will more greatly damage its object.

— An impact given from beneath the string of the crossbow will return downward with great fury, because to that impact is added the natural fall <note>gravity</note>, and it damages its object more.

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of percussion, calculating the force of falling weights and the behavior of colliding projectiles. He notes how the alignment of force and the "natural" tendency of objects (like gravity or tension) influence the power of an impact.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, percussion, impact, gravity, ballistics, crossbow, bombard, physics of motion</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the mechanics of impact and percussion, specifically focusing on the mathematical relationship between weight, height, and the depth of penetration, as well as the behavior of projectiles and the physics of the crossbow.</meta>

# IMPACT — MOTION — WEIGHT.

— If you take a small portion from an equal quality of fresh earth and make a very small ball of it with your hands, then throw it back into its original block, you will see this ball sink into it, partially preserving its round shape in the part that sinks in.

— If you strike together 2 pieces of metal of equal size, the sharper one will sink into the less sharp one.

— Impact and motion [are] interrupted by the resisting object. And although there is motion without impact, there will never be impact without motion.

— The crash made by the bombard <note>A large-caliber medieval cannon</note> is fire making a shock against the air, like that of an arrow <note>original: "flèche" – likely referring to the whistling sound or the speed of a projectile</note>.

— The noise made with a bellows or with a mouth comes from the shock of air against air.

— If a weight of one hundred pounds falls 10 times from a height of 10 cubits <term>brasse: a traditional unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or the length of an outstretched arm</term> onto the same spot, and sinks in by one cubit, how much will it sink in when falling from a height *of one of 100 cubits*?

— If a weight of 10 pounds falls 10 times from a height of 10 cubits onto the same spot and sinks in *by half a cubit*, how much will a weight of 100 pounds sink in, falling from the same height onto the same ground and with the same base?

— It is possible to judge the impact by the weight, and not the weight by the impact.

— What difference is there between the impact toward which the object comes and that which is awaited by the object?

— What difference is there between the impact given to a fleeing object and that which is given to a stationary object?

— The impact is of greater force when its motion has been accompanied by its cause.

— The thing that has been most removed out of its nature [out of its natural position] is that which will return to it most impetuously when it regains its liberty, and this is [the case of] the crossbow.

— The strongest impact will be that for which the center of the place struck, of the weight that strikes, and of the place from which it was sent, are on the same straight line.

— If two balls are thrown in a [straight] line against one another, and they collide with each other and have the same power, the same vehemence [impulse], and the same weight, they will stop as soon as they meet, undone by one another <note>meaning their forces neutralize each other perfectly</note>.

— If two balls are thrown upward so that they meet, the path of the one on the right will take the direction that the path of the one on the left had, and likewise the path of the one on the left will change into that of the right.

— The blow given upon the string of the crossbow will rise faster than it descended and will damage its object more.

— The blow that strikes from beneath the string of the crossbow will return downward with great fury, because to this blow is added the natural fall, and it will damage its object more.

<summary>This page explores the physics of percussion, calculating how weight and height affect penetration depth and analyzing the mechanics of colliding objects and crossbow strings. It establishes that impact is essentially interrupted motion and examines the "impetuous" return of objects to their natural state.</summary>
<keywords>percussion, impact, weight, motion, cubits, ballistics, crossbow, physics of collision, atmospheric shock</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's systematic investigation into the laws of physics, specifically focusing on mechanics, ballistics, and the behavior of moving bodies. The tone is inquisitive, posing a series of "if/then" scenarios to establish universal principles.</meta>

->IMPACT — FORCE — WEIGHT — MOVEMENT<-

— The impact has a greater force when it rebounds backward along the line of incidence <note>The path the object took to hit the surface</note> upon finding a resistant object.
— If the object moves as fast against the impact as the impact moves against the object, the impact will be doubled with a twofold force.
— If the impact is great, the rebound will be great, but examine whether a rebound at equal angles is stronger than one at unequal angles.
— If a crossbow drives an arrow of 4 ounces to 200 fathoms <note>original: "brasses," a measurement of length roughly equal to the reach of a man's arms</note>, at what distance will it launch 4 arrows of one ounce each, launched at the same time?
— If one cord carries 100 pounds of weight, how much will 2 cords similar in thickness, strength, and length carry?
— If you give the crossbow an arrow that weighs half as much again as is appropriate for its range, then another that weighs half as much less [than is appropriate for its range], and these arrows are shot separately, which one will the crossbow send the furthest?
— If you strike a glass vessel 100 times with a spoon, it will not break; if you take a spoon that weighs 100 times the first and strike that same vessel, in a single blow you will break it.
— Many small weights attached to the end of a lever perform the same function as if they were joined into one.
— If you throw a weight that is suited [proportioned] to your strength, then you divide it into 100 parts and throw them at the same time, I ask which will travel the furthest.
— If a crossbow fires 4 arrows, where one weighs 4 ounces, another 3, another 2, and another one ounce, so that between them all they weigh 10 ounces and they are all fired at the same time, I ask which one will go the [furthest] and by how much further will one go than the other?
— The rebound of an impact that has struck the hardest will damage its object less than that of an impact that has struck less hard, while being delivered with the same force; thus the ball that you throw against a wall will do you little harm in returning to you, while the one you throw along a wall will do more harm to whoever awaits its rebound.
— Impact is caused by movement, and the power of the impact is greater as the cause of its movement is swifter.
— An impact that finds obstacle and resistance becomes all the stronger for it.
— The impact will strike the second reflected object [which it reaches by reflection] all the more as it finds less resistance in the first.
— If you strike a flat board, you will see the dust found there gather into small heaps.
— If you move a sieve in a circle, you will see the largest parts of the grain move to the center of that sieve.
— If dust is moved by the wind in a circle, you will see the largest parts make a larger circle.
— If a weight weighing one pound and another weighing one and a half are dropped at the same time from a height of 100 fathoms, how much faster will the first fall than the second? <note>The author is exploring the acceleration of falling bodies, a precursor to Galilean physics.</note>
— If a weight falls 200 fathoms, how much faster will it fall through the second hundred fathoms than the first?
— In the same time that you load two different crossbows....
— As soon as any two crossbows are loaded at the same time and with the same force, it does not matter if they are of different thickness, length, or weight; they will always launch the same weight to the same distance.
— If a thing is launched once faster than another, it will go once further.
— The metal that receives the impact between equal angles will flee equally under that blow.
— And that which receives the impact between unequal angles, that metal will flee under the blow by increasing on the side of the obtuse angle.
— The thing with which you strike another of continuous matter will numb the hand by which it is held.
— He who jousts, when he puts his lance in the rest, transfers the center of his weight toward the front part of the horse.

<summary>A series of mechanical propositions and thought experiments regarding ballistics, the force of impact, and the behavior of falling or thrown weights. The text explores how resistance, angles of incidence, and the distribution of mass affect physical outcomes.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, ballistics, physics, crossbows, force of impact, gravity, acceleration, jousting, reflection, proportions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's empirical studies on mechanics, specifically addressing the physics of impact, the structural properties of wood, and the counter-intuitive nature of ballistics and momentum.</meta>

### [Facsimile Transcription]

<note>The following is translated from Leonardo's mirror script, where he records observations on physical sensations and the laws of motion.</note>

The hand that holds within itself a stone struck by a hammer feels part of the pain that the stone would feel if it were a sentient body. <note>Leonardo often uses biological analogies to explain physical forces like vibration and impact.</note>

Make it so that the small parts correspond to the parts, and the parts to the whole.

**ON MOTION**
If the weight *p* falls so much faster—the weight *n* than the weight *m*—as the line *a. b.* enters into the line *a. c.*
Proof.

**ON BENDING A PIECE OF WOOD**
No body can be bent without its length decreasing. <note>A fundamental observation of compression on the inner curve of a bent object.</note>

**ON THE WEAKEST POINT OF A BENT THING**
That body which is of equal thickness will appear to bend with greater ease at the part furthest from the ends than at any other.

**ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE BENT THING**
If you wish to bend two things of equal thickness and weight, the one that is longer will be bent by less force than the shorter one. <note>An early observation of the principle of leverage in structural mechanics.</note>

**REMINDER TO CONDUCT AN EXPERIMENT**
Conduct an experiment: if a thin piece of wood suspended crosswise over two supports at its ends carries 10 pounds, see if a beam of the same proportion will carry [the same], and watch if the rule of three <term>rule of three: a mathematical method to find a fourth value from three known ratios</term> serves you, because experience makes a good rule.

**ON SQUARING A TREE**
If you want to square the largest <insert>tree</insert>—a tree of the largest square that you can—you will diminish it by 1/4 of its thickness, its weight, and its strength.

**ON A THING THROWN THROUGH THE AIR WITH FURY**
That thing which is thrown through the air with greater power than is appropriate for its lightness will make a shorter movement of its path than if it were moved by lesser power. You will see the experiment by using a ball or a bladder: if you throw it gently it will go far, and if you throw it hard it will travel a short distance. <note>Leonardo observes air resistance; a light object like a bladder is deformed or hindered by the air more violently when struck with great force.</note>

**ON THE MOVED THING**
That thing which is moved out of its natural state returns with fury out of its natural state. Example of a knife stuck into a board: if you pull it and let it go, it will pass further than it was before it was moved. <note>A study of elasticity and potential energy.</note>

# STRIKE — PROPORTIONS — BENDING — SQUARING — WEIGHT
# FORCE — MOTION.

<margin>33</margin> — The hand that holds the stone struck with the hammer feels in part the pain that the stone would feel, if it were a sentient body. <note>Leonardo frequently uses "sentience" as a metaphor to describe the transmission of kinetic energy and vibration through solid objects.</note>

— Make it so that the smallest parts are in proportion with the larger ones, and these latter with the whole. <note>original: "Fais que les moindres parties soient en rapport avec les plus grandes, et ces dernières avec le tout." This reflects Leonardo's belief in universal harmonic proportions, found also in his "Treatise on Painting."</note>

->ON MOTION.<-

— If the weight—the weight *n* will fall faster than the weight *m*, in proportion as the line *a b* enters into the line *a c*.

— Proof. <note>This refers to a diagram in the original manuscript illustrating gravitational acceleration or inclined planes.</note>

->ON THE BENDING OF WOOD.<-

— No body can be bent without its length decreasing.

->ON THE WEAKEST POINT OF THE THING THAT BENDS.<-

— If a body has a uniform thickness, the part furthest from the ends will bend with more ease than any other.

->ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE BENT THING.<-

— If you wish to bend two things of equal thickness and weight, you will bend the longer one with less force than the shorter one.

->REMEMBER TO CONDUCT THIS EXPERIMENT.<-

— Experiment: if a thin piece of wood suspended across its ends on two supports carries 10 pounds, what will a beam of the same proportions carry? Assure yourself if the rule of three <term>rule of three: a mathematical method of finding a fourth value from three known values using ratios</term> is applicable, for experience makes a good rule.

->ON THE SQUARING OF A TREE.<-

— If you want to draw from the largest tree—from a tree—the largest possible square, you will diminish it by a quarter of its thickness, its weight, and its strength. <note>Leonardo is calculating the loss of material and structural integrity when hewing a round log into a square timber.</note>

->ON THE THING THROWN INTO THE AIR WITH FURY [violence].<-

— A thing thrown into the air by a power greater than is suited to its lightness *will have—will make* less movement, or distance, than if it had been moved by a lesser power. You will see this by experimenting with a ball or a bladder; if you throw it gently, it will go far, and if you throw it hard, it will cover little distance. <note>Leonardo is observing air resistance; a light object thrown too hard creates more turbulence and drag, limiting its travel.</note>

->ON THE THING IN MOTION.<-

— A thing that is forced out of its natural state moves with fury to return to it. <note>original: "La chose qu'on fait sortir de sa nature s'en va avec furie hors de sa nature." Leonardo refers to the "natural place" of objects, an Aristotelian concept he was testing through mechanics.</note> Example: a knife fixed into a board; if you pull it and then let it go, it will pass further than it was before being put into motion. <note>This describes the "overshoot" or oscillation of a flexible object returning to equilibrium.</note>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the mechanical properties of materials and the physics of equilibrium, specifically focusing on the center of gravity and the proportional power of levers.</meta>

### On the Center of Weight

Any solid weight that is supported in its center, with the remainder hanging suspended, may be of any strange shape you wish; it will always stabilize itself over its support in equilibrium, even if sometimes the extremities are not equidistant from the center of the weight.

<note>Diagram of a weighted board and lever mechanism</note>

### Example

For example <note>original: "verbigratia"</note>, let **a b** be a piece of a ruler which rests only at the extremity **a**, while the rest remains suspended. This would be impossible to do unless you first unite and join with it the weight **c b**, which acts as such a counterweight that **a** remains in the middle between **c** and **b**. This weight will then be seen to steady itself upon the pole **a**, and the instrument below is subjected to a similar logic.

<note>Geometric diagram of a frame labeled A, B, C, D</note>

### On Force

Every bent body diminishes in its length, and that which is less bent will be straightened with less force, lifting and pushing every heavy and powerful obstacle from its site.

<note>Sketch of a lever mechanism lifting a block</note>

### Example

Take a rod and place it somewhat by force between the floor <note>original: "solaro"</note> where you stand and the beam above, in such a way that if the rod is 8 braccia <term>braccia: an Italian Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm (approx. 23 inches)</term> long, you have bent it by 2 inches. If you then move said rod crosswise to its bend, you will see the entire floor and the walls tremble.

### On the Nature of the Lever

10 pounds at the extremity of a lever performs as much as 20 at the middle, and as much as 40 at the quarter-mark.

I ask: which of these two weights will give greater detriment <note>In this context, "detriment" refers to the strain or pressure felt by the supporting hand.</note> to the hand placed at **a**?

<note>Diagram of a balance beam with hanging weights labeled 10</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the principles of equilibrium and center of gravity, demonstrating how counterweights can stabilize irregular shapes. He also examines the immense force generated by the tension of bent wood and the mathematical proportions of leverage.</summary>

# CENTER OF GRAVITY — EQUILIBRIUM — FORCE — LEVER.

## ON THE CENTER OF WEIGHT.

— A unified weight that is supported by its center, with the remainder suspended, can be of any strange shape whatever, for it will always establish itself upon its support in equilibrium; sometimes the extremities will not be found at an equal distance from the center of the weight.

### EXAMPLE.

— Thus, let **a b** be a piece of a ruler that rests only by the extremity **a**, the rest being suspended; this is impossible to do, unless you first unite and attach to this ruler the weight **c b**, which creates such a counterweight that **a** remains in the middle, between **c** and **b**; and this weight will come to rest on the pole **a e** [and the center of gravity will be at **a e**]. The instrument below is subject to a similar logic [in the second figure the same equilibrium is obtained by placing the weight in the opposite direction, underneath]¹.

## ON FORCE.

— Every body that bends diminishes its length, and that which is less bent will rise and straighten itself with less force, pushing away any heavy and powerful obstacle that opposes its return to its place.

### EXAMPLE.

— Let there be a staff that you place with some effort between the floor where you set your feet and the beam above [and the ceiling]; if the staff is 8 braccia <term>braccia: an Italian Renaissance unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches</term> long, and you bend it by two fingers' width and move it from the side opposite to where it bends, you will see the entire floor and the walls tremble.

## ON THE NATURE OF THE LEVER.

— 10 pounds at the extremity of a lever do as much as 20 in the middle and as much as 40 at the quarter-mark.

— I ask which of the two weights will do more harm to the hand placed at **a**.

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

->**force WHAT IS FORCE**<-

— force is *a way of saying* <note>original: "v dicho" (un modo di dire)</note> it is a spiritual virtue, an invisible power, which is caused by accidental external violence through motion, and is placed and infused into bodies that are withdrawn and bent from their natural use. Giving to these an active life of marvelous power, it constrains all created things to a change of form and position. It runs with fury toward its desired death, and it goes on diversifying itself through its causes: slowness makes it great, and swiftness makes it weak. It is born through violence and dies through liberty; and the greater it is, the sooner it dies and consumes itself. It drives away with fury whatever opposes its destruction; it desires to conquer and kill its cause, its opposition, and *in conquering it dies*. It kills itself; it becomes more powerful where it finds greater opposition, and drives away with fury whatever opposes its death. Everything willingly flees its own death. Being constrained, it constrains everything. Nothing moves without it. The body where it joins is born; it does not grow in weight nor in form. No motion made by it is durable. It increases through labor and fails through rest. The body where it is constrained is deprived of liberty. And often, through motion, it generates a new force within itself.

— force is caused by motion and infused into weight; likewise, the blow <term>colpo: the impact or percussion</term> is caused by motion infused into weight.
— force is the cause of motion, and motion is the cause of force; and motion infuses force and the blow into the weight by means of the object.
— force, in any effect, when it undoes itself, transfers itself into that body which flees before it, and generates through movement a blow of greater efficacy, and afterwards it ruins itself, as appears in the motion of the ball <note>original: "balotta" (cannonball or bullet)</note> which is driven by the force of the bombard <term>bombarda: an early type of cannon</term>.

— force does not extend itself except in 3 offices <note>functions or roles</note> which contain infinite others; these 3 offices are pulling, pushing, and stopping. And said force can be born in 2 different motions: the first is by the sudden increase of a rare body into a dense one, like *the increase and multiplication* of the fire in the bombard, which, not finding a vacuum receptive to its increase, runs with fury to a more ample space, driving away every obstacle that opposes its desire; and the same is done by the course of water and wind, which drives away every obstacle that opposes it.

— The second is that which is created in bodies bent out of their nature, such as the crossbow <note>original: "balestro"</note> or other similar machines, which, once loaded—and they allow themselves to be bent unwillingly—and being loaded, they desire to straighten themselves; and as soon as liberty is given to them, they drive away with fury that thing which opposed their course.
— the blow is born in the dying of motion, and motion through the death of force.

### [Printed Transcription]

— **force WHAT IS FORCE**
— force is *a way of saying* it is a spiritual virtue, an invisible power, which is caused by accidental external violence through motion, and is placed and infused into bodies that are withdrawn and bent from their natural use. Giving to these an active life of marvelous power, it constrains all created things to a change of form and position. It is born through violence and dies through liberty; (and the greater it is, the sooner it dies and consumes itself). It drives away with fury whatever opposes its destruction; it desires to conquer and kill its cause, its opposition, and *in conquering it dies*. It kills itself; it becomes more powerful where it finds greater opposition, and drives away with fury whatever opposes its death. Everything willingly flees its own death. Being constrained, it constrains everything. Nothing moves without it. The body where it joins is born; it does not grow in weight nor in form. No motion made by it is durable. It increases through labor and fails through rest. The body where it is constrained is deprived of liberty. And often, through motion, it generates a new force within itself.

And said force can be born in 2 different motions: the first is by the sudden increase of a rare body into a dense one, like *the increase and multiplication* of the fire in the bombard, which, not finding a vacuum receptive to its increase, runs with fury to a more ample space, driving away every obstacle that opposes its desire; and this same thing is done by the course of water and wind, which drives away every obstacle that opposes it.

— the blow is born in the dying of motion, and motion through the death of force.

<summary>Leonardo defines force as an "invisible power" or "spiritual virtue" that governs the movement and transformation of matter, explaining its origins in the expansion of fire (combustion) and the tension of bent materials (elasticity). He describes force as a paradoxical entity that seeks its own destruction through the very motion it creates.</summary>

# FORCE — BLOW — MOTION — WEIGHT.

### Force. WHAT FORCE IS.

— I say that force is a spiritual virtue <note>In Leonardo's time, "spiritual virtue" referred to an immaterial, non-physical power or energy, rather than something religious.</note>, an invisible power and *which is*, by means of an external accidental violence, caused by motion, introduced and infused into bodies, which find themselves pulled and diverted from their natural habit; it gives them an active life of a marvelous power, it constrains all created things to change their form and place, runs with fury to its desired death, and goes on diversifying itself according to its causes. Slowness makes it great and speed makes it weak; it is born by violence and dies by liberty. And the greater it is, the faster it dies and consumes itself. It chases with fury that which opposes its destruction, desires to conquer and kill the cause of that which stands in its way *and dies and*, conquering, kills itself. It becomes more powerful upon finding greater obstacles *and chases with fury that which opposes its death*. Every thing willingly flees its death. Every thing that is constrained constrains in turn. Nothing moves without it [without force]. The body where *it adds itself* it is born grows neither in weight nor in form. No motion made by it is durable. It grows in labors and disappears through rest. The body to which it is imposed no longer has liberty. And often it engenders *itself*, by means of motion, a new force.

— Force is caused by motion and infused into weight. And likewise the blow <term>coup: the impact or percussion</term> is caused by motion in the infused weight.

— Force is the cause of motion, and motion is the cause of force. Motion infuses force and the blow into the weight, by means of the object.

— Force, in some effect [in certain effects?], when it is lost, transmits itself to the body which flees forward and engenders, by means of motion, the blow [which is] of greater effect, leaving destruction behind it, as is seen in the motion of the ball which is driven by the force of the bombard <note>A "bombard" is an early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th to 17th centuries.</note>.

— Force has only three offices, which comprise an infinity of them. These three offices are: pulling, pushing, and stopping. This force can be born in 2 different motions. — And first it can come by the sudden increase of a rare body within a dense body, like the increase and multiplication of fire in the bombard. This fire, not finding itself in a vacuum that might receive its increase, runs with fury toward a more ample place, expelling everything that opposes its desire. And it is thus also with the course of water and wind, which drive away everything that stands in their way.

— In the second place comes that which is created in bodies bent and twisted against their nature, like the crossbow or other similar machines which, after one has loaded them, do not willingly allow themselves to be bent, and which, when they are loaded, desire to straighten themselves and expel with fury, as soon as the liberty to do so is given to them, the thing which opposed their course.

— The blow is born in the death of motion and motion is born from the death of force.

<summary>This page provides a philosophical and mechanical definition of "force" as an invisible power generated by motion and violence. Leonardo explains how force operates in artillery and tension-based machines like crossbows, concluding with the cyclical relationship between force, motion, and impact.</summary>

<keywords>force, motion, weight, percussion, spiritual virtue, bombard, crossbow, mechanics, physics of motion</keywords>
