<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic breakdown of the four powers of nature—weight, force, motion, and the blow—which he collectively terms "violence." It builds directly on the definitions of force and impetus from the previous folio.</meta>

# — FOLIO 35 (recto). —

<margin>35</margin> **Violence** is composed of **weight** <note>Leonardo uses "violence" to describe any physical action that forces a body out of its natural state of rest or natural motion.</note> in the manner of 4 things: namely, of weight, of force, motion, and the blow. And some say that violence is composed of 3 passions: namely force, motion, and the blow. And that which is most powerful has the shortest life, which is the blow. Second is force. Third, through weakness, would be motion. And if one were to include weight in this number, it is the weakest and most eternal <note>By "eternal," Leonardo means that weight is a constant, natural property, unlike the fleeting "accidental" nature of a blow or force.</note> of any of the aforementioned.

Weight is entirely present throughout its perpendicular opposition, and it is entirely in every degree of that [line].
The oblique [line] is a free opposition; it will not offer resistance to the descent of the weight; rather, it will fall to ruin together with it.

Every weight desires to descend to the center by the shortest way, and where there is greater ponderosity <term>ponderosity: the quality of being heavy or having mass</term>, there is greater desire. And that thing which weighs more, being free, falls more quickly. And that opposition which is less oblique offers it more resistance. But weight, by its nature, passes through all its support, and thus, penetrating from support to support, it passes and burdens from body to body until it satisfies its desire. Necessity pulls it and abundance <note>original: "douitia" (dovizia), here implying a surplus of pressure or gravitational pull</note> drives it. And it is entirely present throughout its perpendicular opposition and entirely in every degree of it. And that opposition which is more oblique will not offer resistance to the descent, but being free, it will fall together with it. In the office of pressing and burdening, it is similar to force. Weight shall be overcome by force, just as force is by weight. Weight by itself is visible without force, and force is invisible without weight. If weight has no neighbor, it seeks one with fury, and force drives it away with fury. If weight desires an immutable location, force willingly flees it. If weight desires firmness, force is always in a desire for flight. Weight by itself is without fatigue, and force is never without it. Weight, the more it falls, the more it increases; and force, the more it falls, the more it diminishes. If one is eternal, the other is mortal. Weight is natural and force is accidental. Weight desires stability and permanence, and force—force desires flight and its own death. Weight, force, and the blow, in the act of pressing, have a similarity among them.

If **a. b. c. d.** <note>Referring to the diagram of the support structure</note> support the planks placed above them weighing four hundred pounds, and you remove half of the base, namely **b. c.**, where before that partition **a. d.** supported two hundred pounds, the weight there will afterwards double and become 400.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the relationship between weight, force, motion, and impact, defining weight as a "natural" and "eternal" desire for the center, while force is an "accidental" and "mortal" power. He also provides a practical demonstration of load distribution when structural supports are removed.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, gravity, violence, force, weight distribution, physics of impact, structural supports</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's mechanical investigations into the fundamental principles of physics, specifically the hierarchy of physical forces and their practical application in engineering.</meta>

# WEIGHT — FORCE — MOVEMENT — IMPACT.

### ON WEIGHT, FORCE, MOVEMENT, AND IMPACT.

— Weight always presses upon its support; it penetrates and passes by the nature of *support* from the structures to their bases; it is present in its entirety throughout the support, in its entirety throughout the base of that support, and in its entirety throughout the foundation of the base, penetrating from support to support until it reaches the center of the world <note>The "center of the world" refers to the center of the Earth, the point toward which all heavy bodies were believed to gravitate in pre-Newtonian physics</note>.

— Weight always presses its support; force is lacking in the body where it *resides* is born. Movement weakens and consumes itself in its course; the impact <note>original: "coup"; refers to the sudden application of force or a strike</note> dies as soon as it is born, leaving its cause stained [?].

— Weight, which acts eternally in the pressure it exerts, is of lesser power than the three other passions <note>The author uses "passions" in the classical sense of "undergoing" or "being acted upon," describing physical states</note> that are within it—that is to say, *movement* force, movement, and impact. The second thing of second permanence is force, which is more powerful than weight, and its office <note>its function or duration of action</note> lasts less time. The third permanence is movement, which is of greater power than force and is engendered by it. *The third of least* The fourth thing of least permanence is impact, which is the son of movement and the grandson of force; and all are born from weight.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF WEIGHT FOR EFFECTIVE PRESSURE.

— If two weights are equal, and one is round while the other is long, and they touch as much ground as one another, they will press as much as one another.

— If two weights are equal, but one of them has—with a thickness equal to the other—a height twice as great, it will press its support once more than the lower weight.

— Two weights equal *although* in base and in weight [heaviness], but of dissimilar proportions, will nevertheless exert an equal pressure.

— A weight whose height fits several times into its width will damage and press less [than another] upon the ground that supports it.

— A weight whose width fits a great many times into its height will sink into the ground more than any other.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF FORCE FOR EFFECTIVE PULLING AND PUSHING.

— The more force extends from wheel to wheel, from lever to lever, or from screw to screw, the more powerful and slow it becomes.

— If two forces are produced by the same movement and by the same force, the one that consumes the most *time* will have more power than any other. And one force will be more powerful weak than another in proportion to how much the time of one fits into the *time* that of the other.

<summary>This page outlines a hierarchical relationship between weight, force, movement, and impact, while providing practical observations on how the shape and duration of a force affect its power and pressure.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, physics, weight, force, movement, impact, pressure, levers, engineering principles</keywords>

# FOLIO 36 (recto).

<margin>
— iron — iron <note>original: "ferro"; likely a reference to the material of the projectile or tool being discussed</note>
</margin>

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF MOTION, THAT IS, OF THE MOVED OBJECT
— The moved object, the less resistance it finds through the air, the further it extends its course. A long and sharp body of pyramidal proportion will go <gloss>will go</gloss> further than a round body of equal weight.

### ON THE CAUSE OF MOTION
— That cause which moves, the swifter it is, the further it will drive the moved object from itself. And a medium speed will drive its weight just as far—even if it is very powerful—as a weak and swift strength will drive its proportionate weight.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE STRIKE
— Regarding a strike made by the same weight, motion, and force: that which makes the greater noise will cause the lesser injury to its object. Take the example of the sword: if it is struck flat against a table, it makes a great noise and little damage; but that which is struck with the edge will make little noise and much, much damage.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE STRUCK OBJECT
— That object which offers more resistance to the motion and the strike will be more injured by it. This is because if the object moves when it is struck, it accompanies the course of the striking thing and remains uninjured in any part. Take the example of the axe when you want to cut the tops of the <insert>p</insert> tips of trees that bend; they are not injured. But if those same tips were cut against a <insert>ch</insert> pumpkin <gloss>? — pumpkin?</gloss> <note>Leonardo uses "zucca" (pumpkin/gourd) as a metaphor for a solid, unyielding backing</note>, they would be immediately severed. Likewise, the hay-scythe will not cut the grass at the tips, but cuts them at their base.

### ON THE STRIKE
— If two bodies are struck, being of the same material, weight, and motion: that which is wider will produce more noise and more injury, and the other less.

### ON THE STRIKE
— A small weight fallen from a height damages its object as much as a medium weight fallen from a low height.

### ON NOISE
— Whether the noise made by the bombard <note>An early type of cannon</note> is made in the trumpet <note>The barrel</note>, or in the mouth, or by the percussion that the fire makes in the air.

### ON SOUND
— Whether the sound made in the air by motion is caused by the moved object or by the air.

### WHETHER A RARE BODY STRUCK WITHIN A RARE MEDIUM WILL MAKE A SOUND
— A rare <note>In this context, "rare" means thin, porous, or low-density, like air or flame</note> body moved with fury, dashed and struck against another rare medium, will make a clatter, just as is heard from the fire exiting the bombard and striking the air.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the shape of an object affects its flight through air and how the resistance of a target dictates the damage it receives. He also questions the acoustic origins of sound, specifically regarding the blast of a cannon.</summary>

# MOVEMENT — STRIKE — SOUND.

<margin>36</margin>

### — IRON. — IRON¹. <note>original: "FER. — FER." These words accompany two figures at the top of the manuscript page.</note>

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF MOTION, THAT IS, OF THE MOVED OBJECT.
— The less resistance the moved object finds in the air, the further it goes. A long and pointed body of pyramidal proportion [shape] will go further than a round body of equal weight.

### ON THE CAUSE OF MOTION.
— The faster *this* cause that moves is, the further it will drive the moved object from itself; and a moderate speed, although very strong [although caused by a great force], will drive its weight as much as a weak but fast force will drive a weight in proportion to it.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE STRIKE.
— If two strikes are given with the same weight, motion, and force, the one that makes the most noise will do the least harm to its object. Take the example of the sword which, if made to strike with the flat side upon a table, will make a great noise and little harm; and if that same table is struck with the edge, it will make little noise and much harm.

### ON THE DISPOSITION OF THE STRUCK OBJECT.
— The thing that offers the most resistance *to the movement* to the strike is the one that will be most damaged by it; indeed, if the object moves when it is struck, it accompanies the course of the striking object and is not damaged in any part. Take the example of the axe: when you want to cut the tops of the crowns of trees, they bend and are not damaged, whereas if these crowns are cut on a [?—block of wood?]² they will be immediately severed. Likewise, the hay-scythe will not cut the grass from the top, but it cuts them near their base.

### ON THE STRIKE.
— If two bodies of the same material, weight, and motion strike, it is the widest one that will make the most noise and the most [the least] harm³ *and the other the least*.

### ON THE STRIKE.
— A small weight fallen from a height damages its object as much as a moderate weight fallen from a low height.

### ON THE NOISE.
— Whether the noise made by the bombard <term>bombard: an early type of cannon or mortar</term> takes place in the soul <note>original: "l'âme"; likely referring to the bore or internal chamber</note> or in the mouth, or in the clash of the fire with the air.

### ON THE SOUND.
— Whether the sound produced in the air by movement is caused by the moved object or by the air.

### WHETHER A THIN BODY STRUCK AGAINST ANOTHER THIN BODY PRODUCES A SOUND.
— A thin body <note>original: "corps rare"; referring to a low-density or porous substance, or perhaps a fluid like air/fire</note> moved with fury, which is struck and reflected against another thin [body], will make a great noise, as is heard in the clash of the fire exiting the bombard with the air.

2. The Italian word *zucca* usually means: *pumpkin, gourd, or squash*. <note>The translator suggests "block of wood" as a logical substitute for a firm backing, though Leonardo literally wrote "pumpkin."</note>
3. The word *more* instead of the word *less* (harm) is evidently the result of a distraction.

He also questions the acoustic origins of gunfire, debating whether sound arises from the weapon itself or the displacement of air.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical mechanics of strikes and motion on the previous folio to the mathematical principles of optics and linear perspective, specifically the "visual pyramid."</meta>

<header>— FOLIO 36 (verso). —</header>

<note>Perspective diagram showing a vertical plane and rays of sight projecting to various points.</note>

All things send their <term>similitude</term><note>The "species" or formal likeness of an object that travels through the air to the eye.</note> to the eye by means of a <term>pyramid</term><note>The "visual pyramid" is the cone of light rays with the object as the base and the eye as the apex.</note>. The closer to the eye these pyramids are intersected, the smaller the similitude of their cause appears. Therefore, you shall intersect the pyramid with the <term>wall</term><note>original: "pariete." In perspective theory, this refers to the transparent vertical plane or "window" upon which the artist draws.</note> so that it touches the base of said pyramid, as is shown in the wall *a. n.*

<note>Geometric diagram illustrating convergence of lines from an eye point.</note>

The eye *f* and the eye *t* are one and the same thing, but the eye *t* [f] denotes the distance—that is, how far you stand to see the thing—and the eye *t* demonstrates the directness—that is, whether you are in the middle, or to one side, or at the corner of the thing you are looking at. And remember that the eye *f* and the eye *t* should always be situated at one and the same height as each other; <term>for example</term><note>original: "verbigratia."</note>, if you lower or raise the eye of the distance *f*, you must do the same for the eye of the directness *t*. And it is done thus: if point *f* shows how far the eye is from the <term>square</term><note>original: "quadro." The picture plane or the object being viewed.</note> but does not show at which part it is opposite, and likewise if point *t* shows the opposite point but not the distance, then to know both, you shall make one with the other and they will be one and the same thing.

<note>Detailed perspective study of a square plane (quadro) with rays of sight originating from an elevated point, showing the principle of the horizon.</note>

If the eye *f* sees a perfect square, each of whose sides is similar to the space between *s. c.*, and at the start of that face which is turned toward the eye a rod or other straight thing is established as appears in *r. s.*, which is fixed by a perpendicular line: I say that if you look at the side face which is toward you, it will strike at the base of the wall *r. s.*, and if you look at the second opposite face, it will appear to you to rise to the height of the wall at point *n*. Therefore, by this demonstration, you can understand that if the eye is higher than infinite things placed upon a plane one after the other, the further they recede, the more they rise, until they meet the height of the eye and the things, and no further. For things placed upon the plane where you place your feet—if said plane were infinite—never pass higher than the eye, because the eye has within itself that point toward which all the pyramids that carry the <term>species</term><note>The visual information or "images" of objects.</note> of the objects to the eye are directed and joined. And this point is always directed with the <term>point of diminution</term><note>The vanishing point on the horizon line.</note>, which appears at the end of the things seen, and from the base of the first pyramid up to the point of diminution — 4 —

---

-> BEGINNING OF PERSPECTIVE <-

— All things send their similitude to the eye by means of a pyramid. The closer to the eye these are intersected, the smaller the similitude of their cause appears. Therefore, you shall intersect the pyramid with the wall so that it touches the base of said pyramid, as is shown in the wall *a. n.*

— The eye *f* and the eye *t* are one and the same thing, but the eye *t* [f] denotes the distance—that is, how far you stand to see the thing—and the eye *t* demonstrates the directness—that is, whether you are in the middle, or to one side, or at the corner of the thing you are looking at. And remember that the eye *f* and the eye *t* should always be situated at one and the same height as each other; for example, if you lower or raise the eye of the distance *f*, you must do the same for the eye of the directness *t*. And it is done thus: if point *f* shows how far the eye is from the square but does not show at which part it is opposite, and likewise if point *t* shows the opposite point but not the distance, then to know both, you shall make one with the other and they will be one and the same thing.

— If the eye *f* sees a perfect square, each of whose sides is similar to the space between *s. c.*, and at the start of that face which is turned toward the eye a rod or other straight thing is established as appears in *r. s.*, which is fixed by a perpendicular line: I say that if you look at the side face which is toward you, it will strike at the base of the wall *r. s.*, and if you look at the second opposite face, it will appear to you to rise to the height of the wall at point *n*. Therefore, by this demonstration, you can understand that if the eye is higher than infinite things placed upon a plane one after the other, the further they recede, the more they rise, until they meet the height of the eye and the things, and no further. For things placed upon the plane where you place your feet—if said plane were infinite—never pass higher than the eye, because the eye has within itself that point toward which all the pyramids that carry the species of the objects to the eye are directed and joined. And this point is always directed with the point of diminution, which appears at the end of the things seen, and from the base of the first pyramid up to the point of diminution — 4 —

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental geometric principles of linear perspective, defining the "visual pyramid" and explaining how objects recede toward a vanishing point at eye level. Leonardo explains the relationship between the viewer's distance, the height of the eye, and the perceived elevation of objects on a horizontal plane.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, visual pyramid, vanishing point, optics, geometry, picture plane, horizon line, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<header>PERSPECTIVE.</header>

## PRINCIPLE OF PERSPECTIVE.

— All things send their image to the eye by means of pyramids; the closer to the eye these are cut, the smaller the image of their cause will be; you shall therefore cut the pyramid with a wall [screen] that touches the base of the pyramid, as is shown with the wall *a n*.

— The eye *f* and the eye *t* are the same thing; but the eye *t* (*f*) indicates the distance, that is to say, from what distance you see the object, while the eye *t* shows the direction, that is to say, whether you are in the middle, or else to one side or the other of the object you are looking at. Remember that the eye *f* and the eye *t* must always be placed at the same height as one another; thus, if you lower or raise the eye of distance *f*, you must do the same for the eye of direction *And let it be done....* If point *f* shows how far the eye is distant from the square and does not show which part it corresponds to by meeting, and if on the other hand point *t* shows the meeting point but does not show the distance, the result will be that to know both, you shall make one with the other, and they are thus the same thing.

— If the eye *f* sees a perfect square of which each side is equal to the space between *s* and *c*, and if at the beginning of the side facing this eye one sets up a staff or other straight object, as seen in *r s*, and this staff is fixed in a perpendicular line, I say that if you look at the side facing you, it will strike [coincide at the bottom with] the base of the wall [of the screen *r s*], and if you look at the second opposite side, it will appear to rise so that its bottom reaches the height *n* of the wall. By this demonstration, you can understand that if the eye is situated higher than an infinity of things on a plain, one behind the other, the further these things recede, the more they will rise, and this continues until they meet the height of the eye *and the things*, but no further. Indeed, things placed on the plain where you set your feet, if said plain is [perfectly] flat, even if there were an infinite number of these things, they would never exceed the height of the eye, because the eye contains within itself the point toward which all the pyramids that carry the <term>species</term><note>The visual forms or "likenesses" of objects traveling through the air.</note> of objects are directed and converge. And this point will always be on the same perpendicular [to the wall] as the <term>point of diminution</term><note>The vanishing point where parallel lines appear to meet at the horizon.</note>, which is seen at the end of the things viewed. From the base of the first pyramid to the point of diminution, — 4 —¹.

# FOLIO 37 (verso).

Regarding the point that comes to the eye, it is understood with greater ease; for if you look into someone's eye, you will see your own likeness <note>original: "similitudine"; Leonardo uses this term to describe the reflected image or visual representation of an object</note> within it. Therefore, if you imagine two lines starting from your ears and running to the ears of the likeness you see of yourself in the other person's eye, you will clearly recognize that those lines narrow in such a way that, shortly after your image is reflected in said eye, they would meet in a single point if continued.

<note>Diagram of perspective and optics showing a tower 'm n' being observed through a measuring rod 'e f'. Lines of sight converge from the object toward an eye/point of observation.</note>

### THE DIMINISHING OF THE PYRAMID <note>The "visual pyramid" is a fundamental concept in Renaissance art theory, suggesting that light travels in a pyramid shape with the object as the base and the eye as the apex.</note>

And if you wished to measure through the air that exists between the thing seen and the eye, you shall do it in this form illustrated below. Let us say that **m. n.** is a tower, and that **e. f.** is a rod <note>original: "uerga"; a measuring stick or staff</note> which you move back and forth until its ends meet the ends of the tower. Then, move it closer to the eye at **c. d.**, and you will see the likeness of the tower appear much smaller, as you see in **r. o.** Then move it closer to the eye and you will see the rod extend beyond the likeness of the tower from **a. b.** and from **t. b.** Then you can recognize that a little further on, the lines converge at a point.

<note>Diagram showing a large horizontal line intersected by vertical and diagonal lines meeting at a central point 'p', representing the geometry of the pyramid of vision.</note>

Every half-base of a pyramid will be as wide as the pyramid is thick at the midpoint of its length, such as **a. b.** is similar to **d. e.** — **m. n.** with **p. q.**

I want to construct a pyramid upon the line **s. t.**, of which I do not tell you the length; but instead, I ask you: what will be the distance between one line and the other at the midpoint of the length that I intend to make?

<note>Small triangular diagram representing a pyramid on base 's t' with an internal parallel line 'p q'.</note>

# PERSPECTIVE (Continued from the front of folio 37).

— 5 — Regarding the point that comes to the eye, it is understood with greater ease; indeed, if you look into someone’s eye, you will see your own image; consequently, if you imagine two lines starting from your ears and meeting at the ears of your image, which you see in the eye of another, you will clearly realize that these lines draw closer to such an extent that if they continue just a little beyond your image seen in that eye, they will touch at a single point.

->THE DIMINISHING OF THE PYRAMID<-

— And if you wish to measure in the air [the space] that lies between the object seen and the eye, you will proceed in the manner illustrated below. Let us suppose that **m n** is a tower and that **e f** is a rod <term>verge: a measuring stick or staff used for sighting</term>. You will move the rod forward and backward until its ends coincide with the ends of the tower <margin>Which occurs, in the figure, at position **e f**.</margin>; you will then bring it closer to the eye at **c d** and you will then see the tower seem *smaller* than it <margin>Seem smaller than the rod.</margin>, as you see in **r o**; you will again bring the rod closer to the eye and you will see the rod exceed the appearance of the tower as **a b** is to **t b**; from then on, you will be able to realize that a little further on, the lines converge at the point [at a point].

— The width of half the base of any pyramid is equal to the width of that pyramid taken at its middle, along its length; thus **a b** is equal to **d e**, and **m n** to **p q**.

— I wish to construct a pyramid on the line **s t**; I do not tell you the length [of the pyramid], but I ask you what the distance will be between the two other lines, at the midpoint of the length I intend to give it.

<summary>Leonardo explains how visual lines of sight converge into a point behind the eye's surface and provides a geometric rule for the proportions of a pyramid's width relative to its midpoint.</summary>

# FOLIO 38 (recto)

### Manuscript Facsimile

<note>Leonardo's text below is written in mirror script; it is transcribed here in standard left-to-right orientation, preserving the original Tuscan dialect and spelling.</note>

<margin>38</margin>

— How the eye sees simply <note>original: "senplicemente"; meaning without the aid of instruments or complex mirrors.</note> the things.

<note>Diagram showing sightlines from three eye positions (s, d, n) towards an arc.</note>

— Simple and natural method, that is, how things appear to the eye without any other medium.

<note>Geometrical diagram of perspective projection from a viewpoint 'm' through a plane 'n o'.</note>

— That thing which is closer to the eye always appears larger than another of equal quality <note>i.e., of equal size</note> that is more <margin>distant</margin>.

— The eye **m**, which sees the spaces **o**, **v**, **x**, recognizes almost no difference from one to the other; and this arises from being close to them. And if one were to lift <insert>record</insert> these spaces onto the wall <term>pariete: a technical term for the flat surface or "picture plane" upon which an image is projected</term> **n o**, the space **o v** will appear on the part of the wall **o r**, and likewise the space **v x** will appear in **r q**. And if you were to put this into practice in some place where one could walk around it, it would seem to you a discordant thing because of the great variation between the space **o r** and **r q**. 

And this results from the eye being so far below <note>or "at such an acute angle to"</note> the wall that the wall foreshortens <note>original: "lischorta"</note>. Therefore, if you indeed wished to put it into practice, it would be necessary for that painting, if it is in perspective <note>original: "prosspettiva"</note>, to be viewed from a single hole which would be at position **m**, or truly that you stand away at least 3 times the size of the thing you see. The wall **o p**, by being always equidistant to the eye, will in one way render things well and fit to be seen from place to place <note>meaning from various vantage points rather than just one fixed hole</note>.

### Printed Transcription

<margin>38</margin>

— How the eye sees simply the things.
— Simple and natural method, that is, how things appear to the eye without any other medium.
— That thing which is closer to the eye always appears larger than another of equal quality that is more <margin>distant</margin>.
— The eye **m**, which sees the spaces **o**, **v**, **x**, recognizes almost no difference from one to the other; and this arises from being close to them. And if one were to lift these spaces onto the wall **n o**, the space **o v** will appear on the part of the wall **o r**, and likewise the space **v x** will appear in **r q**. And if you were to put this into practice in some place where one could walk around it, it would seem to you a discordant thing because of the great variation between the space **o r** and **r q**. And this results from the eye being so far below the wall that the wall foreshortens. Therefore, if you indeed wished to put it into practice, it would be necessary for that painting, if it is in perspective, to be viewed from a single hole which would be at position **m**, or truly that you stand away at least 3 times the size of the thing you see. The wall **o p**, by being always equidistant to the eye, will in one way render things well and fit to be seen from place to place.

<summary>Leonardo explains the geometric distortions that occur when projecting a visual field onto a flat surface (pariete) at a sharp angle. He concludes that for a perspective painting to look natural, the viewer must either look through a fixed peephole or stand at a distance three times the size of the object.</summary>
<keywords>optics, linear perspective, foreshortening, picture plane, visual distortion, viewing distance</keywords>

# PERSPECTIVE — DRAWING.

### — How the eye sees things simply.

### — In what simple and natural way objects, without any other means, appear to the eye.

— The thing that is closest to the eye always appears larger than another thing of the same quality [similar] which is located further away.

— The eye **m**, which sees the spaces **o v**, **v x**, perceives almost no difference between one and the other; this arises from the fact that it is near them <note>original: "per esser visino"; Leonardo likely meant "not near," as the note below suggests</note>; for if one transferred the said spaces onto the wall [the screen] **n o** <margin>Which wall is placed at a short distance from the eye</margin>, the space **o v** would be seen in the part of the wall **o r**, and likewise the space **v x** will be seen in **r q**; and if you put this into practice in some place around which one could walk, it would seem to you a discordant thing, because of the great difference there is between the space **o r** and the space **r q**; this arises from the fact that the eye is so close to the wall that the wall foreshortens them [the intervals]; consequently, if you wanted to put the thing into practice, it would be necessary for the perspective painting to be seen through a single hole which would be at **m**, or else that you stood at a distance of at least three times the size of the thing you are seeing. The wall **o p**, by being everywhere at an equal distance from the eye, will give a good appearance to things and will make them clearly visible from point to point.

---

<note>1. It is clear that it is by error that Leonardo wrote "by being near" instead of "by not being near."</note>
<note>2. [Which wall is placed at a small distance from the eye].</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the distortion that occurs when projecting equal distances onto a flat surface (the picture plane) when the eye is too close, suggesting that a curved wall or a specific viewing distance is required for accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, optics, picture plane, foreshortening, visual perception, Leonardo da Vinci, geometry of vision</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s practical application of perspective theory, moving from the general principles of how the eye perceives distance to a specific technique for creating monumental anamorphic illusions on architectural surfaces.</meta>

# FOLIO 38 (verso).

### — To make a figure on a wall 12 braccia <note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") was a Florentine unit of measurement roughly equal to 23 inches or 58 centimeters, based on the length of an arm.</note> high that appears to be 24 braccia in height.

— Perspective is of such a nature that it makes the flat appear in relief and the relief appear flat.

— If you wish to make a figure or another thing that appears to be 24 braccia in height, you shall do it in this form of figure: first, the wall **m. r.** with half of the man you wish to make; then the other half you shall make in the vault <note>The curved part of a ceiling or arch.</note> **m. n.**

— To make the figure mentioned above, first draw on the floor of a hall the wall in the shape of the actual wall and the vault where you are to make your figure. Then, behind that wall, draw the figure in profile of whatever size you please, and draw all its lines to the viewpoint **f**. And in the manner that they intersect on the wall **m. n.**, so the figure on the wall—which is similar to the drawing—will have all the heights and positions of the figure; and the widths or thicknesses found on the straight wall **m. r.**, you shall make them in their proper form, because the receding of the wall makes the figure diminish by itself. But the figure that goes into the vault, you need to diminish it as if it were straight; which diminution <note>The mathematical reduction of proportions to account for the angle of the viewer's eye.</note> you must do on a very flat floor, and there will be the figure that you will take from the wall **r. n.** with its true thicknesses, and then re-diminish them onto a wall in relief; and this will be a good method.

<note>The diagram illustrates Leonardo's "anamorphic" technique. By projecting a drawing from a single point (f) onto two different surfaces—a vertical wall and a curved ceiling—the artist can trick the viewer's eye into seeing a single, perfectly proportioned giant figure when standing at the specific viewpoint.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes a method for painting a monumental 24-braccia figure across a wall and a ceiling vault using a specific viewpoint to maintain optical proportions. This technique demonstrates his mastery of anamorphic perspective, where distorted drawings appear correct when viewed from a specific spot.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, anamorphism, architectural painting, braccia, optical illusion, geometry of art, vault painting</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical application of perspective theory, specifically addressing the challenge of anamorphic projection—stretching a figure across a curved architectural surface so that it appears proportional from a specific viewpoint.</meta>

# PERSPECTIVE AND DRAWING.

### HOW TO MAKE A FIGURE ON A WALL 12 BRACCIA HIGH, WHICH APPEARS TO BE 24 BRACCIA IN HEIGHT.

— Perspective is of such a nature that it makes what is flat appear in relief and what is in relief appear flat.

— If you wish to make a figure or something else that appears to be 24 braccia <note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") was a traditional Italian unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches, representing the length of an arm.</note> in height, you shall proceed in this manner: first represent the wall [the screen] **m r** equal to half of the man you wish to create, then you shall create the other half within the vault <note>The curved transition between a wall and a ceiling.</note> **m n**.

— To create the aforementioned figure, first draw on the floor of a room the wall of the same shape as the vaulted wall upon which you must make your figure; then draw the figure in profile behind the wall, of whatever size you please, and lead all its lines to the point **f** <note>The "point f" refers to the fixed eye level or vanishing point of the observer.</note>. Then represent on the wall, which is similar to the screen, the manner in which they intersect this screen at **m n**, and you will have all the heights of the figure indicated. For the widths or thicknesses found on the straight part of the wall **m r**, you shall give them their own appearance, for the wall, by receding, diminishes them by itself; but as for the part of the figure that goes into the vault, you must diminish it as if it were straight. For this diminution <term>diminution: the proportional reduction of an object's size in a drawing to create the illusion of depth</term>, you must first make the figure on a perfectly flat room, and the figure you make there will be found on the part **r n** of the screen with its true thicknesses, then diminish it again onto a wall in relief. This will be a good method¹.

---

1. *Treatise on Painting, Manzi edition, p. 317, plate V, fig. 30.* <note>original: "Tratt. del. pit. edit. Manzi, p. 317, tav. V, fig. 30." This refers to the historical 1817 publication of Leonardo's notes by Guglielmo Manzi.</note>

<summary>This page describes a technique for anamorphic projection, explaining how to paint a figure across a flat wall and a curved vault so that it appears twice its actual height and correctly proportioned to the viewer.</summary>

# — FOLIO 39 (verso). —

<note>This page contains a series of geometric studies, primarily focusing on squares, circles, and polygons, some shown in perspective with projection lines. Leonardo's handwriting appears next to a circular diagram.</note>

<note>Geometric diagrams from top to bottom:</note>

1. A square containing diagonals and an internal diamond shape. <note>This is a fundamental exercise in dividing a surface into proportional sub-sections.</note>

2. A square shown in perspective with projection lines extending to a vanishing point on the right.

3. A square with an inscribed octagon or similar polygonal construction.

4. A rectangle or square base shown in perspective with converging lines. <note>Leonardo uses these "orthogonal" lines to show how objects diminish in size as they recede into the distance.</note>

# — FOLIO 40 (recto). —

<note>This folio consists of a series of geometric diagrams illustrating the mathematical principles of linear perspective and the projection of forms. No handwritten annotations are visible on this page of the manuscript facsimile.</note>

### Geometric and Perspective Studies

1. **Fundamental Square Construction**
A diagram showing a square in <term>plan view</term> <note>a "bird's-eye" or flat view from above</note> with its diagonals and internal axes marked. This serves as the basic geometric foundation for more complex projections.

2. **Perspective Projection of a Plane**
A square plane rendered in <term>linear perspective</term>. The lines of recession, known as <term>orthogonals</term>, converge at a single <term>vanishing point</term> on the right, demonstrating how depth is mathematically constructed on a flat surface.

3. **Circle within a Coordinate Grid**
A diagram featuring a circle positioned inside a square grid. This study explores how to map and transform curved forms within a structured coordinate system, a technique used to maintain accuracy when drawing complex shapes.

4. **Perspective Study of a Circular Form**
An ellipse or circle shown within a rectangular frame in perspective. Projection lines extend to a vertex point above, characteristic of studies in <term>optics</term> <note>the science of light and vision</note> and how the eye perceives circular objects at an angle.

5. **Wireframe Cube in Perspective**
A sophisticated wireframe drawing of a cube. The diagram shows both the visible edges and the hidden construction lines receding to a distant vanishing point on the right. This provides a clear visual proof of how three-dimensional volume is represented through geometry.

<summary>This page presents a series of technical diagrams by Leonardo da Vinci demonstrating the mathematical transition from flat geometry to three-dimensional perspective. It focuses on the projection of squares, circles, and cubes toward a vanishing point to simulate depth.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, linear perspective, vanishing point, projection, cube, wireframe, optics, Renaissance mathematics</keywords>

# — FOLIO 40 (verso). —

<margin>
Let no one who is not a <term>mathematician</term><note>Leonardo famously insisted that art must be grounded in mathematical principles, particularly geometry and optics.</note> read me in my principles; let there be no part of them that does not make use of one of those [mathematical] foundations.
</margin>

That thing made in <term>perspective</term><note>The technique of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface.</note> will have the best appearance which is viewed from the same location where its viewpoint was established. 

— If you wish to represent a thing from close up that produces the same effect as natural things, it will be impossible for your perspective not to appear false, with all the deceitful demonstrations and discordant proportions that can be imagined in a wretched work, unless the viewer of said perspective finds themselves with their sight at the exact distance, height, and alignment of the eye—or point—that you established when making said perspective. Therefore, it would be necessary to make a window the size of your face, or rather a hole through which you would look at said work. And if you do this, without any doubt, your work—being well accompanied by <term>shadows</term> and <term>lights</term>—will produce the effect of the natural world, and you will not be able to make yourself believe that those things are painted. Otherwise, do not trouble yourself with it, unless you make your viewpoint at least 20 times as far away as the greatest width and height of the object you are representing; and this will satisfy every viewer situated in any position opposite to said work. —

— If you wish to see the proof briefly, take a piece of wood in the likeness of a small column that is eight <term>thicknesses</term><note>A unit of proportion based on the diameter of the column itself.</note> high, like a column without a base or capital. Then, divide 40 equal spaces upon a flat wall, which correspond to the spaces that would be between 40 columns similar to your small column. Then, let there be fixed 4 <term>braccia</term><note>An Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> away a thin plate of iron which has a hole in the center of those spaces. 4 braccia away from the wall, [place] a thin strip of iron that has in the middle a small round hole the size of a large pearl, and to this hole join a light that touches it. Then go placing your column upon each mark on the wall and mark the shadows; place the shadow and look at them through the said hole in the iron.

<summary>Leonardo explains that perspective paintings only look "real" from a specific viewpoint, suggesting a peephole for close-up works or a 20-fold distance for general viewing. He also describes an experiment using a light source and a metal plate to prove how shadows and proportions shift based on the viewer's position.</summary>

<keywords>perspective, mathematics, optics, shadows, light, viewpoint, artistic theory, experimental method</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s technical instructions on perspective, specifically addressing the distortion that occurs when viewing a painting from the wrong angle and providing a practical experiment to prove his optical theories.</meta>

# PERSPECTIVE AND DRAWING.

— A thing seen in perspective will possess the greatest truth when it is viewed from the exact spot where its viewpoint was established.

— If you wish to represent an object from close up that produces the same effect as natural objects, it is impossible for your perspective not to appear false, with all the deceitful appearances and discordant proportions that can be imagined in a wretched work, unless the viewer of this perspective happens to see from the exact distance, height, and position of the eye—or point—where you placed yourself to create this perspective. Consequently, it would be necessary to make a window the size of your face, or else a hole through which you would look at the said work. If you do this, without any doubt, your work—having a good distribution of light and shadow—will produce the effect of nature, and you will not be able to believe that these things are painted. Otherwise, do not concern yourself with representing anything, unless you take your view from a distance of at least 20 times the greatest width and height of the object you are depicting, in which case your work will satisfy any spectator placed before your work, no matter in what spot.

— If you wish to see the proof of this quickly, take a piece of wood similar to a small column, which is eight times as high as the thickness of the column, without a base or a capital; then divide 40 equal spaces on a flat wall, which correspond to the spaces [of the columns]; these will create among them 40 columns similar to your small column; then let there be established at a distance of 4 <term>brasses</term><note>A "brasse" (or *braccio*) was a historical unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm, approximately 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> — 4¹ — a thin iron plate (?) which has a hole — 4 — facing the middle of these spaces; at four brasses from the wall, a thin strip of iron, in the middle of which is a small round hole the size of a large pearl; you will bring a light close against this hole so that it touches it, you will place your column on each mark on the wall, you will indicate the shadow, you will shade it, and finally you will look through the hole in the iron.

---

1. This number 4 refers to the number 4 which follows the crossed-out words: <note>original: "tola di ferro, etc."</note> *a thin iron plate, etc.*

<summary>Leonardo explains that perspective only looks "true" from a specific viewpoint unless the artist uses a great distance to minimize distortion. He proposes an experiment using a light source and a pinhole to demonstrate how shadows and proportions change based on the observer's position.</summary>

# — FOLIO 41 (recto). —

<note>Manuscript Facsimile Area</note>

<note>Geometric diagram illustrating the optics of perspective. Lines of sight radiate from an apex point 'a' toward a horizontal base 'b', intersecting various parallel planes, including a vertical plane marked 'd'.</note>

— a. b. This is the proof. <note>original: "ella. inpruova"</note>

— A diminished object <note>An object drawn smaller to show distance</note> must be viewed from the same distance, height, and direction that you established for the point of your eye; otherwise, the science <note>The mathematical rules of perspective</note> will not have a good effect.

— And if you do not wish to, or cannot, use such a method—because of the nature of the wall where you are painting, which must be seen by various people—it would require various points, which would make it discordant and false. Therefore, place your viewpoint at a distance at least 10 times the size of the object.

— The smallest error you can make in this case is to place all the foreground objects in their proper form; then, from whatever position you stand, the objects seen will diminish by themselves. However, the spaces found between the bodies will be without reason <note>They will lack mathematical proportion</note>. For if you stand in the middle of a straight line and look at many columns placed upon a single line, you will see that within a few intervals of those columns, the columns touch each other, and after touching, they overlap one another in such a way that the last column will appear only slightly beyond the penultimate one. Therefore, the intervals found between the columns are lost entirely. And if your method of perspective is good, it will produce the same effect; this effect occurs when standing near the line where the columns rest. And this method will be without grace unless the figured object is seen by a small eye <note>A single, fixed vantage point</note> in the center of which your point of sight is placed. If you do this, your work will be perfect and will deceive the viewers, and they will see the columns represented in the form figured below.

<note>Perspective sketch of a series of cylindrical columns viewed head-on. The drawing illustrates how the spacing and visual width of the columns change as they recede from the eye or are viewed from a specific central point, eventually appearing to touch or overlap.</note>

— For these, the eye is in the middle at point **a**, and it is close to the columns. — 4 —

<summary>Leonardo explains that perspective paintings only look "true" from the artist's original vantage point. He notes that for public murals viewed by many people, the artist should set the viewpoint far back to minimize distortion, using a row of columns to illustrate how visual intervals disappear at close range.</summary>

# PERSPECTIVE AND DRAWING.

<margin>4<sup>r</sup></margin>

— a b is there as proof. <note>These words refer to the diagonal line a b in the accompanying figure.</note>

— The diminished object <note>An object rendered smaller to simulate distance</note> must be viewed from the same distance, height, and direction where you placed the point of your eye [the viewpoint]; otherwise, the science <note>The mathematical laws of optics and geometry</note> will not have a good effect. And if you do not wish to, or if you cannot use such a method [rule] because of the wall on which you are painting—which, being seen by many people, would require multiple viewpoints, making your work discordant and false—place yourself at least at a distance 10 times the size of the object.

— The smallest error you can make in this case would be to give all the primary objects their own proper form; then, from whatever place you stand, the objects seen will diminish by themselves; only the spaces found between the bodies will become without reason <note>They will lack logical or mathematical proportion</note>. Indeed, if you stand in the middle of a straight line and look at many columns placed on another line, you will see, after a small number of intervals between the columns, that these columns touch each other, and after having touched, they overlap one another, such that the last column will appear only slightly beyond the second-to-last; thus the intervals found between the columns are entirely lost. And if the manner in which you understood perspective is good, it will produce the same effect as that which one has when standing near the line where the columns are situated; however, it will be without grace if the figured object is not viewed through a small eye [a peephole], in the middle of which your visual point is placed. And if you do this, your work will be perfect and will deceive the spectators <note>A reference to "trompe l'oeil," or the art of tricking the eye into seeing three dimensions on a flat surface</note>. The columns will be represented in the manner figured below.

— The eye is in the middle of these columns at point a and near them.

<summary>Leonardo explains the necessity of a fixed viewpoint for perspective to remain mathematically accurate, warning that without a specific viewing distance, the intervals between objects like columns will appear distorted or vanish entirely. He suggests a practical rule of thumb: placing the viewpoint at ten times the object's size to minimize visual errors for multiple viewers.</summary>

# — FOLIO 41 (verso). —

### [Transcription of Leonardo's Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>The following text is translated from Leonardo da Vinci's mirror script, which he wrote from right-to-left. In this translation, the logic is presented in standard English flow.</note>

If you cannot do this—that is, ensure that the men who look at your work stand at a single fixed point—then draw back with your eye when you are sketching your work at least 20 times the maximum height and width of your work. This <insert>distance</insert> will create so little variation when the eye of the viewer moves that it will hardly be perceived, and it will be highly commendable.
If the point <note>The vantage point or center of vision</note> is at **f**, you shall make the figures placed on the circle **d**, **b**, **e** of one and the same size, each being

<note>Geometric diagram showing a semicircle with labeled points: d, f, a, b, c. It relates to Leonardo's studies on visual perception and the curvature of the field of vision.</note>

near to the point **f** in the same way. Pay attention to the figure below, and you will see why not, and why I will make it smaller at **b** than at **d** and **e**.

It is clearly understood that when placing two equal things between themselves, that which is situated at the third <term>braccio</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement, literally a "arm's length," roughly 23 inches</note> will appear smaller than that which is placed at the second <term>braccio</term>. This matter is more for theoretical dispute than for practical use, because always

<note>Perspective diagram showing a trapezoidal frame containing a curved horizontal arc with labeled points d, b, e. This illustrates the projection of images onto a curved wall or surface.</note>

all the primary objects, whether they be large or small, should be made in their proper form; and if you see them from a distance, they will make their due demonstration <note>They will appear naturally proportioned</note>, and if you see them from nearby, they will diminish by themselves.

Ensure that your <term>pariete</term> <note>The "picture plane" or the flat surface upon which the artist projects the 3D world</note>—upon which you diminish the things seen—is always made of the same shape as the wall where you are to depict the work itself.

---

### [Printed Commentary and Transcription]

— 4 — If you cannot do this: see that the men who look at your work stand in a single point. Draw back with the eye when you figure your work at least 20 times the greatest height and width of your work, and this will make so little variety in the changing of the viewer's eye that it will hardly be understood, and it will be very <term>laudable</term> <note>original: "laldabile" (praiseworthy)</note>.
— If the point is in **f**, you will make the figures placed on the circle **d**, **b**, **e** of one same size, each being near to the point **f**. In the same way, pay mind to the figure below, and you will see why not, and why I will make it smaller in **b** than in **d** and **e**.
— It is clearly understood that by placing 2 things equal among themselves, that which is situated at the third <term>braccio</term> will appear smaller than that which is placed at the second <term>braccio</term>. This thing is more for disputation than for use because always
— All the first things, whether large or small as they may be, make them in their proper form, and if you see them from afar they will make their due demonstration, and if you see them from nearby they will diminish by themselves.
— Make it so that always your <term>pariete</term> <note>picture plane/surface</note> on which you diminish the things seen is made of the same form as the wall where you have to figure the same work.

<note>Vocabulary: picture plane, you diminish, praiseworthy, arm's length (measure), circle, perspective.</note>

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to use a long viewing distance (20 times the work's size) to minimize perspective distortion for viewers moving around a room. He explains that while geometry dictates specific proportions, objects drawn in their "proper form" will naturally appear correct to the human eye from various distances.</summary>

# PERSPECTIVE AND DRAWING (Continued from the front of folio 41).

— 4¹ — If you cannot *make it seen* <note>original: "le faire voir"</note> so that the men who look at your work stand at a single point, draw back when you create your work so that you only see it from a distance of at least 20 times its greatest height or width; and this will create so little variation in the shift of the spectator's eye that it will hardly be understood [and the work will then appear to the spectator, from whatever place he looks at it, so well in agreement that it will create an illusion], and [the work] will be very praiseworthy.

— If the point is at **f**, you shall represent the figures placed on the circumference **d b e** as being of the same size. Each being, in relation to point **f**, in the same manner [each of the points **d** and **e** being at the same distance from the center **f** of the circumference], examine the figure below; you will see that it is not so [you will see that it will not, however, produce that effect] and that I will find myself making it smaller at **b** than at **d** and **e**.

— One understands clearly that if I place two things equal to each other, that which is situated in the third <term>braccio</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm</note> will appear smaller than that which is placed in the second. This matter is more of theory than of practice because always. . . . .

— You will see that all the primary things, whether they be large or small, if you make them in their own form, will present their normal demonstration [their appearance] if you see them from afar, whereas if you see them from nearby, they will diminish by themselves.

— Ensure that your surface <note>original: "paroi"</note>, upon which you diminish the things seen, is always of the same shape as the wall where you have to represent the same work.

---

1. This number 4 corresponds to the number 4 seen at the bottom of the front of folio 41.

# — FOLIO 42 (recto). —

<note>Manuscript facsimile section (Leonardo's mirror script transcribed as normal left-to-right English):</note>

<note>Geometric diagram of a trapezoid labeled 'A'</note>

If a plane is made, and it is shown to me with a mark or a point made upon it at random, and you tell me only whether it is made as a perfect square and not by how many <term>braccia</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches or 58 cm</note>, and [given] the first side, I will be able to tell you how many <term>braccia</term> your sight is distant from that square, and how many <term>braccia</term> away the point made at random in said square is located—which point we shall call **a**—just as appears below in the illustrative figure.

<note>Complex perspective diagram showing a ground plane with points a, r, s, T, a vertical vanishing axis ending in point f, and a lateral distance point g.</note>

Follow line **a-b** and line **d-e**, and where they intersect at **f**, there shall be the height of the eye. And if you wish to know the distance, you shall make the wall <note>original: "pariete" - the vertical plane of the picture</note> **a-n**; then draw the line **c-g**, and where it intersects upon the line **g-f**, there is the point of distance. Then draw the <term>braccia</term> **a, r, s, t, e** to point **f** and to point **g**, and make your finished plane, and you will see where the point **a**, made at random, shall be situated.

<note>Diagram of a circle with internal geometric construction lines, possibly an optical study of the eye or light projection.</note>

If you wished to make a cubic figure <note>original: "figura chuba"</note> as a cubic demonstration in a corner of a wall, first make the thing in its proper form and raise it up on the wall <note>pariete</note> so that it resembles the corner where said body is to be represented.

---

<note>Printed commentary and transcription section:</note>

<margin>42</margin>
— If a plane is made, and it is shown to me with a mark or a point made upon it at random, and you tell me only whether it is made as a perfect square and not by how many <term>braccia</term>, and [given] the first side, I will be able to tell you how many <term>braccia</term> your sight is distant from that square, and how many <term>braccia</term> away the point made at random in said square is located—which point we shall call **a**—just as appears below in the illustrative figure.

And if you wish to know the distance, you shall make the wall **a-n**; then draw the line **c-g**, and where it intersects upon the line **g-f**, there is the point of distance. Then draw the <term>braccia** **a, r, s, t, e** to point **f** and to point **g**, and make your finished plane, and you will see where the point **a**, made at random, shall be situated.

— If you wished to make a cubic figure as a cubic demonstration in a corner of a wall, first make the thing in its proper form and raise it up on the wall so that it resembles the corner where said body is to be represented.

<note>Vocabulary: plane, point, square, braccia (units), sight/view, line, the height of the eye, distance, cubic figure, wall/picture plane</note>

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates how to reverse-engineer a perspective drawing to find the viewer's original distance and eye level. He also provides instructions for projecting a three-dimensional cube onto the corner of a room to create an anamorphic or trompe l'oeil effect.</summary>

# PERSPECTIVE.

— If you make a [square] plane and you show it to me with a mark or a point that has been made there at random, and you tell me only whether it is made as a perfect square or *not*, and by how many <term>braccia</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm, approximately 23 inches</note> the first side measures, I will be able to tell you from how many <term>braccia</term> your sight is distant from that square, and at how many <term>braccia</term> of distance the point made at random in said square is located—a point which we shall suppose to be **a**; you must proceed as it appears in the demonstration figured above.

— Follow the line **a b** and the line **d e** to where they intersect at **f**; there is found the height of the eye. And if you wish to know the distance, you shall make the wall [the screen] <note>original: "la paroi" - referring to the picture plane or the surface upon which the image is projected</note> **a n**, then you shall trace the line **c g**; at its intersection with the line **g f** is the point of distance; then draw the <term>braccia</term> **a r s t e** to point **f** and to point **g**; define the limits of your plane, and you will see where the point **a**, made at random, is situated.

— If you wanted to make a cubic figure of cubic form in the corner of a wall, you would first have to make the thing with its own proper form, then project it onto a wall <note>or "surface"</note> that was similar to the corner where the said body is to be represented.

<note>Vocabulary: Perspective, square plane, braccia, height of the eye, distance point, cubic figure</note>

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for reverse-engineering a perspective drawing to determine the viewer's original distance and eye level. It also briefly touches on anamorphic projection—the technique of painting a 3D shape across a corner so it appears correct from a specific vantage point.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical observations on optics and mechanics, following the previous discussions on light and shadow. The focus shifts here to the practical application of anamorphosis and the physics of simple machines.</meta>

# PERSPECTIVE, DRAWING — SCREW — WEIGHT — WATER.

— If you wish to depict a figure upon a wall, which wall is foreshortened <note>seen at an angle rather than straight on</note>, in such a way that the figure you paint there appears to possess its true form and seems to stand out from the wall, you shall proceed in this manner. Take a thin plate of iron and make a small, round opening; place a light very close to it so that the center of this light touches the opening. Then, place the object or figure that pleases you most against the said wall, so that it touches it, and mark its shadow upon the wall. You shall then shade the drawing and provide it with its highlights. You must then ensure that whoever wishes to view the figure stands at the same opening where the light was originally placed, and you will never be able to convince yourself that the said figure is not detached from the wall. <note>original: "nelmvro" (in the wall). The author likely wrote this by mistake; the opening should be in the iron plate, not the wall itself.</note>

### PERSPECTIVE.

### ON THE PRINCIPLE OF THE SCREW.

— To the extent that the weight is closer to the first degree of ease than to the last, it is that much easier to raise than the last.

— I ask which of these two beams will be *raised* or pulled with the greatest ease.

— Cork. <note>original: "sugero". This label refers to a round object depicted in the original manuscript's diagrams.</note>

— [. . . ?] make it advance at point **a n** by one cubit <term>brasse: an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of outstretched arms</term>, so that by the bending of the crossbow bolt <note>original: "la piega del quadrello". The translation of 'quadrello' as a bolt or square-headed arrow is tentative based on the sketch.</note>, one may see that of **a n**.

— Crossbow bolt [?].

— Test (or: Experiment) whether water flows faster beneath than above.

<summary>This page describes a method for creating anamorphosis—a distorted projection that appears normal when viewed from a specific point—and poses questions regarding the mechanical advantage of screws and the flow of water.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, anamorphosis, optics, mechanics, screw, weight, fluid dynamics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the optical illusions of perspective and the mechanics of screws to the physics of sound and a practical invention for sculptors. It maintains Leonardo’s characteristic style of combining theoretical inquiry with mechanical solutions.</meta>

### ON THE VOICE
— If one instrument is of double the size of another, it will have a double magnitude of voice. <note>Leonardo is exploring the relationship between the physical size of an object and the volume or pitch of the sound it produces.</note>

### ON THE VOICE
— If a voice of double magnitude <note>original: "diduplicata grandeza"</note> is compared to another, will it be heard twice as far away? I say no; for if it were so, two men shouting together would be heard twice as far as one alone. Nevertheless, experience does not confirm this.

### ON THE STATUE
<note>This section describes a method for carving a marble statue based on a clay model, using a frame and measuring rods—a precursor to the modern "pointing machine." The drawing to the right illustrates this apparatus.</note>

— If you wish to make a figure in marble, first make one of clay; when it is finished and dried, place it in a crate that is large enough—after the figure is removed from that place—to receive the block of marble in which you wish to reveal the figure in the likeness of the one in clay. Then, having placed the clay figure in the said crate, have rods <term>bachette: small sticks or rods used for measurement</term> that fit exactly through its holes. Push them in until each white rod touches the figure in different places through each hole. Mark the part of those rods that remains outside the crate with black, and make a counter-mark on the rod and its corresponding hole so that they match at your will. Then, take the clay figure out of the crate and put your piece of marble into it; remove enough of the marble so that all your rods are hidden up to their marks in the said holes. To be able to do this better, make it so that the entire crate can be lifted up, while the bottom of the crate always remains under the marble; in this way, you can remove the stone with your tools with great ease.

— It is impossible that still water should be the cause of its own motion or that of another.

<summary>Leonardo investigates the acoustics of volume and distance before detailing a mechanical invention for sculptors to accurately translate a clay model into marble using a measuring crate. He concludes with a brief observation on the physics of water.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, sculpture, pointing machine, marble carving, measurement, hydraulics, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigations into physical phenomena, moving from the acoustics of the voice to a highly practical mechanical system for sculptors to replicate clay models in marble. It concludes with a brief axiom on the nature of water.</meta>

# ACOUSTICS — SCULPTURE — WATER.

### ON THE VOICE [of sound].

— If an instrument *is larger*, being double the size of another, it produces a double force of voice [of sound]. <note>Leonardo uses "voice" (voix) interchangeably with "sound" to describe the acoustic output of instruments.</note>

### ON THE VOICE [of sound].

— If a voice twice as loud as another is heard, will it carry twice as far? I say no; for if it were so, two men shouting would be heard twice as far as one alone; nevertheless, experience does not confirm this. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that sound intensity does not have a linear relationship with distance in the way he is testing.</note>

### ON STATUES.

— If you wish to make a figure of marble, first make one of clay; when you have finished and dried it, place it in a crate which is large enough—after the figure has been removed—to receive the marble in which you wish to discover a figure similar to the one of clay. After putting the clay figure in the crate, have rods <term>baguettes: measuring sticks used to transfer dimensions</term> that fit exactly through its holes; push them inward, far enough into each hole so that the white rods touch the figure in various places; dye the part of these rods that remains outside the crate black, and make a mark on the rod and its hole which, when you replace the rod, will meet; *and draw it out*—remove the clay figure from the crate and replace it with your block of marble, then remove as much marble as necessary so that all your rods disappear into the holes up to their marks. And, to do this better, make it so the entire crate can be removed, always leaving its base under the marble; in this way, you will be able to remove [the stone] with iron tools with great ease¹.

— It is impossible for still water to be the cause of its own movement or of any other. <note>A fundamental observation on inertia and potential energy; water requires an external force, such as gravity or impact, to move.</note>

---

1. *Treatise on Painting, edited by* Manzi, *p.* 255 (*On the statue*). <note>This refers to the 1817 edition of Leonardo's "Trattato della Pittura" by Guglielmo Manzi.</note>

<summary>Leonardo discusses the physics of sound volume and distance before detailing a mechanical "pointing" method for sculptors to accurately carve marble using a system of measuring rods and a crate. He concludes with a brief note on the stationary nature of water.</summary>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the previous discussion on acoustics and sculpture to a study of dynamics and fluid resistance. Leonardo is investigating the "impetus" of a projectile and how the medium (air) reacts to a body in motion.</meta>

### THE MIDDLE OF THE STRAIGHT PATH TAKEN BY HEAVY BODIES WHICH, THROUGH VIOLENT MOTION, TRAVEL
### THROUGH THE AIR, SHALL BE OF GREATER POWER AND GREATER IMPACT AGAINST ITS OPPOSING OBSTACLE THAN ANY OTHER
### PART OF THAT SAME COURSE. —

— The reason for this is that when the weight <note>original: "peso"; here referring to a projectile or heavy object</note> departs from the force of its mover, although that departure is in the first degree of its power (nevertheless, finding the air without motion), it finds it in the first degree of its resistance. (And although that air is of a greater sum of resistance than the power of the weight pushed into it, nonetheless, by striking a small part of it, it remains the victor). Wherefore, it drives it from its site, and in driving it from the site, it impedes its velocity somewhat. (This air being thus pushed, it pushes and drives other air, and generates behind itself circular movements <note>vortices or eddies</note> of which the weight moved within it is always the center, in the likeness of circles made in water which make a center at the spot struck by the stone). And so, one circle driving the other, the air that is in front of its mover is all prepared for movement along that line, which increases all the more as the weight that drives it presses upon it. Wherefore, finding less resistance from the air, the weight doubles its course with more velocity, in the likeness of a boat pulled through the water, which moves with difficulty in its first motion even though its mover is at its most powerful force; and when that water, with arched waves, begins to take on motion, the boat following that motion finds little resistance, wherefore it moves with more ease. So, similarly, the ball <note>original: "ballotta"; likely a lead bullet or small projectile</note> finding little resistance follows its initiated course until, being somewhat abandoned by the first force, it begins to weaken and decline; wherefore, changing its course, the prepared flight made before it by the fleeing air no longer serves it; and the more it declines, the more it finds various resistances of the air, and it slows down more, until, retaking its natural motion <note>falling due to gravity</note>, it regains more velocity. The boat, in turning, also slows its course. Now I conclude, by the reason of the eighth proportion [proposition?], that that part of the motion found between the first resistance of the air and the beginning of its decline is of greater power, and this is the middle of the path, which is made through the corrected air and a straight line. —

— And because no place can be a vacuum, the place from which the boat departs wishes to close itself again — 4 —

— 4 — in the likeness of a cherry stone pressed by the fingers — it exerts force, and therefore it exerts force and presses and drives the boat. <note>Leonardo uses the analogy of a slippery cherry pit being squeezed between fingers to explain how the closing of the medium behind an object can provide a forward thrust.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of projectiles, arguing that they reach peak power in the middle of their flight because the air in front of them has already been set in motion, reducing resistance. He uses the analogies of a boat in water and a squeezed cherry pit to explain fluid dynamics and the impossibility of a vacuum.</summary>
<keywords>physics, dynamics, air resistance, projectiles, impetus, fluid mechanics, vacuum, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the physics of percussion and ballistics, specifically focusing on how the alignment of a projectile's center of gravity affects the force of impact against a wall.</meta>

# FOLIO 44 (recto).

<note>Diagram showing a bombard (cannon) section on the left and a ball impacting a vertical wall on the right.</note>

That wall which is struck by a <term>bombard</term> <note>A large-caliber medieval cannon used for throwing heavy stone balls.</note> ball will be more or less damaged by it according to whether the part of the ball that strikes is more or less distant from the <term>cause of its motion</term> <note>The "cause" refers to the explosive force or the point of propulsion.</note>.

Whether the point of the ball that strikes is more or less distant from its cause and its motion determines if the blow is more powerful or lighter.

That blow will be more powerful which is furthest from the thing that drives it, because that part of the ball which goes forward is in a direct line with the place that pushed it; and therein it finds itself aligned with the <term>center of weight</term> <note>Center of gravity.</note> and of the <term>flight</term> <note>The momentum or force of the trajectory.</note>. Finding itself equally in the middle of these two powers, they both apply force to it equally. These forces being united, they create a greater percussion and cause greater ruin to the place being struck.

And that blow which is lighter will derive from being closer, because that part of the ball which strikes the wall is closer to the mouth of the bombard. Being close, it is further from the center of the motion and the weight; being outside of this center, the powers and the parts of the powers cannot be equally distant, nor can they load equally upon the part that strikes. Naturally, that part which weighs more pushes more, whereby the ball twists with fury, placing the "after" before the first percussion; that center of flight twists and touches the wall, but because the line of flight twists, the blow will be of little strength.

The greatest percussions of balls of greater power find against them smaller and weaker opposition from the wall. And the smaller ones find greater and stronger resistance.

Because I cannot define it here—due to the space being narrow—look at the two walls figured above, [to see] how equal the greatest line of resistance may be.

<note>Diagram showing a projectile hitting a surface at an angle, with lines indicating the center of gravity and lines of force/flight.</note>
