<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of cannonball impacts, arguing that a projectile's force is maximized when its center of gravity and the line of propulsion are perfectly aligned with the point of impact. He observes that off-center hits cause the ball to "twist" or rotate, dissipating energy and resulting in a weaker blow.</summary>
<keywords>ballistics, percussion, center of gravity, bombard, mechanics of motion, impact force, military engineering</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigations into the physics of motion and impact, specifically focusing on ballistics and the behavior of projectiles (cannonballs) when striking a surface at different angles.</meta>

# Folio 44 (verso)

<note>Facsimile reproduction of a manuscript page by Leonardo da Vinci, containing studies on ballistics and the mechanics of impact. The handwriting is in Leonardo's characteristic right-to-left mirror script, transcribed here in normal orientation.</note>

<note>Diagram showing a projectile (circle) moving along a horizontal line towards a vertical surface.</note>

I say that a blow is at its highest degree of power when it strikes the object between equal angles <note>i.e., at a perpendicular or 90-degree angle</note>.

This effect derives from the fact that when the centers of the powers are in a straight line, the blow is of greater efficacy. The powers that cause this effect are three: namely, force, weight, and motion. And when the front part of the ball strikes the wall and is located on a line between the said centers, it is equally surrounded by the whole sum of the powers; because that part is located on a line with the center of the ball, and that center is equally distant from all parts of the ball. Since these parts are all heavy <term>ponderose: possessing weight or mass</term>, they all exert force at the point of impact.

<note>Sketch of a cannon firing a ball perpendicularly against a wall. A horizontal line connects the cannon's center to the point of impact.</note>

That blow which is caused against the object between angles that are more unequal <note>an oblique or slanted impact</note> will be of greater weakness. And in the rebounding from the said percussion, it will eventually travel with two different motions.

<note>Sketch of a cannon firing a ball at an oblique angle against a wall. Lines indicate the path of the ball and the resulting rotation (spin) caused by the off-center impact.</note>

This blow will be of little damage to the place struck, because the quantity of the weight that remains from the center of the percussion upward is so much greater than that from the center downward, that after the blow it tips over like a balance scale due to the unequal weights. And this tipping shall be the cause of two different movements in the ball, one of much greater velocity than the other: the first will be the due and orderly motion <note>linear momentum</note>, and the second, while moving along the line, will be its rotation within itself <note>spin</note>. This rotation creates a much faster and longer path than the ordinary and straight motion.

<note>Sketch of a long cannon or bombard barrel.</note>

AMONG HEAVY AND SEPARATE PARTS THAT ARE MOVED AT THE SAME TIME BY ONE AND THE SAME CAUSE, THAT WHICH IS OF GREATER WEIGHT WILL TRAVEL A LONGER PATH.

The sound made by the <term>bombarda: an early type of large-caliber cannon</term> is caused by the percussion of the flame against the air. And the more the bombard is braced by its supports, the better it is and the further it drives its ball; just as a man who has his back against a wall and pushes an object with his hands—the more he pushes with his hands, — 4 —
— 4 — the more force he exerts with his back against the wall, because as the arm straightens, it exerts force both upon the man and upon the object.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of cannonball impacts, explaining that perpendicular hits are most powerful because the ball's mass is centered, while angled hits create less damage but cause the ball to spin. He also compares the recoil of a cannon to a man pushing off a wall to explain the necessity of stable bracing.</summary>

<keywords>ballistics, impact, rotation, spin, center of gravity, bombard, recoil, mechanics, geometry of motion</keywords>

ON MOTION AND WEIGHT

<note>Annotation above the top lever diagram:</note>
as of a weight

<note>Annotation within the lever diagram:</note>
(of length)

— Whatever proportion the length of the lever has to its counter-lever, such a proportion you will find in the quality of their weights, and similarly in the slowness of the motion, and in the quality of the path traveled by each of their extremities when they have reached the permanent height of their pole <note>The "pole" refers to the fulcrum or the vertical axis of the lever's rotation.</note> —

THE WEIGHT WHICH PRESSES BENEATH ITSELF A SMALLER QUANTITY OF WATER IN PROPORTION TO ITSELF, WILL DRIVE THAT WATER HIGHER ABOVE ITSELF —

— For example <note>original: "Verbigratia"</note>, if **a. b.** is eleven *braccia* <note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") is an Italian Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm, approximately 23 inches.</note> of stone above one *braccio* of water **b. c.**, the entire *braccio* placed below is pressed by the load placed above; therefore, if one *braccio* of water has 11 *braccia* of stones above it, the water will jump 11 times higher than the *braccio* **e. n.**, which has only one *braccio* of stone. That is, eleven *braccia* of water has above it 11 *braccia* of stone. One could say here: if each *braccio* of water is pressed 11 times less, then by joining together 11 similar pressures, they will satisfy the first proposition; that is, that **e. n.** will throw as much height as **b. c.**, because you are making 11 parts of one thing, just as you are reducing 11 parts into one —

ON MOTION AND WEIGHT

— Whatever proportion the length of the lever has to its counter-lever, such a proportion you will find in the quality of their weights, and similarly in the slowness of the motion, and in the quality of the path traveled by each of their extremities when they have reached the permanent height of their pole —

— For example, if **a. b.** is eleven *braccia* of stone above one *braccio* of water **b. c.**, the entire *braccio* placed below is pressed by the load placed above; therefore, if one *braccio* of water has 11 *braccia* of stones above it, the water will jump 11 times higher than the *braccio* **e. n.**, which has only one *braccio* of stone. That is, eleven *braccia* of water has above it 11 *braccia* of stone. One could say here: if each *braccio* of water is pressed 11 times less, then by joining together 11 similar pressures, they will satisfy the first proposition; that is, that **e. n.** will throw as much height as **b. c.**, because you are making 11 parts of one thing, just as you are reducing 11 parts into one —

# MOVEMENT — WEIGHT — FORCES UNITED AND SEPARATED [WATER PRESSURE].

### ON MOVEMENT AND WEIGHT.

— Whatever proportion the length of the lever has to its counter-lever <note>The "counter-lever" refers to the arm of the lever on the opposite side of the fulcrum.</note>, you will find that same proportion in the quality of their weights, and similarly in the slowness of the movement and in the quality of the path traveled by each of their extremities, when they have reached the permanent height of their pole <term>pole: the fulcrum or central point of support</term>.

->THE WEIGHT WHICH PRESSES BENEATH ITSELF A QUANTITY OF WATER SMALLER IN PROPORTION TO ITSELF, WILL DRIVE THAT WATER HIGHER ABOVE ITSELF.<-

— For example, if **a b** is eleven *braccia* <note>An Italian unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches</note> of stone upon one *braccio* of water **b c**, the entire lower *braccio* is pressed by the superimposed weight; therefore, [consequently], if one *braccio* of water has upon it eleven *braccia* of stone, the water [from **b c**] will jump eleven times higher than that of the *braccio* **e n**, which has upon it only one *braccio* of stone; that is to say [for, in this second hypothesis eleven *braccia* of water *have in them . . .*] have upon them eleven *braccia* of stone. It could be said here, [that:] if each *braccio* of water is pressed eleven times less than by assembling eleven equal pressures, they will satisfy the first proposition; that is to say, that **e n** will raise the water as much as **b c**, because you are doing as much by making eleven parts of one thing as by reducing eleven parts into one thing. . . . .

# — FOLIO 45 (verso). —

### Facsimile

<note>This folio contains studies on the structural mechanics of supports, specifically examining how beams and pillars bend and fail under excessive weight. Leonardo hypothesizes that the center of a support is its weakest point and that failure occurs when the center of gravity of the weight does not align with the center of the support.</note>

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a horizontal beam resting on an anvil-shaped support.</note>
</margin>
->THE MIDDLE OF THE HEIGHT OF SUPPORTS IS MORE ASSAULTED AND MORE OVERCOME BY THE FORCE OF THE EXCESSIVE WEIGHT PLACED UPON THEM THAN ANY OTHER PART —<-

— This is proved by experience; for if you take 3 pieces of wood of equal proportion and plant them in a triangular shape—that is, wide at the foot and narrow at the top—and load them with such weight that makes them bend, you will see the middle will be the first that begins to bend. And the reason is that the weight given from above descends through the whole support and terminates as much at the bottom as at the top; and because, as said above, the weight is "all in all and all in the part" <note>original: "tutto per tutto e tutto nella parte." A common Scholastic philosophical phrasing Leonardo applies here to physical force.</note> of its support, that part which is weakest offers the least resistance. Hence, that part can be called weakest which is furthest from the fixed points—that is, the middle, which is equally distant from the fixed ends, one from the weight and the other from the earth. And if you pull a cord in the manner of a bow <note>Leonardo is comparing the bending wood to a literal archer's bow.</note> fixed at the two ends, you could say that the part of that wood which is furthest from the cord is the weakest, and this is the middle.

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a vertical pillar bending under a rectangular weight marked 'g'.</note>
</margin>
— a — THAT SUPPORT WHICH IS PLACED IN A PERPENDICULAR LINE OUTSIDE THE CENTER OF THE WEIGHT PLACED ABOVE IT WILL DECLINE TOWARD THE GREATER PART OF THAT WEIGHT —

— This also is proved by reason and confirmed by experience. Reason constrains it in this form: that is, if you load a support which is positioned in a perpendicular line in such a way that the center of that support is beneath the center of the weight, it will sooner sink <note>original: "sifichera," meaning to drive into the ground like a stake.</note> than bend, because all parts of the weight correspond to the parts of the resistance. But if the center of the weight is outside the center of its support, it will cause said support to bend.

<margin>
<note>Geometric diagram of a bent beam (arc) with a tension cord.</note>
Experience of the bend in the middle of supports.
</margin>
**IT IS IMPOSSIBLE THAT A SUPPORT WHICH PLACES ITSELF WITH ITS CENTER BENEATH THE WEIGHT PLACED ABOVE IN A PERPENDICULAR LINE CAN EVER BEND; RATHER, IT WILL FIRST PUSH ITS FOUNDATION INTO THE EARTH —**

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a tall vertical beam (labeled 'a') bending under a weight 'g'.</note>
</margin>
— The reason for proposition **a** is that the part of the support found beneath the center of the weight becomes almost a pole <term>pole: a central axis or pivot point</term> for that weight; being so, almost all the force remains there, and because that part of the support which acts as a pole is not surrounded and reinforced <note>original: "armata," literally 'armed' or 'armored'.</note> equally by the whole thickness of the support, that part which is weakest bends.

<summary>Leonardo investigates the physics of structural failure, concluding that supports bend at their furthest point from their anchors (the middle) and that vertical pillars only bend if the load's center of gravity is misaligned with the pillar's axis.</summary>
<keywords>structural mechanics, center of gravity, compression, pillar failure, bending point, resistance, foundations</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the mechanical analysis of structural supports, specifically focusing on the bending of pillars and the alignment of the center of gravity with the support's axis.</meta>

# FORCE OF RESISTANCE AND WEAK POINTS OF SUPPORTS.

->THE MIDDLE OF THE HEIGHT OF SUPPORTS IS MORE ASSAULTED AND MORE OVERCOME BY THE FORCE OF EXCESSIVE WEIGHT THAN ANY OTHER PART.<-

— This is proved by experience. Indeed, if you take three pieces of wood of equal proportion and you plant them in a triangular shape—that is to say, wide at the foot and narrow at the head—then you load them with a weight such that you make them bend, you will see that it is the middle which begins first to bend. And the reason for this is that the given weight from above descends through the whole support and reaches the base as well as the top; for, as was said above, the weight is all in all and all in the part <note>original: "tout en tout et tout dans la partie." This is a recurring concept in Leonardo's physics, suggesting that the force of a weight is present throughout the entire body of the object supporting it.</note> of its support, and the resistance which . . . the part which is weaker offers it less resistance; one can therefore say that the part furthest from the fixed points is that which is weaker, that is to say the middle, which is equally distant from the extremities, fixed, one by the weight, the other by the earth. If you pull a bowstring, you will have it fixed at the two ends, and you could say that the part of the wood which is furthest from the ends of the string is weaker, and that is the middle.

<margin>a¹ <note>In the manuscript, this letter precedes, in the margin, the second proposition, next to the third figure.</note></margin> — THE SUPPORT WHICH IS PLACED UNDER THE WEIGHT IN A PERPENDICULAR LINE, OUTSIDE THE CENTER OF THE WEIGHT, WILL SINK DOWN ON THE SIDE OF THE GREATER PART OF THIS WEIGHT.

— This again is proved by reason and confirmed by experience. Reason constrains [one to admit it] in this manner: If you load a support placed in a perpendicular line, in such a way that the center of this support is under the center of the weight, it will sink in faster than it will bend, *but because* all parts of the weight correspond to the parts of the resistance. But if the center of the weight is outside the center of its support, it will cause the said support to bend.

IT IS IMPOSSIBLE THAT THE SUPPORT WHICH HAS ITS CENTER PLACED UNDER THE CENTER OF THE SUPERIMPOSED WEIGHT BY A PERPENDICULAR LINE CAN EVER BEND, BUT FIRST IT WILL PUSH ITS BASE UNDER THE EARTH.

— The reason for proposition a² <note>See margin note 1.</note> is that the part of the support which is found under the center of the weight comes to be, in a way, the pole <term>pole: point of support or axis</term> of this weight; as soon as it is so, almost all its strength remains with it, and the part of the support which acts as the pole, not being surrounded and armed equally by the whole thickness of this support, its weakest part bends.

<margin>Experience of the bend in the middle of the supports <note>These words are found in the manuscript, in the margin, above the last figure.</note></margin>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the structural failure of vertical pillars, concluding that the middle is the weakest point and that bending occurs primarily when the weight's center of gravity is misaligned with the pillar's axis.</summary>
<keywords>structural mechanics, resistance, center of gravity, pillars, bending, physics, architectural stability</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the strength of materials, specifically focusing on the load-bearing capacity of columns and pillars when their dimensions are doubled or joined together.</meta>

— FOLIO 46 (verso). —

## Manuscript Facsimile

Every support of doubled proportion to a smaller one will support twice as much weight as the smaller one, provided each is united and solid in itself.

<margin>
<note>Diagrams on the right illustrate the structural principles described in the text, showing various configurations of columns and supported weights (marked with numbers like 100, 200, 240).</note>
</margin>

— This reasoning is clearly seen because, although one column may be doubled in the great quantity of another smaller one, and it may appear that the union of the doubling is of greater strength than if they were disunited; nevertheless, because it also increases in height, the weight of the excess height and the length that weakens it—all these contrary factors added together—it comes to pass that, despite the union, it supports no more than exactly as much weight as the smaller one; this is precisely confirmed by experience. —

--- foundations of columns ---

AMONG ALL SUPPORTS OF THE SAME MATERIAL AND THICKNESS, THAT WHICH IS SHORTER WILL SUPPORT AS MUCH MORE WEIGHT THAN THE LONGER ONE AS IT IS SHORTER THAN THE LONGER ONE; and as much more as the weight of the part that exceeds it.

— Although it cannot be demonstrated exactly by number how much the increase of a body of double quantity is compared to another, one can still come somewhat close to the truth. I find that its increase carries 2/3 <note>Leonardo is calculating the proportional strength of a "doubled" column that hasn't been scaled perfectly in all dimensions.</note> of the whole sum of the smaller one, both in height and in diameter. If two columns are joined, one to the other, and their diameter increases by 2/3 of the first thickness, and lacking 1/3 of the due proportion, its support will be of this power: that is, 2/3 more power. And you shall say it in this form: if the first column supports 100 pounds, and the second of double proportion supports 200; and because it does not increase these 2/3 in height as it does in thickness, it is of 1/3 more strength than it would be having its due height according to the smaller column. Wherefore if the smaller... — Wherefore it being 2/3 more powerful than if it were whole (which would support 200), take 2/3 of 200, which is 40 <note>Leonardo's arithmetic here is idiosyncratic; 2/3 of 60 would be 40, suggesting he is calculating a specific fractional bonus to the base 200.</note>, and put it on top of 200, and I say 240 will be the resistance of the doubled and united columns. —

--- foundations

— That power which is found equidistant to two other different powers will resist that weight which is found in the middle by number to the two other extremes supported by the supports. —

IF THE FIRST SUPPORT IS JOINED TO THE SECOND WITHOUT BEING CLAD OR ENCIRCLED, THEY WILL SUPPORT EXACTLY THAT SAME WEIGHT AS BEFORE.

— The reason is this: if a square column is united with another, although the total quantity increases, in the larger face half of its proportion is lost, and it remains on the side in its original weakness; and if the first supported 100, the two in this form will not exceed 200.
--- foundations — foundations —

## RELATIVE STRENGTH OF SUPPORTS.

ANY SUPPORT THAT IS DOUBLE THE PROPORTION [volume] OF A SMALLER ONE WILL CARRY TWICE AS MUCH WEIGHT, PROVIDED EACH OF THESE SUPPORTS IS UNIFIED AND SOLID.

— This reasoning [proposition] is clearly evident, given that, although one column is double another smaller one in quantity, and the forces united as well as doubled appear as though they should be greater than if they were separate; nevertheless, because the column also increases the weight by its height (by that which it exceeds the other column), and because the greater length is a cause of weakness—these contrary effects acting at the same time—the column, we say, despite all the union found therein, comes to support only twice as much weight as the other smaller one; and this is exactly what experience confirms.

— Bases of the columns ¹. <margin>original: "fondamenti delle colonne"</margin>

AMONG ALL SUPPORTS OF THE SAME MATERIAL AND THICKNESS, THE ONE THAT IS SHORTER WILL SUPPORT AS MUCH MORE WEIGHT THAN THE LONGER ONE AS IT IS SHORTER THAN IT, *and by as much more as the part that exceeds it weighs.*

— Although one cannot precisely determine by number what the increase in power of a body of double quantity [volume] is in relation to another, one can nevertheless approach somewhat near the truth. I find that its increase corresponds to 2/5 of the entire sum of the smaller one, both in height and in diameter. And if of two columns one envelops the other, its diameter having grown by 2/5 of the first thickness, and 2/5 being missing from the due proportion, the support is found to be *of that power which is a power increased by* 2/5; here then is what you shall say: If the first column supports 100 pounds, and the second of double proportion supports 200, the increase of 2/5 not bearing upon the height as it does upon the width, it has a force 2/5 greater than it would have if it had its due height in relation to the smaller column; therefore if the small column carried—therefore if it is 2/5 more powerful than it would be if it were whole, and it carried 200 in that case, one must take 2/5 of 200, which is 40, and add them to 200; consequently, I can say that 240 is the resistance of the united and doubled columns.

— *Bases ².* —

*The power that is found equidistant to two other different powers will resist the weight that is found in number in the middle of the two other extremities sustained by the supports.*

IF THE FIRST SUPPORT IS JOINED TO THE SECOND WITHOUT *enveloping or surrounding it*, THESE TWO SUPPORTS WILL SUPPORT PRECISELY THE SAME WEIGHT AS BEFORE.

— Here is the reason. If a rectangular column is joined to another, although half of its largest face merges with that of the other column, it remains by its side in its original weakness; so that, if at first the two columns carried 100 each, they will only carry 200 between the two of them in this new state.

— Bases. — Bases ³. —

---

1. 2. 3. The words "bases of the columns," "bases" (foundations of the columns, foundations) accompany the figures seen in the margin of the facsimile, below the supports, which represent the proportions of the bases of these supports.

<summary>Leonardo explores the mathematical relationship between a column's volume and its load-bearing capacity, noting that increased height introduces weakness that offsets the gains of increased mass. He calculates that a specifically reinforced column can resist 240 units of weight compared to a single column's 100.</summary>

# RELATIVE RESISTANCE OF SUPPORTS TO WEIGHTS.

### ON WEIGHTS.
<note>original: "DE PONDERIBUS"</note>

**IF A WEIGHT OF ONE POUND CAUSES A SUPPORT OF ONE OUNCE TO SINK INTO THE EARTH, HOW MUCH WILL A WEIGHT OF TWO POUNDS CAUSE ANOTHER SIMILAR SUPPORT TO SINK INTO THE SAME EARTH AND IN THE SAME TIME? PERFORM THE PROOF <gloss>the experiment</gloss> IN THIS MANNER:**

— Take a piece of wood and saw it through the middle as accurately as you can; then, use wax to equalize their weights <gloss>the weight of the two pieces</gloss> in the balance, attaching this wax to the one of the two that weighs less; next, take clay used for making jars, handle it, remembering that you must make it uniformly soft and tender; then, flatten it by striking it on a level place; take the two pieces of wood; stand them upright, making them touch the earth with the ends that were originally joined or <insert>and</insert> that the saw separated, so that they are of equal width; secure them at the top by passing them through two equal holes in a thin board <term>ais: a thin wooden plank or board</term>, wider by a thread than the thickness of the wood; then load them, one with a one-pound weight and the other with a two-pound weight; leave them undisturbed for one hour, after having placed them carefully, then you will withdraw them at the same time; finally, measure the holes they have made; but before making a general rule of this case, experiment with it two or three times, and see if the experiments produce the same results.

— EXPERIMENT¹ —

You shall perform the experiment in this manner. — Experiment. — Have 2 iron wires drawn through a square draw-plate <term>filière carrée: a tool used to shape metal wire into a specific gauge or profile</term>; secure one of them at the base with two fasteners, and note the precise moment it begins to bend, using the test of a thread equipped with a counterweight at the base; note with what weight this bending begins, then double the wire into two strands, and unite them with fine silk wound around them; you will realize that this experiment confirms my reasoning <gloss>my sayings</gloss>, and likewise test it with 4 strands <insert>and you can renew the experiment by doubling a second time</insert> and so on, as many times as you please, always binding them with a few turns of silk.

— EXPERIMENT² —

**THE DIAMETER OF A SUPPORT DOUBLE [that] OF ANOTHER WILL CARRY EIGHT TIMES AS MUCH WEIGHT AS THE OTHER DOES, BOTH HAVING THE SAME HEIGHT.**

— This [proposition] is clearly demonstrated, given that if the first support, having in its height eight times its diameter, carries 100, the second, which is squared-doubled <note>meaning its cross-section is doubled in both dimensions, effectively quadrupling the area</note> contains four of the said supports <gloss>four supports identical to the first</gloss>, so that if it also had in its height eight times its diameter, it would carry four times more than the first; but, as it has only four times its diameter <note>because it is twice as thick but the same height</note>, it decreases by half of its due height, and increases by as much again in strength; therefore, if, in the first case, being four times as much as the first support, it would have carried 4 times more than it, it will carry one time more [than that], because it has a double strength, that is to say, it will carry 800 pounds.

***

**1** and **2**. The repeated word: *Experiment*, is placed, in the facsimile, under the first 2 figures.

<vocab>Weights, resistance, supports, experiment, square draw-plate, diameter, height</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines empirical experiments to determine how weight affects the displacement of soft earth and how the physical dimensions of a column—specifically its diameter—drastically increase its load-bearing capacity. Leonardo concludes that doubling a support's diameter while maintaining its height increases its strength eightfold.</summary>

# WEIGHTS — SUPPORTS — LEVER — ARCH.

## ON WEIGHTS. <note>original: "DE PONDERIBUS." This is a traditional Latin title for a treatise on mechanics.</note>

— The suspended body...

— A weight attached at the top and suspended with ropes, or supported from below with beams established with their points of support, ends, and bases in different positions, will distribute a greater portion of its weight to one support than to another based on how much closer one support's point is to the perpendicular line of the center of gravity <term>center of gravity: the point at which the entire weight of a body may be considered as concentrated</term> than the other; that is, being further from the weight than the other—meaning from the perpendicular of the weight—or closer to the center of gravity than the other, and closer to the perpendicular of the center of the neighboring weight.

— In any suspended object that is capable of bending and is of uniform thickness and material, the part that is furthest from the supports will be the part that sinks the most.

— A weight attached to the end of a lever made of any material [no matter how] will lift at the end of the counter-lever <term>counter-lever: the opposite arm of the lever relative to the fulcrum</term> a weight greater than itself by as many times as the counter-lever fits into the length of the lever.

— If an arch, in its tendency to break, exerts a force of 200 pounds <note>original: "livres." A historical unit of weight</note>, oppose it with 200 pounds of weight and force, and it will be stable.

<summary>This page outlines fundamental principles of statics and mechanics, specifically discussing how weight is distributed across supports, the mechanical advantage of levers, and the counter-balancing of forces in arches.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, center of gravity, lever, arch, statics, weight distribution, physics</keywords>

# FOLIO 48 (verso).

-> On the support <- <note>original: "del sosterte<unclear>n</unclear>culo"</note>

A pillar multiplied in thickness will increase in its due power by as much as it lacks in its reasonable height.
Example —
If a pillar ought to be 9 thicknesses high—that is, if it is one <term>braccio</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement, literally an 'arm', roughly 23 inches</note> thick, the rule sets it at 9 braccia—and if you bind 100 of these together by thickness so that it is 10 braccia thick and 9 high; and if the first pillar supported 10,000 pounds; because this second one is only about one thickness high <note>Leonardo is comparing the new total thickness to the height</note> and lacks 8 parts of its length, it will carry eight times more. That is, each bound pillar will happen to support 8 times more than when unbound; so if it previously supported ten thousand pounds, it will now sustain 90,000.

For example <note>original: "Verbi gratia"</note>, if you take a reed that is 100 thicknesses high and place it in a perpendicular line and give it one ounce of weight, it will support it. Then bind 100 reeds together with a continuous binding. These reeds will compose a body of 5 thicknesses <note>Leonardo's math here implies a bundle where the diameter has increased to 5 units</note>. As many times as 5 goes into one hundred, so much more weight will this body support than if it were 100 thicknesses high. And since 5 goes into 100 twenty times, so each of these 100 reeds will support, through said binding, 20 times more weight than before; that is, if it previously carried one ounce, it will then carry 20.

<margin>
<note>Diagrams of columns and bundled reeds marked with letters a, b, c, d, e, f, s.</note>
</margin>

If a spear 100 of its thicknesses long supports 20 pounds, what will one of 5 thicknesses of the same shaft support? As many times as 5 goes into 100, so much less weight will the shaft of 100 thicknesses support than the shaft of 5 thicknesses.

If the reed **e f** supports one ounce and is 100 diameters of its thickness high, **a b**—being also composed of 100 diameters and of 100 reeds—will support 100 ounces, because it is 100 times the reed of the proportion of reed **e f**.

**S**. If you take 100 similar reeds like **e f** and bind them with a tight and continuous binding, because **f e** will be 100 thicknesses and **c d** is 5 thicknesses; 5 enters 100 twenty times, and for this reason each bound reed supports 20 times more than when unbound. Therefore, if **e f** supports one ounce, **c d** supports 2000. <note>100 reeds x 20 times the strength per reed = 2000 ounces.</note>

---

### ON THE SUPPORT

--- The pillar. multiplied. by thickness. will increase. so. much. more. than its. due. power. as. it lacks. of its. reasonable. height.
— example —
— if one. pillar. ought. to be. 9. thicknesses. high. that is. if it. shall be. one. braccio. thick. the rule. sets it. at 9. braccia. if you bind. 100. together. by thickness. let it be. 10 braccia thick and 9 high and if the first. pillar. supported 10,000 pounds. because this. second. is not high except. for about. one thickness and lacking. 8. parts of the length. it will carry. more. eight times that is every. bound pillar. will fall to it to support 8 times more than unbound that is if it first supported ten thousand pounds. now it will sustain 90 thousand.
— For. example. if you take. one. reed. that is. 100. thicknesses high and place it. in a perpendicular line. and give it. one. ounce. of weight. that. will support it. bind. then. 100. reeds together in a continuous. binding. Which reeds compose. a. body. of 5. thicknesses. as. much. as. 5 enters. into. one hundred. so much this body will support. more. weight. than being. of. 100. thicknesses and entering. 5. 20 times into. 100. so these 100 each. reed will support by the said. binding. 20 times more weight. than before. that is if it first supported. one. ounce. it will then support. 20.

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of structural strength, demonstrating that bundling materials (like reeds or pillars) and reducing their relative slenderness significantly increases their load-bearing capacity beyond simple addition.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, structural engineering, pillars, load-bearing, proportions, bundling, material strength, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the mechanics of materials and structural engineering, specifically focusing on the relationship between the height, thickness, and load-bearing capacity of pillars and bundled materials.</meta>

->SUPPORTS (MORE OR LESS ELEVATED, SEPARATE OR JOINED)<-
->THEIR RELATIVE FORCES.<-

ON THE SUPPORT.

— The pillar multiplied by thickness will grow in strength beyond its due force by as much as it is less than *its* reasonable height [the pillar, having become thicker, will grow in strength by as much as it decreases in relative height].

EXAMPLE:

— If a pillar must be nine times its thickness in height—that is, having a thickness of one <term>brasse</term> <note>original: "brasse"; a unit of measurement roughly equivalent to an arm's length or fathom</note>, the rule gives it nine in height—by binding together one hundred such reeds, these will have a thickness of ten <term>brasses</term> and a height of nine; if the first pillar carried 10,000 pounds, the second, being no higher than about once its thickness, and its length being diminished by eight parts, will carry eight times more. That is to say, each bound pillar will find itself carrying eight times more than when it was detached, so that if it first carried 10,000 pounds, it will now carry 90,000.

— For example, if you take a reed that is one hundred times its thickness in height, and you place it in a perpendicular [vertical] line, and you give it one ounce of weight, it will carry it [this will be all it can carry]; then bind one hundred reeds together with a continuous binding, reeds which will compose a body five times its thickness in height. As many times as 5 goes into 100, so much more weight will this body sustain than if it were one hundred times its thickness; and 5 entering 20 times into 100, through these bindings each reed will thus carry, by said binding, twenty times more weight than at first, so that if it first carried one ounce, it will then carry twenty.

— If a lance one hundred times its thickness in length carries 20 pounds, what will one of five thicknesses of the same wood carry? As many times as 5 goes into 100, so much less weight will the stick of one hundred thicknesses carry than the stick of five thicknesses.

— If the reed *e f* carries one ounce and it has one hundred diameters of its thickness in its height, *a b* being also composed of one hundred diameters and one hundred reeds, it will carry one hundred ounces, because 100 times the reed because it is of the proportion of the reed *e f* [because it is in the same ratio with its base as *e f* is with its own].

— If you take one hundred reeds similar to *e f* and you bind them together with a tight and continuous binding, *e f* having one hundred times its thickness and *c d* 5 times its thickness [in its height], 5 entering 20 times into 100, each of the bound reeds carries twenty times more than when it was unbound; therefore, if *e f* carries 1 ounce, *c d* will carry 2,000. <note>Leonardo calculates the total capacity of the bundle: 100 reeds x 20 times the strength = 2,000 ounces.</note>

<vocab>brasse (fathom/arm), pillar, reed, binding, relative force, support, proportion</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the physics of structural supports, demonstrating how bundling materials like reeds or pillars significantly increases their load-bearing capacity by reducing their relative slenderness. Leonardo uses mathematical ratios to show that a shorter, thicker column is exponentially stronger than a tall, thin one.</summary>
<keywords>structural engineering, load-bearing capacity, pillars, reeds, mathematical proportions, compression, architectural mechanics</keywords>

# — FOLIO 49 (recto). —

<note>This page contains a facsimile reproduction of Leonardo's manuscript with drawings of mechanical principles related to leverage and rope strength, accompanied by a modern printed transcription below.</note>

### Manuscript Facsimile Transcription
<note>Leonardo's mirror writing is transcribed here into normal left-to-right English, preserving his original logic and proportional observations.</note>

If a spear of 20 <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia"; a unit of measurement roughly the length of an arm, approx. 23 inches</note> supports 10 pounds, one <term>braccio</term> of a shaft of the same thickness will support 200.
This is because as many times as the short shaft fits into the long one, it sustains that many times more weight than the long one.

If a wooden beam that projects out from a wall by 100 thicknesses supports 10 pounds, what will 100 similar beams support if they are bound and united together with the same projection? I say that if the 100 thicknesses support 10 pounds, then the 5 thicknesses <note>Leonardo is calculating the diameter of a bundle of 100 beams</note> will support 10 times as much as the 100; and if <insert>a b</insert> is 5 thicknesses and supports those 100 beams, it supports 20 thousand.

As many times as a smaller cord fits into a larger one, so much stronger is it than that larger one. <note>Leonardo is likely discussing the relative strength of strands versus the total bulk of a cable.</note>

If one cord supports 100, what will two similar ones support when twisted together?
Whether one single cord, or two single cords, support as much as when they are twisted together or not: I find that by as much as they shorten in the twisting, they are that much more powerful than before.

---

### Printed Commentary/Transcription

49 — If a spear of 20 <term>braccia</term> supports 10 pounds, one <term>braccio</term> of a shaft of the same thickness will support 200.
Because as many times as the *long shaft is [divided], the short one fits into the long one, and sustains that many times more weight than the long one.*

— If a *wooden* beam that projects out from a wall by 100 thicknesses supports 10 pounds, what will 100 similar beams support with the same projection when bound and united together? I say that if the hundred thicknesses support 10 pounds, then the 5 thicknesses will support 10 times as much as the 100; and if <insert>a b</insert> is 5 thicknesses *and supports* those 100 beams, it supports 20 thousand.

— As many times as the smaller cord fits into the larger, so much stronger is it than that larger one.

— If one cord supports 100, what will two similar ones support when twisted together?

— Whether one single cord, or two single cords, support as much as when they are twisted together or not: I find *that by as much as they shorten in the twisting, they are that much more powerful than before.*

<note>Technical vocabulary used: braccia (arms/cubits), libbre (pounds), grosseze (thicknesses/diameters), asste (shafts/poles), muro (wall), chorda (rope/cord), scienpia (single/unplied), avolte (twisted/plied).</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the mathematical proportions of structural strength, concluding that shorter beams are proportionally stronger than long ones and that twisting rope strands increases their power as they shorten.</summary>

## Manuscript Facsimile

<note>Leonardo's mirror writing transcribed into normal left-to-right English. The text is written alongside several architectural diagrams of arches.</note>

On the strength and quality of arches, and where they are strong or weak; and likewise of columns.

— That part of the arch which is flatter will offer less resistance to the weight placed upon it.

— Each half-arch, being overcome by excessive weight, will break at two-thirds of its height, which part corresponds to a perpendicular line above the middle of its base, as appears in **a b**; and this happens because the weight desires to fall and pass through point **r**. And if it desired, contrary to its nature, to fall from point **s**, the arch **n s** would break exactly in its middle. And if the arch **n s** were made of a single piece of wood, the weight placed at **n** would desire to fall toward **m**, and it would break in the middle of the arch **m**. Otherwise, it will break in the upper third at point **a**, because from **a** the arch is flatter than it is from **a** to **o** or **s**, and as much as **p t** is greater than **t n**, so much stronger will **a o** be than **a n**; and similarly, **s o** will be as much stronger than **o a** as **r p** is greater than **p t**.

— That arch which is doubled in the <term>square of its thickness</term><note>Referring to the cross-sectional area</note> will support four times as much weight as the single one supported. And so much more as the diameter of its thickness enters fewer times into its length. That is: if the thickness of the single arch enters 10 times into its length, the thickness of the doubled arch will enter 5 times into its length. Therefore, the thickness of the doubled arch entering its length half as many times as that of the single arch does into its own, it is reasonable that it should carry half as much weight again <note>i.e., an additional factor of 2</note> than would fall to it if it followed the proportion of the single arch. Hence, this arch being doubled, it would seem it should carry 4 times more weight than the single arch, but the aforementioned rule demonstrates that it sustains exactly 8 times as much.

— When the triangle **a z n**, in descending, pushes back 1/3 of each half-arch, namely **a s** and likewise **z m**, the reason is that **a** falls perpendicularly above **b**, and likewise **z** above **f**.

— That pillar which is loaded with more unequal weight will sooner come to ruin.
— Column **c d**, by being loaded with an equal load, will be more permanent; the other two on the outside need as much weight from their center outward as they have from their center inward—that is, from the center of the column to the middle of the arch.

— Arches that stand by force of <term>iron chains</term><note>Tie-rods used to counteract the outward thrust of an arch</note> will not be permanent. The arch will be of much longer duration which has a good counter-resistance to its thrust. The arch by itself desires to fall. And if the arch is 30 <term>braccia</term><note>An Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm</note> and the interval between the walls that support it is 20, we know that 30 will not pass through 20 unless 30 also becomes 30 <note>meaning the arch flattens into a straight line</note>. Thus, the arch being overcome by excessive weight, it straightens itself, and the poorly resistant walls open up and provide a way between their space for the ruin of the arch. But if you do not wish to give the arch its iron cord, you must make such <term>buttresses</term><note>original: "spale" (shoulders)</note> that they offer resistance to its thrust; which thing you shall do by loading the angles **m n** with stones so that the lines of their joints are directed toward the center of the small arch. And the reason that will make the arch permanent will be this: We know clearly that whoever loads the arch in its quarter **a b** with excessive weight, the wall **f g** will be pushed out because the arch will want to straighten itself. And if one loaded the other quarter **b c**, that would pull the wall **f g** inward, were it not for the line of stones **x y** which acts as a support.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mathematical and physical principles of arch stability, calculating how doubling thickness increases load capacity eightfold and explaining how buttressing counteracts lateral thrust.</summary>
<keywords>statics, architectural engineering, arch thrust, structural failure, load-bearing, proportion, tie-rods, buttresses</keywords>

# — FOLIO 50 (recto) —

<note>The following text is translated from Leonardo da Vinci’s famous "mirror writing," which he wrote from right to left.</note>

THAT PART OF THE FOUNDATION OF BUILDINGS WHICH WEIGHS MORE SETTLES MORE, AND LEAVES THE LIGHTER PART HIGHER UP, DISUNITED FROM ITSELF.

AND THAT GROUND WHICH IS MORE PRESSED, BEING POROUS, YIELDS MORE.

— You must always make the foundations so that they project equally beyond the load of their walls and pillars, as appears in **m. a. b.** If you do as many do—that is, making a foundation of equal width up to the surface of the earth, and then placing an unequal load upon it as shown in **b. e.** and in **e. o.**—the part of the foundation **b. e.**, because it is fully occupied by the corner pillar, weighs more and pushes its foundation downward more than the wall **e. o.** does, which does not occupy its foundation entirely. Therefore, it pushes less and settles less. Whereupon, as the pillar **b. e.** settles, it becomes disunited and separates from the wall **e. o.**, as is seen in most buildings that have cracked around such pillars.

### WHAT AN ARCH IS
— An arch is nothing other than a strength caused by two weaknesses. For the arch in buildings is composed of two quarters of a circle; each of these quarter-circles, being very weak by itself, desires to fall. But by opposing each other's ruin, the two weaknesses are converted into a single strength.

### ON THE QUALITY OF THE WEIGHT OF ARCHES
— Once the arch is composed, it remains in equilibrium, for one side pushes the other as much as the other pushes the first. But if one quarter-circle weighs more than the other, the stability is taken away and denied, because the greater weight will overcome the lesser.

### ON THE LOAD GIVEN TO ARCHES
— After the equal weight of the quarter-circles, it is necessary to give them an equal load from above; otherwise, the aforementioned error will be incurred.

### WHERE THE ARCH BREAKS
— The arch will break in that part which passes its midpoint <note>literally "its half"</note> from below the center.

### THE SECOND BREAKING OF THE ARCH
— If an excessive weight is placed in the middle of the arch at point **a.**, that point desires to fall toward **b.**, and it breaks at two-thirds of its height at **c. e.** And it will be as much more powerful in **n.** than in **o. g.** and in **e. a.** as **m. o.** goes into **m. n.** <note>Leonardo is using geometric proportions to calculate the distribution of forces.</note>

### OF ANOTHER CAUSE OF RUIN
— The arch will also fail by being pushed sideways; for when the load is not directed to the feet of the arch, the arch lasts but a short time.

<summary>Leonardo explains that uneven foundation settling causes structural cracking and defines the arch as a "strength caused by two weaknesses" that must be perfectly balanced to remain stable.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, foundations, structural engineering, mechanics of arches, equilibrium, load distribution, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<note>Study of various arch curvatures (semicircular and pointed) and mechanical levers.</note>

An arch with little curvature will be secure in itself, but if it is loaded, the shoulders <note>the "spali" or abutments of the arch</note> must be well reinforced. An arch of great curvature will be weak in itself, and more so if it is loaded; it will cause little trouble to its shoulders, but it will break at points *o* and *p*.

WHY THE BALANCE SCALE DOES NOT PUSH THE GREATER WEIGHT BENEATH ITS PIVOT WHEN PLACED AT ONE OF ITS EXTREMITIES

If the pivot were at the center of the thickness of the balance—as it is along its length, as appears at *b*—and the center of the weight were at the center of the thickness of the balance, the greater weight *d* would fall directly under the center of the balance *b*, and so on.

# — FOLIO 51 (recto). —

<note>The top portion of the page is a facsimile reproduction of a Leonardo da Vinci manuscript folio, featuring mirror-script Italian and technical drawings of arches and beams. The bottom portion is a modern printed transcription of the handwritten text.</note>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

<margin>
It will show itself
to be weaker
in the place where it is
most distant
from its
chord <note>The straight line connecting the two ends of an arch</note>, as appears
in the figure
below.
</margin>

<note>Drawing of two arches showing load distribution and a horizontal beam structure.</note>

Every arch will be weaker the further it is from its chord.
This is shown by experience: every time that the chord *m-n* of the
outer arch *r-a* touches the inner arch *b-x*, the arch will begin
its weakness; and it will become so much weaker as the inner arch
breaks through that chord.

<note>Series of diagrams showing various wooden beam joints and their mechanical response to bending or stress, labeled with letters like r, a, b, s.</note>

<margin>
That arch which
is loaded on one of its sides, the
weight will be charged upon the summit
of the other half, and the weight will pass
all the way to its foundation,
and it will break in that part which is
furthest from its ends
and from its chord.
</margin>

<note>Anatomical study of an arch's curvature on the right with labels m, n, r, a.</note>

---

### [Printed Transcription]

— On protection against earthquakes <note>original: "del riparo aterre moti". Leonardo often considered how architectural forms could resist seismic shocks.</note>

— The arch which transmits the weight perpendicularly to its foundations <note>original: "radice", literally 'roots'</note> will perform its function regardless of whether it is reversed, lying down, or upright.

— The arch will not break if the chord of the outer arches does not touch the inner arch.
This is shown by experience: every time that the chord *a-o-n* of the outer arch *n-r-a* touches the inner arch *x-b-x*, the arch will begin its weakness; and it will become so much weaker as the inner arch breaks through that chord.

— That arch which is loaded on one of its sides, the weight will be charged upon the summit of the other half, and the weight will pass all the way to its foundation, and it will break in that part which is furthest from its ends and from its chord.

<vocab>arch (archo), chord (corda), weakness (deboleza), weight (peso), summit (somita), foundation (fondamento), perpendicular (perpendichulare), against earthquakes (aterre moti)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the structural physics of arches, concluding that an arch's weakness is proportional to its distance from its chord and describing how asymmetrical loads cause structural failure. He also notes the importance of these principles for earthquake-resistant construction.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, structural engineering, arches, geometry, load distribution, earthquakes, physics of materials</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigations into mechanics, specifically focusing on the physics of tension in cords and the mathematical proportions of levers. It follows his typical method of posing a physical paradox and then solving it through geometry and weight distribution.</meta>

- FOLIO 51 (verso). -

# WEIGHTS
<note>original: "DEPONDI" - a Latin-derived term for weights or things that hang.</note>

— It is impossible to straighten a cord whose length is 100 cubits <note>original: "braccia" - a Florentine measure of length, roughly 23 inches.</note> and is suspended between 2 pulleys <note>original: "charruchole"</note> at an interval of 100 cubits, even if at each end there is attached a weight of 1000 pounds. I say that if you attach a weight in the middle of said cord that weighs one hundred pounds, the cord will break with the weight before it rises to straighten itself, its own weight being at **a**. And it seems almost impossible to say that two thousand pounds of weight attached to the ends of the cord should not lift 200 pounds—that is, the weight of the cord and that which is placed in the middle of the cord. The reason for this is that the weight placed in the middle of the cord performs the same function against the counterweight of the 1000 pounds as would an equal weight attached to the extremity of a lever that was 50 cubits long. Therefore, to know the truth of this effect—that is, if it is possible for the weight of 2000 pounds to straighten the cord—measure the diameter of the solid part of the wheel <note>original: "girella" - the sheave or pulley wheel.</note> that sustains the weight of 1000 pounds, and see how many times half of that diameter fits from the center of the wheel to the center of the hundred-pound weight above the line **o. r. a.** And however many times that part of the diameter—that is, **o. r.**—fits, say 200 times up to the top of the middle of the cord, and do the same for the other half, which makes 400. Therefore, 400 times 100 makes forty thousand. And then there is the weight of the cord, which follows the rule of its weight from below; in effect, a cord of great length will not straighten itself unless it breaks.

---

# OF THE LEVER

— The counterweight of the lever, of what thickness <note>meaning mass or weight</note> you shall measure it: thus take the measure of the counter-lever and go dividing and marking it along the lever. That is, if the counter-lever is 1 cubit and the lever is 8, mark 8 cubits on the lever, and let us say that one cubit of the lever weighs one pound. Therefore, the eighth cubit that is found after the seven cubits can be called one pound on 7 cubits of lever, which will come to be sustained by one cubit of counter-lever. It will say that 7 spaces against one space requires 7 of weight in the counter-lever against one pound at the summit of seven spaces of the lever. Therefore, the lever above, which is 8 cubits against one, is 8 pounds against one; so that the first cubit of the lever is discounted by the weight of the cubit of the counter-lever, and take account of the rest up to 7 cubits. And say that the sum of all the numbers of the weight of the 7 cubits weighs 35 pounds; therefore, one pound of weight [at the end] will require [35 pounds] in the counter-lever to stay equal. — 4 —

— here it is shown how the weight <unclear>lifts</unclear> the weight with the lever —

— 4 — But to see the truth of the weight entirely, you know that each cubit by itself in the lever weighs more at its center point which is further from the fulcrum <note>original: "fermamento"</note> of the lever than that which is closer. And to see this exactly, take the middle of the last cubit and attach it to the end of the other half-cubit, and for whatever you find it in weight, put it down as it appears below.

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates that it is physically impossible to pull a long cord perfectly horizontal because the tension required increases exponentially as the angle flattens. He also provides a mathematical breakdown of how weight is distributed across the length of a lever relative to its fulcrum.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, tension, pulleys, levers, weight distribution, physics, geometry, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# FOLIO 52 (recto).

## [Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>Transcription of Leonardo's mirror writing, which explores mechanics of scales and the motion of spherical and conical bodies on inclined planes.</note>

<margin>method of weighing</margin>
You will use this method of weighing in this manner: that is, you will make by experience <note>Leonardo uses "experience" to mean "experiment" or "practical trial"</note> the graduated marks *a, b, c, d*; and by as much as *n* weighs more than *m*, so much will the degrees pass the line *r, f* —

<margin>method of weighing</margin>
If you want to see how much more *g* weighs than *t*, look at how many times *t, s* goes into *s, f*; and just as many times the weight *g* will go into the weight *d* [t] exactly.

<margin>on motion</margin>
A spherical and heavy body will, by its nature, take on a motion that is faster the further its contact point with the place where it runs is from the perpendicular of its central line. <note>Leonardo is describing the relationship between the center of gravity and the point of contact on an inclined plane; the steeper the incline, the further the contact point is from the vertical line passing through the center.</note>

As much as *a, b* goes into *a, c*, and as much as it is longer than *a, c*, so much later will the ball fall along its line than along the line *a, b*. And it will be later by as much as the part *o* is greater or lesser than the part *m*; because, *p* being the pole <note>the point of contact or pivot</note> of the ball, and the part *m* being above *p*, it would fall with faster motion if it were not for that little resistance made against it by the counter-weight of part *o*. And if it were not for the said counter-weight, the ball would descend along the line *a, c* so much faster as *o* goes into *m*; that is, if the part *o* goes into the part *m* 100 times, always lacking in the turning of the ball, the part *o* would descend faster by a hundredth of its <insert>usual</insert> time in *n*. And the central line [mp] and *p* is the pole where the ball touches its plane; and the greater the space from *n* to *p*, the faster its course will be.

A heavy body in the shape of a round pyramid <note>a cone</note> will move around the point of its pyramid *n* when it rolls on an oblique side, and it will not be able to take any other motion than a circular one.

---

## [Printed Commentary]

<margin>52</margin>
### METHOD OF THE BALANCE
— You will use this method of weighing in this manner: that is, you will make by experiment the graduated marks *a, b, c, d*; and by as much as *n* weighs more than *m*, so much will the degrees pass the line *r, f* —

### METHOD OF WEIGHING
— If you want to see how much more *g* weighs than *t*, look at how many times *t, s* goes into *s, f*; and just as many times the weight *g* will go into the weight *d* [t] exactly.

### ON MOTION
— A spherical and heavy body will, by its nature, take on a motion that is faster the further its contact point with the place where it runs is from the perpendicular of its central line.

— As much as *a, b* goes into *a, c*, and as much as it is longer than *a, c*, so much later will the ball fall along its line than along the line *a, b*. And it will be later by as much as the part *o* is greater or lesser than the part *m*; because, *p* being the pole of the ball, and the part *m* being above *p*, it would fall with faster motion if it were not for that little resistance made against it by the counter-weight of part *o*. And if it were not for the said counter-weight, the ball would descend along the line *a, c* so much faster as *o* goes into *m*; that is, if the part *o* goes into the part *m* 100 times, always lacking in the turning of the ball, the part *o* would descend faster by a hundredth of its [usual] time in *n*. And the central line [mp] and *p* is the pole where the ball touches its plane; and the greater the space from *n* to *p*, the faster its course will be.

— A heavy body in the shape of a round pyramid will move around the point of its pyramid *n* when it rolls on an oblique side, and it will not be able to take any other motion than a circular one.

<note>Vocabulary: balance, weighing, motion, spherical body, central line, round pyramid (cone), circular motion</note>

<summary>This page details Leonardo's mathematical approach to weighing objects using graduated scales and his observations on the physics of rolling bodies. He correctly identifies that a cone on an incline must follow a circular path and analyzes the gravitational forces acting on a sphere rolling down a slope.</summary>

# BALANCES — MOTION — WEIGHTS.

### A TYPE OF BALANCE.

— You will employ this method of weighing as follows: after experiment, you will create the decreasing degrees *a b c d*, and by as much as *n* weighs more than *m*, by so much will the degrees pass the line *r f*.

### METHOD OF WEIGHING.

— If you wish to see how much more *g* weighs than *t*, observe how many times *t s* enters into *s f*; exactly that many times the weight *g* will enter into the weight *d* [*t*].

### ON MOTION.

— A spherical and heavy body will take on a more rapid motion in proportion as its contact with the place where it runs is further from the perpendicular of its central line. <note>Leonardo is observing that the speed of a rolling ball increases as the slope of the plane becomes steeper, moving the contact point further from the vertical axis of the sphere.</note>

— By as much as *a b* [*a c*] enters into *a c*, and by as much as it is longer than *a c* [*a b*], by so much more slowly will the ball fall along its line [along the line *a c*] than along the line *a b*. And it will fall more slowly in proportion as the part *o* is larger or smaller than the part *m*; because *p* being the pole <note>original: "pôle" — here meaning the point of contact or the instantaneous center of rotation</note> of the ball, the part *m*, being above *p*, would fall with a more rapid motion if there were not that small resistance made as a counterweight by the part *o*. And if there were not the said counterweight, the ball would descend along the line *a c* faster by as much as *o* enters [more times] into *m*. That is to say, if the part *o* enters into the part *m* 100 times, and the part *o* were missing during the rotation of the ball, it would descend faster by one-hundredth of the ordinary time on *n*. And the central line [*m p*] and *p* [or its extremity *p*] is the pole where the ball touches its plane; and the more space there is between *n* and *p*, the more rapid is its course.

— A heavy body in the shape of a round pyramid <term>cone: Leonardo uses the phrase "pyramide ronde" to describe a cone</term>, will make its motion around [the axis] of its pyramid when it rolls along its oblique side, and it will not be able to take any motion other than a circular [motion]. <note>A cone rolling on a flat surface naturally traces a circular path because the wider base travels further than the narrow tip in a single rotation.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanics of a graduated balance scale and the physics of rolling bodies, explaining how the distribution of mass and the point of contact on an incline affect the speed of a sphere and the path of a cone.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, balances, gravity, inclined plane, rolling motion, spheres, cones, counterweight, center of rotation</keywords>

# ON NOISE — ON WEIGHTS — (SCALES, ETC.)

### ON GREAT NOISE.

— The crash of a bombard <note>A "bombard" was an early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th through 16th centuries.</note> fired over the water will kill all the animals that are found within that water.

### ON NOISE.

— If you take a small vessel or other resonant vase, cover it with a wet calfskin parchment, and then, when it is dry, insert a small waxed cord in this manner: <note>The original manuscript includes a small drawing of the cord here.</note>, and finally pull it back with a glove waxed with a bit of that coating with which bees plug the holes of their hives <note>propolis or bee glue</note>, you will hear a strange noise.

### ON WEIGHTS.

— Equal weights placed at an equal distance from the center or pole <gloss>pivot</gloss> of the scale will maintain the ends of the scale equidistant to the support of the scale; that is to say, if the weights *m* and *n* attached at *c* and *a* are of equal heaviness and equal distance to the pole of the scale *s*, and you move them away from this pole as far as *d* and *b*, if these latter points are at equal distances, the ends of the scale *p* and *r* will remain at an equal distance in relation to *l*.

### ON WEIGHTS.

— If unequal weights are placed at unequal distances on the scale so that it remains level <gloss>horizontal</gloss>, and then these weights are moved away by the same distance, the scale will tip toward the side of the larger weight.

— Thus, if weight *b* weighs one pound and is counterbalanced <gloss>balanced</gloss> by 3 pounds at *c*, the logic <note>original: "raison"</note> is this: If *b* is 3 inches away and *c* is one inch from the pole <gloss>pivot</gloss>, one must ensure that the parts on either side of the pole are equal in total number; that is, if *c* is 4—namely, 3 pounds of weight and 1 unit of space, which makes 4—likewise *b* is 3 units of space and one pound, which makes 4. Having 4 on each side, in both weight and space, the scale will remain level.

— Now, if you wish to move the two weights away from their place in the same manner, the larger weight will pull the arm of the scale down, and the reason is this: If you pull *b* to *a* and likewise *c* to *d*, the spaces from the pole to the weight leave the initial proportion; indeed, *c-o* originally went into *o-b* 3 times; however, by moving the weights to *a* and *d*, *d-o* goes into *o-a* only 2 times and not 3 as before. If one wished to level this scale again, the weight of 3 would have to become 2; then it would be correct; otherwise, the 3 descends.

### ON WEIGHT.

— If 2 men carry a beam up a staircase, and if the wood is of equal thickness and weight, the one who carries from below will feel exactly as much weight as the one who carries from above; if the wood desires to fall toward the center <note>The "center of the earth," as per the Aristotelian view of gravity</note>, all its parts have the same desire, and in wanting to fall straight down <note>original: "à plomb"</note> they produce the aforementioned effect.

— It is impossible for a rope loaded with a weight not to tend toward the center of the earth and for its lowest point not to approach that center. Therefore, if *x* were not located directly under *a-x*, it would be closer to the scale, and that is impossible.

<summary>This page explores the physics of acoustics and the mathematical laws of the lever, demonstrating how balance is maintained through the proportional relationship between weight and distance from a pivot. It also touches upon the uniform pull of gravity on objects, regardless of their orientation on a slope.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, bombard, friction drum, mechanics, equilibrium, lever laws, center of gravity, physics of motion</keywords>

### [Manuscript Facsimile]

<note>Leonardo’s notes on structural engineering, likely intended for a treatise on architecture. He emphasizes the "life" of materials—how wood moves and how weight travels through stone.</note>

That beam which is longer than 10 or 20 times its greatest thickness will have little permanence and will break at 1/3 of its length; and remember that the part which enters the wall should be permeated with hot pitch and wrapped in oak boards, also permeated <note>Pitch (tar) was used as a preservative to prevent the ends of wooden beams from rotting inside damp masonry walls.</note>.

Every beam should pass through its walls and be secured beyond those walls with sufficient chains <term>chains: iron tie-rods used to anchor structural elements</term>, because one often sees during earthquakes that beams slip out of the walls, subsequently ruining the walls and the floors; where they are chained, they will hold the walls firmly together, and the walls in turn steady the floors.

Furthermore, I remind you never to apply plaster <gloss>finishes or coatings</gloss> over woodwork, because in the swelling and shrinking that wood always does due to moisture and dryness, these plasters often crack; once cracked, their divisions gradually turn to powder and create an ugly appearance.

Furthermore, I remind you not to make floors supported by both arches and beams, because over time the floor supported by the beams sags somewhat in its middle, and those parts of the floor supported by the arch remain in their place; whence:

(PROPOSITION) Floors that are supported by two different types of supports appear over time to be shaped like hills <note>Meaning the floor becomes uneven, with high spots over the rigid arches and low spots over the flexible wooden beams.</note>.

**ON COVERING WOOD WITH LIME**

Regarding what happens to woodwork covered with lime—as some do when marbling <note>Creating a faux-marble finish</note> inside walls with beams and other woods—the lime that sits over said wood will separate from the other lime that coats the walls, and cracks will appear. In a short time, the woodwork will remain uncovered; this derives from the swelling of the wood when it incorporates moisture into itself, and when it shrinks as it releases that moisture.

<margin>
The window *a* is well-placed under window *c*, but window *b* is poorly placed under the space *d*, because said space is without support and foundation; so remember never to break <note>i.e., cut an opening</note> under the spaces between windows.
</margin>

<margin>
This wall will break under the arch *e-f*, because the seven whole bricks <gloss>quadrelli</gloss> are not sufficient to support the foot of the arch placed above them, and these 7 bricks will break exactly in the middle as appears in *a-b*. The reason is that brick *a* has only the weight *a-k* above it, while the last brick under the arch has the weight *c-d-x-a* above it. It appears that *c-d* forces the arch toward the shoulder at point *p*, but the weight *p-o* provides resistance, so all the weight goes into the root of the arch; therefore, make the root of the arch like *7-6*, which is twice as strong as *x-z*.
</margin>

### ON THE LENGTH OF BEAMS.

— The beam that is longer than 10 to 20 times its greatest thickness will be unstable and will break at its midpoint. Remember that the part which enters the wall must be permeated with hot pitch and surrounded by oak wood likewise permeated.

— Every beam must extend beyond its walls and be secured outside those walls by sufficient chains <term>chains: iron tie-rods or anchors</term>, because one often sees in earthquakes the beams slip out of the walls and then ruin the walls and the floors where they are linked; whereas if they are sufficiently chained, they will keep the walls united and these walls maintain the floors.

— I remind you again never to put layers of cement <note>original: "ciment" — likely referring to a heavy mortar or concrete-like bedding</note> on wood; for when the wood expands and contracts through moisture and dryness, many times the said floors [the said layers of cement] crack, and, when they have cracked, their pieces fall little by little into dust, making a sorry display.

— I remind you again not to have your floors supported by both arches and beams; for, with time, the floor which is supported by the beams sags a little in its middle, and the part which is supported by the arch remains in its place.

(Proposition.) Floors which are supported by two different natures of supports appear with time to be shaped like a vault <note>original: "acholli" — likely a dialect or archaic term for a curved or humped shape</note>.

### ON THE LIME COATING TO BE GIVEN TO WOOD.

— By covering wood with a coating of lime, as some do, one creates marble <note>meaning a smooth, hard plaster finish</note> on the interior of walls in which there are beams and other timbers; the lime which is on these timbers will separate from the other lime that coats the walls, and cracks will appear, so that in a short time the wood will remain uncovered; this results from the expansion of the wood caused by the moisture that incorporates into it and the contraction of this wood when the moisture leaves it.

— Window **a** is well-placed under window **c**, and window **b** is poorly placed under the space **d**, because that space is without support and without foundation; remember therefore never to make openings under the intervals between windows <margin>1. Below the second figure in the margin, to the right of the facsimile.</margin>. —

— This wall will break under the arch **e f**, because the seven whole tiles <note>original: "carreaux" — can refer to bricks, floor tiles, or squared stones</note> are not sufficient to support the foot of the arch placed upon them; these seven tiles will break right in the middle, as appears in **a b**; the reason is that tile **a** has only the weight **a k** upon it, while the last tile under the arch has the weight **c d x a** upon it; it seems that the force of **c d** bears on the arch toward the shoulder, at point **p**, but the weight **p o** offers resistance, from which it follows that all the weight goes into the root <gloss>at the base</gloss> of the arch; therefore make the root of the arches like **7** and **6**, which are twice as strong as **x** and **z**.

# [MANUSCRIPT FACSIMILE]

<note>Transcribed from Leonardo's mirror-written Italian script</note>

<note>Top section: Studies on the strength and construction of joined beams (trave duplicata)</note>

### On the Quality of Beams
The beam must be doubled, consisting of two beams joined together as shown above. And if you make the height of the doubled beam one <term>braccio: a Florentine unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm</term>, ensure its length does not exceed 16 braccia; and in the other dimension <note>the width</note>, let it be 1/2 braccio.

<margin>
And the notches <note>original: "crenne" — interlocking teeth or grooves</note> should be made every 2 braccia and should be carved 1/6 of a braccio deep. Similarly, the curvature of the beam at point *n* should have 1/6 of a "rise" <note>original: "freccia" — the vertical distance of an arc's curve</note>. The bracket <note>original: "mensola"</note> must be as thick as half of the reinforced wood and must project out by the thickness or height of said beam, which is one braccio; and it must be made of oak, otherwise the notches will not be very durable.
</margin>

The beam must be doubled in such a way that its ends are two squares, and the length should be 32 times the thickness of those squares.
The doubled beam must be one square thick, 2 squares high, and 32 of those squares long.

<note>Bottom section: Studies on the force of impact (colpo) and movement</note>

### On Doubled Impact in the Same Motion
The motion caused by an impact is of greater efficacy than the motion caused by another movement. Therefore, the impact resulting from a motion caused by another impact will be much greater than the impact produced solely by movement.

#### Example
If you suspend the end of a horse bone over the head of an ax for a distance of 1/4 of a braccio, and you provide a support for it made of a single thread of straw, and you strike it from above in its center with a fist or with a tightly bound bundle of straw, you will see that bone break under the blow given to it from above.

### On Impact and Motion Created by Weight or Force
I say that the motion created by a falling weight will be equal to the motion created by force.
The body that receives the impact is not harmed on the opposite side as much as it is at the point of percussion. Experience shows this in a stone struck while in a man's hands: the hand is not harmed while holding the struck stone as much as it would be harmed if it received the blow itself.

### On Impact
By as much as the point of the nail enters the head of the hammer that strikes it, by that much more will it drive into the plank than the hammer would, the impact being of equal movement and force.

---

# BEAMS — IMPACT, MOTION, WEIGHT, FORCE.

### ON THE QUALITY OF BEAMS.

— The beam must be doubled, consisting of two beams joined together in the manner indicated above. If you give the height of the doubled beam one <term>braccio: a Florentine unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm</term>, ensure that its length does not exceed 16 braccia and that in the other dimension it is 1/2 braccio; let the notches be made every 2 braccia and have a depth of 1/6 of a braccio; likewise, the bend [the angle] of the beam *a n* should have 1/6 of a "rise" <note>original: "flèche" — the vertical distance of a curve</note>; the bracket must have half the thickness of the reinforced wood and project by the thickness or height of the beam, that is to say, by one braccio; it must be made of oak, otherwise the notches would be of short duration.

— The beam must be doubled so that its heads [its ends] are two squares and the length is 32 times the thickness of these squares and... <unclear>illegible</unclear>

— The doubled beam must be one square thick, 2 squares high, and 32 of those squares long.

### ON THE DOUBLED IMPACT IN A SINGLE MOTION.

— The motion caused by an impact has more effect than the motion caused by another impulse; consequently, the impact resulting from the motion caused by another impact is much stronger than the impact made only by the motion.

### EXAMPLE.

— If you suspend the end of a horse bone to the head of an axe <note>original: "coignée" — a large felling axe</note> with an interval of 1/4 of a braccio, and you tie it with a straw thread and strike its middle [of the axe] with your fist or else with a [.?] of tightly packed straw, you will see this bone break under the blow given from above.

### ON THE IMPACT AND MOTION PRODUCED BY WEIGHT OR BY FORCE.

— I say that the motion made by a falling weight is equivalent to the motion made by force.

— The body that receives the impact is not damaged in the opposite part as much as in the part struck. The experiment for this is found in the stone struck upon the hands of a man; for the hand does not suffer as much while holding the struck stone as it would suffer by receiving the blow directly.

### ON THE IMPACT.

— As many times as the point of the nail enters the head of the hammer that strikes it, so much more will it sink into the board than the hammer would sink if the blow were of the same motion and force.
