<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the mechanics of nature, specifically drawing a direct parallel between the human body and the Earth.</meta>

### BEGINNING OF THE TREATISE ON WATER

— Man is called by the ancients a "lesser world" <note>original: "mondo minore," referring to the concept of the Microcosm</note>, and certainly the application of this name is well placed; for just as man is composed of earth, water, air, and fire, this body of the earth is similar. If man has within him bones as supporters and framework for the flesh, the world has rocks as supporters of the earth; if man has within him the lake of blood, where the lungs increase and decrease in breathing, the body of the earth has its ocean sea, which likewise increases and decreases every six hours with the breathing of the world. If from the said lake of blood derive veins which go ramifying through the human body, similarly the ocean sea fills the body of the earth with infinite veins of water. The body of the earth lacks nerves, which are not there because nerves are made for the purpose of movement, and the world, being of perpetual stability, no movement occurs; and no movement occurring, nerves are not necessary. But in all other things, they are very similar.

### OF THE VEINS OF WATER ATOP THE SUMMITS OF MOUNTAINS.

— It appears clearly that the entire surface of the ocean, when it is not stormy, is at an equal distance from the center of the earth, and that the summits of the mountains are as much farther from the center of the sea as they rise above the surface of that sea. Therefore, if the body of the earth did not have a similarity to man, it would be impossible that the water of the sea, being so much lower than the mountains, could by its nature rise to the summits of those mountains. Whence, it is to be believed that the same cause that keeps the blood in the summit of the head of man, the same keeps the water in the summit of the mountains.

### OF THE HEAT THAT IS IN THE WORLD.

— Where there is life, there is heat; and where there is vital heat, there is movement of humors. *And if* heat moves the moist, cold stops it. This is proven because it is seen in effect that the heat of the element of fire always draws to itself moist vapors, dense mists, and thick clouds, which it tears from the seas and other marshes, rivers, and moist valleys; and drawing them little by little up to the cold region, the first part stops because the heat and moisture do not agree with the cold and dryness. Whence, the first part stops there and receives the other parts, and so, part joining with part, they become thick and dark clouds. Often they are *carried*, moved and carried by winds from one region to another, where by their density they create such thick weight that they fall in heavy rain. And if the heat of the sun joins with the power of the element of fire, the clouds will be drawn higher and find more cold, in which they freeze and cause tempestuous hail. Now, that same heat which holds so great a weight of water—as is seen raining from clouds—holds it smoothly high up from the bases of the mountains, and conducts it and holds it within the summits of the mountains; which, finding some fissure, continually issue forth and cause the rivers.

<summary>Leonardo explains the theory of the Microcosm, arguing that the Earth functions like a living human body, with the tides acting as its breath and mountain springs acting like blood rising to the head.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, anatomy of the earth, hydrology, tides, vital heat, atmospheric pressure, water cycle</keywords>

### WHETHER HEAT IS THE CAUSE OF THE MOVEMENT OF MOISTURE, AND COLD STOPS IT

This was proved previously by the demonstration of the cold region which stops the vapors <note>original: "invboli" - likely a variant of *nuvoli* (clouds/vapors).</note> drawn up by the element of heat. Regarding the proof that heat draws moisture, it is proved thus: Heat a flask <gloss>ampolla?</gloss> and place it in a vessel with the mouth downward, and place a glowing coal near it; you will see that the moisture, in order to follow the heat that rises, will fill the flask with water, and the air will be displaced through the mouth of that flask which was enclosed in the flask. Also, if you take a wet cloth and show it to the fire, you will see the moisture of that cloth leave its place and run toward the fire; and that part of the moisture which is less material will rise upward, drawn by the company of the fire, which by nature rises toward the region of its own element.

— thus the sun draws moisture upward.

### OF THE CONFIRMATION OF WHY WATER IS IN THE SUMMITS OF MOUNTAINS

— I say that just as the natural heat keeps the blood in the veins at the summit of man, and when that man is dead, that cold blood recedes to the low places; so it is when the sun warms the head of a man, it multiplies and there comes so much blood and humors that, by forcing the veins, it often generates headaches. Similarly, the veins that go branching through the body of the earth, and by the natural heat that is spread throughout the entire body of the continent, the water stays in the veins and is raised to the high peaks of the mountains. And that water which passes through a masonry conduit in the body of that mountain, like a dead thing, will not rise from its first lowliness because it is not heated by the vital heat of the primary vein. Furthermore, the heat of the element of fire and the heat of the sun by day have the power to awaken

— if you take the instrument r. f. and heat it from above, the water will depart from r. f. and, ascending, will pour out through a.

### OF MOUNTAINS

— the moisture of low places and draw it upward in the same way that it draws vapors and awakens their moisture from the bed of the sea.

### THE OPINION OF SOME WHO SAY THAT THE WATER OF SOME SEAS IS HIGHER THAN THE HIGHEST SUMMITS OF MOUNTAINS, AND THEREFORE THE WATER IS PUSHED TO THOSE SUMMITS

— water will not move from place to place unless a lower level draws it. And by a natural course, it can never return to a height similar to the first place where, in emerging from the mountains, it showed itself to the sky. And that part of the sea which, by a false imagination, you said was so high that it poured through the peaks of the high mountains, would have been consumed and poured out through the outlets of those mountains for so many centuries. You may well think that for as long a time as the Tigris and Euphrates <note>Leonardo uses these ancient rivers to illustrate the vast timescales of geological processes.</note>

### CIRCULATION IN THE WORLD AND IN MAN
### DECREASE OF GRAVITY BY HEAT.

->(Continuation from the front of folio 56.)<-

have poured out through the summits of the mountains [with their destructive waters?] one can believe that all the water of the Ocean has passed a very great number of times through the said mouths; now, you do not believe that the Nile has put more water into the sea than there is at present in the entire element of water. It is certain that if this water had fallen outside of this body of the earth, this machine <note>The "machine" refers to the Earth as a mechanical system, a common Renaissance metaphor.</note> would have been without water for a long time already; so that one can conclude that water goes from the rivers to the sea and from the sea to the rivers, always making the same circuit, and that all the sea and the rivers have passed through the mouth of the Nile.

### PROOF THAT THE SURFACE OF THE SEA IS EQUIDISTANT TO THE CENTER OF THE EARTH AND TO THE LOWEST SURFACE OF THE WORLD.

— The lowest parts of the mountains are where they join their valleys. And the lowest part of the valleys is [where flow] their rivers, the causes of these valleys; the rivers have their lowest part where they [?] where they . . . at their reunion with the royal river <note>term: "royal river" (fleuve royal) refers to a main trunk river or a great river like the Po or the Nile into which tributaries flow</note>, where, in losing their forms, they lose their name; finally, the lowest part of the royal rivers is the sea, where the rivers rest from their wanderings with their followers.

### OF THE BLOOD WHICH IS AT THE TOP OF THE HEAD.

— And it would seem according to a simple [a very simple thing] that if someone broke the top of a man's head, there should only come out through this break the blood that is between its lips [edges]; indeed, every heavy thing desires [seeks] low places, blood has weight, and it seems impossible that by itself it should rise upwards like an aerial and light thing. And if you wanted to say that by the expansion made by the lung in the lake of blood <note>term: "lake of blood" (lac du sang) likely refers to the heart or the central vascular reservoir</note>, when this lung, in breathing, fills with air and, deflating, drives the blood from the lake, the blood fleeing into the veins makes them grow and swell, and that it is this swelling that makes the blood escape in the aforementioned rupture of the top of the head, this opinion would be quickly refuted, given that the veins are well capable of providing by themselves a convenient retreat for the increase of blood, without the latter needing to overflow through the rupture of the head as if it lacked space.

### WHY THIS BLOOD FLEES THROUGH THE TOP OF THE HEAD.

— The spiritual parts <note>In this context, "spiritual" refers to "spirits" or "vapors"—the refined, active substances (like heat or air) believed to animate the body.</note> have the power to move and to associate material parts with their course. We see fire send, mixed with vapors and smoke, by means of spiritual heat, terrestrial and heavy matters above the chimney, as is seen with soot, which, if you burn it, you will see reduce to ash. Thus, the heat mixed with the blood, finding a way to evaporate through the rupture of the head, desiring to return to its element, carries in its company the blood with which this heat has infused and mixed itself. — The reason why smoke rises with such fury, and carries with it various matters, is that the fire which attaches to wood, feeds and feasts on a subtle moisture, and when it comes to the stronger moisture which cannot be consumed by the element the heat contained in the fire, the fire. . . — 4¹ — .

---

1. This number 4 refers to the 4 read at the head of the text on the front of folio 57. <meta>Translator note: This confirms the sequence of the manuscript pages for the reader.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo compares the hydrological cycle of the Earth to the circulation of blood in the human body, arguing that heat acts as the "spiritual" force that allows heavy fluids to rise against gravity. He uses the example of the Nile and the phenomenon of blood escaping a head wound to illustrate these macrocosmic and microcosmic movements.</summary>

<margin>57</margin>
— 4 — it wishes to return to its element and carries with it the heated humor, as is seen if you distill quicksilver <note>original: "argiento vivo" (mercury)</note> in an alembic <note>term: "alembic" (an early chemical still used for distillation)</note>; you will see that when this mercury, of such great weight, is mixed with the heat of fire, it rises and, falling back as smoke into its second receptacle, returns to its first nature.

### EXPERIMENT: HOW HEAT MAKES HEAVY BODIES LIGHT

If one of two things of equal weight is placed upon a balance, the one that is heated will be lighter than the other cold one.
— You shall make this proof with 2 copper balls attached to 2 iron wires with the scales; and put one of the 2 into the fire until it is glowing; and when it is made red by the fire, pull it out of the fire so that the evaporation of the rising heat does not push the weight upward; and you will see that the ball which was previously of equal weight with the other when cold, has been made light by the heat.

### OF THE MOVEMENT OF THE SEA EVERY 6 HOURS

— If heat moves the humors, cold stops them; and where there is greater cold, there is greater fixation <unclear>of moisture</unclear> of humors.
— If anyone should wish to say that the moon, being an increaser of cold, was that which every 6 hours caused the sea to rise and fall for the reasons assigned above, it seems impossible; because a thing does not pull its like by similarity, but rather pulls by deformity <note>Leonardo argues that "like does not attract like"; heat does not attract fire, but rather attracts its opposite, cold and moisture.</note>. You do not see heat pulling fire to itself; rather, it will pull cold and moisture. You do not see water pulling other water to itself.

### HOW WATER IS LIGHTER THAN THE EARTH

— Every liquid body is penetrated from above by a heavier body placed upon it, and a heavy, porous body will be penetrated by liquid and thin bodies.
— If you mix earth with water, you will see the earth penetrate that water and make its bed. And if you should see the weight [from the weight?] of water thrown upon the earth penetrate it and pass beneath that earth, this happens because the porosity of the earth, which made a path for the incoming water, was full of air; and the air, being unable to stay beneath the water, immediately abandons those terrestrial pores; and because nothing can remain a vacuum, the water enters and refills the places abandoned by the air <insert>by the air</insert>.

### WHY THE SEA MAKES A CURRENT IN THE STRAIT OF SPAIN MORE THAN ELSEWHERE

— A river of whatever depth will have a greater flow in its narrower width than in its greater width, in proportion to how much the greater width exceeds the lesser.
— This proposition is clearly proven by experience, through reason confirmed by experience; because when a mile's length of water passes through a channel one mile wide, where the river is 5 miles wide, each of the 5 square miles will contribute $\frac{1}{5}$ of itself to restore the square mile of water missing in the sea — 4 —
— Mediterranean Sea —
— Strait of Spain <note>The Strait of Gibraltar</note> —
— 4 — and where the river is 3 miles wide, each of those square miles will contribute a third of its quantity for the missing square mile of the strait, as is shown in f. g. h. for the mile n.

# THE DECREASE OF WEIGHT BY HEAT — TIDES
## THE RELATIONSHIP OF MOVEMENTS (CURRENTS).
->(Continued from folio 56 verso.)<-

<margin>57</margin> — 4 — it wishes to return to its element and carries with it the heated humors <note>The translator clarifies: the heated liquids</note>, as is seen if you distill quicksilver <note>mercury</note> in an alembic; you will see this silver of such great weight, when it is mixed with the heat of fire, lift itself up, and, as smoke, fall back into its second receptacle, resuming its first nature.

->EXPERIMENT ON THE MANNER IN WHICH HEAT MAKES HEAVY BODIES LIGHT.<-

— If two things of equal weight are placed on the balance, the one that is burning hot will be lighter than the other, which is cold.
You shall perform this test with 2 copper balls attached by 2 iron wires to the scales; you will put one of the two in the fire, and you will create wind <note>likely meaning bellows or airflow to stoke the fire</note>, and when it has been made red by the fire, you will remove it, so that the vaporization from the rising heat does not push the weight upward; you will then see that the ball which, being initially cold, was of the same weight as the other, will have become light by the effect of the heat.

->OF THE MOVEMENT OF THE SEA EVERY 6 HOURS.<-

— If heat moves the humors [the liquids], cold stops them, and where the cold is greatest, there is found the greatest solidification of *moisture of humors*.
— If someone wished to say that it was the moon, the increaser of cold, which caused the sea to rise and fall every 6 hours, for the reasons stated above this appears impossible. Indeed, one thing being similar to another, it will not attract by similarity, but by dissimilarity; you will not see hot fire and dry fire attract fire to itself, but on the contrary, it will attract the cold and the moist; you do not see water attracted by other water.

->HOW WATER IS LIGHTER THAN THE EARTH.<-

— Every liquid body is penetrated by the heavier body placed upon it; and the heavy and porous body is penetrated by liquid and subtle bodies.
— If you mix earth with water, you will see the earth penetrate this water and make its bed. And if you see, by means of weight, water thrown upon the earth penetrate it and pass beneath, this comes from the fact that the porosity of the earth, which made a path for the water that came upon it, was full of air; and the air, being unable to remain under the water, abandons these porosities of the earth, and, since nothing can remain empty, the water enters and fills the places abandoned by the water <insert>by the air</insert>.

->WHY THE SEA HAS MORE CURRENT IN THE STRAIT OF SPAIN THAN ELSEWHERE.<-

— A river of uniform depth will have a faster flow [a current] in its narrowest width than in its greatest, in proportion to how much the greatest width surpasses the least.
— This proposition is clearly proven by reason confirmed by experience. Indeed, when a mile in length of water passes through a channel one mile wide, where the river is five miles wide, each of the five square miles will contribute $\frac{1}{5}$ of itself to replace the missing square mile of water in the sea; — 4¹ —
— Mediterranean Sea. —
— Strait of Spain² <note>The Strait of Gibraltar</note>. —
— 4 — And where the river is 3 miles wide, each of these square miles will contribute a third of its quantity for the deficit made by the square mile of the strait, as is demonstrated in f g h for the mile n³.

# RELATIONSHIPS OF VARIOUS MOVEMENTS (MAN, INSTRUMENTS, WATER).
(Continuation from the front of folio 57.)

### EXAMPLE.

— For example, if there is a place that has three different widths contained within one another; if the first, smallest width fits into the second 4 times <note>The French text says 8, then 4 in brackets; the logic of the following sentence suggests 4 is the intended multiplier for the progression.</note>, and the second fits twice into the third, I say that if the men who fill these spaces with their persons must walk together in a continuous manner, when the men in the largest area take one step, those in the second, smaller distance will take two, and those in the third area—which is a quarter the size of the second—will take 8 steps in the same time. This is a proportion you will find in all movements that pass through different lengths of space.

— Consider the pump <note>original: "pompe"</note>: when its piston <note>original: "piston"</note>, which drives the water, moves by one finger’s breadth, the first water that appeared outside has moved away by two fathoms <term>brasse: a unit of measurement based on the reach of outstretched arms, roughly 5-6 feet</term>. Thus, if it were possible to raise a vessel of ten barrels to a height of ten miles, you would find—provided the total exit of the liquid continues—that the first wine to leave the vessel would have traveled the 10 miles before the surface of the wine in the vessel had even lowered by two fathoms. You will find the same thing in the movements of wheels in relation to their pinions <term>pinion: a small gear that engages with a larger wheel</term>, given that if the axle of the wheel is the same size as the pinion during the wheel's rotation, the movement of the pinion and the surface of the wheel is faster than that of its axle, in proportion to how many times the circumference of the pinion fits into the circumference of the wheel.

->EVERY MOVEMENT OF WATER WITH UNIFORM WIDTH AND SURFACE WILL FLOW MORE STRONGLY IN ONE PLACE THAN ANOTHER IN PROPORTION TO IT BEING SHALLOWER IN ONE THAN THE OTHER.<-

— This proposition is clearly proven: indeed, if the river is of unequal depth, even though it is of uniform width and surface, it is necessary that the current of this river also has an unequal movement for the reasons given above, and its movement will be of this nature. Let us assume that the figure marked to the side <note>referring to a diagram in the original manuscript</note> is the river: I say that at *m n* the water has a faster movement than at *a b*, in proportion to how many times *m n* fits into *a b*; it fits four times, therefore the movement will be four times faster at *m n* than at *a b*, three times faster than at *c d*, and twice as fast as at *e f*.

->ALL WATER WHICH, DURING A FLOOD, MOVES ITS SURFACE IN ONE DIRECTION, WILL HAVE A CONTRARY MOVEMENT AT ITS BOTTOM.<-

— This proposition is proven in the following manner: We see, when the sea sends its waves toward the land, that the wave which comes to end at the shore—though it is the last of its companions and is always ridden over and submerged by the second-to-last—nevertheless, the second-to-last does not overtake the last, but is submerged in the place of the last. Now, this submersion being renewed by a continual movement, it is necessary that where the sea meets the land, there is, beneath the incoming wave, a movement contrary to its own, which carries back the preceding wave along the bottom; so that as much water returns from below toward the cause of its movement as the motor <note>the driving force or "mover"</note> has pushed along the top$^1$.

---

1. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water* <note>original Latin/Italian title: "Tratt. del moto e misura dell'acqua"</note>, p. 328, book 3, chapter XXXVI.

<summary>This page explains the principles of fluid dynamics and mechanical advantage, demonstrating how velocity increases as the space through which a substance (water or people) moves decreases. It also describes the "undertow" effect where surface waves moving toward a shore necessitate a return current along the seabed.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, hydraulics, velocity, proportions, mechanical advantage, waves, undertow, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of water</keywords>

The water that shall exit from an unequal opening—unequal in height and in the width of the opening—its course will bend and strike
the bank on the side of the lower part of its exit; and having struck there, the bank will cause it to leap toward the opposite side
with less fury.

the opening stays always full. Because water always declines toward low places, it will run toward the lower part of the opening <note>original: "busa," likely referring to the hole or orifice</note>. —

# ON WATER — ON THE FORM AND THE CENTER OF THE EARTH.

### WHY THE MOVEMENT OF WATER, ALTHOUGH IT IS SLOWER THAN THAT OF A MAN, APPEARS FASTER.

— The reason is that if you look at the movement of water, your gaze cannot fix itself; an effect similar to that which takes place with things seen in your shadow when you walk; for, if the eye seeks to understand the quality <gloss>the nature</gloss> of the shadow, the straws and other things contained within that shadow appear to move rapidly; and it seems that they are much quicker to flee the said shadow than the shadow is to travel¹.

### ON THE CENTER OF THE OCEAN.

— The center of the sphere of water is the true center of the roundness of our world. This world is composed of earth and water in a round form; but if you wished to find the center of the element of earth, it is contained <gloss>situated</gloss> in a place equidistant to the surface of the Ocean and not equidistant to the surface of the earth; for it is easy to understand that this ball of the earth has truly nothing of a perfect roundness, except in those parts covered by the sea, lakes, or other still waters; and every part of the earth that emerges outside of this sea moves away from its center².

### PROOF THAT THE EARTH IS NOT ROUND AND THAT, NOT BEING ROUND, IT CANNOT HAVE A COMMON CENTER.

— We see the Nile depart from the southern regions and water various provinces, running toward the north <note>original: "septentrion"</note> over a space of 3,000 miles³, then empty into the Mediterranean waters on the shores of Egypt; now, if we wish to give to this descent ten cubits <term>brasses: a traditional unit of measurement based on the length of outstretched arms</term> per mile, which is commonly granted to the universality of the course of rivers, we will find that the Nile is, at the end, lower by ten miles <unclear>?</unclear> than at the beginning. We see again the Rhine, the Rhône, and the Danube depart from the Germanic lands, as if from a sort of center of Europe, to take their course toward the seas, one to the east, the other to the north, and the last toward the southern seas; if you consider everything well, you will see that the plains of Europe form a gathering much higher than are the high peaks of the maritime mountains; now, imagine how much their peaks are themselves higher than the sea shores⁴.

### ON SOME WHO SAY THAT THE WATER IS HIGHER THAN THE UNCOVERED LAND⁵.

— Certainly, it is no small wonder that I feel at the common opinion formed against the truth by the universal gathering of men's judgments, which is that all agree that the surface of the sea is higher than the highest peaks of the mountains, alleging many vain and childish reasons, against which I will allege one single, simple, and short one: We see in an evident manner that, if one removes the dikes from the sea, it will cover the earth and make it of perfect roundness; now, consider a little what quantity of earth one would remove to make the marine waves cover the world; therefore, what one would remove would be higher than the shores of the sea⁶.

---

1. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water* <note>original: "Trattato del moto e misura dell'acqua"</note>, p. 307, book I, ch. XLV.
2. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, p. 278, book I, ch. IX.
3. In the Atlantic Manuscript, Leonardo says, regarding canals: "a mile contains three thousand cubits, and the cubit is divided into 12 inches..." (*Atlantic Manuscript* <note>original: "Manuscrit Atlantique"</note>, fº 329 vº — *Essay on the works of Leonardo da Vinci*, plate XXII).
4. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, p. 278, book I, ch. X.
5. Compare with: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, book I, p. 281, ch. XIX.
6. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, book I, p. 282, ch. XX.

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci explores the geometric center of the Earth, arguing that while the water's surface forms a perfect sphere, the exposed land creates an irregular shape that deviates from this center. He refutes the common medieval belief that the sea level is higher than the mountains by using the logic of fluid equilibrium.</summary>
<keywords>water movement, center of the earth, planetary geometry, Nile river, European rivers, sea level, Leonardo da Vinci, hydrodynamics</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's observations on the physics of water, transitioning from the general geometry of the Earth and the Nile's course to the specific mechanics of river erosion, the formation of foam, and the behavior of falling water.</meta>

### WHY RIVERS CHANGE LOCATION AND OFTEN TAKE FROM AND DEPOSIT <gloss>and pile up?</gloss> IN DIFFERENT PLACES

— The movement of water always tends to wear away its support, specifically that part which is softer and offers it less resistance; it departs from its place, leaving behind various cavities where <gloss>where</gloss> the water, swirling in various eddies <term>retrosi: reverse currents or vortices</term>, consumes and hollows out, enlarging the said void. Striking against the new embankments, it leaps back, hitting the banks, consuming and eroding them with the collapse of the opposing side. It changes course by means of these collapses, carrying along with its flow the lighter earth, which it then discharges in quieter places, raising the beds; this diminishes the quantity and the force of the water, sending its fury to the opposite side. If it finds a bank, it erodes it and undermines its foundations until, with great collapses, it uncovers new lands. And if it finds a plain, it occupies it; by lifting and hollowing, it makes a new bed. And if it finds buried stones, it uncovers them and then undermines them; these, often because of their size, offer resistance to the impetuous course, which, struck back by the opposing stones, bounces to the opposite side, breaking and damaging the facing banks.

### WHAT THE FOAM OF THE WATER IS

— Water that falls from a height into other water traps within itself a certain quantity of air which, by means of the blow, is submerged with it into the other water; with rapid motion, it rises back up, reaching the surface it had left, clothed in a thin moisture in the manner of bubbles <term>sonagli: literally 'little bells,' Leonardo's term for bubbles</term> in a spherical body, spreading out circularly from the first impact. Or, the water that falls upon other water departs from its place with various different bifurcated ramifications; it goes about entangling and weaving itself together with hollows which, struck back upon the surface part of the water by the power of the weight and the blow given by the said water, the air—due to the extreme speed—has no time to escape into its own element; instead, it is submerged in the manner described above.

### WHY WATER HOLLOWS OUT ITS BASINS IN A CIRCLE WHERE IT FALLS WITH FURY

— Water that falls in the manner described above does not widen its basin because, in falling vertically, it is a sign that it has little fury of water behind it to drive it; therefore it falls, but thin, disjointed, and slender, almost in a perpendicular line. The air found within this disjointedness, having the weight above it almost equal, cannot escape so quickly that the weight placed above, together with the blow, submerges it. But because air cannot be separated from its element without violence, having obeyed the fury of the blow and weight, it rises immediately and returns in round bubbles to the surface of the water, exiting near the place of impact; since it does not move far from the first strike, it does not offend the banks of the basin. But when the impetuous river, swollen by new rains, flows between its banks, it falls with fury into the low depths <term>pelaghi: deep pools or basins</term>; and not as before, when it rained restfully upon the other water mixed with air, but rather united and powerful, it strikes and opens the struck water down to the hard bottom, removing, undermining, and displacing the firm stones. The covered stones create a new barrier against it there in the depths <note>The text becomes somewhat fragmented here, describing the resistance of the basin walls</note> with the slopes of the already-made basin, a new repair; striking against this and being defeated, it divides upon the said impact into two contrary courses <gloss>currents</gloss> which split into four.

<summary>Leonardo explains the hydraulic mechanics of river erosion, the physical composition of bubbles (foam) as trapped air, and how the volume of a river changes the shape of the basins it carves.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, river beds, foam, bubbles, fluid dynamics, vortices, sediment transport</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's detailed observations on the physics of water, specifically focusing on the mechanical causes of river erosion, the formation of foam (bubbles), and the circular patterns created by falling water.</meta>

# ON WATER (RIVERS, WATERFALLS).

# WHY RIVERS CHANGE THEIR POSITION, AND OFTEN RISE AND SWELL IN VARIOUS PLACES.

The movement of water always tends to destroy its support; the softest part offers the least resistance, and, by changing its place, it leaves various cavities where the water, swirling in various eddies <note>original: "remous" / "retrosi". These are vortices or back-currents where water circles back on itself.</note>, wears away and hollows out these voids by enlarging them. Striking the new embankments, it leaps back while hitting the banks; consuming, gnawing, and ruining that which opposes it, it changes its course amidst the ruins, carrying away in this flow the lighter earth, which it then discharges in calmer places. By raising the bottom, the water diminishes in quantity and force, directing its fury toward the opposite side; if it encounters a bank, it gnaws at it and undermines its foundations until, through a series of collapses, it uncovers new ground; and if it finds a plain, it occupies it. By removing and hollowing out earth, it makes itself a new bed, uncovers the rocks it finds buried, and then undermines them; but many times these rocks, by their great size, resist the impetuous current, which, after having struck against these rocks it could not displace, rebounds toward the other side, breaking and damaging the opposite banks <margin>See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, p. 381, book 6, chapter XVIII.</margin>.

# WHAT THE FOAM OF THE WATER IS.

Water, which falls from a height into other water, encloses within itself a certain quantity of air, which, by means of the blow, is submerged with it into *the other water* and which, with a rapid movement, reappears at the top, reaching the surface it left behind clothed in a subtle moisture, *in the manner of round bubbles, a spherical body*, and escaping in a circle from the first percussion. Or rather, the water that falls upon other water leaves its place with varied and diverse branchings, sometimes bifurcated, intermingled and woven into one another, sometimes hollow, which strike upon the surface of the water; the power of the weight and the blow given by this water acts with such speed that the air does not have time to flee toward its own element and is forced to be submerged in the manner described above.

# WHY WATER HOLLOWS OUT ITS EXCAVATIONS IN A CIRCLE WHERE IT FALLS WITH VIOLENCE.

Water falling in the aforementioned manner does not widen its excavation because it falls straight down, which is a sign that there is little violence in the water pushing it from behind; therefore it falls *but thin, disunited and thin*, almost in a perpendicular line. The air found within the said separation, having a weight upon it almost equal to itself, cannot flee so quickly that it is not submerged by the weight at the same time as the blow; but as air cannot be separated from its element without violence, immediately after having obeyed the fury of the blow and the weight, it suddenly rises again and returns in round bubbles to the surface of the water, outside of the place near the spot struck; therefore, not moving far from the first percussion, it does not damage the edges of the excavation. But when the impetuous river, made proud <note>The author uses "superbe" (proud/haughty) to describe a river swollen and powerful from recent rains.</note> by new rains, flows between its banks, it falls with violence toward lower sheets of water; but then the water no longer rains down tranquilly upon the other water as in the first case mixed with air; it is united and robust, it falls, strikes into, and opens the water it hits, down to the hard part of the bottom where it lifts, undermines, and removes the covered rocks; with the materials it has taken from the excavation it has already formed, it opposes itself in the depths with a new embankment <insert>with the resistant ones a new embankment</insert>; then it strikes this embankment, remains defeated there, and divides upon the said percussion into two contrary currents which carry themselves — 4 <margin>The number 4 refers to the number 4 found at the beginning of the text on the back of folio 59.</margin> —

---

<margin>1. We read in the *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water* (p. 342, *fourth* book, ch. I): "An eddy <note>original: "Retroso"</note> is the impression of a revolving reflected percussion of water, made either in the water, or against the objects of the bank, or on the bottom."</margin>

# WATER

## (RIVERS, WATERFALLS, THE LOWEST PART OF THE EARTH)
### (Continuation from the front of folio 59.)

— 4 — 4¹ — in two semi-circles, and gnawing and destroying every obstacle, they enlarge their bottom into a round shape. <note>Leonardo is describing the circular erosion patterns formed by swirling water.</note>

— But, to speak more accurately, when the rivers are full, the waterfalls are less high; indeed, the quantity [the mass] of the water striking the lowest places, the water that follows the impact does not flee with the same violence as that which falls [the first]; as soon as this is the case, the latter creates resistance, and this resistance causes the water to rise and the fall to be shorter; consequently, it does not trap as much air, because, in the said fall, the lower parts of the water hardly separate from the rest of the water; therefore, little air enters it, from which it results that the impact and the weight of the water having nothing to push it back up, its blow goes unified to the bottom, pushing aside the earth found there, surrounding and stripping the stones, and enlarging the depths of the pools [enlarging the bed of the lower currents].

— Water falling with air.
— Swollen river.

#### BY WHAT MANNER EXCAVATIONS ARE PRODUCED IN THE COURSE OF RIVERS.

— The reason is that there are always stones of different sizes at the bottom of rivers; and the water, finding the largest [stones], comes, in lowering itself beyond them, to strike the place where it descends; the impact pushes aside the smaller stones from the struck area and the bottom becomes larger; the fall increases, becomes more powerful and further hollows out the initiated excavation (and this stems from the fact that rivers *both always gnaw the mud of their bottom and always undermine and uncover rocks of various shapes and dimensions*).

— Cause of excavations in rivers.

#### WHY THERE ARE ALWAYS DIFFERENT BUMPS AND CAVITIES ON THE SURFACE OF THE RUNNING WATER OF RIVERS.

The reason for this is that just as *the stockings that cover the legs* show on the outside what they hide on the inside, so the surface part of the water shows the quality of its bottom; indeed, the part of the water that washes its bottom, finding different bumps caused by stones, strikes against these and leaps upward, raising with it all the other water flowing above it².

— Globular water surface.

— Of the sum of the universal machine of the earth, the lowest part is that which supports and is submerged by the sea. <note>Leonardo often refers to the Earth as a "machine" or "organism" to describe its physical systems.</note>

***

1. These numbers 4 correspond to the number 4 found at the bottom of the front of folio 59.
2. Chapter XXXV of book 4 of the *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water* <note>original: "Trattato del moto e della misura dell'acqua"</note> p. 351, differs from this passage only by a few words.

<summary>Leonardo explains how riverbeds are eroded into circular hollows and how the surface of a river mirrors the obstacles on its floor, using the metaphor of stockings on a leg. He also notes that the sea floor constitutes the lowest point of the "earth machine."</summary>

# ON WATER.

->WHY, IF THERE IS A SINGLE ROCK AT THE BOTTOM OF A RIVER WITH A FLAT BED, THE WATER FORMS MANY SPHERES BEYOND IT.<-

— The reason for this is that the water, after having struck this rock, descends lower and digs out a small hollow; then, after having sought, in its course, the bottom of the hollow it has formed, it leaps upward again, after which it falls once more, doing the same and so on many times, just like a ball struck against the ground which, before ending its movement, makes many leaps that become smaller and smaller¹. <note>The author uses the analogy of a bouncing ball to explain the "standing wave" effect seen in fluid dynamics.</note>

->WHAT CAUSES THE WHIRLPOOLS OF WATER².<-

— The wind is in every way similar in its movement to that of water.

— Universally, all things desire to maintain themselves in their nature. Thus, the course of moving water seeks *its course* to maintain itself according to the power of its cause; and if it finds an opposition that acts as an obstacle, it ends the length of its begun course [its course begun in a straight line] with a circular and spinning movement.

— Water that pours out through a narrow mouth <note>original: "embouchure," referring here to a narrow opening or inlet</note>, descending with violence into the slow currents of large expanses of water—because *the greatest power in the greatest quantity is the greatest power*, and the greatest power offers resistance to the lesser—in this case, the water arriving in the large expanse of water strikes it in its slow progress; but this water, being supported by the other, cannot give way with sufficient promptness, and the arriving water, not wishing to delay its course, turns back after its impact with the river, and, pursuing the first movement in circular retreats, fulfills its impulse at the bottom of the river. Because in the said retreats there is nothing found other than its own movement—a movement accompanied by circles contained one within the other—it is necessary that in this circulation the water makes a longer circular revolution; the course of the water is longer and more continuous because it finds no obstacle but itself; and this movement gnaws at the banks, which are consumed by their collapse and their ruin and²...

— No surface of water can be, by its nature, lower than that of the sea.

— No free water, without movement, [can] remain outside the sphere of its element. <note>This reflects the Aristotelian view that elements seek their natural place in the universe.</note>

***

1. See: *Treatise on the motion and measurement of water* <note>original: "Trattato del moto e della misura dell' acqua"</note>, p. 351, book 4, chapter XXXVI, and plate 15, fig. 84.
2. See: *Treatise on the motion and measurement of water*, p. 349, book 4, chapter XXVII.

# ON WATER (MOVEMENT — PROPORTION OF THE THING MOVED TO THAT WHICH MOVES IT — CONSERVATION OF FORCE).

->ON WATER (MOVEMENT — PROPORTION OF THE THING MOVED TO THAT WHICH MOVES IT — CONSERVATION OF FORCE).<-

— THE MORE A MOVEMENT IS DRIVEN BY A GREATER POWER, THE MORE IT FOLLOWS ITS DIRECT PATH; THEREFORE, WATER DRIVEN BY A GREAT WEIGHT STRIKES THE SURFACE OF THE LOWER WATER AND PENETRATES TO THE BOTTOM, WHICH IT STIRS AND DESTROYS BY A CIRCULAR REVOLUTION.

— It is a true thing that, if water falls with violence upon other water, *and it and* <note>The original French transcription "et la et" suggests a repetitive or stuttered emphasis in the manuscript</note> that it does not find sufficient resistance at the first impact, it traverses [the second body of water] with the same violence until the bottom, where, finding resistance, it turns back in great circles—circles which diminish in size as they approach the surface, because the fury ends roughly in that place where the other water also has little movement.

IT IS FITTING THAT EVERY MOVEMENT PRODUCED BY FORCE SHOULD RUN A COURSE PROPORTIONAL TO THE RATIO OF THE THING MOVED TO THAT WHICH MOVES IT. And if it finds a resistant opposition, it will complete *its due journey* the length of its due journey by circular movement and by various other leaps and bounds which, when the time and the journey [path] are fully accounted for, will have acted as if the course had taken place without any contradiction¹.

### EXPERIMENT: — PROOF THAT ALL WATER VORTICES ARE WIDE AT THE BOTTOM AND NARROW AT THE TOP:

— Take a rod made as you see above, and make it with small wings of sheet metal² <term>sheet metal (tôle): likely referring to thin iron or copper plates used to catch the current</term>; give it enough weight at the foot so that the bottom part goes to the floor, tie it with a string that suspends it from a stick, and drive [plunge] a part of it under the water. You will then observe whether the top part, in the spinning that takes place, bends or not, and as much.....

---

1. See above, folio 24 recto of manuscript A.
2. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, page 356, book 4, chapter 55.

It includes a practical experiment using a weighted rod with metal fins to measure the shape and intensity of water currents.</summary>

# - FOLIO 6r (recto). -

### [MANUSCRIPT FACSIMILE]

<note>This section translates Leonardo’s famous mirror writing, where he recorded his observations on the physics of nature.</note>

->WATER AT THE BOTTOM CREATES EDDIES WITH A MOTION CONTRARY TO THAT AT THE SURFACE<-

— The reason is that if the circles, which are wide at the top, are reduced to a single point, they submerge; and following their motion along the course they have begun, they end up at the bottom creating a motion contrary to the one above when they disperse from their center.

— Although the voices <note>original: "voci" - here meaning sounds or acoustic waves</note> that penetrate this air depart with circular movements from their causes, (nevertheless, circles moved by different principles encounter one another without any impediment, and penetrate and pass through one another) always maintaining their causes at the center.

Because in all cases of motion, water has a great similarity to air, I will use it as an example for the aforementioned proposition. I say that if you throw two small stones at the same time, some distance from each other, onto a pool of water <term>pool of water (pelago dacqua): a deep or still body of water</term> that is without motion, you will see two separate sets of circles caused around the two said impacts <term>impacts (perchuissioni): the physical strike of the stone on the surface</term>. These quantities [of circles], as they grow, come to encounter one another and then incorporate themselves by intersecting one circle with the other, always maintaining the places struck by the stones as their centers. And the reason is that, although there appears to be some demonstration of movement, the water does not leave its site; because the opening made in it by the stones was immediately closed. That motion, caused by the sudden opening and closing of the water, creates in it a certain shaking <term>shaking (riscotimento): a jolting or vibrating disturbance</term> which can be called a tremor <note>original: "tremore"</note> rather than a movement. And so that what I say may be more manifest to you, pay attention to those straws <note>original: "fesstuche" - small bits of straw or light debris</note> which, because of their lightness, stay on top of the water; they do not move from their original site despite the wave made under them by the arrival of the circles. Therefore, this disturbance of the water being a tremor rather than a movement, the circles cannot break one another upon meeting; because water, having all its parts of one same quality, must necessarily pass this tremor from one part to the next without changing their place. For since the water stays in its site, it can easily take this tremor from neighboring parts and pass it to other neighbors, always diminishing its power until the end.

---

— The reason is that if the circles, which are wide at the top, are reduced to a single point, they submerge; and following their motion along the course they have begun, they end up at the bottom creating a motion contrary to the one above when they disperse from their center.

These quantities, as they grow, come to encounter one another and then incorporate themselves by intersecting one circle with the other, always maintaining the places struck by the stones as their centers. <summary>Leonardo explains that wave propagation in water is a "tremor" or vibration rather than the physical transport of water molecules, using this to explain how waves and sounds can intersect without destroying each other. He also notes how surface vortices create counter-movements at the bottom of a water body.</summary>

# ON WATER (WHIRLS, CIRCULAR MOTIONS).

->**WATER AT ITS BOTTOM CREATES EDDIES WITH A MOTION CONTRARY TO THAT AT THE SURFACE.**<-

— The reason for this is that if the circles, which are wide at the surface, reduce themselves to a point and submerge while continuing their motion in the direction they first began, a motion is created at the bottom that is contrary to the one above when it separates from its center <note>Leonardo is describing a vortex or "sinkhole" effect where the surface rotation and the deep-water return flow move in opposing directions</note>.

— Although the voices <note>original: "voix" - here referring to sound waves</note> that penetrate this air move away from their sources in circular motions, nevertheless, circles set in motion by different origins meet without any hindrance; they penetrate and pass through one another, always keeping their original sources as their centers.

— Since, in all cases of motion, water has a great similarity to air, I will cite it as an example to demonstrate the aforementioned proposition. I say that if you throw two small stones at the same time, some distance from each other, onto a large expanse of still water, you will see two separate sets of circles produced around the two impacts <term>impacts (percussions): the strike of an object against a surface</term>. These sets of circles, as they increase in size, will come to meet and incorporate [penetrate] one another; all the circles of each set will cut through the others while maintaining the spots struck by the stones as their centers. And the reason is that, although one sees some appearance of movement there, the water does not change its place, given that the openings made by the stones were immediately closed again. This rapid opening and closing of the water gives it a certain shock, which can be regarded as a trembling rather than a movement. So that what I say may appear more evident to you, consider those straws <note>original: "fétus" - small bits of straw or chaff used as floats to track current</note> which, because of their lightness, remain on the water; the arrival of the circles in the wave beneath them does not make them change their position. This impression in the water being, therefore, a trembling rather than a movement, the circles cannot break when they meet; for, since all parts of the water are of the same quality [identical], it is necessary for its parts to transmit the trembling to one another without changing place. Because the water does not change its place, it can easily take the trembling from neighboring parts and transmit it to other neighbors, its force always diminishing in this transmission until the end.

---

**1** <margin>See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water*, p. 356, book 4, chapter LIII.</margin>

<summary>Leonardo explains that wave propagation in water and air is a transmission of "trembling" or energy rather than the physical displacement of the medium itself, using the example of intersecting ripples and floating straws.</summary>

- FOLIO 61 (verso). -

<note>Manuscript facsimile section (mirror script transcribed left-to-right)</note>

oil lamp

<note>Diagram of a multi-pulley system with a horizontal beam and vertical tackle detail</note>

reasoning <note>original: "ragione" — here meaning the mathematical or logical principle behind the machine</note>

Suppose that *a. g.* is one pulley block and similarly *b. h.* is the other one below, which I wish to raise to the level of the line *m. n.* I say that to do this, it is necessary that the final part of the rope *g. h.* be lowered in such a way that the part *g.* descends to *h.* Wherefore it is necessary that the rope which will remain between *g. n.* be that which will come out of the places *m. b. o. p. d. q. r. f. s.* Therefore, to return to the reasoning promised above: when *m. b. o.* comes out of its place, it will be pulled by *p. d. q.*, which will come out of its place with a doubled rope; because in coming out of its place, the beam *b. h.* would not rise at all if as much rope came to it as there is in *m. b. o.* And likewise the rope *r. f. s.* will come out of its place tripled, because the rope *m. b. o.* passes through there, and likewise *p. d. q.* Therefore, when the angle *b.* moves one cubit <term>braccio: an Italian measure of length, literally an 'arm', roughly 23 inches</term> of rope, the angle *d.* will move 2, and the angle *f.* will move 3; whereby it will endure 3 times as much labor as *b.*, because it moves 3 times faster than *b.* I do not deny that each rope feels an equal weight, but the unequal movement will be more laborious for its mover. For example <note>original: "verbi gratia"</note>: let a weight of [certain] pounds be drawn one cubit with slow movement; then let that same mover draw that said weight with fast motion; he will endure so much more labor and feel so much more distress as the second course is further [faster] than the first.

<note>Geometric/optical diagram of light passing through an aperture</note>

That perforation of a round quality which is half-closed in *e* will appear in the form *a b*, and part *c* will be the light; *h* and *n* will be the enclosed part. And this same thing happens to the moon when it is half-luminous.

<note>Printed commentary and transcription section</note>

### ON MOTION AND FORCE

— Among things moved by a single cause, that which is of faster motion will be of more detriment to its mover. —

### EXAMPLE

— Suppose that *a. g.* is one pulley block and similarly *b. h.* is the other below, which I wish to raise to the level of the line *m. n.* I say that to do this, it is necessary that the final part of the rope *g. h.* be lowered in such a way that the part *g.* descends into *h.* Wherefore it is necessary that the rope which will remain between *g. n.* be that which will come out of the places *m. b. o. - p d q - r. f s.* Therefore, to return to the reasoning promised above: when *m. b. o.* comes out of its place, it will be pulled by *p. d. q.*, which will come out of its place with a doubled rope; because in coming out of its place, the beam *b. h.* would not rise at all if as much rope came to it as there is in *m. b. o.* And likewise the rope *r. f. s.* will come out of its place tripled, because the rope *m. b. o.* passes through there, and likewise *p. d. q.* Therefore, when the angle *b.* moves one cubit of rope, the angle *d.* will move 2, and the angle *f.* will move 3; whereby it will endure 3 times as much labor as *b.*, because it moves 3 times faster than *b.* I do not deny that each rope feels an equal weight, but the unequal movement will be more laborious for its mover. For example, let a weight of [certain] pounds be drawn one cubit with slow movement; then let that same mover draw that said weight with fast motion; he will endure so much more labor and feel so much more distress as the second course is further than the first.

— That perforation of a round quality which is half-closed in *e* will appear in the form *a. b.*, and part *c.* will be the light; *h* and *n.* will be the enclosed part. And this same thing happens to the moon when it is half-luminous.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical advantage of pulley systems, noting that increased speed in a machine requires greater effort from the mover. He also includes a brief optical study comparing light through a partial aperture to the phases of the moon.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, pulleys, force, velocity, work, optics, lunar phases, geometry</keywords>

# ON MOVEMENT AND FORCE (PULLEYS) — LIGHT.

## ON MOVEMENT AND FORCE.

— Among things moved by the same cause, that which has a faster movement will cause more damage to its motor <note>The "motor" refers to the source of power or the mechanical component driving the action.</note>.

### EXAMPLE:

— Suppose that **a g** is one of the sides of a pulley block <term>moufle: a system of several pulleys in a single frame, often called a block and tackle</term>, and similarly **b h** is the other one below, and that I wish to raise this latter one *up* to the line **m n**. I say that to obtain this, it is necessary that the last part of the rope **g h** be lowered in such a way that the part **g** descends to **h**; from which it follows that [the part of] the rope which will remain between **g** and **n** will be that which comes out of the positions **m b o**, **p d q**, **r f s**. Therefore, to return to the demonstration promised above, when **m b o** comes out of its place, it will be pulled by **p d q**, which will come out of its place with a doubled rope, because, in coming out of its place, the beam **b h** would not rise at all if there only came as much rope as is in **m b o**. And thus, the part of the rope **r f s** will come out of its place tripled, because the part of the rope **m b o** passes through there as well as **p d q**; therefore, when the angle **b** moves one cubit <term>brasse: a unit of measurement based on the reach of the arms, roughly 5 to 6 feet</term> of rope, the angle **d** will move two and the angle **f** will move three, so that it will endure three times as much fatigue as **b**, because it moves with three times more speed than **b**. I do not deny that each rope feels an equal weight, but the unequal movement is more tiring for its motor; as for example, if a weight of two pounds is pulled for one cubit by a slow movement, and then the same motor pulls *this* said weight with a rapid movement, it will endure a fatigue and experience a hardship [a damage] that is greater in proportion to how much more the second path is prolonged than the first.

---

— The hole of round shape which is half closed *and in* will appear to have the shape **a b**, the part **c** being the light, and **h** and **n** being the closed part. And this same effect occurs for the half-luminous moon.

<summary>This page explains the mechanical principle that increased speed in a pulley system results in greater strain or "fatigue" on the components, followed by an optical note on how partially obscured circular apertures create crescent-shaped light similar to the moon.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, pulleys, velocity, mechanical advantage, friction, optics, light, lunar phases</keywords>

<note>Study of human facial proportions featuring a profile, a faint intermediate profile, and a frontal view with alignment lines and lettered reference points.</note>

<margin>63</margin>
The cut or rather the edge of the lower lip of the mouth is the midpoint between the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the chin. — *the width* <note>The artist uses "taglio" (cut) to refer to the horizontal line where the lips meet or the defined edge of the lip.</note>

The face forms a square within itself; that is, its width is from one outer corner of the eye to the other, and its height is from the top end of the nose to the bottom of the lower lip of the mouth. Then, what remains above and below this square has the height of a similar square.

— **a. b.** is equal to the space between **c. d.** — **d. n.** and likewise **n. c.** and similarly **s. r.** — **q. p.** — **h. k.** are equal among themselves — the distance from **m. s.** is the same as from the bottom of the nose to the chin.

— The ear is exactly as long as the nose — the distance from **x. s.** is the same as from the nose to the chin — the cut of the mouth in profile is directed toward the angle of the <term>jawbone</term><note>original: "massciella"</note> — the ear should be as high as the distance from the bottom of the nose to the top of the eyelid.

— The space between the eyes is as great as the size of one eye — the ear falls in the middle of the neck in profile — the distance from **4.** to **5.** is the same as from **5.** to **r.**

- FOLIO 63 (verso). -

<note>This page contains Leonardo da Vinci’s observations on hydraulic engineering, specifically how river water erodes its banks. Leonardo uses the analogy of a bouncing ball to explain how water reflects off one bank to strike the other, creating dangerous whirlpools and deep hollows.</note>

<note>Drawing: A horizontal sketch illustrating water striking a river bank at an angle, creating serpentine currents and a large spiral vortex (eddy) downstream from the point of impact.</note>

... the serpentine courses of the waters, which are caused by the rebounds of the percussions <note>impacts</note> made by them between the banks, will hollow out the bed of the river beneath them more than in any other part; and in their percussions, they will be the cause of great depth and excavations; and the water that is near these depths will swirl around and be the cause of hollows and the ruin of the embattled banks —

It is clearly seen and recognized that the waters which strike the bank of rivers act in the likeness of balls struck against walls, which depart from them at angles similar to those of the percussion <note>the law of reflection: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection</note> and go on to strike the opposite faces of the walls. So these waters, having made the first percussions between the banks, rebound to the opposite ones and there make a great percussion and excavation, because in that place there is a greater concourse of water. The reason is that the water which rebounds from one bank to another hollows out that part of the bottom of the river which finds itself beneath it; and the other water of the river, which cannot be received in this low point, remains pushed and repelled somewhat along the straight line of the river. And after the force of its flight has failed, it returns to its natural course; that is, finding the bottom of the river lower beneath the twisted paths made by the aforementioned percussions of the waters, this second water—which has lost its accidental flight—takes up its natural course again and falls into the low places of the river and strikes the banks in the same place where the percussion of the aforementioned rebounds is made. Since the said bank is struck by two different percussions, a greater cavity is caused there, because one strikes and erodes the bank from above, and the other, with greater declination <note>downward slope</note>, gnaws and undermines the bank at the bottom; and this is the cause of the aforementioned ruins and collapses of the banks —

---

->THE SERPENTINE COURSES OF THE WATERS WHICH ARE CAUSED BY THE REBOUNDS OF THE PERCUSSIONS MADE BY THEM BETWEEN THE BANKS WILL HOLLOW OUT THE BED OF THE RIVER BENEATH THEM MORE THAN IN ANY OTHER PART AND IN THEIR PERCUSSIONS THEY WILL BE THE CAUSE OF GREAT DEPTH AND EXCAVATIONS AND THE WATER THAT IS NEAR THESE DEPTHS WILL SWIRL AROUND AND BE THE CAUSE OF HOLLOWS AND THE RUIN OF THE EMBATTLED BANKS —<-

— It is clearly seen and recognized that the waters which strike the bank of rivers act in the likeness of balls struck against walls, which depart from them at angles similar to those of the percussion and go on to strike the opposite faces of the walls. So these waters, having made the first percussions between the banks, rebound to the opposite ones and there make a great percussion and excavation, because in that place there is a greater concourse of water. The reason is that the water which rebounds from one bank to another hollows out that part of the bottom of the river which finds itself beneath it; and the other water of the river, which cannot be received in this low point, remains pushed and repelled somewhat along the straight line of the river. And after the force of its flight has failed, it returns to its natural course; that is, finding the bottom of the river lower beneath the twisted paths made by the aforementioned percussions of the waters, this second water—which has lost its accidental flight—takes up its natural course again and falls into the low places of the river and strikes the banks in the same place where the percussion of the aforementioned rebounds is made. Since the said bank is struck by two different percussions, a greater cavity is caused there, because one strikes and erodes the bank from above, and the other, with greater declination, gnaws and undermines the bank at the bottom; and this is the cause of the aforementioned ruins and collapses of the banks —

<summary>Leonardo explains how river water erodes banks through a "rebound" effect similar to a bouncing ball, creating serpentine flows and deep vortices. He notes that banks collapse because they are struck simultaneously by surface reflections and deep-current undermining.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, river erosion, fluid dynamics, vortices, percussion, reflection, embankments, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s study of hydraulic dynamics, specifically focusing on the "serpentine" or winding movement of river currents and how they erode banks through reflected force, following the principles established on the previous page.</meta>

# ON WATER (ACTION OF CURRENTS).

THE WINDING CURRENTS OF THE WATERS, WHICH ARE CAUSED BY THE REBOUNDS OF THE IMPACTS THAT THESE WATERS HAVE MADE AGAINST THE DIKES, WILL HOLLOW OUT THEIR BED BENEATH THEM MORE THAN IN ANY OTHER PART; THEY WILL BECOME, IN THEIR IMPACTS, THE *causes of excavations* OF GREAT DEPTHS, AND THE WATER WHICH, NEAR THESE DEPTHS, SWIRLS AROUND, WILL BE THE CAUSE OF EXCAVATIONS AND RUINS FOR THE BANKS THAT THE RIVER ATTACKS¹.

— And one clearly sees and understands that the waters which strike the banks of rivers act in the same way as balls that strike against walls, returning at an angle equal to that of the impact and then going on to strike the opposite faces of the walls. It is indeed in this way that the waters, after having first struck one bank, rebound toward the other, which they strike and hollow out forcefully, because at this latter location there is a greater gathering of water. The reason for this is that the water which rebounds from one bank to the other hollows out the part of the riverbed that lies beneath it; and the other water of the river, which cannot be received in this low part, remains somewhat pushed back and rejected into the direct current of the river, and, as *the force of its flight fails it* <insert>and as it cannot escape</insert>, it returns to its natural course; that is to say, the riverbed being lower beneath the winding paths made by the aforementioned impacts of the waters, *the water which—the second water, which has lost its accidental flight* <note>Leonardo refers to "accidental" motion as the forced movement caused by the rebound, as opposed to "natural" motion caused by gravity</note>, resumes its natural course, falls into the low places of the river, and strikes the banks at the same spot where the impact of the aforementioned rebounds occurs. The said *place bank* being thus attacked by two different impacts, greater excavations are produced there, because, while one strikes *and gnaws* the bank from above, the other, which has a steeper slope, gnaws and undermines it from the bottom, and, in doing so, is the cause of the aforementioned ruins and demolitions of the banks.

---

1. See: *Treatise on the Motion and Measurement of Water* <note>original: "Trattato del moto e misura dell'acqua"</note>, p. 382, book 6, chapter XX.

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of river erosion using the analogy of a bouncing ball, describing how water rebounds from one bank to undermine the opposite side. He details how the combination of surface impact and deep-water undermining leads to the collapse of riverbanks.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, river currents, erosion, bank stability, angle of incidence, fluid dynamics, Leonardo da Vinci, Treatise on Water</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigations into optics, specifically focusing on how light behaves when passing through apertures and how the human eye perceives the size of distant luminous bodies like candles and the sun.</meta>

— The intersection of the luminous rays <note>original: "razi luminosi"</note> made between the sides of the square aperture <note>original: "spiracolo," a small hole or vent</note> occurs beyond said sides. And the intersection made in its corners occurs within the thickness of the corner.

— Every aperture, over a long distance, projects the form of its source onto the object.

— No aperture can change the convergence of luminous rays in such a way that, over a long distance, they do not project the likeness of their cause <note>i.e., the shape of the light source itself</note> onto the object.

— It is impossible for luminous rays passing through parallel lines to demonstrate the form of their cause upon the object.

— Because all effects of luminous bodies are demonstrators of their causes. The moon, being boat-shaped <note>original: "navichulare," referring to a crescent moon</note>, when passed through an aperture, will figure a boat-shaped body upon the object.

— Because the eye sees distant things as larger than they measure upon the wall <note>referring to the plane of projection or the retina</note>.

### PROOF OF HOW LUMINOUS BODIES APPEAR LARGER FROM AFAR THAN THEY ARE

— If you place two burning candles near one another, half a cubit <note>original: "braccio," a Florentine measure of about 23 inches</note> apart, and you move 200 cubits away from them, you will see that through the growth of each, they become a single luminous body made of two lights, and it will appear as a single light one cubit wide.

### PROOF TO SEE THE TRUE SIZE OF LUMINOUS BODIES

— If you wish to see the true size of these luminous bodies, take a thin board <note>original: "assetta"</note> and make a hole in it the size of a small lace-tag <note>original: "puntale," the metal tip of a lace</note>. Hold it as close to the eye as you can, so that by looking through the hole at the aforementioned light, you see a great deal of air space around it. Thus, by quickly removing and replacing this board from your eye, you will quickly see that light grow and shrink.

### PROOF OF THE INCREASE OF THE SUN IN THE WEST <note>original: "ponente," the setting sun</note>

— Some mathematicians demonstrate that the sun increases in the west because the eye always sees it through air of greater thickness, alleging that things seen in the fog <note>original: "nebia"</note> and in water appear larger. To whom I respond: no. For things seen within fog are similar in color to distant things, and not being similar in their diminution <note>the way they shrink with distance</note>, they appear to be of greater size. Furthermore, nothing increases in flat water, and you can test this by looking at a board half-submerged under the water. But the reason the sun grows is this: every luminous body appears larger the further it is removed.

<summary>Leonardo explores the "camera obscura" effect and the deceptive nature of visual perception, arguing that luminous objects like the sun or candles appear to expand at a distance due to optical principles rather than atmospheric magnification.</summary>
<keywords>optics, light rays, aperture, visual perception, sun, atmospheric perspective, geometry of vision</keywords>

**Visual Description:**
The background of this page features a traditional marbled paper pattern. This decorative style, often called a "snail" or "shell" pattern, consists of swirling, organic shapes. The colors include a deep terracotta red that forms the "veins" of the pattern, surrounding larger cells of golden yellow and a mottled grey-green. This type of paper was a standard choice for high-quality library bindings in the late 19th century to protect the inner contents of the manuscript facsimile.
